Commentary on Luke

Latin source: Ambrose's exposition of the Gospel according to Luke, in a prologue and ten books, reworked from sermons he preached at Milan (about 377–389), from the critical text of M. Adriaen, Corpus Christianorum, Series Latina 14 (Turnhout, 1957), pp. 1–400, verified against the page images and collated with Schenkl's edition, Corpus Scriptorum Ecclesiasticorum Latinorum 32/4 (Vienna, 1902), with the edition's misprints corrected. The lemmata from Luke and all other Scripture are translated as Ambrose quotes them, from his own Old Latin text; italics mark the lemmata and the other Scripture he quotes. Psalms keep the Latin (LXX) numbering, with the Hebrew number in parentheses where it differs. The section numbers are the Maurist ones shared by all editions. Greek words are kept as printed, and Latin words Ambrose discusses as words are kept with an English gloss in square brackets. Adriaen's supplements are read into the text, the words he brackets as spurious are omitted, the one lacuna (Book VII §15) is marked ⟨...⟩, and his critical apparatus is not translated. This English translation is released into the public domain. Other English translations: Íde M. Ní Riain, Commentary of Saint Ambrose on the Gospel according to Saint Luke (Dublin: Halcyon Press, in association with Elo Publications, 2001; ISBN 978-1-902232-26-3), complete.

Prologue

1. As we set out to write on the book of the Gospel which Saint Luke arranged with a certain fuller distribution of the Lord's deeds, we think we ought first to explain its very style; for it is historical. For although divine Scripture empties out the discipline of worldly wisdom, because that wisdom is painted over with a greater display of words than it is propped up by the reason of things, yet if anyone seeks in the divine Scriptures even those things which they think marvellous, he will find them.

2. For there are three things which the philosophers of this world have thought most excellent, namely that wisdom is threefold: that it is either natural or moral or rational. These three we have been able to observe already in the Old Testament as well. For what else do those three wells signify, of which one is the well of vision, another of abundance, the third of the oath, but that this threefold virtue was in the patriarchs? The well of vision is rational, because reason sharpens the sight of the mind and purifies the gaze of the soul; the well of abundance is ethical, because when the foreigners withdrew, under whose likeness the vices of the body are figured, Isaac found the water of a living mind — for good morals flow forth pure, and goodness itself, generous to the people, abounds for others while more sparing toward itself — the third is the well of the oath, that is, of natural wisdom, which comprehends the things that are above nature or belong to nature; for what one affirms and swears to, as it were with God as witness, embraces divine things too, since the Lord of nature is called in as witness to good faith. What too do the three books of Solomon, one of Proverbs, another Ecclesiastes, the third of the Song of Songs, show us but that holy Solomon was skilled in this threefold wisdom? He wrote of rational and ethical things in Proverbs, of natural things in Ecclesiastes, because vanity of vanities, and all is vanity that is set in this world; to vanity, indeed, the creature has been subjected; and of wonderful and rational things in the Song of Songs, because when the love of the heavenly Word is poured into our soul and the mind is joined to reason in a kind of holy fellowship, wondrous mysteries are revealed.

3. And what wisdom do you think the evangelists too lacked, of whom, though some are filled with various kinds, yet each excels in a different kind? For natural wisdom is truly in the book of the Gospel which is written according to John. For no one, I dare to say, has seen the majesty of God with such sublimity of wisdom and unlocked it to us in his own speech. He passes beyond the clouds, passes beyond the powers of heaven, passes beyond the angels, and found the Word in the beginning and saw the Word with God. And who, following out each thing according to man, has set forth for us precepts of living more morally than Saint Matthew? What is more rational than that wonderful joining which Saint Mark thought should be placed right at the beginning: Behold, I send my angel and the voice of one crying in the desert, so that he might both stir wonder and teach that man must please by humility and abstinence and faith, just as that Saint John the Baptist climbed to immortality by these steps: his clothing, his food, his message?

4. But Saint Luke kept, as it were, a kind of historical order and revealed to us more of the miracles of the Lord's deeds, yet in such a way that the history of this Gospel embraced the virtues of all wisdom. For what is more excellent for natural wisdom than his disclosing that the Holy Spirit was the creator even of the Lord's Incarnation? So he teaches natural things, if the Spirit creates. Hence David too, teaching natural wisdom, send forth he says your Spirit, and they shall be created. He teaches moral things in the same book, when in those beatitudes he teaches me morals: how I ought to love my enemy, how I ought not to strike back and return the blow of one who strikes me, how to do good, to lend with no hope of recovery and with the recompense of a reward; for the reward follows more readily the one who does not expect it. He taught rational things too, when I read that he who is faithful in the least is faithful also in the greater. What more shall I say of natural things? That he taught that the powers of heaven are moved, that the only-begotten Son of God alone is Lord, at whose Passion darkness came about during the day, the earth was darkened, the sun fled back?

5. Therefore all the primacy which worldly prudence falsely claims for itself, spiritual wisdom truly possesses, especially since — to venture something rather boldly — our very faith, the very mystery of the Trinity, cannot exist without this threefold wisdom, unless we believe that he is naturally Father who begot the Redeemer for us, and that he is ethical who, obedient to the Father unto death according to man, redeemed us, and that he is the rational Spirit who poured into human hearts the reason for worshipping the Godhead and ruling life. Nor let anyone think that we have made a difference of authority or of power, since with this slander he could assail even Paul. For neither did he make a difference when he said: There are divisions of graces, but the same Spirit; and there are divisions of ministries, but the same Lord; and there are divisions of operations, but the same God, who works all things in all. For the Son works all things and in all, as you have elsewhere that Christ is all and in all. The Holy Spirit works too, because one and the same Spirit works all things, dividing to each as he wills. So there is no difference of operations, no distinction, where, whether in the Father or in the Son or in the Holy Spirit, there is a fullness of power second to none.

6. Let us therefore consider these things carefully when we read, so that they may shine out more clearly for us in the passages themselves. For he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it shall be opened. Diligence opens for itself the door of truth; and so let us obey the heavenly precepts. For not idly was it said to man, as to none of the other living creatures: In the sweat of your face you shall eat your bread; for to those animals which are irrational by nature the earth was commanded by God's order to supply fodder, but to man alone, that he may exercise the reason he received, the course of life is prescribed in labor. For he who is not content with the fodder of the other animals, for whom the fruit-bearing tree given to all in common for food is not enough, but who searches out for himself the delicacies of varied banquets, fetches delicacies for himself from lands beyond the sea, sweeps delicacies out of the waves, ought not, since he seeks his living by labor, to refuse to undergo a brief labor for eternal life. So if anyone, coming into these contests of sacred disputations, strips off the anxiety of this life, exposed as it is to error, and, an athlete naked of malice, anointed with the spiritual oil of devotion as on certain limbs of the soul, takes up the contests of truth, he will without doubt earn the everlasting prizes of sacred crowns. For the fruit of good labors is noble, and the more the contests, the more excellent the crown of virtues.

7. But let us return to our subject. We have said that this book of the Gospel is arranged in historical style. Indeed, we see that more abundant zeal has been spent, in comparison with the others, on describing events than on setting out precepts. And the evangelist himself, in the manner of a historian, took his beginning from a narrative. There was he says in the days of Herod king of Judaea a certain priest named Zechariah, and he pursues that history in full arrangement. Hence too those who have thought that the four forms of living creatures which are revealed in Revelation are to be understood as the four books of the Gospel will have this book figured by the likeness of a calf; for the calf is a priestly victim. And this book of the Gospel fits the calf well, because it began with priests and ended with the calf who, taking on himself the sins of all, was sacrificed for the life of the whole world; for that calf too was priestly. For the same one is both calf and priest: priest, because he is our propitiator — for we have him as advocate with the Father — calf, because he redeemed us with his blood. And it fell out well that, since we said that the book of the Gospel according to Matthew is moral, an opinion of this kind should not be passed over; for morals are properly called human.

8. Many, however, think that our Lord himself is figured in the four books of the Gospel by the four forms of living creatures, because the same one is man, the same lion, the same calf, the same eagle: man, because he was born of Mary; lion, because he is the stronger; calf, because he is a victim; eagle, because he is resurrection. And so in the individual books the form of the living creatures is figured in such a way that the course of each book seems to fit either the nature or the power or the grace or the miracle of the living creatures set before us. And though all these are in all, yet in each there is a certain fullness of particular virtues. One described more fully the birth of the man, and also instructed the morals of man with more abundant precepts; another began with the expression of divine power, telling how, King from King, Strong from Strong, True from True, he scorned death with living power; the third put first the priestly sacrifice and spread out the very sacrifice of the calf in a certain fuller style; the fourth expressed more copiously than the others the miracles of the divine resurrection. One, then, is all things and one in all, as it is read, not unlike himself in each, but true in all. But now let us take up the very discourse of the Gospel.

Book One

1. Since he says many have attempted to set in order a narrative of the things. Most of our affairs, like those of the ancient Jews, are shaped by like kinds and causes, and agree in the like use and outcome of similar examples, and accord in the beginning and the end of things. For just as many among that people, filled with the divine Spirit, prophesied, while others promised that they would prophesy and betrayed their profession by a lie — for they were false prophets rather than prophets, like Ananias son of Azot; but the people had the grace of discerning spirits, so that it might know whom it ought to reckon in the number of the prophets, and whom, like a good money-changer, it ought to reject, those in whom corrupted matter rather showed foul than the splendor of true light shone back — so now too in the New Testament many have attempted to write gospels, which the good money-changers did not approve; but they judged that one alone, arranged in four books, was to be chosen out of them all.

2. And indeed another gospel is circulated, which the Twelve are said to have written. Basilides too dared to write a gospel, which is called the gospel according to Basilides. Another gospel too is circulated, which is entitled according to Thomas. I know another written according to Matthias. We read some things so that they may not be read; we read so as not to be ignorant; we read, not to hold them, but to repudiate them, and so that we may know what sort of things they are in which these magnificent men lift up their heart. But the Church, though it has four books of the Gospel, abounds with evangelists throughout the whole world; the heresies, though they have many, do not have one. Many indeed have attempted, but they are bereft of the grace of God. Many too have crammed into one, out of the four books of the Gospel, whatever they thought agreeable to their poisoned assertions. Thus the Church, which has one Gospel, teaches one God; but those who assert one God of the Old Testament and another of the New have made out of many gospels not one God, but many.

3. Since many he says have attempted. Those surely attempted who were not able to complete it. So Saint Luke too testifies with a richer testimony that many began and did not complete, saying that very many attempted. For he who attempted to set in order attempted it by his own labor, and did not complete it. For the gifts and the grace of God are without effortful attempt: when it has poured itself in, it is wont to water, so that the writer's talent does not lack but overflows. Matthew did not attempt, Mark did not attempt, John did not attempt, Luke did not attempt; but, the divine Spirit supplying abundance of words and of all things, they completed what they had begun without any striving. And so he says well: since many have attempted to set in order a narrative of the things which have been fulfilled in us, or which abound in us.

4. For what abounds fails no one, and no one doubts what is fulfilled, since the effect builds up faith and the outcome reveals it. And so the Gospel is fulfilled, and abounds for all the faithful throughout the whole world, and waters the minds of all and strengthens the spirit. So he who is founded on the rock and has taken up the whole fullness of faith and the firm support of constancy rightly says: which have been fulfilled in us, since those who describe the saving deeds of the Lord, or who direct their mind to his wonders, distinguish true and false not by signs and prodigies but by the word. For what is so reasonable as that, when you read of those deeds which are above man, you should believe them to belong to a higher nature, but when you read of those which are mortal, you should believe them to be the sufferings of the body he assumed? Thus our faith is founded on word and reason, not on signs.

5. As they handed down to us he says who from the beginning themselves saw and were ministers of the word. This manner of speaking is not fitting, that we should believe there was a ministering of the word rather than a hearing of it. But since it is not the uttered word that is signified but the substantial Word, that Word which was made flesh and dwelt in us, let us understand not a common word but that heavenly Word, to which the apostles ministered. And yet in Exodus it is read that the people saw the voice of the Lord, and certainly a voice is not seen but heard. For what is a voice but a sound, which is not discerned by the eyes but perceived by the ear? But Moses, with the loftiest insight, wished to declare that the voice of God is seen; for it is seen by the gaze of the inner mind; but in the Gospel it is not a voice that is seen but that which is more excellent than a voice, the Word. Hence Saint John the Evangelist too: That which was he says from the beginning, which we have heard and which we have seen, we have looked upon with our eyes, and our hands have searched concerning the Word of life: and the life appeared, and we have seen and we testify and we announce to you concerning the life which was with the Father and appeared to us. You see then that the Word of God was both seen by the apostles and heard. For they saw the Lord not only according to the body but also according to the Word; for they saw the Word who with Moses and Elijah saw the glory of the Word. For these saw Jesus, who saw him in his glory; others did not see him, who could see only the body; for Jesus is seen not with bodily but with spiritual eyes.

6. Indeed the Jews did not see him, though they were seeing him. Abraham saw him, for it is written: Abraham saw my day and rejoiced. So Abraham saw him, who certainly did not see the Lord in the body; but he who saw in the spirit saw in the body, whereas he who saw in the body and did not see in the spirit did not see, even in the very body, what he was seeing. Isaiah saw him, and because he saw in the spirit, he saw also in the body. Indeed he had he says no form nor comeliness. The Jews did not see him: blinded indeed was their foolish heart. He himself too testifies that he cannot be seen by the Jews, saying: Blind guides, straining out a gnat but swallowing a camel. Pilate did not see him; those did not see him who were crying out: Crucify, crucify him; for if they had seen, they would never have crucified the Lord of majesty. So he who saw God saw Emmanuel, that is, he saw God with us; but he who did not see God with us could not see him whom the virgin bore. Indeed those who did not believe the Son of God did not believe the Son of the virgin either.

7. What then is it to see God? I do not want you to ask me: ask the Gospel, ask the Lord himself, nay rather, hear him saying: Philip, he who has seen me has seen the Father also, who sent me. How do you say: Show us the Father? Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in me? Certainly a body is not seen in a body, nor a spirit seen in a spirit, but only that Father is seen in the Son, or this Son is seen in the Father; for unlike things are not seen in unlike things, but where there is unity of operation and of power, both the Son is seen in the Father and the Father in the Son. The works which I he says do, he also does. In the works Jesus is seen; in the works of the Son the Father too is discerned. He saw Jesus who saw that mystery in Galilee, since no one but the Lord of the world could change the elements. I see Jesus when I read that he smeared the eyes of the blind man with clay and restored his sight; for I recognize him who formed man from clay and breathed into him the spirit of living and the light of seeing. I see Jesus when he forgives sins; for no one can forgive sins but God alone. I see Jesus when he raises Lazarus, and those who saw did not see. I see Jesus, I see the Father also, when I lift my eyes to heaven, turn them to the seas, bend them back to the earth; his invisible things indeed are clearly seen, being understood through the things that are made.

8. As they handed down to us who from the beginning saw and were ministers of the word. There is a twofold virtue in the perfect man, that there be both intention and action. Both virtues, therefore, the holy evangelist ascribes to the apostles; for he says not only they saw, but also they were ministers of the word. Intention belongs to seeing, ministry to action; but the end of intention is action, the beginning of action is intention. And to use the apostles' own example: it is intention that Peter and Andrew, on hearing the voice of the Lord saying: I will make you fishers of men, left the oar without any delay and followed the Word. But action is not at once present in intention. Not even there is there yet action, but intention, where Peter says: Lord, why can I not follow you now? I will lay down my life for you. For there was the intention of suffering, but there was not yet the action, although there was already action in fasts, in vigils, in contempt of bodily pleasures; for this is the action of a Christian. For intention and action are not present together in all things, but while there is action in one matter, there is still intention in another. For Peter himself too, though he had carried through the enduring of many things with apostolic virtue, yet afterwards, when the Lord said to him: Follow me, took up his cross and followed the Word and underwent the action of suffering. But granted that in Peter, Andrew, John and the other apostles there was an equality both of intention and of action:

9. yet there is sometimes more in intention than in action, or more in action than in intention, as we see in the Gospel that there was a difference between Saint Mary and Martha. For the one was listening to the word, the other was busy about serving. She stood and said: Lord, do you not care that she has left me to serve alone? Tell her then to help me. And he said to her: Martha, Martha, Mary has chosen the best part, which shall not be taken from her. So in the one the zeal of intention overflowed, in the other the ministry of action; yet each had at hand the zeal of both virtues, since Martha too, had she not heard the word, would not have undertaken the ministry, and her action is a token of intention; and Mary gained so much grace from the perfection of both virtues that she anointed the feet of Jesus and wiped them with her hair and filled the whole house with the fragrance of her faith. There is also sometimes the greatest intention and an empty action, as if someone should apply his mind to medicine and, though he has learned all the precepts of healing, should not carry out its duties; and so it comes about that, because the action is empty, the intention too is empty. There is also in some at times a richer action and a thinner intention, as if someone should receive the sacraments of saving baptism and be unwilling to apply his mind to learning the precepts of the various virtues: it often happens that through neglect of intention he loses the fruit of action. Therefore the fullness of both virtues is to be sought, which the apostles were able to attain, of whom he says: who from the beginning saw and were ministers, so that by their having seen the intention of divine knowledge may be understood, and by their having been ministers their action may be declared.

10. It seemed good he says to me. What he declares seemed good to him can have seemed good not to him alone. For it did not seem good by human will alone, but as it pleased him who he says speaks in me, Christ, who works so that what is good may seem good to us also; for whom he pities he also calls. And so he who follows Christ, when asked why he wished to be a Christian, can answer: It seemed good to me. When he says this, he does not deny that it seemed good to God; by God indeed the will of men is prepared. For that God should be honored by a saint is the grace of God. Indeed very many wished to write a gospel, but only four, who merited divine grace, were received.

11. It seemed good he says to me also, having followed all things diligently from the beginning in order. No one will doubt that this book of the Gospel is longer than the others. And so he claims for himself not things that are false but things that are true. Indeed he merited a testimony to his diligence from the apostle Saint Paul as well. For thus he praises Luke: whose praise he says is in the Gospel throughout all the churches. And truly praiseworthy is he who merited to be praised by so great a teacher of the Gentiles. So he says that he followed not a few things but all, and having followed all, it seemed good to him to write not all things but out of all; for he did not write all things, but he followed all things, since the things which Jesus did he says if they were all written, I do not think that even the world itself could contain them. For you notice that he deliberately passed over even those things which had been written by others, so that a varied grace might shine forth in the Gospel and the several books might stand out by certain wonders, proper to each, of mysteries and of deeds. For the soldiers of Christ divided among themselves the garments of the Lord, which will be explained more fully in its place.

12. Now the Gospel was written to Theophilus, that is, to him whom God loves. If you love God, it was written to you; if it was written to you, receive the gift of the evangelist. Keep carefully the pledge of a friend in the inner chambers of your mind; guard the good deposit through the Holy Spirit, who has been given to us; look into it frequently, examine it more often. Faith is owed first of all to a pledge; diligence follows faith, lest moth or rust consume the pledges entrusted to you; for what has been entrusted to you can be consumed. The Gospel is a good pledge, but see that neither moth nor rust consume it even in your mind.

13. The moth consumes if you believe badly what you have read well. The moth is the heretic, the moth is Photinus, your moth is Arius. He rends the garment who separates the Word from God. Photinus rends the garment when he reads: In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and God was; for the garment is whole if you read: and God was the Word. He rends the garment who separates Christ from God. He rends the garment who reads: Now this is eternal life, that they may know you, the only true God, unless he knows Christ also. For it is not only knowing the Father as truly God that is eternal life, but also knowing Christ as true God, true from true, God from God, is life everlasting. It is a moth to know Christ without faith in his divinity or the sacrament of his body. Arius is a moth, Sabellius is a moth. The spirit of the wavering suffers these moths; the spirit suffers these moths which does not believe that the Father and the Son are one in divinity. He rends what is written: I and the Father are one, who divides the One through separate substances. The spirit suffers this moth which does not believe that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh, and it is itself a moth; for he, he says, is the antichrist. But those who are of God hold the faith, and so cannot suffer the moth, which divides the garment. For everything that is divided against itself, like the kingdom of Satan, cannot be lasting.

14. There is also a rust of the mind, when the edge of religious intention is overcast with the filth of worldly desires, or the purity of faith is discolored by the cloud of unbelief. Rust of the mind is the desire of household property, rust of the mind is negligence, rust of the mind is the craving for honors, if the highest hope of the present life is placed in these. And so, turning to divine things, let us sharpen our wits, exercise our affection, so that that sword which the Lord bids be bought by selling a garment we may have always ready and bright, laid up as it were in the sheath of the mind. For spiritual arms, mighty through God for the destroying of fortifications, ought always to be at hand for the soldiers of Christ, lest, when the leader of the heavenly army comes, offended by the neglect of our arms, he should separate us from the fellowship of his legions.

There was he says in the days of Herod, king of Judaea, a certain priest named Zechariah, of the course of Abijah, and his wife was of the daughters of Aaron, and her name was Elizabeth. And they were both righteous before God, walking in all the commandments and justifications of the Lord without complaint.

15. Divine Scripture teaches us that not only the character of those who are worthy of praise, but also their parents ought to be praised, so that an inheritance of spotless purity, handed down as it were, may stand out in those whom we wish to praise. For what other intention had the holy evangelist in this place than that Saint John the Baptist should be ennobled by his parents, his miracles, his character, his office, his passion? So too Hannah, the mother of holy Samuel, is praised; so Isaac received from his parents a nobility of devotion, which he left to his descendants. Zechariah, then, was a priest, and not only a priest but also of the course of Abijah, that is, noble among the women of earlier times.

16. And his wife he says was of the daughters of Aaron. Not only from his parents, then, but also from his forebears the nobility of Saint John is propagated, not lofty by worldly power, but venerable by a succession of religion. For the forerunner of Christ ought to have such forebears, so that he might be seen to proclaim faith in the Lord's coming not as conceived of a sudden, but as received from his forebears and infused by the very right of nature.

17. They were he says both righteous, walking in all the commandments and justifications of the Lord without complaint. What do they reply to this who, holding out consolations for their own sins, think that a man cannot be without frequent sins, and use the little verse, because it is written in Job: No one is clean from filth, not even if his life is of one day; on earth his months are numbered by him? To them it must be answered that they first define what it is for a man to be without sin: whether never to have sinned at all, or to have ceased to sin. For if they think that to be without sin is this, to have ceased to sin, I too agree — all indeed have sinned and lack the glory of God — but if they deny that he who has corrected his old error and transformed himself into such a quality of life that he refrains from sin abstains from offences, I cannot come round to their opinion, since we read that the Lord so loved the Church as to present her to himself glorious, not having spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but that she might be holy and spotless. For since the Church has been gathered from the Gentiles, that is, from sinners, how can she be spotless out of the spotted, unless first by the grace of God, in that she has been washed from offence, and then in that by the quality of not sinning she abstains from offences? Thus she is not spotless from the beginning — for this is impossible for human nature — but through the grace of God and her own quality, because she no longer sins, it comes about that she is seen to be spotless.

18. Nor was it idly that he said righteous before God, walking in the commandments and justifications of the Lord, by which he includes the Father almighty and the Son. That it is the Son who brought the Law and prescribed the commandments the holy evangelist too declares. And well righteous before God; for not everyone who is righteous before man is righteous before God. Men see in one way, God in another: men in the face, God in the heart. And so it can happen that someone, by an affected popular goodness, seems righteous to me but is not righteous before God, if his righteousness is not formed from simplicity of mind but feigned through flattery; for man will not be able to detect what is hidden in it. Perfect praise, then, is to be righteous before God. Hence the Apostle too: whose praise he says is not from men but from God. Blessed indeed is he who is righteous in the sight of God; blessed he of whom the Lord deigns to say: Behold an Israelite indeed, in whom there is no guile; for he is a true Israelite who sees God and knows that he is seen by God, and lays open to him the secrets of his heart. For he alone is the more perfect who is approved by him who cannot be deceived; the judgments indeed of the Lord are true, but the judgments of men are often deceived, so that they frequently ascribe the credit of righteousness even to the unrighteous, and either persecute the righteous man with hatred or discolor him with lies. But the Lord knows the ways of the spotless, and holds neither the sinner for praiseworthy nor the praiseworthy for a sinner, but judges each by the reckoning of the merits that befit him; for he is the same judge of the mind and of the deed. The divine judgments measure the merit of the righteous by the disposition of the mind, not by any outcome of deeds; for often either a good mind is disfigured by the outcome of a blameworthy deed, or a wicked thought is veiled by the comeliness of some deed. But even what you have done well, if you have thought badly, cannot be approved by the divine judgment; for it is written: Justly shall you pursue what is just. For unless it were possible for you to do what is just unjustly, it would never have been said: Justly shall you pursue what is just. And certainly the Savior himself taught us that what is just can be done unjustly, saying: When you give alms, do not sound a trumpet before you. And: When you pray, you shall not be as the hypocrites. For mercy is good, prayer is good, but it can be done unjustly, if someone gives to a poor man for the sake of boasting, so as to be seen by men.

19. Hence the holy evangelist says not only righteous before God, and walking in all the commandments and justifications of the Lord, but also walking without complaint. This agrees wonderfully with the prophetic saying which holy Solomon used in Proverbs, saying: Provide good things always before God and before men. So there is no complaint where goodness both of mind and of deed are in accord; and often a harsher righteousness stirs up the complaint of men.

20. But observe carefully how fitting the very distinction of the words is, and how the order agrees. Walking he says in all the commandments and justifications of the Lord. For first comes the commandment, second is the justification. So when we obey the heavenly commandments, we walk in the commandments of the Lord; when we judge, and judge fittingly, we are seen to hold the justifications of the Lord.

21. Complete, then, is the praise, which includes lineage, character, office, deed, judgment: lineage in the forebears, character in equity, office in the priesthood, deed in the commandment, judgment in the justification.

And it came to pass, while Zechariah was performing the priesthood in the order of his course before the Lord God according to the custom of the priesthood, that it fell to him by lot to put on the incense, entering into the temple of the Lord; and the whole people was praying outside at the hour of incense.

22. Saint Zechariah seems here to be designated a high priest, because, as we have read, from the first tabernacle, into which the priests always entered in accomplishing their ministries, he entered into the temple at one time only of the year: but into the second, once a year, the high priest alone, not without blood, which he offers for himself and for the offences of the people. This is that high priest who is still sought by lot, because the true one is still unknown; for he who is chosen by lot is not comprehended by human judgment. That one, then, was being sought, and another was being figured. That one was being sought, the true priest for ever, to whom it is said: You are a priest for ever, who would reconcile God the Father to the human race not by the gore of victims but by his own. But then blood was shed in semblance, in semblance a priest was ordained; now, because the truth has come, let us leave the semblance and follow the truth. And then indeed there were courses, but now there is perpetuity. So he was, and certainly he was, whose place was even then being filled by turns.

23. By lot, then, the priest was chosen to enter the temple. If therefore in the type no witness could be brought in, what else was signified but that that priest would come whose sacrifice would not be in common with the rest, who would sacrifice for us not in temples made with hands, but in the temple of his own body would empty out our sins? By lot, then, the priest was sought. For that reason perhaps the soldiers too cast lots for the garments of the Lord: so that, since the Lord was preparing to offer sacrifice for us in his own temple, the shaking of the lot around him too might fulfil the precept of the Law. On this account he says: I have not come to destroy the Law, but to fulfil it, surely so that he might be the one who would be seen both as awaited by the Old Testament and as chosen by the commandment of God. Indeed the lot fell also upon the apostle Matthias, lest the choice of an apostle should seem to depart from the command of the old Law.

24. And there appeared to him an angel standing on the right of the altar of incense. Not without reason is an angel seen in the temple, because the coming of the true priest was already being announced, and a heavenly sacrifice was being prepared, in which angels would minister. And well is he said to have appeared to him who caught sight of him suddenly; and divine Scripture is wont to keep this especially of angels or of God, that what cannot be foreseen is said to appear; for so you have: God appeared to Abraham at the oak of Mamre. For he who is not perceived beforehand but is seen with a sudden sight is said to appear. For sensible things are not seen in the same way as he in whose will it lies to be seen, and whose nature it is not to be seen, but whose will it is to be seen. For if he does not will, he is not seen; if he wills, he is seen. For God appeared to Abraham because he willed; to another, because he did not will, he did not appear. Stephen too, when he was being stoned by the people, saw heaven opened, and Jesus too was seen standing at the right hand of God, and he was not seen by the people. Isaiah saw the Lord Sabaoth, but another could not see him, because he appeared to whom he pleased.

25. And why do we speak of men, when even of the heavenly virtues and powers themselves we have read that no one has ever seen God? And he added what is beyond the heavenly powers: the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he himself has declared. Either, then, it must be acknowledged, if no one has ever seen God the Father, that the Son was seen in the Old Testament, and let the heretics cease to give a beginning from the virgin to him who was seen before he was born of the virgin; or certainly it cannot be refuted that either the Father or the Son or certainly the Holy Spirit — if indeed there is a vision of the Holy Spirit — is seen in such a form as the will has chosen, not as nature has formed, since we have learned that the Spirit too was seen in a dove. And so no one has ever seen God, because no one has beheld that fullness of divinity which dwells in God, no one has grasped it with mind or eyes; for has seen is to be referred to both. Indeed when it is added: the only-begotten Son himself has declared, the seeing of minds rather than of eyes is made known; for a form is seen, but power is declared; the one is grasped by the eyes, the other by the mind.

26. But why speak of the Trinity? The seraph appeared when he willed, and Isaiah alone heard his voice. An angel appeared, and he is present now too, but he is not seen; for it is not in our power to see, but in his power to appear. Yet, though there is no power of seeing, there is the grace of meriting, so that we may be able to see. And so he who had the grace merited the opportunity; we do not merit the opportunity, because we do not have the grace of seeing God.

27. And what wonder if in the present world the Lord is not seen except when he wills? In the resurrection itself too it is not easy to see God, except for those who are pure in heart. And so: blessed are the pure in heart; for they shall see God. How many blessed ones he had already numbered, and yet he had not promised them the faculty of seeing God! If then those who are pure in heart shall see God, surely others shall not see him; for neither shall the unworthy see God, nor can he who has not wished to see God see God. Nor is God seen in a place, but with a pure heart; nor is God sought with bodily eyes, nor circumscribed by sight, nor held by touch, nor heard by address, nor perceived by his gait. And when he is thought absent he is seen, and when he is present he is not seen. Indeed not all the apostles were seeing Christ. And so he says: So long a time I am with you, and yet you have not known me! For he who has known what is the breadth and length and height and depth, and the love of Christ surpassing knowledge, has seen both Christ and the Father. For we no longer know Christ according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit; the Spirit indeed before our face is Christ the Lord, who in his mercy may deign to fill us unto all the fullness of God, so that he may be seen by us. For this reason, then, the angel appeared to Zechariah on the right of the altar of incense: because he appeared when he willed, and did not appear as long as he did not will.

28. And he appeared on the right of the altar of incense because he was bringing the token of divine mercy; the Lord indeed is at my right hand, that I may not be moved; and elsewhere: The Lord is your protection upon your right hand. And would that for us too, as we kindle the altars and bring the sacrifice, an angel may stand by, nay rather may offer himself to be seen! For you need not doubt that an angel stands by when Christ stands by, when Christ is sacrificed; for indeed Christ our pasch has been sacrificed. Nor should you fear that your heart may be troubled at the sight of the angel — for we are troubled and estranged from our own disposition when we are dazzled by the encounter of some higher power —; for the same angel who meets us will be able to strengthen us, as he strengthened Zechariah, troubled before, in mind, saying: Do not fear, Zechariah, for behold, your prayer has been heard, and your wife Elizabeth shall bear a son, and you shall call his name John; and he shall be a joy to you, and many shall rejoice at his birth.

29. Divine benefits are always full and overflowing, not confined within a small number but heaped up in a rich accumulation of good things, as here: first the fruit of prayer, then the childbearing of a barren wife, then the gladness of very many and greatness of virtue. A prophet of the Most High too is promised; nay more, lest there should be any doubt, the name of the one to come is also designated. Since so many things, then, were flowing beyond his prayer, not without reason is silence the penalty of distrust, which we shall explain in what follows. But there is a solemn gladness at the birth and begetting of saints; for a saint is not only a grace to his parents but also the salvation of very many. Hence we are admonished in this place to rejoice at the begetting of saints.

30. Parents too are admonished to give thanks no less for the birth than for the merits of their children; for it is no small gift of God to give children, propagators of the race, heirs of the succession. Read that Jacob rejoiced in the begetting of twelve sons; a son is given to Abraham; Zechariah is heard. The fruitfulness of a parent, then, is a divine gift. Let fathers therefore give thanks because they have begotten; sons, because they have been begotten; mothers, because they are honored with the rewards of marriage; for sons are the wages of their warfare. Let the earth bloom unto the praise of God because it is tilled, the world because it is known, the Church because it is increased by the number of a devoted people. Nor was marriage joined by the command of God at the very beginning of Genesis to no purpose, but so that heresy might be destroyed. For God so approved marriage as to join it, so rewarded it that divine love granted children to those whom barrenness had denied them.

31. And he shall be great before the Lord. Here he has declared a greatness not of body but of soul. There is before the Lord a greatness of soul, a greatness of virtue; there is also a smallness of soul and a childhood of virtue. For we reckon the ages of soul and of body alike, not by the reckoning of time but by the quality of virtue, so that he is called a perfect man who is free from the error of childhood and through maturity of mind does not feel the slipperiness of youth, but he is called little who seems to have made as yet no progress in virtue. Hence that saying in Jeremiah, when the Lord had pity on Ephraim weeping and begging pardon for his own sins: From my youth he says Ephraim is a beloved son to me, a child in delights. For had he not been a child in delights, he would never have sinned. And well did he say both, that he was in delights and that he was a child; for he is a child who does not sin. Behold my child, whom I have chosen. Through delights, then, he sinned who had been so formed by the Lord as to be ignorant of error. So if he had not been a child in delights, and if he had advanced into the perfect man by the age of virtue, he would never have fallen, so as to need to ask pardon for his offences, when he ought rather to have hoped for the rewards of merits. This our Lord too seems to express in the Gospel, when he says: Do not despise one of these little ones. But let more be kept for its own place. So the little one is the opposite of the great. And if, according to the Apostle, the little child is under the elements — when indeed we were little children, we were under the elements of this world — then the great one is above the elements of the world.

32. John, then, shall be great, not by strength of body but by greatness of soul. Indeed he did not extend the borders of any empire, nor did he prefer triumphs won with the glories of warlike contest; but, what is greater, preaching in the desert he subdued the delights of men and the wantonness of the body by great virtue of soul. Little, therefore, in the world, great in the spirit. Indeed, as one who was great, he was not caught by the allurements of life, nor did he change the constancy of his judgment through desire of living.

33. And with the Holy Spirit he says he shall be filled while still in his mother's womb. There is no doubt that this promise of the angel was true, since Saint John, before he was born, while still placed in his mother's womb, showed the grace of the Spirit he had received. For though neither his father nor his mother had done any wonders before, leaping in his parent's womb he proclaimed the good news of the Lord's coming. For so you have it that, when the mother of the Lord had come to Elizabeth, she said: Behold, as your greeting came into my ears, the infant leaped in my womb; for he did not yet have the spirit of life, but the spirit of grace. Indeed elsewhere too we have been able to observe that the grace of sanctifying can run ahead of the substance of living, when the Lord says: Before I formed you in the womb, I knew you, and before you came out of the womb, I sanctified you and set you as a prophet among the nations. For the spirit of this life is one thing, the spirit of grace another. The one takes its beginning in being born, its ending in dying; the other is not confined by times or by ages, is not extinguished by death, is not shut out by a mother's womb. Indeed both Saint Mary, filled with the Holy Spirit, prophesied, and Elisha, when dead, raised a man's dead body by the touch of his own body, and Samuel after death, by the testimony of Scripture, did not keep silent about things to come:

34. And with the Holy Spirit he says he shall be filled; for to whom the spirit of grace is present nothing is lacking, and in whom the Holy Spirit is poured there is a fullness of great virtues.

35. Indeed many he says of the sons of Israel he shall turn to the Lord their God. We need no testimony that Saint John turned the hearts of very many. In this the prophetic and evangelical Scriptures support us — the voice indeed of one crying in the desert: Prepare the way for the Lord, make straight his paths — and the baptisms thronged by the peoples declare that the progress of the converted people was not small, because while John is believed, Christ is believed. For it was not of himself but of the Lord that the forerunner of Christ preached.

36. And so he shall go before in the sight of the Lord in the spirit and power of Elijah. These are well joined; for never is the Spirit without power nor power without the Spirit. And so perhaps in the spirit and power of Elijah, because holy Elijah had both great power and grace: power, to turn back the minds of the peoples from unbelief to faith, the power of abstinence and patience, and the spirit of prophesying. In the desert Elijah, in the desert John; that one was fed by ravens, this one on brambles, and, trampling down every allurement of pleasure, he preferred frugality and despised luxury. That one did not seek the favor of King Ahab, this one spurned that of Herod; that one divided the Jordan, this one turned it to the saving washing; this one dwells with the Lord on earth, that one appears with the Lord in glory; this one is the forerunner of the Lord's first coming, that one of the following. That one after three years watered the earth with rains, and this one after three years drenched the drying soil of our body with the shower of faith. Do you ask what the three years are? Behold he says it is three years since I come seeking fruit on this fig tree, and I do not find it; for a mystical number was owed, that salvation might be restored to the peoples: one year in the patriarchs — indeed there was then such a yield of men from the year as there was not afterwards upon the earth — another in Moses and the other prophets, the third in the coming of the Lord Savior. Behold, he says, the acceptable year of the Lord and the day of retribution. And that householder who planted a vineyard sent collectors of the fruit not once but more often. First he sent servants, the second time other servants, but the third time he sent his son.

37. John came, then, in the spirit and power of Elijah; for the one cannot be without the other, as will also be found in what follows, when it is said: The Holy Spirit shall come upon you, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow you. But perhaps this example may seem to be above us and above the apostles; for that running back of the river's waters to the source of the stream, when the river was divided under Elijah, as Scripture has said: The Jordan was turned back, signifies the mysteries to come of the saving washing, through which those who have been baptized are re-formed as little ones from malice to the first beginnings of their nature. What of the fact that the Lord himself also promised his apostles that the power of the Spirit was to be bestowed, saying: You shall receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you? Then in what follows: there came he says suddenly a sound from heaven, as though the Spirit were borne with great force. Well with great force, because by the spirit of his mouth is all their power. And that is the power which the apostles obtained from the Holy Spirit.

38. Rightly too shall Saint John go before the face of the Lord, who was born a forerunner and died a forerunner. And perhaps this mystery is celebrated even today in this life of ours. For a certain virtue of John runs ahead of our soul when we prepare to believe in Christ, to prepare the ways of our soul for faith and make straight paths for our course out of the winding track of this life, lest we slip in the bend of error, so that every valley of our soul may be filled with the fruits of virtue, and every height of worldly merits may prostrate itself before the Lord in more humble fear, knowing that nothing can be lofty which is frail.

And Zechariah said to the angel: Whence shall I know this? For I am old, and my wife has advanced in her days. And the angel answered him, saying: I am Gabriel, the angel who stands before the Lord, and I have been sent to announce these things to you. And you shall be silent and shall not be able to speak until the day on which all these things come to pass, because you did not believe my words, which shall be fulfilled in their time.

39. The unbelief of the priest is condemned by silence, and the faith of the prophets is approved by an oracle. Cry he says, and I said: What shall I cry? All flesh is grass. You see the command of the one who orders, the compliance of the one who obeys, the disposition of the one who asks, the oracle of the one who complies. For he believed who asks what he should cry, and because he believed, he prophesied. But Zechariah, because he did not believe, could not speak, but he was nodding to them and remained dumb. It is not the mystery of one man nor the silence of one man.

40. The priest is silent, the prophet is silent. Unless I am mistaken, in one man the voice of the whole people became dumb, because in one man the whole people used to speak to the Lord through Moses. The ceasing of the sacred rites and the silence of the prophets: that is the speechlessness of the prophet and the speechlessness of the priest. I will take away he says the mighty power, the prophet and the counselor. And truly he took away the prophets, from whom he took away the Word, which was wont to speak in the prophets. And truly he took away power from those from whom the power of God withdrew; he took away the counselor from those whom the angel of great counsel turned away from; he took away the voice, because the voice belongs to the Word, the Word does not belong to the voice; for unless that Word works in us, there is no sound of voice. John is a voice — the voice indeed of one crying in the desert — Christ is the Word; this Word works, and so, where it ceased to work, like some tongue of the soul, suddenly dumb and lacking the spirit, the voice fell silent. For the Word of God has passed over to us, and in us it is not silent. Indeed the Jew can no longer say what the Christian can say: You seek a proof of him who speaks in me, Christ.

41. And he was nodding to them. So Zechariah remained dumb and was nodding to them. What is a nod but a kind of bodily act without a word, striving to indicate the will and not expressing it, a kind of dumb speech of the dying when the voice has been suppressed by death at the last? Does not the people of the Jews seem to you like him, so irrational that it cannot give an account of its acts, a people which, set in the last decease of living hope, has lost the voice it had, which by the act of a tottering body longs to set forth the sign of a word, not the word? Dumb, then, is the people, without reason, without the Word. For why should he seem to you more dumb who does not know the sound than he who does not know the mystery? There is surely a voice of works and a cry of faith, as we have read: The blood of your brother cries to me. And he cries who cries in his heart all day. So he who has lost the cry of the heart has lost that of the tongue; for he who does not hold the distinctness of faith, how can he hold that of words? And before, indeed, Moses had said that he could not speak, but after he had spoken, he received the word and brought forth the brightness of good works. So as Moses was a type of the people and a type of the Law, so too Zechariah was silent.

42. We must consider, then, how each thing concurs: the word in the womb, the Law in silence; John is named and Zechariah speaks; the word is brought forth, the Law is released, but the release of the Law is the unfolding of the word; and so he who has said the word speaks, even if he did not speak before. Zechariah is commanded by an angel to be silent, by an angel the voice of the Jews is suppressed; for this is the command not of human but of divine power, that he should not speak to God who did not believe Christ. And so let us believe, so that we may speak; let the Jew believe, so that he may be able to speak. Let us speak mysteries in the spirit; let us understand the reason of the ancient sacrifices, the riddles of the prophets. He is dumb who does not understand the Law, he is dumb who does not understand the sequence of the divine Scriptures; for our voice is faith. And so: I would rather speak five words in the church with my mind, so as to instruct others, than ten thousand words in a tongue; for tongues are for a sign not to the faithful but to the unfaithful, but prophecy not to the unfaithful but to the faithful.

And after these days Elizabeth his wife conceived and hid herself five months, saying: Why has the Lord done so to me in the days in which he looked upon me to take away my reproach among men?

43. Great is the care of the saints for modesty, so that often there is shame even in their wishes, as we observe in this place that Saint Elizabeth, who certainly desired to have children, hid herself five months. What cause was there for hiding but shame? For to every duty an age is prescribed, and what is becoming at one time is not becoming at another, and the change of age often changes the nature of each act. There are also certain times prescribed for marriage itself, when it is seemly to give attention to children: while the years are vigorous, while there is hope of having children, while the use of begetting accords with example, and the use of union too with eagerness. But when ripe old age has come on, and an age more fit for ruling children than for creating them, it is a shame to bear the tokens of intercourse, lawful though it be, and to be weighed down by the burden of another age, and for the belly to swell with a fruit not of its time. For the old are shut off by their very age, and by a just shame of intemperance are called back from doing the works of marriage. Young people themselves often put forward the wish for children, and think the heat of their age must be excused by the desire of begetting. How much more shameful for the old to do what the young are ashamed to confess! Nay, even young men themselves, who temper their hearts, sober with the fear of God, often renounce the works of youth once offspring have been received.

44. And why wonder at men, if beasts too, by a kind of dumb deed, declare that they have a zeal for begetting, not a desire for coupling? For once they have felt their belly heavy and the seed received in the genital field, they no longer indulge in mating nor take on the wantonness of a lover, but the care of a parent. But men spare neither what is conceived nor God: they defile the one, they provoke the other. Before I formed you he says in the womb, I knew you, and in your mother's womb I sanctified you. To restrain your wantonness, you observe certain hands of your Maker forming man in the womb. He is at work, and do you defile the secret place of the sacred womb with lust? Either imitate the beast or revere God. And why do I speak of beasts? The earth itself often rests from the work of generating, and if it is occupied with seeds sown frequently by the impatient eagerness of men, it punishes the shamelessness of the farmer and changes its fruitfulness into barrenness. So there is a certain shame of nature, in the very elements and in beasts, at not ceasing from the use of generating.

45. Rightly, then, did Saint Elizabeth blush at the grace, even though she did not acknowledge a fault. For although she conceived of her husband — for it is not right to believe otherwise of the birth of a man — yet she blushed at the age of her childbearing. And again she rejoiced that she was free of reproach; for it is a shame for women not to have the rewards of marriage, for whom this is the sole cause of marrying. So she consoles herself that this reproach has been taken away at the cost of her own shame, that shame, as I said, which she felt on account of her age. Whence it can be understood that they no longer came together in conjugal intercourse; for she who did not blush at intercourse in old age would not blush at the childbearing; and yet she blushes at the burden of a parent so long as she does not know the mystery of religion.

46. She who hid herself because she had conceived a son began to boast because she was bearing a prophet; and she who before blushed now blesses, and she who before doubted is strengthened. For behold she says, as the voice of your greeting came into my ears, the infant leaped in joy in my womb. And so she cried out with a loud voice when she perceived the Lord's coming, because she believed the birth to be a holy one; for there was no cause for shame, where the birth of a prophet won faith for a begetting that was given, not sought.

Book Two

At that same time the angel Gabriel was sent by the Lord to a city of Galilee named Nazareth, to a virgin betrothed to a man whose name was Joseph, of the house of David; and the virgin's name was Mary.

1. The divine mysteries are indeed hidden, and, according to the prophetic saying, no one among men can easily know the counsel of God; yet from the other deeds and precepts of the Lord Savior we can understand that this too belonged to a well-weighed counsel, that she who was betrothed to a man was the one chosen in particular to bear the Lord. But why was she not filled before she was betrothed? Perhaps so that it might not be said that she had conceived in adultery. And Scripture rightly set down both, that she was both betrothed and a virgin: a virgin, that she might be seen to have no part in union with a man; betrothed, that she might not be branded with the infamy of violated virginity, since her heavy womb would seem to display the mark of corruption. And the Lord preferred that some should doubt about his birth rather than about his mother's chastity — for he knew that a virgin's modesty is tender and the repute of chastity slippery — and he did not think that faith in his birth should be built up at the cost of wrongs to his mother. So Saint Mary's virginity is kept as inviolable in repute as it was intact in chastity; for the saints must have a good testimony also from those who are outside, and it was not fitting that virgins living under an ill report should be left the cover of an excuse, in that the mother of the Lord too would seem to have been defamed.

2. And what could have been laid to the charge of the Jews, what to the charge of Herod, if they had seemed to persecute one born of adultery? And how could he himself say: I came not to destroy the Law, but to fulfil it, if he had seemed to begin with a violation of the Law, since the childbearing of an unwedded woman is condemned by the Law? Moreover, a richer witness to her chastity is brought in, the husband, who could both have grieved at the wrong and avenged the disgrace, had he not recognized the sacrament. What of the fact that greater credit, too, is won for Mary's words and the motive for lying is removed? For an unwedded woman with child would have seemed to want to cloak her fault with a lie. An unbetrothed woman had a motive for lying; a betrothed woman had none, since for women childbearing is the reward of marriage and the grace of wedlock.

3. Not a slight reason, too, is that the virginity of Mary might deceive the prince of the world, who, when he saw her betrothed to a man, could not hold her childbearing in suspicion. That it was the counsel of the Lord himself to deceive the prince of the world, his own words declare, when the apostles are bidden to be silent about Christ, when the healed are forbidden to boast of their cure, when the demons are commanded to be silent about the Son of God. That it was the counsel, as I have said, to deceive the prince of the world, the Apostle too declared, saying: But we speak the wisdom of God hidden in a mystery, which none of the princes of this world knew. For had they known it, they would never have crucified the Lord of majesty; that is: they would never have brought it about that I should be redeemed by the death of the Lord. So he deceived for our sake, he deceived in order to conquer, he deceived the devil, when he was tempted, when he was questioned, when he was called the Son of God, so as nowhere to confess his own divinity. But yet he deceived the prince of the world still more; for the devil, though at one time he doubted, when he said: If you are the Son of God, cast yourself down, yet came to know him, late though it was, and departed from him. The demons too knew him, who said: We know who you are, Jesus, Son of God. Why have you come before the time to torment us? And they knew that he had come because they had foreknowledge that he would come. But that the princes of the world did not know him, by what greater proof can we show than by the Apostle's sentence: For had they known it, they would never have crucified the Lord of majesty? For the malice of demons easily detects even hidden things, but those who are taken up with the vanities of the world cannot know divine things.

4. The evangelists divided the matter well among themselves, so that Saint Matthew should bring in Joseph being warned by an angel not to put Mary away, and that Luke the evangelist should there bear witness that they had not come together, and here Mary herself should confess it, when she says to the angel: How shall this be, since I know not a man? But Saint Luke himself too declared her a virgin, saying: and the virgin's name was Mary; and the prophet made it plain, who says: Behold, a virgin shall conceive in the womb; and Joseph showed it, who, since he saw with child her whom he had not known, was eager to put her away; and the Lord himself, set upon the cross, made it plain, when he says to his mother: Woman, behold your son, and then says to the disciple: Behold your mother. Both too bore witness, the disciple and the mother, since from that hour the disciple took her to his own. Surely, had they come together, she would never have left her own husband, nor would a just man have suffered her to depart from him. And how would the Lord have commanded a divorce, when it is his own sentence that no one ought to put away his wife except for the cause of fornication?

5. Saint Matthew beautifully taught what a just man ought to do who has detected the disgrace of his wife: that he ought to keep himself unstained by bloodshed and pure of adultery; he who in fact is joined to a harlot is one body. So everywhere in Joseph the grace and the character of a just man are preserved, so that he may be a witness of standing; for the mouth of the just man knows no lie and his tongue speaks judgment, his judgment speaks the truth. Nor let it trouble you that Scripture often calls her wife; for it is not the taking of virginity but the attestation of the marriage that is declared by the celebration of the nuptials. Indeed, no one puts away a woman he has not taken, and so he who wished to put her away was confessing that she had been taken.

6. At the same time, what the evangelist says ought not to trouble us: he did not know her until she bore a son; for that is either an idiom of Scripture — as you have elsewhere: even until you grow old, I am. Did God cease to be after their old age? And in the psalm: The Lord said to my Lord: Sit at my right hand, until I make your enemies the footstool of your feet. Will he not sit afterwards? — or else because one who pleads a case thinks it enough to say what belongs to the case, and does not look for what is superfluous; for it is enough for him to establish the case he has taken up and to put off what is incidental. And so he who had undertaken to prove the incorrupt mystery of the Incarnation did not think that the testimony to Mary's virginity needed to be pursued more fully, lest he be thought a defender of the virgin rather than an asserter of the mystery. Certainly, when he showed Joseph to be just, he declared clearly enough that Joseph could not violate the temple of the Holy Spirit, the mother of the Lord, the womb of the mystery.

7. We have learned the sequence of the truth, we have learned the counsel: let us learn the mystery. Rightly betrothed, yet a virgin, because she is a type of the Church, which is immaculate, yet married. A virgin conceived us of the Spirit, a virgin brings us forth without groaning. And so perhaps Saint Mary was married to one and filled by another, because the individual churches too are filled by the Spirit and by grace, yet are joined to the outward form of a temporal priest.

And the angel came in to her and said: Hail, full of grace, the Lord is with you, blessed are you among women. But she, when she saw him, was troubled at his entrance.

8. Learn the virgin by her conduct, learn the virgin by her modesty, learn the virgin by the oracle, learn her by the mystery. It is the way of virgins to tremble, to be afraid at every entrance of a man, to dread every address of a man. Let women learn to imitate this purpose of modesty. Alone in the inner rooms, she whom no man might see, the angel alone might find: alone without a companion, alone without a witness, lest she be corrupted by some base address, she is greeted by the angel. Learn, virgin, to shun wantonness of words; Mary was afraid even of the angel's greeting.

9. Yet she was he says pondering what manner of greeting this was, and so with modesty, because she was afraid, with prudence, because she wondered at the new form of blessing, which had nowhere been read, nowhere met with before. This greeting was reserved for Mary alone; for rightly is she alone called full of grace, who alone obtained the grace that no other had merited, to be filled with the Author of grace. Mary therefore blushed, Elizabeth also blushed, and so let us recognize what the difference is between the modesty of a married woman and that of a virgin. The one blushed for a cause, the other out of modesty; in the married woman a measure of shame is kept, in the virgin the grace of shame is increased.

And the angel of the Lord said to her: Fear not, Mary; for you have found grace with God. And behold, you shall conceive in the womb and bear a son, and you shall call his name Jesus. He shall be great.

10. It was said indeed by the angel of John too that he shall be great; but that one great as a man, this one great as God; for great indeed is the Lord and greatly to be praised, and of his greatness there is no end. And truly that one too is great, because among those born of women there is no greater prophet than John the Baptist. Yet he has one greater, because he who is least in the kingdom of God is greater than he. John, then, is great. But before the Lord even the great John drinks no wine or strong drink, while this one eats and drinks with tax collectors and sinners. Let the one, who had no power by nature, gather merit by abstinence; but Christ, who had by nature the means to forgive sins, why should he shun those whom he could make better than the abstinent?

11. At the same time, in the mystical sense, he does not disdain the banquet of those to whom he is to give the sacrament. So this one eats, the other fasts, a type of both peoples: in the one there is fasting, in this one feeding. But Christ too fasted, so that you might not shun the precept; he ate with sinners, so that you might see his grace and recognize his power. John, then, is great too, but his greatness has a beginning, has an end; whereas the Lord Jesus is himself the end and the beginning, himself the first and the last. Nothing before the first, nothing beyond the last.

12. Nor let the custom of human generation draw you into error, so that you think he is not first because he is Son. Follow the Scriptures, so that you cannot go wrong. The Son is called first. It is read too that the Father also is alone, who alone has immortality and dwells in light inaccessible, as you have read: and to the immortal, only God; but neither first before the Father nor alone without the Son. If you deny the one, you assert the other: follow both, and you confirm both. He did not say: 'I am the former and I am the latter', but: 'I am the first and I am the last'. The Son is first and therefore coeternal; for he has the Father, with whom he is eternal. I dare to say: 'The Son is first, but he is not alone', and I say it rightly and I say it devoutly. Why do you prick up your ears for impiety, heretics? You have fallen into the snares that you lay. The Son is first and is not alone: first, because he is always with the Father; not alone, because he is never without the Father. It is not I who say this, but he himself said: And I am not alone, because the Father is with me. The Father alone, because one God; the Father alone, because the divinity of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit is alone, and what is one is alone. The Father alone, the Only-begotten alone, the Holy Spirit too alone; for he who is Son is not also Father, nor he who is Father also Son, nor he who is the Holy Spirit also Son. The Son is one, the Holy Spirit another; for we read: I will ask the Father, and he will give you another Paraclete. God alone, because one God, from whom are all things; the Son alone, because one Lord, through whom are all things. It is the divinity that makes one alone. Generation bears witness both to the Father and to the Son, so that nowhere is the Son seen to be without the Father or the Father without the Son. So he is not alone, because he is not alone immortal; nor does he alone dwell in light inaccessible, because no one has ever seen God except the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, who sits at the right hand of the Father. And some dare to say that the light which the Father dwells in is inaccessible to him. Is the light, then, better than the Father? But what light is inaccessible to him to whom the Father is not inaccessible, the Father who is himself the true light and the begetter of the eternal light, of which it was said: That was the true light, which enlightens every man coming into this world? Consider whether that is not the inaccessible light which the Father dwells in, and the Son dwells in too, because the Father is in the Son and the Son in the Father.

13. Rightly, then, great; for the power of God is poured out far and wide, the greatness of the heavenly substance extends far and wide. The Trinity has nothing prescribed, nothing circumscribed, nothing measured out, nothing measured off. It is not shut in by place, not grasped by opinion, not enclosed by estimation, not changed by age. The Lord Jesus indeed gave greatness to men; for their sound indeed went out into all the earth, and their words into the ends of the world; yet not into the ends of the universe, not into the ends of heaven, not beyond the heavens. But in the Lord Jesus all things were created in the heavens and on the earth, visible and invisible, and he is before all, and all things hold together in him. Look at heaven, Jesus is there; gaze at the earth, Jesus is present; ascend in word into heaven, descend in word into the underworld, Jesus is present. For if you ascend into heaven, Jesus is there; if you descend into the underworld, he is present. Today, while I speak, he is with me, within this point, within this moment; and if a Christian is speaking now in Armenia, Jesus is present; for no one indeed calls Jesus Lord except in the Holy Spirit. If in thought you penetrate the abysses, there too you will see Jesus at work; for it is written: Do not say in your heart: Who has ascended into heaven? that is, to bring Christ down; or who has descended into the abyss? that is, to bring Christ back from the dead. Where, then, is he not, who has filled the things of heaven, the things below, the things of earth? Rightly, then, is he great whose power has filled the world, who is everywhere and will be always, because of his kingdom there shall be no end.

14. And Mary said to the angel: How shall this be, since I know not a man? Here Mary seems not to have believed, unless you attend carefully; for it is not right that she who was chosen to beget the only-begotten Son of God should seem to have been unbelieving. And how could it be — granted that the prerogative of the mother is kept safe, to whom indeed more deference was due; but as her prerogative was greater, so greater faith too had to be reserved for her — how, then, could it be that Zechariah, who had not believed, should be condemned to silence, while Mary, if she had not believed, should be exalted by the infusion of the Holy Spirit? But Mary ought neither to disbelieve nor to presume so rashly: neither to disbelieve the angel nor to lay claim to divine things. For it was not easy to know the mystery hidden from the ages in God, which not even the higher powers could know. And yet she did not refuse faith, did not decline the duty, but adapted her disposition and pledged her obedience. For when she says: How shall this be? she did not doubt the outcome, but asked about the manner of the outcome itself.

15. How much more measured is this reply than the words of the priest! She says: How shall this be? He answered: Whence shall I know this? She is already dealing with the matter, he is still doubting the message. He who denies that he knows denies that he believes, and seeks, as it were, yet another guarantor for his faith; she professes that she does it and does not doubt that what she asks how it can be done is to be done; for so you have it: How shall this be, since I know not a man? An unbelievable and unheard-of generation had first to be heard of, that it might be believed. For a virgin to bring forth is the sign of a divine mystery, not a human one. Indeed, take he says a sign for yourself; behold, a virgin shall conceive in the womb and bear a son. Mary had read this, and so she believed that it would be; but how it would come about she had not read before; for how it would come about had not been revealed even to so great a prophet. For the mystery of so great a message was to be brought forth not by the mouth of a man but by that of an angel. Today for the first time is heard: The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and it is heard and it is believed.

16. Then behold she says the handmaid of the Lord; be it done to me according to your word. See her humility, see her devotion. She calls herself the handmaid of the Lord, she who is chosen as his mother, nor was she lifted up by the sudden promise. At the same time, by calling herself handmaid she claimed for herself no prerogative of so great a grace, as one who would do what she was bidden; for, about to bear the meek and humble one, she too had to show humility. Behold the handmaid of the Lord; be it done to me according to your word. You have her obedience, you see her wish; for behold the handmaid of the Lord is the readiness of service, be it done to me according to your word is the conception of the wish.

17. How quickly, then, Mary believed, even in an unequal condition! For what is so unequal as the Holy Spirit and a body? What so unheard-of as a virgin with child, against law, against custom, against modesty, whose care is dearer to a virgin? Zechariah, however, disbelieved not on account of an unequal condition but on account of old age; for the condition was fitting. From man and woman birth is the usual thing, nor ought it to seem unbelievable where nature agrees. For since age belongs to nature, not nature to age, it often happens that age hinders nature; yet it is not unreasonable that the lesser cause should yield to the greater, and the stronger prerogative of nature exclude the use of the inferior age. Add to this that Abraham and Sarah had received a son in their old age, and Joseph is a son of old age.
