But if Sarah is rebuked because she laughed, more justly is he condemned who believed neither the oracle nor the example; but Mary, when she says: How shall this be, since I know not a man? does not seem to have doubted about the fact but to have inquired about the manner of the fact. For it is clear that she had believed it was to be done, she who asked how it would be done. Hence she also deserved to hear: Blessed are you who believed. And truly blessed, who was more excellent than the priest. When the priest had denied, the virgin corrected the error. Nor is it any wonder if the Lord, about to redeem the world, began his work from Mary, so that she through whom salvation was being prepared for all should be the first to draw the fruit of salvation from her offspring.

18. And rightly she asked how it would be; for she had read that a virgin would bring forth, she had not read how a virgin would bring forth; she had read, as I have said: Behold, a virgin shall conceive in the womb; but how she would conceive, the angel first spoke of in the Gospel.

And Mary, rising up in those days, went into the hill country with haste, to a city of Judea, and entered the house of Zechariah and greeted Elizabeth.

19. It is a moral rule for all that those who demand faith should support faith. And so the angel, when he announced hidden things, so that faith might be supported by an example, announced to the virgin Mary the conception of an older and barren woman, so as to assert that everything that pleases God is possible to him. When Mary heard this, not as unbelieving of the oracle, nor as uncertain of the message, nor as doubting the example, but as joyful for her wish, devout for her duty, hastening for joy, she went into the hill country. For whither should she, already full of God, make with haste but to the heights? The grace of the Holy Spirit knows no slow efforts.

20. Learn you too, holy women, the diligent care you ought to show to kinswomen who are with child. Mary, who before kept alone in the innermost rooms, was held back neither from going in public by virginal modesty, nor from her zeal by the roughness of the mountains, nor from her duty by the length of the journey. Into the hill country with haste the virgin, the virgin mindful of duty, unmindful of hardship, with affection, not her sex, prevailing, left her home and went on her way.

21. Learn, virgins, not to run about through other people's houses, not to linger in the streets, not to join in conversations with anyone in public. Mary, slow at home, quick in public, stayed with her kinswoman three months; for she who had come for a service held fast to the service. She stayed three months, not because another's house delighted her, but because to be seen too often in public displeased her.

22. You have learned, virgins, the modesty of Mary: learn her humility. The kinswoman came to her relative, the younger to the elder; and not only did she come, she even greeted her first; for it is fitting that the more chaste a virgin is, the more humble she should be. Let her know how to defer to her elders; let her in whom is the profession of chastity be a teacher of humility. There is in this too a ground of devotion, there is also a rule of teaching. For one must observe that the superior comes to the inferior, that the inferior may be helped: Mary to Elizabeth, Christ to John; then also later, to sanctify the baptism of John, the Lord came to baptism. Quickly, too, the benefits of Mary's coming and of the Lord's presence are made known; for at once as Elizabeth heard the greeting of Mary, the infant leaped in her womb, and she was filled with the Holy Spirit.

23. See the distinction, and the propriety of each of the words. Elizabeth was first to hear the voice, but John was first to feel grace: she heard by the order of nature, he leaped by reason of the mystery; she sensed the coming of Mary, he the coming of the Lord; the woman that of the woman, the offspring that of the offspring; the women speak grace, the children work within and set about the mystery of devotion for their mothers' advancement, and by a double miracle the mothers prophesy by the spirit of their little ones. The infant leaped, the mother was filled. The mother was not filled before the son, but when the son had been filled with the Holy Spirit, he filled his mother too. John leaped, and Mary's spirit leaped too. As John leaps, Elizabeth is filled; but Mary, we know, is not filled with the Spirit, but her spirit leaps — for the Incomprehensible was working incomprehensibly in the mother — and the one is filled after conception, the other before conception.

Blessed are you among women, and blessed is the fruit of your womb. And whence is this to me, that the mother of my Lord should come to me?

24. The Holy Spirit knows his own speech and never forgets it, and prophecy is fulfilled not only by the wonders of events but also by the propriety of words. Who is this fruit of the womb but he of whom it was said: Behold, the inheritance of the Lord, sons; the reward, the fruit of the womb? That is: the sons are the inheritance of the Lord, who are the reward of that fruit, of him who came forth from the womb of Mary. He is the fruit of the womb, the flower of the root, of whom Isaiah prophesied well, saying: A rod shall come forth from the root of Jesse, and a flower shall rise up from the root; for the root is the family of the Jews, the rod Mary, the flower of Mary Christ, who, like the fruit of a good tree, according to the progress of our virtue now flowers, now bears fruit in us, now is restored by the resurrection of the body living again. And whence is this to me, that the mother of my Lord should come to me?

25. She does not say it as one ignorant — for she knows that it is the grace and working of the Holy Spirit that the mother of the prophet should be greeted by the mother of the Lord for the advancement of her offspring — but as one who recognizes that this is the gift not of human merit but of divine grace, so she says: Whence is this to me? That is: how great a good has befallen me, that the mother of my Lord should come to me? I recognize nothing of mine. Whence is this to me? By what righteousness, by what deeds, for what merits? These are not the usual services of women, that the mother of my Lord should come to me. I sense the miracle, I recognize the mystery: the mother of the Lord is fruitful with the Word, full of God.

For behold, as soon as the voice of your greeting sounded in my ears, the infant leaped with joy in my womb. And blessed are you who believed.

26. You see that Mary did not doubt but believed, and therefore obtained the fruit of faith. Blessed she says are you who believed. But you too are blessed, who have heard and believed; for every soul that has believed both conceives and brings forth the Word of God and recognizes his works. Let the soul of Mary be in each one, to magnify the Lord; let the spirit of Mary be in each one, to exult in God. If according to the flesh there is one mother of Christ, yet according to faith Christ is the fruit of all; for every soul receives the Word of God, if only, immaculate and free from vices, it guards its chastity with inviolate modesty.

27. Whatever soul, then, has been able to be such magnifies the Lord, as the soul of Mary magnified the Lord and her spirit exulted in God the Savior. For the Lord is magnified, as you have read elsewhere too: Magnify the Lord with me, not that anything can be added to the Lord by a human voice, but because he is magnified in us: for Christ is the image of God, and so, if the soul does anything just and devout, it magnifies that image of God to whose likeness it was created; and therefore, while it magnifies it, it becomes more exalted by a certain participation in its greatness, so that it seems to express that image in itself by the splendid color of good deeds and by a certain emulation of virtue. And the soul of Mary magnifies the Lord, and her spirit exults in God, because, devoted in soul and spirit to the Father and the Son, she venerates with devout affection one God, from whom are all things, and one Lord, through whom are all things.

28. Mary's follows: the better the person, the fuller the prophecy. Nor does it seem idle that Elizabeth prophesies before John's birth and Mary before the Lord's birth; for already the first attempts at human salvation are creeping forward. For as sin began from women, so too good things are begun from women, so that women too, laying aside womanish works, may renounce weakness, and the soul, which has no sex, may, like Mary, who knows no error, imitate chastity with devout zeal.

29. And Mary stayed with her three months and returned to her own house. Saint Mary is well represented as having both rendered a service and kept a mystical number; for the cause of her staying long was not only their familiarity, but also the advancement of so great a prophet. For if at her first entrance so great a progress came about, that at Mary's greeting the infant leaped in the womb and the infant's mother was filled with the Holy Spirit, how much do we think the presence of Saint Mary added through the use of so long a time? And Mary stayed with her three months. So the prophet was anointed and, like a good athlete, was trained in his mother's womb; for his strength was being prepared for the greatest of contests. Indeed, Mary stayed just so long, until Elizabeth fulfilled the time of her delivery. But if you attend carefully, you will find this set down nowhere except in the birth of the just. Indeed, the days were fulfilled that Mary should bring forth, the time was fulfilled that Elizabeth should bring forth, the time of life was fulfilled when holy men departed from this course of life. The life of the just has fullness, but the days of the ungodly are empty.

30. So Elizabeth bore a son and the neighbours rejoiced with her. The birth of saints brings joy to many, because it is a common good; for righteousness is a common virtue. And so at the birth of a just man a token of his future life is sent ahead, and the grace of virtue that is to follow is signified by the rejoicing of the neighbours, which prefigures it. Beautifully is the time described in which the prophet was in the womb, so that the presence of Mary might not be passed over in silence; but the time of his infancy is passed over, because he was strengthened in the womb by the presence of the Lord's mother, he who knew not the hindrances of infancy. And so in the Gospel we read nothing about him except his birth and the oracle, his leaping in the womb, his voice in the desert. For he felt no age of infancy who, beyond nature, beyond age, placed in his mother's womb, began from the measure of the perfect age of the fullness of Christ.

And his mother answered and said: No, but he shall be called John. And they said to her: There is no one in your kindred who is called by this name. So they made signs to his father as to what he wished him to be called. And he took a writing tablet and wrote, saying: John is his name. And all marveled. And at once his tongue was loosed and his mouth was opened, and he spoke, blessing God.

31. Wonderfully did the holy evangelist think it should be set down first that very many thought the infant should be called by his father's name, Zechariah, so that you might observe that it was not that the name of some unworthy person displeased the mother, but that it had been poured into her by the Holy Spirit, which had been foretold to Zechariah beforehand by the angel. And indeed he, being mute, could not make known the name of their son to his wife, but Elizabeth learned through prophecy what she had not learned from her husband. John she says is his name, that is: we do not give a name to him who has already received a name from God. He has his own name, which we have recognized, not one we have chosen. The merits of the saints have this, that they receive their name from God. Thus Jacob is called Israel, because he saw God; thus our Lord Jesus was named before he was born, to whom not an angel but the Father gave the name. For there will be revealed he says my son Jesus with those who will rejoice with him, who have been left in four hundred years. And it will be after these years, and my son Christ will die, and the world will be turned. You see that the angels announce what they have heard, not what they have presumed. Nor wonder if the woman affirmed a name she had not heard, since the Holy Spirit, who had given the charge to the angel, revealed it to her. Nor could she who had prophesied Christ be ignorant of the Lord's forerunner.

32. And it is well added that no one in her kindred was called by this name, so that you may understand that the name belonged not to the family but to the prophet. Zechariah too is asked by a nod; but because unbelief had taken from him speech and hearing, what he could not say with his voice he spoke with his hand and with letters; for he wrote indeed saying: John is his name; and here the name is not given but confirmed; and deservedly at once his tongue was loosed, because faith loosed what unbelief had bound. Let us too, then, believe, that we may speak, that our tongue, which is bound by the chains of unbelief, may be loosed by the voice of reason. Let us write mysteries in the spirit, if we wish to speak; let us write the forerunner of Christ not on tablets of stone, but on the fleshly tablets of the heart. For he who speaks John prophesies Christ. Let us speak John, let us speak Christ too, so that our mouth also may be opened, which, in so great a priest, the reins of a wavering faith held in, as though it were an irrational beast's.

33. And Zechariah his father was filled with the Holy Spirit and prophesied, saying. See how God, good and ready to forgive sins, not only restores what had been taken away, but also grants what was not hoped for. He who was lately mute prophesies; for this is the greatest grace of God, that those who had denied him confess him. Let no one, then, lose confidence; let no one, conscious of old sins, despair of divine rewards. God knows how to change his sentence, if you know how to amend your fault. And you, child, shall be called the prophet of the Most High.

34. Beautifully, when he was prophesying about the Lord, he turned his words to the prophet, so as to show that this too was a benefit of the Lord, lest, while he publicly recounted his own blessings, he should seem, as if ungrateful, to have passed over in silence the benefits he had received, which he recognized in his son. But perhaps some may think it an irrational excess of mind that he addresses an infant eight days old. But if we keep this in mind, we understand indeed that the newborn could hear his father's voice, he who heard Mary's greeting before he was born. The prophet knew that a prophet has other ears, which are opened by the Spirit of God, not by the age of the body; he had the sense to understand who had the affection to leap.

35. At the same time, observe in how few words Elizabeth prophesies, in how many Zechariah. Both spoke filled with the Holy Spirit, but the discipline is kept, that a woman should be eager rather to learn divine things than to teach them. Nor do we readily find that any woman prophesied more fully than the mother of the Lord. Miriam the prophetess herself, the sister of Aaron, how quickly she brought the words of her song to a close! And the same woman, when she spoke at greater length with her brother, by no means escaped punishment for her speech.

Now it came to pass in those days that a decree went out from Caesar Augustus that the whole world should be registered in a census.

36. As we are about to speak of the birth of the Savior, we do not think it out of place to inquire at what time he was born. For what would a registration of worldly men have to do with the Lord's birth, unless that we may observe this too to be a divine mystery, that while a worldly registration is put forward, a spiritual one is fulfilled, to be made not to the king of the earth but to the King of heaven? This registration of faith is a census of souls; for when the old census of the synagogue had been abolished, a new census of the Church was being prepared, which would not exact torments but take them away, and which spiritually, in the type of the people, was already giving in its names to Christ. Here it is not the extent of lands but that of minds and souls that is assessed; boundaries are not marked out but carried forward; no age is set apart, but every age is enrolled; for no one is exempt from this census, because every age pays its dues to Christ, whom wailing infants confess by martyrdom, to whom those set within the womb bear witness by their leaping. Fear nothing terrible in this census, nothing harsh, nothing sad; faith alone marks each one. Would you hear Christ's census-takers? They are commanded to assess without rods, to seek out the people not by terror but by grace, to put up the sword, not to possess gold. By such census-takers the world was won.

37. Indeed, that you may know that the census is not Augustus's but Christ's, the whole world is commanded to register. When Christ is born, all register; when the world is shut in, all are in peril. Who, then, could demand the registration of the whole world but he who held dominion over the whole world? For not Augustus's, but the Lord's is the earth and its fullness, the world and all who dwell in it. Augustus did not rule the Goths, he did not rule the Armenians: Christ ruled them. Certainly they received Christ's census-taker, they who brought forth martyrs of Christ. And perhaps it is for this that they defeat us, as present events show, because the very one whom they confessed by the offering of their blood, the Arians called into question as to his generation.

38. This he says was the first registration made. And yet histories tell us that very many parts of the earth had often been enrolled before. This, then, is the first registration, not of lands but of minds, in which all register, because no one is excepted, at the summons not of a herald but of a prophet, who long before foretold: All nations, clap your hands, shout to God with the voice of exultation, for God is most high, terrible, a great king over all the earth. Indeed, that you may know that it is a census of righteousness, Joseph and Mary come to it, that is, a just man and a virgin: he to guard the Word, she to bear it. Where do the just man and the virgin register but where Christ is born? Every indeed spirit that confesses that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is from God. And where, in a deeper sense, is Christ born but in your heart and in your breast? For the word is near, in your mouth and in your heart.

39. Beautifully, too, he added the name of the governor, to mark the sequence of time. When Quirinius was governor, he says, this first registration was made, so that the evangelist seems to have written a kind of consul, as it were, on this book for the sake of a mark of date. For if consuls are written on deeds of purchase, how much more ought the time to be written on the redemption of all! You have, then, everything that is customary in contracts: the name of the one who holds supreme power there, the day, the place, the cause. Witnesses too are usually brought in; these too Christ brought in for his nativity and generation according to the flesh, to seal the Gospel, saying: You shall be my witnesses in Jerusalem.

And it came to pass, while they were there, the days were fulfilled that she should bring forth. And she brought forth her firstborn son and wrapped him in swaddling clothes and laid him in a manger, because there was no place in that inn.

40. Briefly Saint Luke has set forth how and at what time and in what place Christ was born according to the flesh. But if you ask about his heavenly generation, read the Gospel of Saint John, who, beginning from heavenly things, came down to earthly ones. There you will find when he was and how he was and what he was, what he had done, what he was doing, and where he was, and whither he came, how he came, at what time he came, for what cause he came. In the beginning he says was the Word: you have when he was; and the Word was with God: you have how he was. You have also what he was: and the Word he says was God; what he had done: all things were made through him; what he was doing: That was the true light, which enlightens every man coming into this world; and where he was: he was in this world; whither he came: he came to his own; how he came: the Word was made flesh. When he came, John bears witness of him, saying: This is he of whom I said: He who comes after me has been made before me, because he was before me. For what cause he came, John himself testifies: Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sin of the world. If, then, we have come to know both generations and the one person of both, and have observed the cause for which he came, namely that, taking upon himself the sins of a perishing world, he who could not be conquered might abolish in himself the stain of sin and the death of all, it follows that now too Saint Luke the evangelist should teach us and show the ways of the Lord as he grew according to the flesh.

41. Nor should it trouble anyone that we said John's infancy was passed over by a higher counsel, whereas we assert that Christ's infancy was described; for it is not for all to say: I became weak to the weak, that I might gain the weak; I became all things to all, nor can it be said thus of anyone else that he was wounded for our iniquity and made weak for our sins. He, then, was a little one, he was a tiny infant, so that you might be able to be a perfect man; he was wrapped in swaddling clothes, so that you might be loosed from the snares of death; he in mangers, so that you might be at altars; he on earth, so that you might be among the stars; he had no other place in that inn, so that you might have many mansions in the heavens. He who, though he was rich he says, became poor for your sakes, so that by his want you might become rich. That poverty, then, is my inheritance, and the weakness of the Lord is my strength. He chose to be in want himself, so that he might abound for all. The weeping of that wailing infancy washes me, those tears have washed away my sins. I owe more, then, Lord Jesus, to your injuries, by which I am redeemed, than to your works, by which I am created. It would profit nothing to be born, had it not profited to be redeemed.

42. But let no one shut up the whole form of the divinity within the condition of the body. The nature of the flesh is one thing, the glory of the divinity another. Weakness for your sake, power in himself; poverty for your sake, wealth in himself. Do not judge by what you see, but recognize that you are redeemed. You see that he is in swaddling clothes, you do not see that he is in the heavens. You hear the wailing of an infant, you do not hear the lowing of the ox recognizing its Lord; the ox in fact has known its owner, and the she-ass the manger of her lord, or rather I should say praesaepium [manger], as he who made the translation wrote; for to me there is no difference in the word where there is none in the sense. For if the orator of those who pursue the trappings of speech denies that the fortunes of Greece depend on whether he used this word or that, but thinks that the matter is what must be looked to; if their very philosophers, who spend whole days in disputation, made less use of Latin and accepted words in order to use terms of their own; how much more ought we to disregard words and look to mysteries, in which plainness of speech prevails, because the miracles of the divine works, adorned by no words, have shone with the light of their own truth? Indeed, that rational she-ass was fed by the holy mangers not with the gloss of dainties but with the sap of natural nourishment.

43. This is the Lord, this the manger, by which a divine mystery was revealed to us: that the Gentiles, living in their stalls after the irrational manner of cattle, were to be fed with the richness of sacred nourishment. So the she-ass, that is, the figure and form of the Gentiles, knew the manger of her lord. And therefore she says: The Lord feeds me, and I shall want nothing. Is God proved by slight signs, in that angels minister, magi adore, martyrs confess? He is brought forth from the womb, but he flashes from heaven; he lies in an earthly inn, but he is mighty with heavenly light. A married woman bore him, but a virgin conceived him; a married woman conceived him, but a virgin brought him forth. For Saint Matthew taught us no slight mystery, which Saint Luke, because it had already been fully set out, thought should be passed over in silence, believing that he would be rich enough if out of all things he claimed for himself the Lord's manger.

44. This little one, then, whom you, who are an unbeliever, think of no account, the magi coming from the east followed over so long a distance, and falling down they adore him and call him king and confess that he will rise again, offering from their treasures gold, frankincense and myrrh. What are these gifts of true faith? Gold for a king, frankincense for God, myrrh for one dead; for the first is the emblem of a king, the second the sacrifice of divine power, the third the honour of burial, which does not corrupt the body of the dead but preserves it. Let us too, who hear and read these things, bring forth such gifts, brethren, from our treasures; we have indeed a treasure in earthen vessels. If, then, in yourself you ought to reckon what you are not from yourself but from Christ, how much more in Christ ought you to reckon not what is yours but what is Christ's!

45. So the magi offer gifts from their treasures. Do you want to know how good a merit they have? The star is seen by them, and where Herod is, it is not seen; where Christ is, it is seen again and shows the way. So this star is the way, and the way is Christ, because according to the mystery of the Incarnation Christ is the star; there shall rise indeed a star out of Jacob, and a man shall spring up out of Israel. Indeed, where Christ is, there the star is too; for he himself is the bright and morning star. So he marks himself out by his own light.

46. Take another proof. The magi came by one way, they return by another; for having seen Christ, having understood Christ, they go back better, surely, than they came. For there are two ways, one that leads to destruction, the other that leads to the kingdom. The one is the way of sinners, which leads to Herod; this way is Christ, by which one returns to the fatherland; for here our sojourn is for a time, as it is written: my soul has long been a sojourner. Let us beware, then, of Herod, the ruler for a time of worldly power, that we may attain the eternal dwelling of the heavenly fatherland.

47. These rewards are set before not the elect only but all, since Christ is all and in all; for you see that it was not idly that Abraham, from among the Chaldeans, who are reckoned more skilled in numbers, believed God, or that the magi, who, though they spend their zeal on winning over the deity to themselves by magical arts, believed in the Lord's birth on earth; not idly, I say, but so that from hostile nations a testimony to holy religion and an example of the fear of God might be taken.

48. And yet who are these magi but those who, as a certain history teaches, draw their descent from Balaam, by whom it was prophesied: There shall rise a star out of Jacob? These, then, are heirs no less of his faith than of his line. He saw the star in the spirit, they saw it with their eyes and believed. They had seen a new star which had not been seen since the creation of the world, they had seen a new creature, and not only on earth but in heaven too they were seeking the grace of the new man, according to what Moses set down prophetically, that there shall rise a star out of Jacob, and a man shall spring up out of Israel. And they knew this to be the star which signifies man and God. They adored the little one. Surely they would not have adored him, had they believed him only a little one. The magus, then, understands that his arts are coming to an end, and do you not understand that your gifts have come? He confesses what belongs to another, and do you not recognize what was promised to you? He believes against himself, and do you not think you should believe for yourself?

49. So the magi announce that a king is born: Herod is troubled, gathers the scribes and the chief priests and asks where the Christ is to be born. The magi announce only a king, Herod seeks the Christ; so he confesses the very king about whom he asks. Then, when it is asked where he was to be born, he is surely shown to have been foretold; for one who had not been foretold could not be sought. O senseless Jews, you do not believe that he has come whom you see; you do not believe that he has come whom you say is to come! Bring me word he says so that I may come and adore him. Herod indeed lays a snare, but he does not deny God, whom he declares must be adored. Then he orders the infants to be slain. To whom else but God was such a victim owed? Though infancy is without understanding, yet it confesses God, for whom it is slain. We have taken these few things from Matthew, so that it might be clear that the time of his infancy was by no means void of the works of divinity. But if the age of the flesh was idle for work, surely it was God who exercised the age of the flesh in the works of divinity, who also made the shepherds in that region keep watch, keeping the watches of the night over their flock.

50. See the beginning of the rising Church: Christ is born and the shepherds began to keep watch, to gather into the Lord's fold the flocks of the Gentiles, which before had lived like cattle, so that they might not suffer the attacks of spiritual beasts through the darkness of the night spread around. And rightly do the shepherds keep watch, whom the good shepherd forms. The flock, then, is the people, the night the world, the shepherds are the priests. Or perhaps that one too may be a shepherd to whom it is said: Be watchful and strengthen, because the Lord has not only ordained bishops to guard the flock, but has also appointed angels. Behold, an angel of the Lord stood before them.

51. See how the divine care builds up faith. An angel instructs Mary, an angel Joseph, an angel the shepherds. It is not enough that one be sent once; for at the mouth of two and three witnesses every word stands. And there was with the angel a multitude of the heavenly host, praising God and saying: Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to men of good will.

52. Rightly is it called an army of angels, for they followed the leader of the host. To whom, then, should the angels speak praise but to their Lord, according to what is written: Praise the Lord from the heavens, praise him in the heights. Praise him, all his angels? So the prophecy was fulfilled. The Lord is praised from the heavens and seen on earth, of whom Saint Mark says that he was with the beasts and the angels ministered to him, so that in the one you may recognize the emblem of mercy, in the other the token of divine power. It is for your sake that he suffers the beasts, it is his own that he is proclaimed by angels. And they say: Let us see this word which has come to pass, as the Lord has shown us. And they came in haste.

53. You see the shepherds make haste; for no one seeks Christ with sloth. You see that the shepherds believed the angel: you too, believe the Father, the Son, the Holy Spirit, the angels, the prophets, the apostles. See how expressly Scripture weighs the force of each word. 'They make haste' it says 'to see the word'. For when the flesh of the Lord is seen, the Word is seen, which is the Son. Let not this seem to you a slight example of faith, nor the person of the shepherds a mean one. Certainly, the meaner in point of learning, the more precious in point of faith. The Lord sought out not schools full of choirs of wise men but a simple people, which did not know how to deck out and color what it had heard; for simplicity is sought, ambition is not desired. Nor should you think the words of the prophets to be despised as though they were mean. From the shepherds even Mary gathers faith, by the shepherds the people is gathered to reverence for God; they marvelled indeed at the things that were told them by the shepherds.

54. But Mary kept all these words, pondering them in her heart. Let us learn the chastity of the holy virgin in all things, who, no less chaste in speech than in body, pondered in her heart the proofs of faith. If Mary learns from shepherds, why do you refuse to learn from priests? If Mary is silent before the apostolic precepts, why do you after the apostolic precepts desire to teach rather than to learn? Learn that the fault belongs to the person, not to the sex; for the sex is holy. Indeed, Mary did not receive the precept, she set forth the example.

55. So the child is circumcised. Who is that child but he of whom it was said: A child is born to us, a son is given to us? For he was made under the Law, that he might gain those who were under the Law.

56. What it means to be presented to the Lord in Jerusalem I would say, had I not said it before in my commentaries on Isaiah; for one circumcised from vices was judged worthy of the Lord's regard, because the eyes of the Lord are upon the just. You see that the whole course of the old Law was a type of what was to come — for circumcision too signifies the cleansing of sins — but since the frailty of human flesh and mind, through a certain headlong desire for sinning, is entangled in inextricable vices, therefore by the eighth day of circumcision the future cleansing of all guilt was prefigured in the age of the resurrection. For this is that saying: for every male opening the womb shall be called holy to the Lord; for by the words of the Law the childbearing of the virgin was promised. And truly holy, because immaculate. Indeed, that he is the one who is marked out by the Law, the words repeated in the same manner by the angel declare: for what shall be born he says shall be called holy, the Son of God. For no union with a man unlocked the secret places of the virginal womb, but the Holy Spirit poured immaculate seed into an inviolable womb; for of all those born of woman the Lord Jesus alone is holy in all things, who by the newness of his immaculate birth did not feel the contagion of earthly corruption, and by his heavenly majesty drove it away.

57. For if we follow the letter, how is every male holy, when it is no secret that many have been most wicked? Was Ahab holy? Were the false prophets holy, whom the fire, avenger of the wrong done to heaven, consumed at the prayers of Elijah? But he is holy whom the devout prescriptions of the divine Law marked out as the figure of the mystery to come, in that he alone was to open, for the begetting of the peoples of God, the secret womb of the holy Church, a virgin of immaculate fruitfulness. He alone, then, opened the womb for himself. Nor is it any wonder; for he who had said to the prophet: Before I formed you in the womb, I knew you, and in your mother's womb I sanctified you, he, then, who sanctified another's womb, that a prophet might be born, is the one who opened his own mother's womb, that he might come forth immaculate.

58. And behold, there was a man in Jerusalem whose name was Simeon, and this man was just and God-fearing, awaiting the consolation of Israel. Not only from angels and prophets, from shepherds and parents, but also from elders and just men does the generation of the Lord receive testimony. Every age and both sexes and the wonders of events build up faith: a virgin begets, a barren woman bears, a mute man speaks, Elizabeth prophesies, a magus adores, one shut in the womb leaps, a widow confesses, a just man waits. And rightly just, for he sought not his own grace but the people's, desiring himself to be released from the chains of bodily frailty, but waiting to see what was promised; for he knew that blessed are the eyes that would see.

59. Now he says dismiss your servant. See the just man, shut up as it were in the prison of the bodily mass, wishing to be dissolved, so that he may begin to be with Christ; to be dissolved indeed and to be with Christ is far better. But let him who wishes to be dismissed come into the temple, come into Jerusalem, wait for the Christ of the Lord, receive the Word of God in his hands and embrace him as it were with the arms of his faith. Then he will be dismissed, so as not to see death, he who has seen life.

60. You see the abundant grace of the Lord poured out upon all by his birth, and that prophecy was denied to the unbelieving, not to the just. Behold, Simeon too prophesies that the Lord Jesus Christ has come for the fall and the rising of many, to distinguish the merits of the just and the wicked and, as a true and just judge, to decree either punishments or rewards according to the quality of our deeds.

61. And your own soul he says a sword shall pierce: neither the letter nor history teaches that Mary departed from this life by suffering a bodily death; for not the soul but the body is pierced through by a material sword. And so it shows the prudence of Mary, not ignorant of the heavenly mystery. Living indeed is the word of God, and strong, and sharper than any sharpest sword, piercing even to the division of soul and spirit, of joints and marrow; and it searches the thoughts of the heart and the secrets of minds, because all things of minds are naked and open to the Son, whom the secrets of conscience do not escape.

62. So Simeon prophesied; she who was joined in marriage had prophesied, the virgin had prophesied; a widow too had to, so that no state of life and no sex should be lacking. And so Anna is brought in, such both in the service of her widowhood and in her conduct that she may be believed to have been fully worthy to announce that the Redeemer of all had come. Since we have described her merits elsewhere, when we were exhorting widows, we do not think they should be repeated in this place, as we are hastening on to other things. Yet not idly did he state the eighty-four years of her widowhood, since both seven twelves and two forties seem to signify a sacred number.

63. And when he had reached twelve years. From the twelfth year, as we read, the beginning of the Lord's teaching is taken; for this number of those preaching the Gospel was owed to the proclaiming of the faith. Nor is it idly that he, unmindful of his parents according to the flesh, he who according to the flesh was being filled with the wisdom of God and with grace, is found after three days in the temple: it was to be a sign that after three days of that triumphant Passion he who was believed dead would rise again and present himself to our faith in the heavenly seat and in divine honour.

64. Why is it that you were seeking me? Did you not know that I must be in the things that are my Father's? There are two generations in Christ: one is from the Father, the other from the mother; that from the Father is the more divine, while that from the mother is the one that came down to our toil and use. And so the things that are done above nature, above age, above custom are not to be ascribed to human powers, but must be referred to divine might. Elsewhere his mother urges him on to the mystery; here the mother is reproved, because she still asks for what is human. But since here he is described as twelve years old, there he is shown to have disciples, you see that the mother had learned from her son, so as to ask a mystery from him when he was grown, she who had marvelled at a miracle in him when he was younger.

65. And he came to Nazareth and was subject to them. For what should the master of virtue do but fulfil the duty of dutiful love? And do we wonder that he defers to the Father, who is subject to his mother? Surely this subjection belongs not to weakness but to dutiful love, though the serpent of unbelief, sent out from its sinister lurking-places, should lift up its head and vomit poisons from its serpentine breast. When the Son says that he was sent, the heretic calls the Father greater, so as to call the Son imperfect, as one who can have a greater, so as to assert that he who is sent needs the help of others. Did he then need human help too, that he obeyed his mother's command? He deferred to a human being, he deferred to a handmaid — for she herself says: Behold the handmaid of the Lord — he deferred to a reputed father: and do you wonder if he deferred to God? Or is it dutiful love to defer to a man, but weakness to defer to God? Rather, weigh divine things from human ones and recognize what love is owed to the Father. The Father honours the Son: do you not want the Son to honour the Father? The Father with a heavenly voice professes that he is well pleased in the Son: do you not want the Son, clothed in the garment of human flesh, when he speaks with a human voice and a human affection, to say that the Father is greater? For if great is the Lord and greatly to be praised, and of his greatness there is no end, surely a greatness that has no end has no increase either. But why should I not receive with devout ears that the Son was obedient to the Father in the taking up of a body, when I devoutly receive that the Father defers to the Son?

66. Learn rather precepts for your own profit and recognize examples of dutiful love. Learn what you owe your parents, when you read that the Son does not differ from the Father in will, in work, in time. Though they are two in persons, they are one in power, and certainly that heavenly Father underwent no toil of generation; but you owe to your mother the wrong done to her modesty, the loss of her virginity, the danger of childbirth; to your mother long weariness, to your mother long perils — to her who, poor woman, meets a greater danger in the very fruit of her prayers, and when she has brought forth what she longed for, is freed from the birth, not from fear. Why should I speak of fathers anxious for their sons' advancement, and of estates multiplied for the use of others, and of the seeds sown by the farmer to profit the ages of those who come after? Ought not at least obedience to be repaid for these things? Why should a father's life seem too long to an undutiful son, and the sharing of the inheritance too narrow, when Christ does not shun co-heirs?

67. The word of God came upon John the son of Zechariah in the desert. The Son of God, about to gather the Church, works first in his little servant. And so Saint Luke well set down that the word of God came upon John the son of Zechariah in the desert, so that the Church might begin not from man but from the word. For she herself is the desert, because more are the children of the deserted one than of her who has a husband. Indeed, to her it is said: Rejoice, barren one; and: Exult, O desert, because she was not yet tilled by any works of a people come from abroad, nor did those trees which could bear fruit yet raise any crown of their merits. He had not yet come who would say: But I am like a fruitful olive tree in the house of the Lord; that heavenly vine was not yet supplying fruit to its branches through a kind of layering of its words. So the word came, so that the land which before was a desert might produce fruit for us; the word came, the voice followed; for the word works first within, the service of the voice follows. Hence David too says: I believed, therefore I have spoken.

68. He first believed, so that he might be able to speak. And the word came, so that the holy Baptist John might preach repentance. And so many assign to Saint John the type of the Law, because the Law could denounce sin but could not forgive it; for the Law calls back from error each one who was following the way of the Gentiles, restrains him from crime, urges repentance on him, that he may obtain grace. The Law, however, and the prophets until John, and John is the forerunner of Christ. So the Law is the forerunner of the Church, and repentance of grace. Well, then, did Saint Luke use brevity, declaring John a prophet by saying that the word of God came upon him. He was to add nothing else; for no one who abounds in the word of God needs any token of himself. So he said one thing and declared everything.

69. But Saint Matthew and Mark wished to declare him a prophet by his clothing, his girdle and his food, in that he had a garment of camel's hair and a leather belt about his loins, and ate locusts and wild honey. For the forerunner of Christ did not suffer the skins of unclean beasts to perish, signifying by the very token of his clothing too that Christ would come, who, taking upon himself the sins of the unclean Gentile world, woven of bestial filth for the deformity of our merits, would strip off on that trophy of the cross the garment, as it were, of our flesh.

70. And what does the girding with a leather belt mean but that this flesh, which before was wont to weigh down the mind, after the coming of the Lord began to be to it not a hindrance but a girdle? For, according to David, we have hung our instruments on the willows, and, according to the Apostle, we have no confidence in the flesh and we have confidence in the body; we have none in pleasures, we have it in sufferings, so that our affection, fervent in spirit, may be strong, and we may be girded for every obedience to the heavenly precepts, intent with devotion of mind and unencumbered in the readiness of the body.

71. His food too is an index of his prophetic office and a messenger of the mystery. For what is so idle, as regards a man's duty, as to look for locusts, and what so full, as regards a prophet's mystery? For the more useless locusts are for fruit, the more idle for use, the more fleeting to the touch, roving in their leaping, shrill in their sound, the more fittingly is the figure of the Gentile people likened to them, a people who, with no practice of labour, with no fruit of its own work, without weight, without a voice, uttered a sound of complaint and knew not the word of life. This people, then, is the food of the prophets; for the more numerous the people gathered, the more richly the use of the prophetic mouth increases. The grace of the Church too is prefigured in the wild honey, not found within the hive of the Law as the produce of the Jewish people, but spread abroad in the fields and leaves of the forest of Gentile error, according to what was said: We found it in the fields of the forest.

72. And this one, indeed, ate wild honey, announcing that the peoples were to be filled with honey from the rock, as it is written: and with honey from the rock he filled them. So too the ravens fed Elijah in the desert with food brought from elsewhere and with drink gained, that it might be a sign that the peoples of the nations, foul with the dark color of their merits, who before sought their food in fetid carcasses, would now supply from themselves nourishment brought in for the prophets; for the food of the prophets is the fulfilment of the divine will, as the Lord himself declared, saying: My food is to do the will of him who sent me.

73. The voice of one crying in the desert. Rightly is John called the voice, the forerunner of the Word. For John himself, when he was asked: What do you say of yourself? says: I am the voice of one crying in the desert. Therefore he says: He who comes after me has been made before me, because the voice, the lesser, goes before, the Word, which excels, follows. Therefore he also wished to be baptized by John, because among men the word is consecrated by the voice of a teacher. Therefore perhaps Zechariah too received his voice back, because he uttered the voice.

Generation of vipers, who showed you to flee from the wrath to come? Bear, therefore, fruits worthy of repentance, and do not begin to say: Our father is Abraham. But I say to you that God is able from these stones to raise up children to Abraham;

74. the malice of the Jews seems indeed to be rebuked, in that, variegated with the venom of a harmful mind, they practise serpentine slidings and earthly lairs, and not any mystery of divine knowledge; but yet, when it is said: Who showed you to flee from the wrath to come? it is shown that prudence was poured into them by the compassion of God, so that they should repent of their sins, fearing with provident devotion the terror of the judgment to come. The comparison with vipers, then, must be referred to their begetting, not to their lineage. Or perhaps, according to what is written: Be prudent as serpents, they are shown to have a natural prudence, as those who see what will profit them and ask for it of their own accord, but do not yet abandon what is harmful.

75. And so they are admonished to claim for themselves the distinction of works rather than the nobility of birth, since there is no prerogative in descent unless the inheritance of faith supports it, an inheritance which he revealed in prophetic speech was by God's will to be transferred to the peoples of the Gentiles, saying: God is able from these stones to raise up children to Abraham. For although God can convert and change diverse natures, yet because the mystery profits me more than the miracle, in the forerunner of Christ I ought to recognize nothing more than the building up of the rising Church, which, built not of rocky stones but of living stones into a dwelling of God and the pinnacle of a temple, has risen by the conversion of our souls. For God was preparing to soften the hardness of our minds and from stones of stumbling to raise up worshippers of religion. For what else were they held to be but stones, who served stones, like indeed to those who made them? So it is prophesied that faith was to be poured into the stony breasts of the Gentiles, and the oracles promise that those would become sons of Abraham through faith, in whom, through hardness of mind, a kind of stony habit of an insensible and irrational nature had grown. For if the apostolic sentence compared men made firm by the strength of faith to living stones, according to what is written: And you, as living stones, be built up a spiritual house, into a holy priesthood, offering spiritual sacrifices, much more deeply do men here seem to be compared to stones by the prophetic voice, men who had so lost the sense of the human mind that, while they think that the reason of some divinity is present in stones, they themselves were turned into the nature of stones, not by the use of the body but by the habit of the mind. Indeed, those who sprang from Abraham's line according to the flesh are called both princes of Sodom and whitewashed walls. So likeness of conduct, rather than the order of ancestors, lays claim to the prerogative of birth. Moreover, that you may know that men were compared to stones, the prophet also compared men to trees, adding that now the axe is laid at the roots of the trees.

76. The example was changed for this reason, that by that progress in the comparison a certain milder advance of man might be understood. For those who before were unsightly for use, bare of adornment, barren of fruit, without reason for progress, are now figured in the likeness of trees, which, by a kind of rational gift of nature comely for use, lovely to the sight, rich in fruit, rise up with their tops, spread out with their branches, are filled with fruits, are clothed with leaves. And would that we could imitate the habit of fruitful trees and, as our merits grow, founded on the root of long-lasting humility, lofty from the ground, comely to the sight, raise up the full-grown tops of fruitful works, so that the axe of the husbandman of the Gospel may not cut out the root of a wild trunk. Woe indeed is me, if I do not preach the Gospel — but that voice is the Apostle's — woe is me, if I do not weep for my sins; woe is me, if I do not rise at midnight to confess to you; woe is me, if I deal deceitfully with my neighbour; woe, if I do not speak the truth. Already the axe is at the root; let him who can bear the fruit of grace, let him who ought bear that of repentance. The Lord is at hand, to look for fruit, to give life to the fruitful, to find out the barren. It is three years since he came, and he could not find fruit among the Jews: would that he may find it in us! He is about to order the unfruitful to be cut down, lest they take up the ground. But let those who as yet have no fruit strive to bring it forth in the future. That good tiller of the field will intercede for us, the unfertile, for us, the unfruitful, that time may be given, patience granted, if perchance we too may be able to bear some fruit to God.

77. The holy Baptist also gave a fitting answer to each class of men, one to all: thus to the tax collectors, that they should not exact more than was prescribed; to the soldiers, that they should not make false accusations or seek plunder, teaching that pay was established for military service for this reason, lest, while provisions are sought, the plunderer should run riot. But these and the other precepts of duties are proper to each; mercy is of common use, therefore a common precept, necessary for all offices, all ages, and to be shown by all. Not the tax collector, not the soldier is excepted, not the farmer or the townsman, rich and poor: all in common are admonished to give to him who has not — for mercy is the fullness of the virtues, and therefore it is set before all as the form of perfect virtue — and not to spare their clothing and their food. Yet of mercy itself a measure is kept according to the possibility of the human condition, so that each one should not strip himself of everything, but share what he has with the poor.

78. And as the people were reckoning and thinking in their hearts about John, whether perhaps he might be the Christ, he answered, saying: I indeed baptize you with water in repentance. So John saw the secrets of the heart. But let us consider whose grace this is. How the secrets of the heart become plain to prophets Paul shows, saying: The secrets of his heart too will be made manifest, and falling on his face he will worship God, declaring that God is truly among you. It is, then, the gift of God, who reveals, not the power of man. Such a one is helped by a divine gift rather than seeing by a natural faculty. And what does this thought of the Jews serve for, except to prove that Christ came according to the Scriptures? For there was one who was awaited, and surely it was the very one who was awaited that came, not one who was not awaited. But what is more foolish than that he who is supposed to be in another is not believed to be in himself? Whom they thought would come through a woman, they do not believe has come through a virgin. What generation according to the flesh was more worthy of God than that the immaculate Son of God should keep the purity of an immaculate generation even in taking up a body? And surely the sign of the divine coming was set in the childbearing of a virgin, not of a married woman. I he says baptize you with water.

79. He quickly proved that he was not the Christ, since he works by a visible office. For since man consists of two natures, that is, of soul and body, what is visible is consecrated through visible things, what is invisible through the invisible mystery; for by water the body is washed, by the Spirit the sins of the soul are cleansed. We do one thing, we invoke another, though even in the font itself the sanctification of the divinity breathes; for the washing is not wholly by water; but these cannot be divided from each other, and so the baptism of repentance was one thing, the baptism of grace is another: this is from both, that from one. For since sins are common to mind and body, the purification too had to be common. And well did Saint John, indicating that he had understood what they were thinking in their hearts and yet, as though he had not understood, declining the odium of majesty, declare not by word but by deed that he was not the Christ. For it is the work of man to repent of sins, it is the gift of God to fulfil the grace of the mystery.

80. But one stronger than I is coming. He made no comparison, as if he had said that Christ was only so much stronger than himself — for there could be no comparing the Son of God and a man — but he says it because there are many strong. For the devil too is strong; no one indeed can plunder the goods of a strong man unless he first binds the strong man. Many, then, are strong, but none stronger except Christ. Indeed, so far was he from making a comparison that he added: whose sandals I am not worthy to carry, showing that the grace of the preaching of the Gospel had been conferred on the apostles, who are shod for the Gospel.

81. Yet he seems to say this because John often took on the person of the Jewish people. Hence they refer to this what he says: He must increase, but I must decrease, meaning that the Jewish people had to become less, and the Christian people to increase in Christ. Indeed, Moses too took on the person of the people, but he also carried not the Lord's sandal but that of his own feet. And these were shod perhaps with a sandal not of their own feet, whereas he is bidden to loose the sandal of his own feet, so that the step of his soul and mind, loosed from the bonds of bodily ties, may tread a spiritual path. But the apostles had laid aside the bodily sandal when they were sent without sandal, without staff, without wallet, without belt; but they did not at once carry the Lord's sandals. Perhaps they began to carry them after the resurrection; for before, they were warned not to tell anyone the deeds of the Lord. Indeed, afterwards it is said to them: Go into the whole world and preach the Gospel, so that, putting forth the footstep of the preaching of the Gospel, they might carry the sequence of the Lord's deeds round the whole world. The nuptial sandal, then, is the preaching of the Gospel. But we shall explain something about this more fittingly elsewhere.

82. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and in fire, having his winnowing fan in his hand, and he will cleanse his threshing floor and gather the wheat into his barn, but the chaff he will burn with unquenchable fire. Having his winnowing fan in his hand. By the token of the winnowing fan the Lord is declared to have the right of distinguishing merits, because while the grain is winnowed on the threshing floor, the full are separated from the empty, the fruitful from the worthless, as by a kind of testing of the blowing breeze. Through this comparison, then, the Lord is shown to distinguish on the day of judgment the merits and fruits of solid virtue from the fruitless lightness of empty boasting and of meagre deeds, as he is to place the men of more perfect merit in the heavenly mansion. For he is the more perfect fruit who has deserved to be conformed to him who, like a grain of wheat, fell, that he might bring forth many fruits in us, hateful to the chaff, no friend to empty merits. And so before him a fire will burn that is not harmful by its own nature, since it burns away the evils of wickedness and heaps up the splendor of goodness.

83. Now it came to pass, when all the people had been baptized, and when Jesus had been baptized and was praying, that heaven was opened and the Holy Spirit descended in bodily form like a dove upon him, and a voice came from heaven: You are my Son, in you I am well pleased. So the Lord was baptized, not wishing to be cleansed but to cleanse the waters, so that, washed by the flesh of Christ, which knew no sin, they might have the right of baptism. And so whoever comes to the washing of Christ lays aside his sins. Beautifully, too, Saint Luke the evangelist has abridged what was said by the others, and has left it to be understood, rather than expressed, that the Lord was baptized by John. What the cause of the Lord's baptism was, the Lord himself declares, saying: Let it be now; for thus it becomes us to fulfil all righteousness.

84. Since, then, God by divine favour granted such great things for the building up of his Church that, after patriarchs, prophets, angels, the only-begotten Son of God should come down and come to the washing, have we not come to know that it was truly and divinely said of the Church: Unless the Lord builds himself a house, they have laboured in vain who build it? Nor is it any wonder if man cannot build, when he cannot guard: Unless the Lord guards the city, they have watched in vain who guard it. So much from a certain psalm. Yet I too dare to say that a man cannot set out on the way unless he has the Lord going before him. Hence it is written: You shall walk after the Lord your God; and: By the Lord are a man's ways directed. Indeed, he was the more perfect who understood that he could not walk without the Lord. Your ways he says teach me. And to come to the history — for we ought not only to drink in the simple sequence of what was done, but also to refer our own acts to the imitation of what is written — the people went out of Egypt. It did not know the way that would lead to the holy land. God sent a pillar of fire, that the people might learn the way by night. He sent also by day a pillar of cloud, that they might turn aside neither to the left nor to the right. But you are not such, O man, as to deserve a pillar of fire too; you do not have Moses, you do not receive a sign; for now, after the coming of the Lord, faith is required, signs are put away. Fear the Lord and take confidence in the Lord; the angel indeed of the Lord will encamp round about those who fear him and will deliver them. You surely see that everywhere the power of the Lord cooperates with human efforts, so that no one can build without the Lord, no one guard without the Lord, no one begin anything without the Lord. And so, according to the Apostle: Whether you eat or drink, do all things in the glory of God, in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ; for in two epistles he bade you act, in one in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, in the other in the glory of God, so that you may know that the glory of the Father and of the Son is the same and the power the same, and that the Father and the Son, who do not differ in regard to our protection, differ in nothing in regard to their divinity. So David taught me that no one without the Lord builds a house or guards a city.

85. Moses too taught me that no one but God made the world; in the beginning indeed God made heaven and earth. He taught also that God made man by his own work, and not idly did he set down: God formed man from the slime of the earth and breathed into his face the breath of life, so that you may observe a certain act of God in the building of man, after the manner of a bodily working. He taught also that God made the woman too; God indeed cast a deep sleep upon Adam, and he slept; and he took one rib from his side and filled up his flesh. And the Lord God built the rib which he took from Adam into a woman. Not in vain, as I have said, does Moses bring in God working on Adam and Eve with bodily hands, as it were. God commanded the world to be made, and it was made, and by one word Scripture indicates that the work of the world was finished; when it comes to man, the prophet was at pains in some way to show you the very hands of God at work.

86. The works of God, so carefully wrought, compel me to understand something more in them than I read. The Apostle comes to the help of one perplexed, and what I did not understand — what is meant by: bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh and she shall be called woman, because she was taken from her man — he revealed to me by the divine Spirit, saying: This sacrament is great. What sacrament? That the two shall be in one flesh, and that a man shall leave father and mother and cleave to his wife, and that we are members of his body, of his flesh and of his bones. Who is this man for whose sake the woman leaves her parents? The Church leaves her parents, she who was gathered from the Gentile peoples, to whom it is said prophetically: Forget your people and your father's house. For the sake of what man, unless perhaps him of whom John says: After me comes a man who has been made before me? From whose side, as he slept, God took a rib; for he it is who slept and rested and rose again, because the Lord upheld him. What is his rib but power? For then, when the soldier opened his side, at once there came out water and blood, which was poured out for the life of the world. This life of the world is the rib of Christ, this is the rib of the second Adam; the first indeed Adam unto a living soul, the last Adam unto a life-giving spirit; the last Adam is Christ, the rib of Christ is the life of the Church. We therefore are members of his body, of his flesh and of his bones. And perhaps this is the rib of which he said: I perceive that power has gone out from me; this is the rib which went out from Christ and did not diminish his body; for it is not a bodily rib but a spiritual one, and the Spirit is not himself divided, but divides to each as he wills. This is Eve, the mother of all the living. For if you understand you seek the living among the dead, you understand that those are dead who are without Christ, who are not partakers of life; for this is what it is not to be partakers of Christ, since Christ is life. The mother of the living, then, is the Church, which God built with Christ Jesus himself as the chief cornerstone, in whom the whole structure, joined together, grows into a temple.

87. Let God come, then, let him build the woman: that one indeed the helper of Adam, this one the helper of Christ — not that Christ needs help, but because we seek and desire to come to the grace of Christ through the Church. Now she is being built, now she is being formed, now the woman is being fashioned, now she is being created. And so Scripture used a new word, because we are built up upon the foundation of the apostles and prophets. And now a spiritual house rises into a holy priesthood. Come, Lord God, build this woman, build the city. Let your Servant come too; for I believe you when you say: He will build the city for me.

88. Behold the woman, the mother of all; behold the spiritual house; behold the city that lives for ever, because it knows not how to die. For she is the city of Jerusalem, which is now seen on earth, but will be caught up above Elijah — for Elijah was but one — she will be translated above Enoch, whose death is not found; for he was caught up, lest wickedness should change his heart, but she is loved by Christ as glorious, holy, immaculate, without wrinkle. And how much better it is that the whole body is taken up than that he was taken up! For this is the hope of the Church. She will surely be caught up, she will be taken up, she will be translated to heaven. Behold, Elijah was caught up in a chariot of fire; the Church will be caught up. You do not believe me? Believe at least Paul, in whom Christ spoke. We shall be caught up he says in the clouds to meet Christ in the air; and so we shall always be with the Lord.

89. To build her, then, many are indeed sent: patriarchs are sent, prophets are sent, Gabriel the archangel is sent, countless angels are dispatched, and the multitude of the heavenly host praises God, because the building of this city was drawing near. Many are sent to her, but Christ alone builds her; yet he is not alone, because the Father is present; and if he alone builds, yet he does not alone claim the grace of so great a building. It is written of the temple of God which Solomon built, in which there was a type of the Church, that there were seventy thousand who carried on their shoulders and eighty thousand stonecutters. Let those angels come, let the stonecutters come, let what is superfluous in our stones be hewn away, let the rough places be made smooth; let those also come who carry on their shoulders; for it is written: They shall be carried upon shoulders.

90. So he came to John, since you have learned the rest. He came to John's baptism, but John's baptism held repentance of sins. And therefore John forbids him, saying: 'I ought to be baptized by you, and do you come to me? Why do you come to me, you who have no sin? For he must be baptized who has sin; but why should he who has done no sin seek the washing of repentance?' Let it be he says now — that is: while the Church is being built — it becomes us to fulfil all righteousness. What is righteousness but mercy? He has scattered indeed, he has given to the poor: his righteousness remains for ever. He gave to me, poor as I was, he gave to me, needy as I was, the grace which I did not have before; so his righteousness remains for ever. What is righteousness but that you yourself should be the first to begin what you wish another to do, and should exhort others by your example? What is righteousness but that, because he took up flesh, he should not as God exclude the feeling or the service of the flesh, but as man should conquer the flesh, that he might teach me to conquer? For he taught by what means I can bury, as to its crimes, the filth of the guilty flesh compacted of earthly vices, and renew it in virtues.

91. O truly divine foresight in the very humility of the Lord! For the more abject the humility, the more divine the providence. By the bitterness of the injuries he suffered God is revealed, and by his use of remedies, he who needed no remedies, he is proved God. For what is so divine for calling forth the peoples as that no one should shun the washing of grace, when Christ did not shun the washing of repentance? Let no one say he has no share in sin, when Christ came to the remedy of sins. If Christ washed for us, nay rather washed us in his own body, how much more ought we to wash away our own sins? By what work, then, by what mystery more than by this is God proved, though he is God in all things, when, through the whole world, wherever the condition of the human race is spread, through the partings and expanses of separate regions, in one moment, in one body, God abolished the fraud of the ancient error and poured out the grace of the heavenly kingdom? For one was immersed, but he raised up all; one descended, that we might all ascend; one took upon himself the sins of all, that in him the sins of all might be cleansed. Purify yourselves, then, as the Apostle says, because he purified himself for us, he who had no need of purification. So much concerning us.

92. Now let us consider the mystery of the Trinity. We say one God, but we confess the Father and we confess the Son. For though it is written: You shall love the Lord your God and him alone shall you serve, the Son denied that he was alone, saying: But I am not alone; for the Father is with me. Nor is he alone now; for the Father testifies that he is present. The Holy Spirit is present; for the Trinity can never be separated from itself. Indeed, heaven was opened, the Holy Spirit descended in bodily form like a dove. How, then, do the heretics say that he is alone in heaven, who is not alone on earth? Let us observe the mystery. Why like a dove? Because the grace of the washing requires simplicity, that we may be simple as doves. The grace of the washing requires peace, which in the ancient type a dove once brought to that ark which alone was untouched by the flood. He taught me whose type that dove was, he who has now deigned to descend in the form of a dove; he taught that in that branch, in that ark, was the type of peace and of the Church, since amid the very floods of the world the Holy Spirit brings fruitful peace to his Church. David too taught it, who, discerning with prophetic spirit the sacrament of baptism, says: Who will give me wings like a dove?

93. So the Holy Spirit came, but attend to the mystery. He came to Christ; all things indeed were created through him and hold together in him. But see the kindly Lord, who alone submitted himself to injuries, alone did not seek favour. And where did he build the Church? I will ask he says the Father, and he will give you another Paraclete, that he may be with you for ever, the Spirit of truth, whom this world cannot receive, because it does not see him nor know him. Rightly, then, did he show himself in a body, since in the substance of divinity he is not seen.

94. We have seen the Spirit, but in bodily form; let us see the Father too. But because we cannot see him, let us hear him; for the kindly God is present, he will not forsake his temple. He wishes to build up every soul, he wishes to form it for salvation, he wishes to transfer living stones from earth to heaven. He loves his temple; let us too love him. If we love God, let us keep his precepts; if we love him, let us know him; he who indeed says that he knows him and does not keep his precepts is a liar. For how can he love God who does not love the truth, since God is truth? Let us hear the Father, then; for the Father is invisible. But the Son too is invisible according to his divinity; God indeed no one has ever seen. Since, then, the Son is God, in that in which he is God the Son is not seen; but he willed to show himself in a body, and because the Father did not bear a body, the Father therefore willed to prove to us that he is present in the Son, saying: You are my Son, in you I am well pleased. If you wish to learn that the Son is always present with the Father, read the voice of the Son, saying: If I ascend into heaven, you are there; if I descend into the underworld, you are present. If you seek the testimony of the Father, you have heard it from John. Believe him to whom Christ entrusted himself to be baptized, to whom the Father commended the Son by a heavenly voice, saying: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased.

95. Where are the Arians, who are not pleased with the Son in whom the Father was well pleased? It is not I who say this, nor has any man spoken it, nor God through a man, nor through angels nor through archangels, but a voice sent from heaven by the Father himself has sealed it. Then elsewhere the same Father repeats it, saying: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased; hear him, hear him, that is, when he says: I and the Father are one. He, then, who does not believe the Son does not believe the Father. He is a witness concerning the Son. If there is doubt about the Son, the Father's testimony is not believed. Then, when he says: in whom I am well pleased, he praises in the Son not what is another's, but what is his own. For what is it to say: in whom I am well pleased but that all that the Son has is mine, as the Son said: All that the Father has is mine? For the power of an undivided divinity does not separate between the Father and the Son, but imparts one power to the Father and the Son. Let us believe the Father, whose voice the elements sounded forth; let us believe the Son, to whose voice the elements rendered obedience. The world believed in the elements, let it believe in men; it believed in lifeless things, let it believe in the living; it believed in mute things, let it believe in those who speak; it believed in things that have no sense, let it believe in those who have obtained sense to know God.

Book Three

1. And Jesus himself was beginning, about thirty years of age, being thought to be the son of Joseph. Being about to speak of the generations, between which we see that there is some difference in the Gospel according to Matthew and in this one, whose interpretation we have in hand, let us show with all the zeal we can that they did not say discordant things, since it is not credible that holy men could have said things contrary to one another, above all concerning the deeds of the Lord Savior.

2. And first of all, no one ought to be troubled that it is written thus: being thought to be the son of Joseph. For rightly thought, since he was not so by nature, but he was thought so because Mary, who was betrothed to Joseph her husband, had borne him; for so you have it: Is not this the son of Joseph the carpenter? We have said above for what reason the Lord Savior willed to be born of a virgin, we have said also for what reason of a betrothed woman and why at the time of the census; it seems not out of place also to declare for what reason he had a carpenter for a father. For by this type he shows that his Father is he who, the craftsman of all things, founded the world, according to what is written: In the beginning God made heaven and earth. For although human things are not to be compared with divine, yet the type is complete, in that the Father of Christ works with fire and spirit, and like a good carpenter of the soul hews away our vices all round, quickly putting the axe to unfruitful trees, skilled to cut away what is puny, to keep what is lofty for the roof-tops, to soften the rigid parts of minds with the fire of the spirit, and to shape the whole human race for various uses by the diverse quality of its services.

3. But why the generation of Joseph rather than of Mary is described, since Mary bore Christ of the Holy Spirit and Joseph seems a stranger to the Lord's generation, we might doubt, did not the custom of the Scriptures instruct us, which always seeks the origin of the man. For so you have it: Perez was the son of Judah, the prince of the tribe. He begot Hezron, and Hezron begot Aram, and Aram begot Amminadab, and Amminadab begot Nahshon, and Nahshon begot Salmon, and Salmon begot Boaz, and Boaz begot Obed, and Obed begot Jesse, and Jesse begot David. For it is the person of the man that is sought, who even in the senate and in the other councils of cities asserts the dignity of the family. And how unseemly, if, the man's origin being left aside, the origin of the woman were sought, so that he who was to be preached to the peoples of the whole world should seem not to have had a father!

4. But let us show that elsewhere too a genealogy has been run through in a different order, lest here also the evangelists, who have followed the ancient order, should seem to disagree. For so you have it: There was a man of Arath, and his name was Elkanah, son of Hieremiel, son of Heli, son of Ozi. You see that the description of descent is woven together in the ancient manner both from fathers to sons and from sons to fathers; you see that everywhere a family is run through by the generations of men: do not wonder if Matthew ran through the order of generations from Abraham down to Joseph, Luke from Joseph up to Adam and to God. Do not wonder that Joseph's descent has been described. For, born according to the flesh, he had to follow the usage of the flesh, and he who came into the world had to be described after the manner of the world, especially since in the descent of Joseph there is also the descent of Mary. For since Joseph was a righteous man, he surely took a wife from his own tribe and from his own country, nor could a righteous man act against what is prescribed by the Law.
