For so you have it that the sons of Israel will cleave each one to the inheritance of the tribe of his country and will not pass from tribe to tribe, and every daughter who has an inheritance of the tribes of the sons of Israel shall be wife to one of the people and of the tribe of her father. And so at the time of the census too Joseph went up, of the house and of the country of David, to be enrolled with Mary his wife. She who makes her declaration from the same house and from the same country surely shows that she is of the same tribe and of the same country.

5. Elizabeth too is introduced as Mary's kinswoman, first because all Jews are kin, as the Apostle also taught, saying: For I wished that I myself were accursed for my brothers, my kinsmen according to the flesh, who are Israelites. Kinswomen, then, because both were Israelites, and at the same time kinswomen because both were of the tribe of Judah. You have learned that Mary was of the tribe of Judah; learn it of Elizabeth. For Mary, rising in those days, went into the hill country with haste, to a city he says of Judaea, and entered the house of Zechariah. For since Moses prescribed that each should dwell according to his tribe, surely, since she lived in a city of Judaea, she was also in the tribe of Judah, especially since there were priests of Elizabeth's family, whose portion is God. At the same time, how beautiful that, since the one bore the forerunner of Christ and the other Christ, and the one conceived of the Holy Spirit while the other, filled with the Holy Spirit, prophesied, they should be seen to have been kinswomen according to the flesh as well, who according to God did not lack the fellowship of a spiritual kinship! But if, according to the holy Apostle, the man is the head of every woman, and according to the divine law the two are in one flesh, how surely could it happen that those who were one flesh and one spirit should seem to have a divided country and tribe? There is this besides, that the angel Gabriel too foretold of the Lord that the Lord will give him the throne of David his father. It is certain, therefore, that Mary too sprang from the line of David. At the same time we learn also that it makes no difference in what order the series of a genealogy is set out, since the road is passable from this side and from that.

6. But why Saint Matthew began to enumerate the generation of Christ from Abraham, while Saint Luke carried it from Christ up to God, seems to need explaining. But first I think we must by no means pass over why Saint Matthew, though he began the order of the generation from Abraham, did not put it thus: 'The book of the generation of Abraham', but: The book of the generation of Jesus Christ, son of David, son of Abraham, and why he named these two above all. For not idly are the most faithful founders of the line chosen, that we may understand that in the very generation of the flesh a spiritual succession is the more sought; for these are the two men to whom the divine promises flowed down.

7. First Abraham, who before the law of Moses and before the people of the Jews, leaving what was his own and knowing God, earned the testimony of faith, because he believed God and it was reckoned to him for righteousness; who also received an oracle from God, who said to him: Go out from your land and from your kindred and from the house of your father into the land which I will show you, and I will make you into a great nation, and I will bless you and magnify your name, and you will be blessed, and I will bless those who bless you and curse those who curse you, and in you all the tribes of the earth will be blessed. You see, then, that the gatherings of the Gentiles and the assembly of the most holy Church were promised by divine oracle to him first. And therefore he had to be designated founder of the line who first earned the pledge of the Church that was to be established.

8. David too is rightly himself declared founder of the line, because he received with an oath the answer that Christ would come from him according to the flesh; for so it is written: The Lord swore truth to David, and it will not repent him: Of the fruit of your womb I will set upon my throne; and elsewhere: Once have I sworn by my holiness, if I lie to David; his seed will remain for ever, and his throne as the sun in my sight; and in Chronicles: And it will be, when your days are fulfilled and you sleep with your fathers, I will raise up your seed after you, who will be from your womb, and I will prepare his kingdom. He will build me a house, and I will set up his throne for ever. I will be to him a father, and he will be to me a son, and I will not scatter my mercy from him, as I scattered it from those who were before you. Through Isaiah too the same Lord God revealed the generation of the Lord, saying: A rod will come forth from the root of Jesse and a flower will rise up from the root, and the Spirit of God will rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and understanding; and below: And there will be the root of Jesse, and he who will rise up to rule the Gentiles; in him the Gentiles will hope; and elsewhere: A child is born to us, a son is given to us, whose rule is upon his shoulders, and his name will be called the angel of great counsel; for I will bring peace upon the princes and salvation to him. Great is his power, and of his peace there is no end, on the throne of David and in his kingdom. In which, in Aquila's translation too, we see that the promise was made not as of a man but of him who was beyond man. For he translated: A child is born to us, a son is given to us, and his measure was made upon his shoulder, and his name will be called wonderful counsellor, my counsellor, strong, mighty, father of the age, prince of peace. His discipline has been fulfilled, and of his peace there is no end upon the throne of David and upon his kingdom.

9. That all these things fit Christ is marked by evident oracles. Nor could the fruit of the divine power be diverted to the favour of Solomon, who was David's son, whose end is known beyond doubt; for there was an end of Solomon's kingdom and of his peace, as is shown by the readings of Kings. Christ is the one whose kingdom has no end. Then, Solomon ruled over no nations, but Christ gathered the Church from the nations. Lastly, while David was living Solomon was both born and attained the kingdom, but he who is promised is shown to be going to arise after David's death, as you have it: When your days are fulfilled and you sleep with your fathers, I will raise up of your seed one who will be from your womb, and I will prepare his kingdom. He will build me a house, and I will set up his throne for ever. Did Solomon reign for ever, who reigned only forty years? I will be he says to him a father, and he will be to me a son — who is that proper Son of God, but he to whom it was said: You are my Son, today I have begotten you? — And I will not scatter my mercy from him, and I will keep him faithful in my house and in his kingdom for ever? But that Solomon erred so gravely, perhaps for this reason, lest men should err and it be believed that the promise had flowed down to him, we have learned from the series of the divine readings; for he built a temple to the idol Astarte for love of a woman, and the Lord was angry with Solomon. If, then, he both began to reign while David was alive — for so you have it that, when it was announced to David that Solomon was reigning, the king worshipped on his bed and said: Blessed be the Lord God of Israel. Who has given me today of my seed one sitting on my throne, and my eyes see it — if he erred, if he offended, you see that the series of the promise pledged Christ.

10. And therefore the evangelist chose these two founders of the line: the one who received the promise of the gathering of the peoples, the other who obtained the oracle of the generation of Christ. And therefore, though he is later in the order of succession, he is nevertheless described before Abraham in the Lord's generation, because it is more to have received a promise concerning Christ than concerning the Church, since the Church itself exists through Christ. So the one is prince of the line according to the flesh, the other prince of the line according to the spirit; the one according to the grace of seed, the other according to the faith of the peoples; for he who saves is greater than he who is saved. And therefore he is called son of David: The book of the generation of Jesus Christ, son of David; for he had to be called in the first place the son of him to whom a son was promised, although the Apostle says that Christ was promised to Abraham as well; to Abraham, indeed, were the promises spoken, and to his seed. He does not say: and to seeds, as in many, but as in one: and to your seed, which is Christ, so that to the one the property of generation, to the other the princedom may be assigned. To the one it was granted that Jesus should be called his son; to the other, as to the prince of the family and of the Gentiles, the prerogative is kept that the beginning of the Lord's generation should flow from Abraham; for he who is the author of faith, Scripture too had to declare the author of the divine generation.

11. Hence Luke too thought that his origin should be referred to God, because God is the true begetter of Christ, either as Father according to true generation, or as author of the mystical gift according to the regeneration of the washing. And therefore he did not begin to describe his generation at the outset, but after he had set forth his baptism, desiring to show that God is the author of all things through baptism, he declared that Christ too flowed from God in the order of succession, weaving all things together, so as to show him Son of God according to nature, according to grace and according to the flesh. And what clearer sign of the divine generation than that, about to speak of the generation, he set first the Father himself speaking: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased?

12. Here too some are wont to raise questions: that Matthew enumerated forty-two generations from Abraham down to Christ, but Luke fifty, and that Matthew described the generation as flowing through some persons, Luke through others. In which you can already prove what we said: that although Matthew wove into the order of generation some ancestors of the Lord's line and Luke others, yet each marked the remaining founders of the line from Abraham and David.

13. That Matthew thought the generation should be derived through Solomon, but Luke through Nathan, seems to show the one a royal, the other a priestly family of Christ. Which we ought not to take as if the one were truer than the other, but as the one agreeing with the other in equal faith and truth. For he was truly, even according to the flesh, of a royal and a priestly family, a king from kings, a priest from priests. Although the oracle is expressed not of carnal but of heavenly things, since both the king rejoices in the power of God, he to whom judgment is committed by the Father, the King, and he is a priest for ever. According to which it is written: You are a priest for ever according to the order of Melchizedek. So each rightly kept faith: Matthew, to prove the origin led through kings, and Luke, by tracing through priests the series of the line handed down from God into Christ, to declare that origin itself the holier. At the same time, in this the figure of the calf too is signified, in that he thinks the priestly mystery must everywhere be preserved.

14. Nor should you wonder if according to Luke there are more successions from Abraham down to Christ, fewer according to Matthew, since you admit that the generation has run through different persons; for it can happen that some lived a long life, while the men of the other generation died at an untimely age, since we see a good many old men living with their grandsons, while other men die as soon as they have had sons.

15. We notice this too, that Saint Matthew recorded that Jacob, who was the father of Joseph, was the son of Matthan, but Luke described Joseph, to whom Mary was betrothed, as the son of Heli, and Heli as the son of Melchi. How are there two fathers of one man, that is, Heli and Jacob? How also two paternal grandfathers, Matthan and Melchi? But if you follow it up, you will find that, according to the prescription of the old Law, two brothers begot of one wife different sons born of the same womb. For it is handed down that Matthan, who drew his line from Solomon, begot a son Jacob and died, his wife surviving him, whom Melchi afterwards took as wife, and of her Heli was begotten. Again, when Heli's brother died without children, Heli was joined to his brother's wife and begot a son Joseph, who according to the Law is called Jacob's son, since according to the course of the old Law the brother raised up the seed of the dead brother. So he was called the son of two, not begotten by both, but because he was made the son of the one by generation, of the other by law. In this prescription of the Law the people of the Jews did not understand that a future perpetuity of the seed of the dead was promised to us, but, taking it according to the letter, spoiled the grace of the oracle. For there was another brother who would raise up again the seed of dead brothers, a brother not according to kinship of flesh, but according to purity of grace. And is that perhaps why a brother does not redeem: a man will redeem? Because it was not a brother by birth, but the Lord and the mediator of God and men, the man Jesus Christ who spread abroad the grace of the resurrection; although there is also another interpretation of that little verse, which we shall give in its place.

16. Nor does it seem absurd that Saint Matthew thought the generations should be divided into fourteens, the mystical number being doubled: from Abraham to David, from David to the deportation to Babylon, from the deportation to Babylon to Christ; in which he likewise marked the turns of the changes. For in the times from Abraham to David the people of the Jews was without kings — for rightful kingship began from David — then the whole nation of the Jews was governed by kings, and their kingdoms remained inviolate until their deportation; but after the deportation the nobility of the degenerating people, cut short, sank toward its setting. As for the fifty successions which Luke thought should be woven together from Abraham, it is clear enough that the grace of a mystical number has been kept; for both the tenth and the seventh number are mystical, and each, repeated three times, signifies a mystery; and the Pentecost, whose grace Luke followed, and the Tesseracoste, whose grace Matthew followed, have revealed the mystical number more than enough.

17. Many also wonder why Matthew thought that mention of Tamar, a woman of ill repute, as it seems to them, should be woven into the Lord's generation, why also of Ruth, why also of that woman who was Uriah's wife and, her husband slain, passed into marriage with David, especially since he nowhere made mention of Sarah and Rebekah and Rachel, holy women. For so you have it: Abraham begot Isaac, Isaac begot Jacob, Jacob begot Judah and his brothers, and Judah begot Perez and Zerah of Tamar. Not idly, then, did the evangelist insert the name of this woman, about whom it seems we must treat separately for the moment.

18. For first, if you turn your mind to the truth, this woman is not so much notorious as righteous; for she did not seek the use of a passing lust, but desired the grace of succession; for it was unseemly to have no children, which was even punished by the authority of the civil laws. Judah had promised her to his son and for a long time had put off the covenant of the agreed marriage. Through the delay of the promise the bridegroom died. Not yet, before the coming of Christ, did the grace of virginity, not yet that of widowhood flourish. Grieving that she had remained without sons, out of zeal for generation she devised a stratagem and forestalled Judah by a plan, so as to offer herself to him adorned, after she learned that his wife had died. You see that the woman's life is everywhere approved, in that she did not snatch another's bed, nor was she adorned as a harlot from a harlot's zeal; for she did not court a wandering lust, but, long cheated of her father-in-law's promises, by turning the stratagem she wished to obtain the fruit of succession from the family she had chosen. Who, indeed, was more chaste? She who waited so long for what was promised, or he who could not bear a love that was offered? She who did not flee the family of her betrothed, or he who took her for a harlot? She who did not yield the border of her body to those who wished it for intercourse, or he who, what he began from the eagerness of error, brought to completion for the grace of succession through the chastity of the woman? She who had no sons and feared that the time of conception was being shut out by the delay of marriage, she who chose the gravity of a riper man, or he who fell in love with the age of a younger woman? Finally he himself confessed, saying: Tamar is justified rather than I, because I did not give her to Selom my son. And so she wished to put to the test the very man who exacted her chastity. Finally she never afterwards knew a man; after that union she put on the garment of widowhood; he, unable to endure for one hour, who had exacted years of chastity from a girl, put away his mourning, changed his garment, cut his hair, forsook the pyre, and as a lover climbed into bed.

19. But we do not defend her in such a way as to accuse him — rather, let us excuse both; yet it is not we who excuse them, but the mystery which the fruit of that union expressed; for the woman bore Perez and Zerah as sons, she bore twins. Hence not idly did Matthew indicate both, when the reason for mention required only Perez; Perez, indeed, begot Hezron, Hezron begot Aram, then each one in order. But why, when Isaac begot two and Jacob several, did the series of Scripture make mention only of single ones, whom the reason of the Lord's succession required, yet recorded both of these, unless because here there is a mystery in both?

20. We have treated the moral passage, that she did not ply the works of a harlot but chose the gifts of fruitfulness; let us treat the historical and winnow the mystical; for it cannot be without a mystery that she received a ring and a necklace and a staff. Not mean is the person who merits to receive ornament, seal and power: the seal of deeds, the ornament of the breast, the badge of royal freedom. So, to begin with the history: when Tamar was giving birth, you have read that one put forth his hand from her womb first, and the midwife, seizing it, tied scarlet on it, saying: This one will come out first. But when the child drew his hand back into his mother's womb, at once his brother came out. And the midwife said: Why has the hedge been cut through because of you? And she called his name Perez. And after him came out his brother, on whose hand was the scarlet, and she called his name Zerah. You see how many enigmas reveal the mystery: the hand put forth, the scarlet tied, the hand drawn back, the twofold saying of the midwife, that the one would come out first, that the other cut through the hedge.

21. But why did the one put forth his hand from the womb, while the other went before in the coming forth of birth, unless because through the mystery of the twins a twofold life of the peoples is described, one according to the Law, the other according to faith, one according to the letter, the other according to grace? Grace is prior to the Law, faith prior to the letter. And therefore the type of grace put forth his hand first, because the action of grace went first, which was in Job, Melchizedek, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, who lived through faith without the Law; Abraham, indeed, believed God, and it was reckoned to him for righteousness. Melchizedek too, going before, showed in a figure the grace of the holy sacrifice; for the holy patriarchs, anticipating the Law, released from the bonds of precepts, shone with the free grace of the Gospel, like to ours.

22. The younger brother of these is the second order of devotion; for the first is in the patriarchs, the second in the kings and priests. For both lives are according to God. For those too who served a religious and dutiful warfare under the law of Moses are not without share in grace and honour, but the fruit of devotion was earlier in the founders than in the heirs. For Zerah was first, who by interpretation signifies 'the East'; for the light of devotion is the splendour of the true East, of him surely who said: The East is my name, whose ray of light first shone in the patriarchs. For these first put forth the action of their life into this world, and in their hand the figure of our action too went before, as of a fuller body, we who were still held as it were in the womb of generating nature. But the observance of the Law was set in the middle like a hedge thrown across, and in a manner the life of the forefathers seems to have been cut through; of which that midwife in the type — perhaps righteousness, which received us when we were sent forth from the belly of mother nature — is reported to have said: This one will come out first. And truly wonderful was that order of devotion, impressed not by Moses nor by any man nor through man, but by a certain gift of wisdom pouring itself in of its own accord.

23. So the first discipline of devotion is according to the Gospel, because we believe through the cross and blood of Christ, whose day Abraham saw and rejoiced; whose grace, figured in the type of the Church, Noah foresaw with spiritual knowledge; whose part in the sacrifice Isaac did not refuse; whom Jacob worshipped when he was prevailing; the redness of whose garments Isaiah saw — for the life of the prophets too is according to the Gospel — in whose blood, amid the destruction of the world, that Rahab, a harlot in type, the Church in mystery, showed the future sign of public salvation; the Church which does not refuse union with many strangers, and the more joined to many, the more chaste: an immaculate virgin, without wrinkle, entire in modesty, plebeian in love, a chaste harlot, a barren widow, a fruitful virgin: a chaste harlot, because she is frequented by many lovers with the allurement of love and without the defilement of sin — he, indeed, who cleaves to a harlot is one body — a barren widow, who knows not how to bear when her husband is absent — the husband came and begot this people and this multitude — a fruitful virgin, who bore this multitude with the fruit of love, without the use of lust.

24. But to return to the history, what do the words of the midwife mean, when she says: This one will come out first, unless it is that he bore the type of him who, later in the birth of the body, being first in virtue and truth, claimed for himself the primacy over all? Hence John too: After me comes a man who was made before me. What does it mean that she tied scarlet on his hand, unless that he was a type of him who by the sign of his cross and the shedding of his blood made human action illustrious? And so, after he drew back his hand, his brother came out as if the hedge had been cut through, he whom the Apostle named as it were the middle wall of the hedge or of the enclosure, and who himself received his name from the cutting; for Perez is division. Hence the Pharisees too were so called, because they separated themselves from association with the many. But it would have been more blessed and much better for the hedge not to be cut through, but to remain one and the same and undivided, which could have happened if the warfare of the life that followed had been consistent with the life which put forth its hand first, that is, showed its action. For it would have been much better if the circumcised people had been willing to imitate the life of the forefathers; for thus there would have been one hedge, one enclosure, one building of those before and those after. But since the weakness of the later life could not fulfil that first action of life, the hedge or enclosure, which had been built according to God, was beyond doubt cut through, and as it were a middle wall was set in between, so that that hedge, that is, the perpetual and continuous building of good morals, was broken. For a hedge is that by which a fruitful field is surrounded and the incursion of the thief kept off, which shuts in what is cultivated and separates off what is waste. An enclosure too shuts in a house. If this remains, the house is safe. Finally I will take away he says its enclosure, and it will be for plunder.

25. Let us pray that this enclosure of our house may be whole, of the spiritual house which is within us; for it cannot be built up by man, but by the living God, who says: And I put an enclosure around it. Those therefore lost salvation who lost the enclosure. Let the enclosure remain, then; let this hedge remain. Do you wish to know how useful a hedge is? Hedge your ears with thorns and thistles and do not listen to a wicked tongue; for this hedge defends you from guilt.

26. So the Lord Jesus, who afterwards came into the light according to the flesh, restoring the defence of that old hedge, re-formed us into the action of the forefathers and the ancient simplicity of faith. Hence the prophet too said of him: You will be called the builder of the hedge. For he took away that barrier which divided the unity of mind and body and the course of a simple life, and he himself became our peace, who made both one and loosed the middle wall of the hedge. Which wall the Apostle explains as the enmities in the flesh. These enmities, then, the Lord took away, and he restored peace and made void the law of commandments in decrees, so as to create the two in one new man; in which he signifies not only the outer and the inner man, but also the Jew and the Greek, that Christ might be all and in all. For the Lord took away the superstition of the sabbath, of the bodily sabbath, and as it were loosed the middle wall of the Law, which by the difficulty of its decrees kept us from that devotion which is according to God, because under the law of Moses it was not easy or possible for the Gentiles to serve God, since the empty superstition of the Jews held back the pure disposition of the Gentiles from undertaking the observance. What then? Is the Law useless? Far from it; but useful to unbelievers and necessary to the weak, to restrain the slippery and the straying by the severity of a saving precept and to hem them in by attention to observances. But the Law is good, because it is spiritual. It is not good, then, to him who does not think it spiritual, who, made small and low in mind, could not see that which is above the Law, namely the majesty of Christ. Which surely that Isaiah, lofty in mind, looking as it were over that wall, saw: the glory of God, which is seen on the mountains, not on the hills.

27. So the Church taught us in the Song of Songs that this wall could not stand in the way of our Lord Jesus Christ, nor of him who has followed Christ, when she said: The voice of my brother; behold, he comes leaping over the mountains, bounding over the hills. My brother is like a roe or a young hart on the mountains of Bethel. Behold, he is here behind our wall, looking out through the windows, looking out through the nets. My brother answered and said to me: Arise, come, my neighbour, my beautiful one, my dove, for behold, the winter has passed, the rain has gone. It has gone its way. Flowers have appeared on the earth, the time of cutting has come, the voice of the turtledove has been heard. The flowers are the apostles, the time of harvest is the fruit of Christ, the voice of the turtledove is the voice of the Church. Rightly, then, the Son of God, after he saw men earthly and not stepping toward the things above, and confined within bodily narrownesses — there was none, indeed, who did good, there was none, not even one — himself deigned to descend to earth, to take away that wall of the Law, that is, a kind of mass and superstition of bodily understanding, which as it were pressed down and overshadowed the hearts of the peoples. Better, then, is the enclosure than the wall. Finally, not good is the whitewashed wall, which was not said idly to the chief of the priests, because he was keeping the hindrances of the middle wall, which the Lord Jesus took away as a hard warfare, so as to pour in a clearer observance of religion, so that now not the one race of the Jews, shut in as it were by a bodily prescription of the Law, but all nations should be called to the worship of God through the Gospel.

28. The two twins, then, are two twin lives, two twin warfares, such that the former is better than the one that follows. And therefore what is better has been restored. And who would deny that the Gospel excels the Law? Yet the Law is good, if you raise your mind above the letter; the letter, indeed, kills. But what grace would this history have, if we did not see the light of so great a mystery? For the holy Apostle taught us to seek the secret of truth in the simplicity of history and to carry the sense over into certain discussions not intelligible according to the letter, when he wrote: Tell me, you who have read the Law, have you not heard the Law? For it is written that Abraham had two sons, one from the slave woman and one from the free woman; but he who was from the slave woman was born according to the flesh, and he who was from the free woman according to the promise. Which things he says are spoken by allegory. For these are the two testaments; and below: But the Jerusalem which is above is free.

29. Let us then strive, with loftiness of mind, toward her whom the middle wall could not cut through and divide. For that wall of the old testament has been loosed according to bodily understanding; the slave woman has been cast out, the free woman is kept. Of the free woman we are free; for the Church is free, the synagogue has been cast out — for the people of the Jews was in slavery — removed is the yoke of slavery, which pressed down, as it were, the necks of our soul, so that we could not look beyond the wall of the former life. We have a good and light yoke, which with the reins of peace and the bonds of grace raises rather than bows down those joined to it. This is the Lord whose type went before in Zerah, because from the tribe and from the seed of that Zerah the Lord Jesus was born according to the flesh, not only of a woman but also under the Law, to redeem those who were under the Law at the price of his blood. His figure went before in the hand of that Zerah for this reason: to promise us that he would come who would recall the practice of the old life and restore in the last Adam the freedom he had granted to that first Adam, so that the human race should now be without the law of slavery.

30. If, then, we have learned that Tamar was recorded among the Lord's generations because of a mystery, we ought to judge that Ruth too was without doubt not passed over, for a like reason; of whom the holy Apostle seems to have been thinking when, foreseeing in the spirit that the calling of the foreign nations was to be celebrated through the Gospel, he said that the Law is not laid down for the righteous, but for the unrighteous. For how did Ruth, though she was a foreigner, marry a Jew? And for what reason did the evangelist think that mention of her marriage should be made in the generation of Christ, when it was forbidden by the course of the Law? Did the Savior not, then, descend from a lawful generation? It seems unseemly, unless you return to the apostolic saying that the Law is not laid down for the righteous but for the unrighteous. For since she was a foreigner and a Moabitess, especially since the law of Moses forbade these marriages and excluded the Moabites from the Church — for so it is written: The Moabites shall not enter into the Church of the Lord to the third and fourth generation and for ever — how did she enter into the Church, unless because, holy and spotless in her morals, she was raised above the Law? For if the Law is laid down for the impious and for sinners, surely Ruth, who went beyond the bounds of the Law and entered into the Church and became an Israelite and merited to be counted among the forebears of the Lord's line, chosen for kinship of mind, not of body, is a great example to us, because in her went before the figure of all of us who have been gathered from the Gentiles to enter the Church of the Lord. Let us then emulate her, so that, as she by her morals merited this prerogative of being admitted into fellowship, as the history teaches, we too, for the choice of our morals, may be enrolled in the Church of the Lord with our merits to support us.

31. For when, in the earlier times in the days of the judges, famine pressed upon the Israelites, a man went from Bethlehem, a city of Judah, in which Christ was born, to dwell in the land of Moab with his wife and two sons; the man's name was Elimelech, the woman's Naomi. His sons took Moabite wives for themselves — the name of the one Orpah, the name of the second Ruth — and they lived there about ten years and died. But the woman, left by her two sons and bereft of her own husband, learning that God was visiting Israel and preparing to return home, began to urge her sons' wives to go back to their own homes. One gave way, but Ruth stayed with her mother-in-law. When her mother-in-law said to her: Behold, your sister-in-law has now gone back to her people and to her gods; you too return together with your sister-in-law, Ruth answered: May it not befall me to leave you and return to my god, for wherever you go I will go with you, and where you dwell I will dwell. Your people is my people, and your God my God. And where you die I will die, and where you are buried I will be buried. And so the two came to Bethlehem. When Boaz, David's great-grandfather, learned of these morals and this holiness toward her mother-in-law, this devotion toward the dead, this religion toward God, he chose her as his wife according to the law of Moses, to raise up the seed of his dead kinsman.

32. And it is worth noticing that, found in a field full of harvest, she was gathering sheaves, as it is written, and keeping the produce for her mother-in-law, and that she did not go after a young man but followed a mature man; whence she also merited to hear: For you are a woman of virtue, or: For you have done your last mercy better than the first; for the last mercy, that of the Church gathered together, excels the earlier. This we say more briefly here, because we have set it out more fully in the books which I wrote on the faith. He drew near who was far off, because he who was nearest withdrew far off, and, having received the woman, he acquired the shoe of the nearest kinsman. For it was the custom that he who was nearest, if he did not wish to take his kinswoman in marriage, should loose his shoe and give way to another. In this there is no small mystery, in that he who took the foreign woman in type received the power of preaching the Gospel.

33. Finally, that this marriage was in type is attested by the blessing of the elders, who said: May the Lord grant the woman who enters your house to be like Rachel and like Leah, who built the house of Israel, and to work power in Ephrathah, and may there be a name in Bethlehem. And may your house be like the house of Perez, whom Tamar bore to Judah; of your seed may the Lord give you from this girl. And Boaz took Ruth and she became his wife, and she bore Obed, the father of Jesse, the grandfather of David. Rightly, then, Saint Matthew, being about to call the Gentiles to the Church through the Gospel, recorded that the Lord himself, the author of the gathering of the Gentiles, took on according to the flesh a generation from foreigners, so that there should be a sign even then that that generation would bring forth the caller of the Gentiles, whom we all, gathered from foreigners, should follow, leaving our ancestral ways and saying to him who called us to the worship of the Lord, for example to Paul or to any bishop: Your people is my people, your God my God. So Ruth, like Leah and Rachel, forgetting her people and her father's house, loosing the bond of the Law, entered into the Church.

34. He looses his shoe who does not take the Church. And to Moses it is said: Loose the shoe of your feet, lest he himself should be believed to be the bridegroom of the Church. He alone did not loose it who is the true bridegroom. And therefore John says: The strap of whose shoe I am not worthy to loose. So here too there is a type, and he built the house of Israel.

35. And how fitting it was that mention of her should be inserted into the Lord's lineage is declared by the expression of a higher mystery, by which it was prophesied that Christ would be born of her line in Ephrathah, when it is said: May the Lord grant you to work power in Ephrathah, and may there be a name in Bethlehem. For what is the power, unless that which gathered the peoples of the Gentiles through Christ? And what is the name, unless that by which Bethlehem became the homeland of the Lord, born according to the flesh? Hence it was said by prophecy: And you, Bethlehem of Judah, are not least among the princes of Judah; for from you will come out a prince who will rule my people Israel. We see, then, that history, morals and mystery agree in the mention of the women.

36. Yet I do not deny, while I defend Tamar and Ruth, that sinners too were counted among the forebears of the Lord's line; and Saint Luke, wishing to avoid mention of them, kept another order of successions and did not think Ahab or Jeconiah or, lastly, the wife of Uriah should be named, so as to declare the series of the priestly line immaculate. But just as his plan has a reason beneath it, so too the plan of Saint Matthew does not shrink from the justice of reason. For since he was proclaiming in the Gospel that the Lord, who was to take up the sins of all, subject to injuries, submitted to the Passion, was begotten according to the flesh, he did not think he should be declared without share even in this devotion, that he should not refuse the injury of a stained origin too; and at the same time, that the Church should not be ashamed to be gathered from sinners, since the Lord was born of sinners. Lastly, that he might begin the benefit of redemption even from his own forebears, lest anyone should think that a stain of origin could be a hindrance to virtue, or insolently boast of the nobility of his line, or, more bashful, blush at the crimes of his parents, since he is given the means of overshadowing his origin by the flower of virtue.

37. Or is not holy David, though many things of his are figured into a mystery, all the more exalted in this, that he knew himself to be a man and thought that the sin committed above, in carrying off Uriah's wife, must be washed away with the tears of repentance, showing us that no one ought to trust in his own virtue? For we have a great adversary, who cannot be overcome by us without God's favour. And often you will find that there were grave sins in illustrious and blessed men, so that you may know that as men they lay open to temptation, lest for their eminent virtues they be believed to be more than men. For if David, because, lifted up by presumption of virtue, he had said: If I have repaid evil to those who repaid me; and elsewhere: But I said in my abundance: I shall not be moved for ever, recorded that he at once suffered the penalty of this insolence, saying: You turned away your face from me, and I became troubled; if the very founder of the Lord's line incurred offence for insolence, how much more must we other sinners, whom no prerogative of merits supports, fear the rock of insolence, on which the good suffer shipwreck, especially since so great a man is for us the author both of teaching and of example, who thought that a kind of palinode must be sung afterwards to win back the Lord's favour, saying: Lord, my heart is not exalted, nor are my eyes lifted up on high; and: The Lord is at my right hand, that I may not be moved; for he knew that, when he trusted in himself, he fell. Finally he showed that there is nothing else in man except that he knows God; for so you have it: What is man, that you have made yourself known to him, or the son of man, that you take account of him? If, then, David condemns insolence and puts on humility, rightly in the history of Uriah's wife this teaching of striving after humility is drawn in.

38. And yet, if from her was born that peaceable Solomon, let us see whether perhaps there is a mystery in this, that, when he was removed from the midst who before by his marriage laid claim to the people of the Gentiles, the Church was married to another husband, the true David. For Christ was called David, taken into the name of his forebear, according to what is written: I have found David my servant. To him the Church was married, which, filled with the seed of the Word and with the Spirit of God, brought forth the body of Christ, that is, the Christian people. She, then, is the woman who while her husband lives is bound to the law, and therefore her husband died, so that she would not be an adulteress if she were with another man. The mystery, then, is in the figure, the sin in the history; the fault through man, the sacraments through the Word. Since we have spoken of this history more fully elsewhere, it seems we must pass over it quickly here. And rightly holy David wrote about this history the mystical Psalm 50 (51), because of his marriage with Bathsheba, saying: Wash me abundantly from my unrighteousness and cleanse me from my sin. If the friend of God acknowledges his iniquity and that sin stands in the way of his merits, if, finally, he confesses that he has sinned against God, why should you blush at confessing a crime, since it is the contriving of a crime, not the confession of it, that brings shame?

39. So, since David did not pass over the history of Bathsheba in his psalms, that he might teach us in it either a mystery or the act of perfect repentance, rightly we see that she was not passed over in the Lord's generations either, since David himself, who took her as his wife, is declared the forerunner of the Lord's generation according to the flesh. His special merit it was, as we have said, to see the rise of the Church in this mystery and to receive the oracle that Christ would be born of his line. For the one thing belongs to the Church, what he said: Behold, we heard of her in Ephrathah, we found her in the fields of the forest; the other belongs specially to the pledge of the Lord's body, which is revealed by a most manifest oracle when it is said: Of the fruit of your womb I will set upon my throne. Yet do not be careless of so great a promise; for it was not given without a condition, but, if you keep the covenant and observe the testimonies of the Lord, which in the Gospel he promises he will speak, to you too access to the eternal throne will lie open. So much for Uriah's wife.

40. As for Ahab, it is clear enough, whose wife was Jezebel, and as for Jeconiah, Jeremiah is an amply sufficient witness that he was guilty of the greatest offence, and he even took from him the name that he had. And therefore he who in the books of Kings is called Joachim was named Jeconiah by Jeremiah, when he said: Jeconiah has been cast away like a vessel, there is no use in it, wherefore he has been cast out, he and his seed. Earth, earth, hear the word of the Lord: write this man down as disowned, for there will not arise of his seed one sitting on the throne of David, a prince still in Judah. For while he was reigning the Babylonians laid Judaea waste, and never afterwards could anyone of his seed obtain the kingdom in Judaea; for afterwards the people, released from captivity, was under priests and tetrarchs. Hence tetrarchs remained right up to the generation of Christ, and not even they, as far as history teaches, kept the dignity of a royal line.

41. For it is reported by those who have handed it down to us, whether arguing it, or teaching it simply, or truly establishing it, that Idumaean robbers, having entered Ashkelon, a city of Palestine, carried off among others as a captive from the temple of Apollo, which was near the walls, Antipater, the son of a certain Herod, a temple servant, whom his father could not ransom because of poverty. He, then, steeped in the disciplines and mysteries of the Jews, was joined in friendship to Hyrcanus, king of Judaea, and Hyrcanus sent him on his own behalf as envoy to Pompey; and because he gained the fruit of the embassy, through that favour he aspired to a part of the kingdom. When Antipater was killed out of envy at his good fortune, his son Herod was afterwards, under Antony, by decree of the senate, appointed to reign over the Jews; his son was Herod, and the other tetrarchs. This we thought should be carried over from the histories of the Greeks, so that it may be clear that Herod, akin by no tie to the line of the Jews, sought the kingdom by adulterous fraud. Finally, conscious of his low birth, lest any question should be raised against his descendants from the old record, he burned their writings, thinking that, if he removed the evidence from public view, by no other testimonies could it be made clear that he did not descend from the stock of the patriarchs or of the ancient proselytes. But, as is the way of most human contrivances, he could not prejudice the inquiry and the tracking down of the truth.

42. Yet lest it may prejudice us, let us see, we who say that Christ is of a true and royal line, and that the Lord's line was carried on through true and noble kings. But when an adulterous offshoot aspired to the kingdom, the order not of power but of generation preserved the succession of his nobility; yet we have not received Christ himself as king according to the honour of this world. How then is it: Of the fruit of your womb I will set upon my throne? How does the angel too say of him that the Lord God will give him the throne of David his father, and he will reign in the house of Jacob? How is he promised to reign and not shown to reign? Or how is it said through the prophet that none of the seed of Jeconiah will reign? For if Christ reigned, and Christ is of the seed of Jeconiah, the prophet has lied, and the oracles too have lied. But there it is not denied that there would be descendants of the seed of Jeconiah, and therefore Christ is of his seed, and that Christ reigned is not against the prophecy; for he did not reign with worldly honour, nor did he sit on the thrones of Jeconiah, but he reigned on the throne of David.

43. But since Jeconiah himself sat on the throne of David, how is it resolved what was said, that the descendants of Jeconiah will not sit on the throne of David, when the throne of both seems to have been the same? So we too cannot deny that it was the throne of David, and yet Christ did not sit on the same throne of King David that Jeconiah sat on; indeed, no one else of the line of David could sit on his throne but Christ, because in no one else is his seed eternal, but in Christ, as God himself disclosed, saying: Once have I sworn by my holiness, if I lie to David: his seed will remain for ever, and his throne as the sun in my sight. Whom, then, does he speak of here? Surely not Solomon, not Rehoboam, not Nathan, but him of whom alone he can say: I will set his hand in the sea and his right hand in the rivers. He will call me: 'You are my father', and: I will set his seed for ever and ever, and his throne as the days of heaven. Surely Solomon did not sit on this throne, nor Rehoboam, nor Jeconiah. Do you wish to know who sat on it? It is he of whom the angel says to Mary: Behold, you will conceive in the womb and bear a son, and you will call his name Jesus. He will be great and will be called the Son of the Most High, and the Lord God will give him the throne of David his father, and he will reign in the house of Jacob for ever, and of his kingdom there will be no end. If you do not believe the angel, you will at least believe the Lord himself, saying: You say that I am a king. Did he too, then, lie, because he said that he reigned, he who did not reign on earth? How is the Scripture resolved, which says that he reigns and does not establish that he reigns?

44. We have come to a precipice in the argument; we stick fast in the shallows and toss in a kind of shipwreck of the truth. Let us therefore rouse Christ, let us question him himself, let him himself answer. Let us question the Scriptures. We find that the kingdom of the Lord is not of this world; for he himself said: My kingdom is not of this world. He who says that his kingdom is not of this world shows that it is above the world. So his kingdom both was and was not: it was not in the world, it was above the world. There was, then, another kingdom of the true David, which Christ alone received, and there was another seed of David, which remains for ever, of which Christ alone was begotten, who alone is the true son of David, whose name too he alone received, according to what is written: I have found David my servant, with my holy oil I have anointed him. That this is said not of the prophet David but of the Lord, the words that precede declare; for it is written: I have laid help upon one who is mighty and have exalted one chosen from my people; for Christ alone is mighty, he alone is chosen. For the seed of the saints is greater in faith than in generation; and therefore the Apostle says: For if those who are of faith, these are the sons of Abraham.

45. This too we think must not be passed over, that from the times of David down to Jeconiah, that is, down to the captivity, though there were seventeen kings of Judaea, Saint Matthew set down fourteen generations, and again from Jeconiah down to Joseph, though twelve generations are counted man by man, he afterwards recorded that fourteen generations had been described. For so you have it: All the generations from Abraham to David are fourteen generations, and from David to the deportation to Babylon fourteen generations, and from the deportation to Babylon to Christ fourteen generations. And first we must recognize what we have already said before, that there can be more successions and fewer generations; for some can live longer and beget later, or indeed be wholly without share in generation. So the times of kings are not the same as the times of generations. Hence Matthew too passed over those whom he did not think belonged to the generation. For if his purpose had been to describe the successions, we would reasonably be troubled why, though the books of Kings and Chronicles agree that after Joram there reigned Ahaziah and Iodam and Amaziah, and that Uzziah succeeded Amaziah, Saint Matthew passed over those three kings, Ahaziah, Iodam and Amaziah, and set Jehoshaphat next after Joram. But he set him next not in the succession of kings but in the generation; indeed he declared that he was a recorder of generations. And it could have happened that Joram both begot late and Jehoshaphat received the kingdom later, and so he succeeded Joram his father in generation, though he did not succeed him in power.

46. As for the evangelist seeming to have enumerated twelve generations after Jeconiah, if you attend carefully, here too you will be able to find the reckoning of fourteen generations; for twelve are counted up to Joseph, not up to Christ; Christ, born of Joseph, is the thirteenth. But it makes no difference whether there is a falsehood in two generations or in one; yet not even here will you find some rock of Caphareus and a shipwreck of the truth. For history indicates that there were two Joachims, that is, two Jeconiahs, one born before the deportation, the other in the deportation itself, that is, father and son. So the father, who succeeded Josiah, was counted among the earlier generations; the son, who succeeded his father, that is, Josiah's grandson, among the later. And that there were two, the books of Kings indicate. For so it is written: And Pharaoh set Joachim, son of Josiah, over Israel, reigning in Judaea in place of Josiah his father, and changed the name of Joachim in his reigning, and he reigned eleven years in Jerusalem. To which he added: And the rest of the words of Joachim and all that he did, are they not written in the book of words and in the days of those who reigned in Judaea? And Joachim slept with his fathers, and Joachim his son reigned in his place. Joachim was eighteen years old, and when he reigned, he reigned three months in Jerusalem; his mother's name was Mesola. And all that his father did under the eyes of the Lord, he too did. And in his days Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, came up to Jerusalem. You see, then, that the son of Josiah was one, his grandson another: the son was the one to whom Jeremiah gave a name, the grandson the one who was called by his father's name. And well did Saint Matthew not wish to differ from the prophet, so that he named not Joachim but Jeconiah. At the same time, as we said above, he established a greater fruit of the Lord's devotion, if the Lord did not require nobility of line in all, but willed fittingly to be born of captives and sinners, he who was coming to preach release to captives. So the evangelist did not suppress either one, but marked both, because each was called Jeconiah. So, with the younger Jeconiah added, fourteen generations are counted. So much, then, for Matthew.

47. Beautifully, too, did Luke, since he could not include the many sons of Jacob, lest he should seem to wander outside the generations in a superfluous series, nevertheless judge that in others, that is, in much later ones, the names of the patriarchs were by no means to be passed over, but chosen before the rest: Joseph, Judah, Simeon and Levi. For in those whose descendants these are we know there were four kinds of virtues: in Judah, that is, the earlier one, the mystery of the Lord's Passion was prophesied through a figure; in Joseph an example of chastity went before; in Simeon the avenging of wounded modesty; in Levi the office of the priest. Through Nathan too we notice the dignity of prophecy expressed, so that, since Christ Jesus, being one, is all things, in each of his forebears too different kinds of virtue should go before.

48. Nor ought mention of righteous Noah to be passed over among the Lord's generations, so that, since the builder of the Church was being born, he might be seen to have sent before him, as founder of his line, him who had founded the Church beforehand in type. For what shall I say of Methuselah, whose years are counted beyond the flood, so that, since Christ alone is the one whose life felt no age, he might seem in his forebears too not to have felt floods? And is not Enoch a manifest sign both of the Lord's devotion and of his divinity, in that the Lord too did not feel death and returned to heaven, since the founder of his line was carried up to heaven? Hence it is manifest that Christ too could have not died, but did not will it, so that that death might profit us. And Enoch indeed was carried off, lest malice should change his heart, but the Lord, whom the malice of the world could not change, returned by the majesty of his nature to the place from which he had come. The murderer of his brother is indeed passed over in silence; for it was not reasonable that he should number among the Lord's forebears him who had struck down his brother, since the Lord saved even lowly servants, that he might endow them with the honour of the name of brothers. Nor is it idle that Seth was not passed over, whom Adam received in a later generation, so that, since the peoples are two generations, it might be signified in type that the Lord Jesus Christ was to be numbered in the later rather than in the earlier generation.

49. Now as for Adam himself, who according to the Apostle received the figure of Christ, what more beautiful could fit than that the most holy generation should begin from the Son of God and be led up to a son of God, and that the one created should go before in figure, so that the one born should follow in truth; that the one made after the image should precede, for whose sake the Image of God would descend? And if we examine the mystery of the first error, he tasted of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, the devil deceiving, Eve persuading, so that this one, before he knew evil, might choose good for us, and, the Church persuading, might make void the snares of the dread serpent, according to what is written: For before the child knows good or evil, he does not trust in malice, that he may choose what is good; and: Before he knows how to call father or mother, he will receive the power of Damascus and the spoils of Samaria. For this is the child whose cradle the Magi filled with the spoils of the East, because the nation that was unbelieving before Christ, its faith now changed, offered to the Lord triumphal booty from the trophies stripped from the idols.

50. These things about the generation of Christ, brother, not unknown to you, I have thought fit to pursue at some length, lest anyone, going over them in the Gospel with too little attention, should waver somewhat. These things the holy evangelists, hastening to greater miracles and more divine deeds of the Lord, thought should be touched on briefly rather than spread out at length. So, in the manner of those who think it enough to point out to people ignorant of the roads certain marks, as it were, and paths of the journey, gathering the paths of the spiritual way — I shall see whether with a favourable outcome as to the truth, at least under the devout guidance of faith — we press on into the secrets of the mysteries, fearing lest anyone, when he has read these things, should, as the proverb says, be 'like a boy with a sword', not knowing through infancy how to handle strong weapons, and should feel a wound from want of foresight rather than salvation from the reading. For the weak are wounded by their own weapons, nor can he use arms well who does not know how to bear them. And therefore in matters of faith a mature man is required, in whom infancy does not crawl along the ground of knowledge, in whom no feeble age, lacking the senses of mystical knowledge, totters, an age which, having lost the strength of youth, does not seek the crown of the glorious contest, and, like an ageing eagle, which before was wont to seize a hare or a goose with its feet, worn out with age seeks the featherless young of smaller birds, which cannot provide more solid food.

Book Four

1. It was not out of place, I think, that we completed a work on the generation of the Lord; certainly it has not been fruitless to linger rather long over the Lord's forebears. For if those who prepare to cross a great sea by coasting along the shore, avoiding, as we do, out of weakness of confidence, the shortcut of a course on the deeper sea, often visit, captivated by the beauty of the places, the countryside and the cities set down along the shore, how much more ought we, placed in so great a deep, not of the elements but of heavenly deeds, to skirt the nearer harbors and to love frequent excursions, lest anyone, wearied by the tedium of a long voyage, be unable to hold back the vomit of disgust. Certainly if anyone has perceived the treacherous faults of an unsafe vessel, then, as though the frequent harbor of the books were set before him, he may lower the sails of his ears and fix the anchor of his reading. He who goes down into harbor does not seem to have deserted the ship, but to have completed his course.

2. And in many places perhaps the very pleasantness of the places may invite the one passing by. For if that Ulysses, as the fables relate — although the prophet too has said: daughters of the Sirens shall dwell in it, and even if the prophet had not said it, no one would rightly find fault, since Scripture has included both the giants and the valley of the Titans — if then that Ulysses, hastening to his fatherland after ten years of exile, in which war was waged at Ilium, and ten years of wandering, could be detained by the Lotus-eaters with the sweetness of their berries, if the gardens of Alcinous delayed him, if finally the Sirens, enticing him with the song of their voice, had almost led him to that famous shipwreck of pleasure, had he not closed the ears of his companions with wax put into them against the sounds of the alluring song, how much more is it fitting that religious men be charmed by wonder at heavenly deeds! And here it is no longer the sweetness of berries that is to be drunk in, but that bread which came down from heaven; it is not the vegetables of Alcinous that are to be gazed at, but the sacraments of Christ; for let him who is weak eat vegetables. So the ears are not to be closed but to be opened, that the voice of Christ may be heard; whoever has received it will not fear shipwreck; he is not to be tied to the mast with bodily bonds like Ulysses, but his mind is to be bound to the wood of the cross with spiritual ties, so that he may not be moved by the enticements of wantonness and twist the course of nature into the peril of pleasure.

3. For the fable is colored with poetic fictions, so that certain maidens are said to have dwelt on a rocky shore of the sea, who, if with their sweet voice they had driven any to turn their ship aside for the sweetness to their ears, would lead them onto hidden shoals and, deceived by a treacherous anchorage, destroy them by the lot of a pitiable shipwreck. This was composed for show and painted with an ambitious comparison, so that a sea, a woman's voice, and shoaling shores are invented. But what sea is more precipitous than the world, so treacherous, so changeable, so deep, so stormy with the blasts of unclean spirits? What does the figure of the maidens mean but the enticement of unmanning pleasure, which makes effeminate the constancy of the captured mind? And what are those shoals but reefs to our salvation? For nothing is so blind as the danger of worldly sweetness, which, while it soothes the mind, overwhelms the life and dashes the sense of the mind against certain bodily rocks.

4. Rightly, then, does our Lord Jesus by his fasting and by the desert train us against the enticements of pleasures, and the Lord of all suffers himself to be tempted by the devil, that in him we all might learn to conquer. Let us notice, then, that the Evangelist has described three things, not idly ordained by the Lord as chief. For there are three things that serve the use of human salvation: the sacrament, the desert, the fast; for no one is crowned unless he has striven lawfully, and no one is admitted to the contest of virtue unless he has first been washed from all the stains of sins and consecrated by the gift of heavenly grace.

5. So the Lord came to the washing, that the grace of the mystery might be proved to us both by sight and by sense. And since the law is promulgated with heaven and earth as witnesses, so that you might believe the mystery of the divinity, hidden from the ages in God, to be more than the law, heaven is no longer called to bear witness, but performs the office of a witness, when the voice of God is borne down from heaven. At the same time, lest you stumble at the mystery of faith through doubt of mind, invisible things are visibly declared to you to be at work.

6. The Lord came to the washing; for he became all things for you. To those who are under the law, as though he were under the law. Though he himself was not under the law, he was circumcised, that he might win those who are under the law; to those who were without the law he joined himself in the fellowship of a banquet. That he might win those who lived without the law. He became weak to the weak through the suffering of the body, that he might win them; finally he became all things to all, a poor man to the poor, a rich man to the rich, weeping with the weeping, hungry with the hungry, thirsty with the thirsty, overflowing to those who abound. He is in prison with the poor man, he weeps with Mary, he feasts with the apostles, he thirsts with the Samaritan woman, he hungers in the desert, that the food of the first man, which he had tasted by transgression, might be undone by the fast of the Lord. To our peril Adam broke his hunger for the knowledge of good and evil; to our gain this one took hunger upon himself.

7. Then Jesus was led into the desert by the Spirit, to be tempted by the devil. It is fitting to remember how the first Adam was cast out of paradise into the desert, that you may notice how the second Adam returned from the desert to paradise. For see how the old sentences are untied by their own knots, and the divine benefits restored in their own footsteps. Adam from virgin earth, Christ from a virgin; the one made to the image of God, this one the image of God; the one set above all irrational animals, this one above all living things — through a woman folly, through a virgin wisdom, death through a tree, life through the cross — the one, naked of spiritual things, covered himself with the coverings of a tree; this one, naked of worldly things, did not desire the coverings of a body. Adam in the desert, Christ in the desert; for he knew where he could find the condemned man, whom he would call back to paradise once his error was undone. But since, clothed in worldly coverings, he could not return, and no one can be a dweller in paradise unless he is naked of guilt, he put off the old man and put on the new, so that, since the divine decrees cannot be undone, the person rather than the sentence might be changed.

8. But since in paradise, without a guide, he lost the road he had received, how could he, without a guide, find again from the desert the road he had lost, where temptations are very many, the striving toward virtue difficult, the slip into error easy? For the nature of virtue is the same as that of the trees of the woods: if they are still small as they lift themselves from earthly things toward things above, while their youth is growing with tender leaves, they are exposed to the venom of a cruel tooth and can easily be either cut down or burned; but if the tree has grounded itself with a deep root and raised itself with the height of its branches, then in vain is the sturdy tree assailed, whether by the bites of wild beasts, or the arms of countrymen, or the storms of the various winds.

9. Whom, then, was he to appoint as guide against so many enticements of the world, against so many wiles of the devil, when he knew that the wrestling of us is first against flesh and blood, then against powers, against the rulers of the world of this darkness, against the spiritual forces of wickedness which are in the heavenly places? Was he to appoint an angel? He too fell; legions of angels have scarcely profited individuals. Was he to send a seraph? It too came down on earth into the midst of a people with unclean lips, and cleansed the lips of one prophet only, by applying a coal. Another guide had to be sought, whom we might all follow. Who could be so great a guide as to profit all, but he who is above all? Who could set me above the world but he who is greater than the world? Who could be so great a guide as to be able to lead male and female, Jew and Greek, barbarian and Scythian, slave and free with a single leading, but Christ alone, who is all things and in all?

10. For there are many snares wherever we go: snares in the body, snares in the law; snares are laid by the devil on the pinnacles of temples, on the ledges of walls; snares in philosophers, snares in desires — for the eye of a harlot is the sinner's snare —, a snare in money, a snare in religion, a snare in the pursuit of chastity. For the human mind is tipped by the slightest weights, and is often driven hither and thither by the cunning of the one who persuades. The devil sees some religious man, reverently deferring to God and esteeming what is sacrosanct as capable of no injury: in that very religion he trips him up, making him not believe that the Son of God truly took this flesh of ours, this body of ours, this frailty of our members, though in fact the suffering was the body's, while the divinity remained exempt from injury. Thus from religion comes guilt; every one indeed who denies that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is not of God. He sees a man whole and untouched in chastity: he persuades him to condemn marriage, so that he may be cast out of the Church and, by the pursuit of chastity, be separated from the chaste body. Another has heard that one God, from whom are all things, and he adores and venerates: the devil lies in wait for him, closes his ears so that he does not hear that one Lord, through whom are all things; so by excessive devotion he drives him to be impious, so that, while he separates the Father from the Son, he confounds the Father and the Son, and thinks that the person is one, not the power. And so, while he does not know the measure of faith, he falls into the misery of unbelief.

11. How then shall we avoid these snares, so that we too may be able to say: our soul has been rescued like a sparrow from the snare of the hunters; the snare is broken, and we are set free? He does not say: 'I broke the snare'; David did not dare to say this; but rather: our help is in the name of the Lord, to show from where the snare would be loosed, to prophesy that there would come into this life one who would break the snare prepared by the devil's fraud.

12. But the snare could not better be broken than if he showed the devil some prey, so that, while he hastened to the prey, he might be bound in his own snares, so that I might be able to say: they prepared snares for my feet, and they themselves fell into them. What could the prey be but a body? This fraud, then, had to be practiced on the devil, that the Lord Jesus should take a body, and this body corruptible, a weak body, that he might be crucified out of weakness. For if it had been a spiritual body, he would not have said: the spirit is ready, but the flesh is weak. Hear, then, both voices, of the weak flesh and of the ready spirit: Father, if it is possible, let this cup pass from me: this is the voice of the flesh. But not what I will, but what you will: you have the devotion and the vigor of the spirit. Why do you reject the condescension of the Lord? It is of condescension that he took my body, of condescension that he took my injuries, took my infirmities, which the nature of God certainly could not feel, since even the nature of man has learned to despise them, or to sustain and endure them. And so let us follow Christ according to what is written: you shall walk after the Lord your God and cleave to him. To whom shall I cleave but to Christ, as Paul said: he who cleaves to the Lord is one spirit? So let us follow his footsteps, that we may be able to return from the desert to paradise.

13. See by what roads we are led back. Now Christ is in the desert; he leads man, instructs, forms, exercises him, anoints him with spiritual oil; when he saw him stronger, he leads him across through sown and fruitful fields, when the Jews complained that his disciples were plucking ears of grain from the crop on the sabbath — for he had already placed his apostles in a cultivated field and in fruitful work — afterwards he set him in paradise at the time of the Passion; for so you have it: when Jesus had said these things, he went across with his disciples over the brook of the cedar, where there was a garden, into which he himself entered with his disciples. For that a garden is better than a fertile field the prophet teaches in the Song of Songs, saying: a garden enclosed is my sister, my bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed; your shoots are a paradise. For that is the pure and spotless virginity of the soul, which is drawn away from faith by no terror of punishments, by no enticements of worldly pleasure, by no love of life. Finally, that man has been called back by the power of the Lord, this Evangelist testifies above the rest, who alone brings in the Lord saying to the thief: amen I say to you, today you shall be with me in paradise.

14. Jesus, then, full of the Holy Spirit, is driven into the desert: by design, to challenge the devil — for had the one not fought, this one would not have conquered for me —; by mystery, to free that Adam from exile; by example, to show us that the devil envies those who strive toward better things, and that then one must be more on guard, lest weakness of mind desert the grace of the mystery.

15. Forty moreover days: you recognize a mystical number. For you remember that for so many days the waters of the abyss were poured out, and that, when the prophet had been sanctified by a fast of so many days, the clemency of a clearer sky was poured back; by a fast of so many days holy Moses merited the receiving of the Law; for so many years the fathers, settled in the wilderness, obtained the bread of angels and the grace of heavenly nourishment, and did not merit to enter the land of promise before they had fulfilled the time of the mystical number; by a fast of so many days the Lord's entrance into the Gospel lies open to us. Hence if anyone desires to obtain the glory of the Gospel and the fruit of the resurrection, he must not be a transgressor of the mystical fast, which both Moses in the Law and Christ in his Gospel prescribed, by the authority of both Testaments, to be the faithful contest of virtue.

16. But what does it mean that the Evangelist noted that the Lord was hungry, when we see nothing of the kind expressed of the fast of Moses and of Elijah? Is the endurance of men stronger than God? But he who could not be hungry in forty days showed that he hungered not for the food of the body but for salvation, and at the same time he did so to provoke the adversary, who was already afraid, having been wounded by the fast of forty days. And so the Lord's hunger is a holy deceit, so that the devil, who, fearing greater things, was already on his guard against him, might, lured by the appearance of hunger, tempt him as a man, lest the triumph be hindered. At the same time learn this mystery: that it was the work of the Holy Spirit, that is, the divine judgment, that Christ should offer himself to be tempted by the devil.

17. And the devil said to him: if you are the Son of God, tell this stone to become bread. We are taught chiefly three weapons of the devil, with which he is wont to arm himself to wound the mind of man: one of gluttony, another of boasting, the third of ambition. And he began from the point where he had already conquered. And so I begin to conquer in Christ from the point where I was conquered in Adam, if only Christ, the image of the Father, be to me the example of virtue. Let us learn, then, to beware of gluttony, to beware of luxury, because it is a weapon of the devil. A snare is laid when the table of a royal banquet is prepared, which often bends the constancy of the mind. For not only when we hear the words of the devil, but also when we see his plenty, ought we to avoid the snare. You have learned, then, the devil's weapon: take up the shield of faith, the breastplate of abstinence.

18. But what is the meaning of such an opening of speech: if you are the Son of God, except that he had known that the Son of God was to come? But he did not think that he had come through this weakness of the body. One thing belongs to one who probes, another to one who tempts: he both professes that he believes God and tries to mock the man.

19. But see the arms of Christ, with which he conquered for you, not for himself. For he who showed that by his majesty stones could be turned into bread through the transfusion of another nature teaches that you must do nothing at the devil's pleasure, nor for the sake of displaying power. At the same time, in the temptation itself learn the crafty cunning of the devil. He tempts so as to probe, he probes so as to tempt. The Lord, on the contrary, deceives so as to conquer, and still conquers so as to deceive. For had he changed the nature, he would have betrayed the Creator. So he answered in a middle way, saying: it is written that not by bread alone does man live, but by every word of God.

20. You see what kind of arms he uses, with which he defends man against the onset of spiritual wickedness, fenced and fortified against the incitements of gluttony. For he does not use his power as God — for what would that have profited me? — But as man he summons for himself the common help, so that, intent on the food of divine reading, he neglects the hunger of the body and acquires the nourishment of the heavenly word. Intent on this, Moses did not desire bread; intent on this, Elijah did not feel the hunger of a rather long fast. For he who follows the Word cannot desire earthly bread, since he receives the substance of heavenly bread — for there is no doubt that divine things surpass human ones, and spiritual things bodily ones — and so he who desires the true life waits for that bread which, through its imperceptible substance, strengthens human hearts. At the same time, when he says: man does not live by bread alone, he shows that it was man that was tempted, that is, what he took up from us, not his divinity.

21. There follows the weapon of boasting, by which one readily falls into sin, because while men desire to boast of the glory of their virtue, they depart from the place and station of their merits. And he led him he says to Jerusalem and set him on the pinnacle of the temple.

22. For such is boasting, that while each one thinks he is climbing to higher things, he is thrust down to lower things by laying claim to lofty deeds. And he said to him: if you are the Son of God, cast yourself down.

23. Truly a devilish voice, which strives to cast the mind of man down from a higher step of merits. For what is so proper to the devil as to persuade each one to cast himself down?

24. Learn, then, you too, to conquer the devil. The Spirit drives you: follow the Spirit. Let not the enticement of the flesh call you back; full of the Spirit, learn to despise pleasures. Fast, if you wish to conquer. It follows that the devil thinks you are to be tempted through a man; Christ, as the stronger, is tempted face to face, you through a man. And it is a word of the devil when a man says: 'you are strong: eat and drink, and stay as you are'. Do not trust yourself to yourself; do not blush to need safeguards which Christ did not need. And yet he did not neglect them, so as to teach you, saying: take heed lest your heart be weighed down with wine and drunkenness. Paul did not blush, who says: so I strive, not as one beating the air. The Apostle indeed was not beating the air, but he was scourging the powers of the air. But I chastise he says my body and bring it into slavery, lest perhaps, when I have preached to others, I myself be found reprobate.
