25. At the same time the devil marks out his own weakness and malice, because the devil can harm no one unless that one has cast himself down. For he who leaves heavenly things and chooses earthly ones falls into a kind of voluntary precipice of a slipping life. At the same time, since the devil saw his weapon blunted, he who had subjected all men to his own power began to judge him more than a man. But the Lord again did not think that even the things which had been prophesied of him were to be done at the devil's pleasure, but, keeping the authority of his own divinity, he met his cunning, so that, since he had held out the example of the Scriptures, he might be conquered by the examples of the Scriptures; for God has power to conquer; for me, Scripture conquers.

26. Learn here also that Satan transforms himself as if into an angel of light, and often lays a snare for the faithful out of the divine Scriptures themselves. So he makes heretics, so he disembowels faith, so he assails the rights of devotion. Therefore let not the heretic capture you because he can bring forth some examples from the Scriptures, nor let him claim anything for himself because he seems learned. The devil too uses the testimonies of the Scriptures, but not to teach; rather, to ensnare and deceive. He has come to know someone devoted to religion, famous for virtues, very powerful in signs and works: he lays the snare of boasting, to puff up such a man with swelling, so that he does not entrust himself to devotion but trusts to boasting, and does not ascribe it to God but claims it for himself. And therefore the apostles commanded demons not in their own name but in the name of Christ, lest they seem to claim anything for themselves. So Peter heals the paralytic, saying: in the name of Jesus the Nazarene, rise and walk. Learn also from Paul to flee boasting. I know he says a man, whether in the body or out of the body I do not know, God knows, that he was caught up into paradise and heard ineffable words, which it is not lawful for a man to speak. Of such a one I will glory, but of myself I will not glory, except in my infirmities.

27. So here too the devil, since he perceived him to be strong, holds out boasting, which deceives even the strong; but the Lord answered him: you shall not tempt the Lord your God. In this you recognize that Christ is both Lord and God, and that the Father and the Son are of one power, according to what is written: I and the Father are one. And therefore, if the devil puts forward 'one', meet him with the fact that it is written 'are', and distinguish 'one', so that you do not divide the power; distinguish 'one', so that you do not separate the Father and the Son. And the devil led him again onto a very high mountain and showed him all the kingdoms of the world in a moment of time.

28. Well are worldly and earthly things shown in a moment of time. For it is not so much the speed of the sight that is indicated as the frailty of a perishable power that is expressed; for in a moment all those things pass away, and often the honor of the world has gone before it has come. For what of the world can be lasting, when the ages themselves are not lasting? We are taught here to despise the gusts of empty ambition, because all worldly dignity lies subject to the devil's power, frail for use and empty for fruit.

29. But how is it that here the devil gives power, and elsewhere you read that all power is from God? Can anyone serve two masters or receive power from two? Is it then a contradiction? Not at all. But see that all things are from God. For the world is not without God, because the world too was made through him; but though it was made by God, yet its works are evil, because the world is set in the evil one; and the ordering of the world is from God, the works of the world from evil. So too the ordering of powers is from God, the ambition for power from evil. Finally there is no power he says except from God, and those that are, are ordained by God: not given, but ordained; and he who resists the power of God he says resists the ordinance. Here too, though the devil says that he gives power, yet he does not deny that all those things have been permitted him for a time. And so he who permitted it ordained it, and it is not the power that is evil, but the one who uses the power evilly. Finally: Do you wish not to fear the power? Do good, and you will have praise from it. So it is not power that is evil, but ambition. Finally, so far is the ordering of power from God, that he who uses power well is God's minister. Of God he says he is the minister to you for good. So the fault is not in the office at all, but in the minister; nor can the ordinance of God displease, but the conduct of the one who administers it. For, to draw an example from heavenly things down to earthly ones, the emperor gives an honor and has praise. But if anyone uses the honor badly, the fault is not the emperor's but the judge's. Crimes have their own culprit, since it is not the power but his own service that entangles each one.

30. What then? Is it good to use power, to be eager for honor? Good, if it is conferred, not if it is seized. Yet distinguish this very good. For one practice is good in the world, another belongs to perfect virtue; for it is good that the pursuit of knowing the divinity be hindered by no occupations. For even if there are many goods, yet eternal life is one. But this is eternal life, that they may know you, the only true God, and him whom you have sent, Jesus Christ. And therefore the greatest fruit is that of eternal life, and God alone is the rewarder of eternal life: let us adore only our God and Lord and serve him alone, that he alone may reward us with the most ample fruit; let us flee all things that lie subject to the devil's power, lest, like an evil tyrant, he use the cruelty of the power he has received against those whom he finds within his kingdom.

31. So power is not from the devil, but it is nevertheless exposed to the snares of the devil. Nor yet is the ordering of powers evil on that account, because the powers are exposed to evil; for it is good to seek God, but a certain winding and error creeps into the very search. For if the inquirer is turned into sacrilege by a perverse interpretation, the offense of the seeker turns out worse than if he had not sought. Yet the fault is not the inquiry's but the inquirer's, nor is it the inquiry that is exposed to evil, but the disposition of the seeker. But if he who seeks God is often tempted because of the frailty of the flesh and the narrowness of the mind, how much more exposed is he who seeks the world! And ambition is all the more pernicious for this very reason, that it is a kind of flattering little matchmaker of dignities; and often those whom no vices delight, whom no luxury could move, no avarice undermine, ambition makes criminal. For it has a public grace and a private crime, and, in order to lord it over others, it first serves. It bows in compliance in order to be presented with honor, and, while it wishes to be loftier, it becomes more abject, since in the power itself that which excels is another's; for it commands the laws, but is a slave to itself.

32. Someone will perhaps say that only he who has done evil fears; yet he who sails the sea fears more, and on the other hand one who stands on the immovable station of the land is not wont to fear shipwreck; but if he embarks on the mobile element, he becomes exposed to more frequent dangers. Flee, then, the sea of the world: you will not fear shipwreck. Even if the blasts of blowing winds often beat the tops of trees, yet when the roots are firm there is no fall; but at sea, when the winds rage, even if there is not shipwreck for all, yet there is danger for all. So too, against the adverse blasts of spiritual wickedness no one is safe, whether on the sand or on the open sea, and the ships of Tarshish are often shattered by a violent wind.

33. So much in the moral sense. But as regards the mystical order, you see the chains of the old error undone in their own footsteps, so that first the snare of gluttony, second that of levity, third that of ambition might be loosed. For Adam was enticed by food, and, having gone over through levity of judgment to the place of the forbidden tree, he incurred also the crime of rash ambition, while he aspired to the likeness of the divinity. And therefore the Lord first loosed the bonds of the old injury, so that we, with the yoke of captivity shaken off, might learn to conquer crimes with the help of the Scriptures.

34. But if the Lord Jesus did not desire what was his own, why should you seek what is another's? If the Creator of all despised the glory of the world by the virtue of undergoing poverty, why should you disdain what you were born to, and covet what is not due to you? Why should you demand that things which cannot last for your use should last for your punishment? Beware of snares, beware of frauds. And by this very fact that the crafty devil shakes the whole world with fraud to overthrow man, and fights with all the enticements of the world, his blandishments are all the more to be guarded against by you. It was not food that had swayed Eve, not forgetfulness of the commandments that had made her fall away, but the alluring ambition for the promised honor deceived her. If she had been willing to adore the Lord alone, she would not have sought what was not due to her. And therefore a remedy is given by which you may blunt the weapon of ambition: that we should serve the Lord alone; for religious devotion is free of ambition.

35. And when every temptation was finished, the devil departed from him until a time. These three kinds of vices are shown to be the springs of almost all crimes; for Scripture would not have said 'when every temptation was finished' unless in these three lay the matter of all sins, whose seeds are to be guarded against in their very origin. So the end of temptations is the end of desires, because the causes of temptations are the causes of desires. And the causes of desires are the delight of the flesh, the show of glory, the greed for power. How religious it seems not to refuse the company of a Christian woman! But frequent temptation comes from it. If the devil sees men intent on God, he makes suggestions to deceive them; but you, however much you may be confident of your resolve, beware of temptation, knowing nature. These three, if you remember, Paul too prescribed to be guarded against, marking out that there are three kinds of sins, free of which one is to await the crown of righteousness. For neither he says were we in a word of flattery nor in an occasion of avarice, God is witness, nor seeking glory from men; and therefore he conquered the devil, he sought the crown.

36. You see, then, that the devil himself is not persistent in his zeal, that he is wont to yield to true virtue. And though he does not cease to envy, yet he dreads to press on, because he shrinks from being triumphed over more often. So, when the name of God was heard, he departed he says until a time; for afterwards he came, not to tempt, but to fight openly.

37. Divine Scripture, then, teaches you that your contest is not against flesh and blood, but against spiritual snares. You see the magnificence of the Christian man, who contends with the rulers of the world, and, though set on earth, fights by strength of mind against the spiritual forces of wickedness which are in the heavenly places. For we do not contend over earthly things, so as to fight on earth; but since spiritual rewards are set before us, the kingdom of God and the inheritance of Christ, spiritual hindrances must first be overcome. A crown is set before us; contests must be undergone. No one can be crowned unless he has conquered; no one can conquer unless he has first contended. Of the crown itself, too, the fruit is greater where the labor is greater; narrow indeed and strait is the way that leads to life, but broad and spacious that which leads to death. And therefore we ought in no way to fear temptation; for it is the cause of victory, the matter of triumphs.

38. That rich man, who felt no temptation in the world, is in torments in the underworld; that poor man Lazarus, who was so afflicted and worn down by poverty and sickness that his sores were licked by dogs, wretched through the toil of this life, sought the crown of eternal glory; for many are the tribulations, not of anyone at all, but of the righteous. Finally, those whom the Lord loves he often chastises. Peter was tempted so that he denied; he denied so that he wept. And what shall I say of the rest? Job, surely, was approved by God; but though he was approved, yet he was not a victor. His devotion had been approved, yet he did not have the prize of virtue; and therefore he is offered up to be tempted, that he might be rendered more glorious.

39. In that contest too it matters that you look at the steps. The devil has not one weapon; he multiplies his darts, to conquer either by reward or by weariness. First he wounds through desire, second through dutiful love, third through health; for he fights with the sores of mind and body alike. The very diversity of the temptations, too, matches the diversity of those contending. The rich man is pressed by loss to his avarice, the father by the loss of his sons, the man by pains, the body by sores. How great are these weapons! Hence the Lord did not wish to have anything that he might lose, and therefore he came here poor, so that the devil would have nothing to take away. Do you wish to know how true this is? Hear the Lord himself saying: the prince of this world comes, and in me he will find nothing. He did not wish even to be father of a few, so that he might be father of all — and in vain would he be tempted with sores of the body who despised all the sufferings of the body — and at the same time to show us that a spotless victory was due to him once the enemy of the body was driven off. But the one, as a man, is tempted through his own things, this one through public things; from the one his patrimony is taken away, to this one the kingdom of the world is offered. Nor is the devil wholly without fraud, for he feared to exasperate the Son of God: the one he tempts with injuries, this one with rewards. The one, like a little slave, says: the Lord gave, the Lord has taken away; this one, conscious of his nature and of his dispensation, laughs that his own things are offered to him. And to return to the other: messenger rushes in upon messenger, wounds are redoubled, and yet the strong athlete is not troubled in mind. A woman is brought in, the enticement of the first fraud; this one, born of a virgin, had no woman liable to error. Friends are brought in to crush with wicked counsels the one who resists; but in all that befell him Job never sinned with his lips in the sight of God.

40. For that he curses the day, saying: let that day perish on which I was born; and below: and let him curse it who cursed that day, who is to crush the great sea-monster, pertains to prophecy, because our Lord Jesus Christ crushed the devil as the sea-monster of this stormy world; and he desires that the day of his carnal generation perish, so that his day may be counted in regeneration. 'Let perish' he says 'the worldly day, that the spiritual day may arise'. So in temptation holy Job spoke mysteries; for he who was conquering the world saw Christ.

41. Let us, then, not fear temptations, but rather glory in temptations, saying: 'when we are weak, then we are powerful'; for then the crown of righteousness is woven. But that was perhaps suited to Paul; we, however, since there are many crowns, ought to hope for one or another. In the world the laurel is a crown, the shield is a crown. But for you there is set a crown of delights; a crown indeed of delights shall overshadow you. And elsewhere: with the shield of his will he will surround you; with glory also and honor the Lord has crowned him whom he loved. So he who wishes to give a crown suggests temptations; and if ever you are tempted, know that a crown is being prepared. Take away the contests of the martyrs, you have taken away the crowns; take away the tortures, you have taken away the beatitudes.

42. Is not the temptation of Joseph the consecration of virtue? Is not the injury of prison the crown of chastity? How could he have obtained a share of the kingdom in Egypt had he not been sold by his brothers? That this was done by the will of God, so that the righteous man might be proved, he himself showed, saying: how it should come to pass, as on this day, that a great people should be fed? So we ought not to fear as evils the temptations of the world, by which good rewards are gained, but rather to ask, in consideration of the human condition, that we may undergo such temptations as we can bear.

43. And Jesus returned in the power of the Spirit into Galilee. In this place is fulfilled the prophecy of Isaiah, who says: the region of Zebulun and the land of Naphtali, and the rest who inhabit the coast, and those along the seaside and beyond the Jordan, of Galilee of the Gentiles, the people that sat in the shadow of death, have seen a great light. For who is the great light but Christ, who enlightens every man coming into this world?

44. Then he took the book, to show that he himself is the one who spoke in the prophets, and to remove the sacrileges of the unbelieving, who say that there is one God of the Old Testament and another of the New, or who say that the beginning of Christ is from the Virgin; for how did he begin from the Virgin who before the Virgin was speaking: the Spirit of the Lord is upon me? You see the Trinity, coeternal and perfect.

45. Scripture speaks of Jesus himself as God and man, perfect in both; it speaks also of the Father and of the Holy Spirit. For the Holy Spirit is shown as co-worker when, in bodily form like a dove, he descended upon Christ, when the Son of God was baptized in the river and the Father spoke from heaven. What greater testimony, then, do we seek than that he declared with his own voice that he was the one who spoke in the prophets? He is anointed with spiritual oil and heavenly power, that he might water the poverty of the human condition with the eternal treasure of the resurrection, turn away the captivity of the mind, enlighten the blindness of minds, proclaim the year of the Lord, spread over endless times, a year which knows no return into a round of toil, and which grants men a continuance of fruit and of rest. And he so bent himself to every service that he did not even disdain the office of reader; but we were impious, who, looking at his body, refused to gather faith in his divinity from the miracles of his works.

46. Amen I say to you that no prophet is accepted in his own country. Envy is betrayed in no small measure, which, forgetful of the love due to fellow citizens, turns the causes of love into bitter hatreds. At the same time by this example and oracle alike it is declared that you would vainly expect the help of heavenly mercy if you envy the fruits of another's virtue; for the Lord is a despiser of the envious, and from those who persecute the divine benefits in others he turns away the miracles of his power. For the acts of the Lord's flesh are an example of his divinity, and his invisible things are shown to us through the things that are visible.

47. Not idly, then, does the Savior excuse himself for having worked no miracles of power in his own country, lest perhaps anyone should think that our affection for our country ought to be the cheaper. For he who loved all could not fail to love his fellow citizens; but those who envy have disowned for themselves the love of their country; love indeed does not envy, is not puffed up. Nor yet is his country without a share of divine benefits. For what greater miracle than that Christ was born in it? See, then, what evil envy brings. Because of envy the country is judged unworthy for a citizen to work in it, which was worthy for the Son of God to be born in it.

48. In truth I say to you: there were many widows in the days of Elijah. Not because the days were Elijah's, but they were those in which Elijah worked; or because he made day for those who in his works saw the light of spiritual grace and were converted to the Lord. And therefore heaven was opened to those who saw the eternal and divine mysteries, and was closed when there was famine, because there was no abundance of the knowledge of the divinity.

49. But we have written of this more fully when we wrote about widows. And there were many lepers in the times of Elisha the prophet, and none of them was cleansed except Naaman the Syrian. Clearly this word of the Lord Savior instructs us and exhorts us to zeal in venerating the divinity, since no one is shown to have been healed and freed from the disease of a spotted body except him who sought health with religious duty; for divine benefits are conferred not on those who sleep, but on those who keep watch. And well, by the fitting example of a comparison, is the arrogance of the envious citizens beaten back, and the Lord's deed is taught to agree with the ancient Scriptures, since in the books of Kings too we read that a Gentile man, Naaman, was freed from the spots of leprosy by the prophet's oracle, while leprosy of body and mind alike consumed very many of the Jews; for indeed the history relates that those four too, who, compelled by famine, were the first to make for the camp of the king of Syria, were lepers. Why, then, did the prophet not cure his brothers, not cure his fellow citizens, not heal those who shared his lot, while he healed strangers, healed those who had neither the observance of the law nor fellowship in religion, unless because medicine belongs to the will, not to the race, and the divine gift is drawn out by prayers, not conferred by right of nature? Learn to ask for what you desire to obtain; the gains of heavenly gifts do not attend fastidious men.

50. But though the simple exposition shapes the moral disposition, yet the grace of the mystery is not hidden. For while later things are derived from earlier ones, so also earlier things are confirmed by later ones. For we said in another book that in that widow to whom Elijah was sent a type of the Church was set forth beforehand. It is fitting that the people should follow the Church. That people gathered from foreigners, that people once leprous, that people once spotted, before it was baptized in the mystical river, the same people, after the sacraments of baptism, washed from the spots of body and mind, began to be no longer leprosy but a spotless virgin without wrinkle. Rightly, then, is Naaman described as great in the sight of his lord and admirable in countenance, since in his type salvation to come is declared to the Gentiles; he, warned by the counsel of a holy girl, who, when the strength of her fellow citizens was broken, had come as a captive under hostile power, to hope for health from the prophet, is healed not by the command of an earthly king but by the generosity of divine mercy.

51. Why is he ordered to plunge himself a mystical number of times? Why is the river Jordan chosen? Are not good he says Abana and Pharpar, rivers of Damascus, rather than the Jordan? But in anger he preferred these; on reflection he chose the Jordan; for anger does not know the mystery, faith knows it. Learn the grace of saving baptism: he who had plunged as a leper emerged a believer. Learn that spiritual sacraments are signified: a remedy for the body is sought, a remedy for the mind is gained. The flesh is washed, the disposition is washed. For I see that the leprosy of the body was cleansed no more than that of the mind, when after baptism, the filth of the old error wiped away, he declares that he will not offer to strange gods the victims which he pledges to the Lord.

52. Learn also the precepts of a fitting virtue: he proved his faith who refused rewards. Learn from the twofold teaching of words and of deeds what you should follow. You have the Lord's precept, the prophet's example: freely to receive, freely to give, and not to sell the ministry but to offer it; for the grace of God is not assessed at a price, nor is gain sought in the sacraments, but the service of the priest.

53. Yet it is not enough if you yourself do not seek gain: the hands of your household too are to be restrained. Nor is this alone demanded, that you keep yourself alone chaste and spotless; for the Apostle did not say 'yourself alone', but keep yourself chaste. So what is required is that not only you, but also your house, be kept chaste from markets of this kind; it behooves indeed the priest to be irreproachable, set well over his house, having children subject with all chastity. But if anyone does not know how to preside over his own house, how will he take care of the Church of God? Instruct, then, your household, exhort it, guard it; and if a little slave deceives you — for I do not rule out what is humanly possible — let him, once caught, be rejected after the prophet's example. Leprosy quickly follows shameful pay, and money ill gotten stains body and soul. You have received he says money, and you will possess from it a field and a vineyard and olive groves and flocks. And the leprosy of Naaman will cleave to you and to your seed for ever. You see that by the deed of the author the succession of the heir is condemned; for the guilt of a mystery sold is inexpiable, and the vengeance of heavenly grace passes to descendants. Finally, the Moabites and the rest shall not enter to the third and fourth generation: so long, that is, to interpret more simply, until the succession of many generations should abolish the guilt of the authors.

54. But since those who sinned against God by the error of idolatry seem to have been punished to the fourth generation, surely the sentence seems harsher by which the seed of Gehazi is condemned for ever by prophetic authority for the desire of having, especially since our Lord Jesus Christ has given remission of sins to all through the regeneration of the washing — unless you are to understand the seed of vices rather than of race? For just as those who are children of the promise are reckoned as good seed, so too those who are children of error are reckoned as evil seed. For the Jews too are of their father the devil, certainly not by succession of the flesh, but of crime. So all the covetous, all the avaricious possess the leprosy of Gehazi with their riches, and by ill-gotten pay have gathered not so much a patrimony of wealth as a treasure of crimes, with eternal punishment and brief fruit. For while riches are perishable, the punishment is perpetual, because neither the avaricious nor the drunkard nor the idolater will possess the kingdom of God.

And all in the synagogue, hearing these things, were filled with anger, and they rose up and cast him out of the city.

55. The sacrileges of the Jews, which the Lord had foretold long before through the prophet — and in a little verse of a psalm he had declared what he would suffer when set in the body, saying: they repaid me evil for good — he teaches in the Gospel to have been fulfilled. For while he himself spread benefits through the peoples, they inflicted injuries. Nor is it any wonder if they lost salvation who cast out of their borders the one who brings salvation; for the Lord is moral, and, being one who taught his apostles by his own example to become all things to all, he neither repudiates the willing nor binds the unwilling, nor struggles against those who cast him out, nor fails those who ask. So elsewhere he left the Gerasenes, as weak and ungrateful, when they could not bear his mighty works.

56. At the same time understand that the suffering of the body was not of necessity but voluntary, and that he was not taken by the Jews but offered by himself. For when he wills he is taken, when he wills he slips away, when he wills he is hung up, when he wills he is not held. And here he had gone up to the brow of the mountain to be cast down, and behold, through the midst of them, the minds of the raging men suddenly changed or stupefied, he came down; for not yet had the hour of the Passion come. Indeed, he preferred still to heal the Jews rather than to destroy them, so that by the ineffectual outcome of their fury they might cease to will what they could not accomplish. You see, then, that here what he wrought was of his divinity, and there his being taken was of his will. For how could he be held by a few, who is not held by a people? But he did not want the sacrilege to be that of many. That he might turn back the odium of the cross upon the authors of the guilt, and be fastened by a few indeed, but die for the whole world.

And in the synagogue there was a man having an unclean spirit; and below: but rising from the synagogue he entered the house of Simon and Andrew. And Simon's mother-in-law was held by great fevers.

57. See the clemency of the Lord Savior. Neither moved by indignation nor offended by crime nor violated by injury does he desert Judaea; indeed, unmindful of injury, mindful of clemency, now by teaching, now by freeing, now by healing, he soothes the hearts of the faithless people. And well did Saint Luke set first the man freed from the spirit of wickedness, and place after it the woman's healing. For the Lord had come to cure both sexes, and he who was created first had to be healed first; nor was she to be passed over who had sinned more by fickleness of mind than by depravity.

58. He signifies that the works of the Lord's medicine were begun on the sabbath, so that the new creation might begin where the old creation had before left off, and that he might show at the very beginning that the Son of God is not under the law but above the law, and that the law is not undone but fulfilled. For the world was made not through the law but by the word, as we read: by the word of the Lord the heavens were made firm. So the law is not undone but fulfilled, that there may be a renewal of man, who was already falling. Hence the Apostle too says: stripping off the old man, put on the new, who has been created according to Christ. And well did he begin on the sabbath, to show himself the Creator, who wove works into works and pursued the work which he himself had already begun; as when a builder sets about renewing a house, he begins to undo the old not from the foundations but from the roof. And so he first puts his hand where he had before left off.

59. Then he begins from lesser things to arrive at greater. Men too can free from a demon, but by the word of God; to command the resurrection of the dead belongs to the divine power alone.

60. Nor should it disturb anyone that in this book the devil is brought in as the first to have spoken the name of Jesus the Nazarene. For Christ did not receive from him the name which the angel brought from heaven to the Virgin: it is a mark of his impudence that he is the first among men to usurp something and to carry it to men as though new, so as to strike them with terror of his power. Finally, in Genesis too he was the first to name God to man; for so you have it: and he said to the woman: why indeed has God said that you should not eat of every tree? So both were deceived by the devil, and healed by Christ.

61. Follow the rest, and learn the mysteries of the Gospel reading, and in the healing of the two recognize the sacrament of the common salvation. As indeed in Adam all die, so in Christ all are made alive. Who is that man who in the synagogue had the unclean spirit of a demon but the people of the Jews, who, bound as if by a serpent's coils and knotted in the devil's snares, defiled a feigned purity of body with the filth of the inner mind? And well was the man who had an unclean spirit in the synagogue, because he had lost the Holy Spirit. For the devil had entered where Christ had gone out. At the same time it is shown that the nature of the devil is not wicked, but his works are unjust. For him whom by his higher nature he confesses as Lord he denies by his work. And in this appear his wickedness and the depravity of the Jews, that he so poured over the people the blindness of a frenzied mind that the people deny him whom demons confess. O inheritance of the disciples, worse than the master! He tempts the Lord by word, these by deed; he says: cast yourself, these attack him to cast him.

62. If we weigh these things with deeper counsel, we ought to understand the healing both of mind and of body, so that the mind, which was suffering from the serpent's snares, was set free first. For the soul would never be overcome by the body unless it had first been tempted by the devil. For since the soul drives, quickens and governs the body, how could it be carried away captive into the body's enticements unless it too were bound by the chains of some higher power? Finally, Eve did not hunger before the serpent's cunning tempted her; and therefore the medicine of salvation had to work first against the very author of sin.

63. Perhaps too, in the type of that woman, the mother-in-law of Simon and Andrew, our flesh was languishing with the various fevers of crimes and burning with the immoderate enticements of diverse desires. Nor would I say that the fever of love is less than the fever of heat. So the one fever inflames the mind, the other the body; for our lust is a fever, in that desires are fiery. Hence the Apostle too says: if they do not contain themselves, let them marry; for it is better to marry than to burn. Our luxury is a fever, our wrath is a fever, which, though they are vices of the body, yet entwine fire in the bones and assail the understanding, the mind and the senses. This is first solicited by the devil's art. For a good field, a garment, a necklace are the persuasion of the serpent. The favor of honors, the height of powers, the sweetness of banquets, the beauty of a harlot are the snare of the devil, and as it were an alluring address of spiritual wickedness, which, through the enticement of the flesh, which is quickly softened with a kind of feminine levity, casts down the mind too from its step; for the mind does not desire the beauty of a woman before the eye of the body does. Finally, what you have not seen you will not love; but when the flesh has desired, the constancy of the mind too, suffering with it, flags, and the mind is bent by the fellowship of love — for the two are in one flesh — and so death creeps in by the effect of crime, the devil tempting, the flesh persuading.

64. Yet the fever of the mind is more violent than that of the body, and therefore for the pleasure of the mind the health of the body is very often despised, and there is no abstaining from dangers. Hence it does not seem out of place to recall how Theotimus, when he was suffering from a grave affliction of the eyes and loved his wife, though the physician had forbidden him the faculty of intercourse, being impatient of desire and carried away by the impulse of lust, was unable to restrain himself. For knowing and aware that he would lose his eyes, before he came together with his wife, in the very heat of burning desire and in the preparation of their wonted intimacy, 'farewell' he said 'beloved light'. So lust burns more fiercely than a fever, and hurls down and inflames more gravely.

65. But when anyone has come back to his senses from the frenzy, then the sight of the inner conscience is opened, and repentance for the deed follows, and each one blushes at the shameful things of his own crime. Then God is feared and the sinner wishes to hide himself, but cannot; then the flesh is blamed, the devil accused, the one as a procuress of vices, the other as author of the error. The deformity lies open; for every secret is naked to God, nor are the secrets of shameful deeds veiled by the leaves of that fig tree, that is, by bodily covering or by worldly boasting. And each one, with a mind conscious of sin, dreads the divine judgment, saying: 'what mountains are to fall upon me, in what clefts of the rocks shall I hide myself, when he comes to shatter the earth?' Then the flesh breeds thistles and thorns for the mind, that is, the bites of cares and of anxieties, and the heats which the mind itself has put around itself through the concupiscence of the flesh. For the soul is fastened as if by certain nails to bodily pleasures, and once it has clung to them, sunk in earthly desires, it can hardly fly back without the favor of God to the height from which it came down. For, bound by the snares of its own acts and exposed to the enticements of worldly delights, it is now held fast.

66. This Adam, then, this Eve the Lord came to free; of whom the one was made to the image of God, the other received the strength of her husband; as long as she was subject to the stronger, they bore in one spirit one will pleasing to God, and, set in the paradise of God, they worked the nourishment of heavenly life. But after the flesh began to persuade otherwise and not to keep its proper law, they were made exiles from paradise and fell back, as their sin deserved, into this lower and sunken place. Nor let anyone think it incongruous if Adam and Eve are reckoned as a type of soul and body, since they are reckoned as a type of the Church and of Christ. For when the Apostle said that the two are in one flesh, he added: This sacrament he says is great, but I speak of Christ and of the Church. In that, then, in which there is a mystery of God on high, there can much more be a mystery of our mind.

67. But it clings, it is fastened, it is captured, and, kindled by bodily fevers, it sickens through fellow-suffering with the flesh. A physician must be sought. But who is this one so great as to heal the wounds of the stricken mind? What man is so great as to be able to help others, when he cannot help himself? Who could restore life to others, when he himself cannot escape death? All indeed died in Adam, because through one man sin entered into this world, and through sin death, and so it passed through to all men, in whom all sinned. His guilt, then, is the death of all. Finally saints were sent, prophets too were sent, to speak the divine oracles, and they could accomplish nothing. Let us seek, then, a physician from among the angels or archangels. But how can they bring me protection, so that I may not sin, when an archangel himself could not abstain from sin? How will an angel be able to call me back to paradise, when Satan himself and his angels cannot keep the seat which they received?

And getting into one boat, which was Simon's, he asked them to put out a little from the land.

68. When the Lord had imparted various kinds of healing to many, the crowd began to be held back neither by time nor by place from its eagerness to be healed. Evening came on, they followed; the lake stood in the way, they pressed on. And therefore he went up into Peter's boat. This is that boat which according to Matthew is still tossed by the waves, according to Luke is filled with fish, so that you may recognize both the beginnings of the Church tossed by the waves and the later times of the Church overflowing; for they are fish who sail through this life. There Christ still sleeps for the disciples, here he gives commands; for he sleeps for the fearful, he keeps watch for the perfect. But how Christ sleeps you have heard; you have learned it in the prophet who says: I sleep, and my heart keeps watch.

69. And Saint Matthew rightly thought that the sign of eternal power was not to be passed over, where he commands the winds. For it is not human teaching, as you have heard the Jews say: with a word he commands the spirits, but the mark of heavenly majesty. In that the troubled sea is calmed, and the elements obey the command of the divine voice, and insensible things receive the sense of obeying, the mystery of divine grace is revealed. In that the waves of the world grow gentle and the unclean spirit is quieted by a word, the one does not refute the other, but both are celebrated. You have a miracle in the elements, you have a lesson in the mysteries.

70. So, since Saint Matthew had touched on those things first, Saint Luke chose for himself that boat in which Peter was fishing. This one, which has Peter, is not troubled; that one, which has Judas, is troubled. Although many merits of the disciples sailed there, yet the unbelief of the traitor still tossed it. Peter is in both, but he who is firm by his own merits is troubled by another's. Let us beware, then, of the unbelieving man, let us beware of the traitor, lest through one we many be tossed by the waves. So this boat is not troubled, in which prudence sails, unbelief is absent, faith breathes. For how could that boat be troubled over which he presided in whom is the firmament of the Church? There, then, is trouble, where faith is small.

71. Here is security, where love is perfect. Finally, though the others are commanded to let down their nets, yet to Peter alone is it said: put out into the deep, that is, into the depth of discourses. For what is so deep as to see the depth of riches, to know the Son of God, and to take up the profession of the divine generation? Though the human mind cannot comprehend it by the investigation of full reason, yet the fullness of faith embraces it. For though it is not permitted me to know how he was born, yet it is not permitted me not to know that he was born. I do not know the sequence of the generation, but I acknowledge the authority of the generation. We were not present when the Son of God was born of the Father, but we were present when he was called the Son of God by the Father. If we do not believe God, whom do we believe? For all that we believe we believe either by sight or by hearing. Sight is often deceived; hearing is in faith. Or is the person of the one who asserts it called into question? If good men said it, we would think it wicked not to believe: God asserts it, the Son proves it, the sun fleeing confesses it, the trembling earth bears witness. Into this deep of discourse the Church is led by Peter, to see on the one side the Son of God rising again, on the other the Holy Spirit flowing forth.

72. And what are the nets of the apostles which they are commanded to let down but the interweavings of words, and, as it were, certain folds of speech and recesses of discourses, which do not let go those whom they have caught? And well are nets the apostolic instruments of fishing, which do not kill those they catch, but keep them, and draw them out of the deep to the light, and lead those tossed by the waves from the lowest things to the things above.

73. There is also another apostolic kind of fishing, the kind with which the Lord bids Peter alone to fish, saying: cast a hook and take the fish that comes up first. Great indeed and spiritual is the lesson by which Christian men are taught that they ought to be subject to the higher powers, lest anyone think that the ordinance of an earthly king may be dissolved. For if the Son of God paid the tax, who are you, so great, that you think it ought not to be paid? And he paid the tax who possessed nothing; but you, who follow the gain of the world, why do you not acknowledge your duty to the world? Why do you carry yourself above the world with a certain arrogance of mind, when you are subject to the world by a wretched greed?

74. So the didrachma is paid, which was the price of our redemption and of our body, set forth beforehand in the Law, paid in full in the Gospel, found not idly in the mouth of a fish; out of your mouth indeed you will be justified. For our confession is to us the price of immortality, because, as it is written, with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.

75. And perhaps this first fish is the first martyr, having in his mouth the didrachma, that is, the price of the tax. Our didrachma is Christ. That first martyr, then, namely Stephen, had a treasure in his mouth when he spoke Christ in his passion. But let us return to the passage set before us and learn the apostolic humility.

76. Master, he says, laboring through the whole night we have caught nothing; but at your word I will let down the net. I too, Lord, know that it is night for me when you do not command. No one has yet given in his name; I still have night. I have cast the javelin of my voice through Epiphany, and still I have caught nothing; I have cast it through the day. I wait for you to command; at your word I will let down the nets. O empty presumption, O fruitful humility! Those who had before caught nothing enclose at the word of the Lord a great multitude of fish. This is not the work of human eloquence, but the gift of the heavenly calling. The disputations of men give way; the people believe by their own faith.

77. The nets break, yet no fish slips away. Their partners who were in the other boat are called to help. What is that other boat but perhaps Judaea, from which John and James are chosen? Judaea has been made indeed his sanctification. These, then, came together from the synagogue to Peter's boat, that is, to the Church, so as to fill both boats; for all bend the knee in the name of Jesus, whether Jew or Greek; Christ is all things and in all. But this heap is suspect to me, lest by its fullness the boats be almost sunk; for there must also be heresies, that the good may be proved.

78. We can, however, understand the boat of the other as another church too; for from the one many churches are derived. See another anxiety of Peter, to whom his own catch is now suspect. But the perfect man, who knows how he can catch the scattered, knows how he can keep those stored away. Those whom he catches at the word he gives back to the word; he denies that the catch is his, he denies that the gift is his.

79. Depart he says from me, Lord, for I am a sinful man. For he marveled at the divine gifts, and the more he had merited, the less he presumed. You too say: depart from me, Lord, for I am a sinner, that the Lord may answer you: do not fear. Do not fear to confess your sin to the indulgent Lord; do not fear also to bring to the Lord what is yours, since he has granted us what is his. He knows not how to envy, knows not how to snatch, knows not how to take away. You see how good the Lord is, who grants so much to men that they have the power of giving life.

Book Five

And it came to pass, when he was in one of the cities, behold, a man full of leprosy; and he, falling on his face, besought him, saying: Lord, if you will, you can make me clean. And stretching out his hand he touched him, saying: I will; be made clean.

1. Fittingly, where the leper is cleansed, no definite place is named, to show that it was not the one people of some particular city, but the peoples of all, that were healed. Fittingly too, according to Luke, this man is healed in the fourth sign since the Lord came to Capernaum; for if God lit up the fourth day with the sun and made it brighter than the other days, when the elements of the world were beginning to shine, we ought to esteem this work too as brighter. And according to Matthew this man is brought in as the first healed by the Lord after the blessings, so that, since the Lord had said: I have not come to destroy the Law, but to fulfil it, he who was shut out by the Law, presuming that he would be cleansed by the power of the Lord, might judge that the grace which could wash away the leper's stain is not from the Law, but above the Law.

2. But as the authority of power is declared in the Lord, so the constancy of faith is declared in him. He falls on his face, which belongs to humility and modesty, so that each may blush for the stains of his own life; but shame did not hold back confession. He showed the wound, he asked for the remedy, and the confession itself is full of reverence and faith: If you will, he says you can make me clean. He ascribed power to the Lord's will; and of the Lord's will he did not doubt as one who disbelieved in his loving-kindness, but as one conscious of his own filth he did not presume.

3. To him the Lord answered with a certain moral holiness: I will; be made clean. And immediately his leprosy departed from him. For there is nothing between the work of God and his command, because the work is in the command. Indeed he spoke, and they were made. You see therefore, what cannot be doubted, that the will of God is power. If then his will is power, those who assert that the Trinity is of one will surely assert that it is of one power. So the leprosy departed at once: that you may understand the affection of the healer, he added truth to the work.

4. Indeed, according to Mark the Lord had pity on him, which is beautifully put. And the evangelists set down many such things, wishing to ground us in both, describing signs of power for faith, expressing works of virtue for imitation. Hence he both touches, not disdaining, and commands, not distrusting; for this is the token of power, that as one having the power of healing and the authority of commanding he does not shun the testimony of the deed. I will then he says on account of Photinus, he commands on account of Arius, he touches on account of Mani.

5. Nor is the leprosy of one man only cured, but of all those to whom it is said: Now you are clean because of the word which I have spoken to you. If then the word is the medicine of leprosy, surely contempt of the word is the leprosy of the mind. But lest the leprosy should pass over to the physician, let each one, by the example of the Lord's humility, shun boasting. For why is he commanded to tell no one, except to teach that our benefits are not to be published but suppressed, so that we abstain not only from the reward of money but also from that of favour? Or perhaps this too was the reason for the silence enjoined, that he thought those better who had believed by a spontaneous faith rather than for benefits hoped for.

6. He is commanded, moreover, according to the Law to offer himself to the priest, not that he should bring another, but that he should offer himself to God as a spiritual sacrifice, so that, the filth of his past conduct having been wiped away, he may be consecrated, through the knowledge of faith and the discipline of wisdom, as a victim to please God; every victim indeed shall be salted with salt. Hence Paul also says: I beseech you therefore, brethren, by the mercy of God, that you present your bodies as a victim acceptable, pleasing to God.

7. At the same time this is wonderful, that he healed him in the very manner in which he had been besought. If you will, you can make me clean. I will he says, be made clean. You have the will, you have also the effect of loving-kindness. And stretching out his hand he touched him. The Law forbids lepers to be touched, but he who is Lord of the Law does not obey the law, but makes the law. He touched him, then, not because he could not cleanse without touching, but to prove that he was not subject to the Law and did not fear contagion as men do, but that he who was freeing others could not be defiled; and at the same time, conversely, that the leprosy, which was wont to defile the one who touched it, should be put to flight by the Lord's touch.

8. And he is ordered to show himself to the priest and to make an offering for his cleansing, so that, as he offers himself to the priest, the priest might understand that he had been healed not by the order of the Law but by the grace of God above the Law; and, as the sacrifice is enjoined according to the precept of Moses, the Lord might show that he was not destroying the Law but fulfilling it, he who, while walking according to the Law, was seen to heal above the Law those whom the remedies of the Law had not healed. And well did he add: as Moses commanded; for the Law is spiritual, and therefore he is seen to have enjoined a spiritual sacrifice.

9. Indeed he added: that this may be for a testimony to you, that is: if you believe God, if the leprosy of impiety departs, if the priest is a knower of hidden things, if he is a witness of a pure disposition, by what can he be seen to be a priest more than he from whom hidden things are not hid, to whom it is said: You are a priest for ever according to the order of Melchizedek?

And behold, men carrying on a bed a man who had been paralysed, and seeking to bring him in and lay him before him; and not finding by what way they might bring him in because of the crowd, they went up on the roof and let him down through the tiles on the bed into the midst before Jesus.

10. Not idle nor narrow is the healing of this paralytic, since it is first stated that the Lord prayed, not indeed for the sake of support, but for the sake of example; for he gave a pattern for imitation, he did not seek the ambition of obtaining. And when the teachers of the Law had gathered from all Galilee and Judaea and Jerusalem, among the remedies of the other infirm the healing of this paralytic is described. First of all, as we said before, every sick man ought to bring in intercessors to ask for his health, through whom the loosened frame of our life and the lame steps of our actions may be restored by the remedy of the heavenly word. Let there be, then, some admonishers of the mind, who may raise to higher things the soul of a man, though it be numb with the weakness of the outward body. By their supports, again, made ready both to lift himself up and to humble himself, let him be placed before Jesus, worthy to be seen by the Lord's gaze; for the Lord regards humility, because he has regarded the humility of his handmaid.

11. Whose faith he says when he saw. Great is the Lord, who for the merit of some pardons others, and while he approves some, remits the errors of others. Why, O man, should your fellow not prevail with you, when with the Lord a servant has both the merit of interceding and the right of obtaining? Learn, you who judge, to pardon; learn, you who are sick, to obtain. If you despair of pardon for grave sins, bring in intercessors, bring in the Church to pray for you, in consideration of which the Lord pardons what he could deny to you.

12. And although we ought not to neglect faith in the history, so as to believe that the body of this paralytic was truly healed, yet recognize the health of the inner man, to whom sins are forgiven. And when the Jews assert that these can be granted by the Lord alone, they surely confess him to be God and by their own judgment betray their unbelief, so that they affirm the work and deny the person. So the Son of God both received from them the testimony of his own work and does not require the support of their voice; for unbelief can confess, it cannot believe. So testimony is not lacking to his divinity; faith is lacking to their salvation. For what the unwilling confess is the stronger for faith, and what is denied by those who are refuted by their own assertions is the more ruinous for guilt. Great therefore is the madness of the faithless people, that when it has confessed that to forgive sins belongs to God alone, it does not believe God when he forgives sins. But the Lord, wishing to save sinners, shows himself to be God both by the knowledge of hidden things and by the wonder of his deeds, adding: Which is easier, to say: 'Your sins are forgiven you', or to say: 'Rise and walk'?

13. In this place he shows the full form of the resurrection, he who, healing the wounds of mind and body, forgives the sins of souls and drives out the weakness of the flesh; for this is for the whole man to be cured. Although therefore it is a great thing to forgive men their sins — who indeed can forgive sins but God alone, who forgives also through those to whom he has given the power of forgiving? — yet it is far more divine to grant resurrection to bodies, since the Lord himself is the resurrection.

14. This bed which he is ordered to take up, what is it other than that the human body is ordered to be lifted up? It is the bed which is washed by David every night, the bed of pain, on which our soul lay sick with the heavy torment of conscience. But if anyone conducts himself according to the precepts of Christ, it is no longer a bed of pain but of rest. For by the compassion of the Lord what was death has begun to be rest, since he turned for us the sleep of death into the grace of delight. And he is bidden not only to lift his bed but also to return to his own house, that is, to go back to paradise; for that is the true house, which first received man, lost not by right but by fraud. Rightly then is the house restored, since he had come who should abolish the bond of fraud and restore the right.

15. No delay of healing intervenes: there is one moment for the words and for the remedies. The unbelievers watch him rising, marvel at him going away, and prefer to fear the wonders of the divine work rather than to believe them. For if they had believed, they would surely not have feared but loved; perfect love indeed casts fear out of doors. And therefore these men, because they did not love, made false charges. But to those making false charges he says: Why do you think evil things in your hearts? Who says this? The great priest. He saw leprosy in the hearts of the Jews; he shows them to be worse than the leper. The one, cleansed, was bidden to offer himself to the priest; these the priest rejects, lest their leprosy should defile others too.

16. There follows the mystical calling of the tax collector, whom he bids follow not by the step of the body but by the affection of the mind. So he, who formerly turned greedily to his profit, from tolls, hard gains from the toils and dangers of sailors, called by a word leaves his own things, he who used to seize the things of others, and abandoning that mean seat walks after the Lord with the whole step of his mind. He also sets out the preparations of a great banquet; for he who receives Christ into the inner dwelling is fed with the greatest delights of overflowing pleasures. So the Lord willingly enters and reclines in the affection of him who has believed.

17. But again the envy of the unbelieving is kindled, and a figure of future punishment is foreshown; for while the faithful feast and recline in that kingdom of heaven, unbelief will be tormented fasting. At the same time it is shown how much difference there is between the devotees of the Law and of grace: that those who follow the Law suffer the eternal hunger of a fasting mind, but those who have received the Word into the inner parts of the soul, refreshed by the abundance of heavenly food and of the fountain, cannot hunger and thirst. And therefore those who were fasting in mind murmured, saying:

18. Why does he eat and drink with tax collectors and sinners? That voice is the serpent's. Indeed the serpent uttered this as his first word, saying to Eve: Why indeed has God said: Do not eat of every tree? So they pour out their father's venom who say: Why indeed does he eat and drink with tax collectors and sinners? Hence the Lord, by eating with sinners, does not forbid us to enter into a banquet even with Gentiles, saying: Those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick.

19. A new master has brought a new medicine. This did not sprout from the earth; for all creation was ignorant of this compound. Come, all you who have fallen into the various passions of sins, use the foreign medicine by which the serpent's venom is expelled, which has not only taken away the scar of the passions but has also cut away the cause of the dire wound. This medicine does not cut off hunger, but supplies food to the soul. There came indeed the Son of Man eating and drinking, and they say: He has a demon; and therefore our mind is not fasting. They fast from whom Christ is absent and who lack the riches of good merits; but he for whom virtue suffices by its own delight, who receives Christ in his own house, holds a great banquet, that is, the spiritual banquet of good works, which the rich people lacks and on which the poor feasts. And therefore, he says, the sons of the bridegroom cannot fast, as long as the bridegroom is with them.

20. But the days will come, when the bridegroom shall be taken away from them. What are those days in which Christ is taken away from us, especially since he himself said: I am with you even to the consummation of the world, he himself said: I will not leave you orphans? For it is certain that if he leaves us, we cannot be saved. No one can take Christ away from you, unless you take yourself away. Let not your boasting take you away, let not arrogance take you away, and do not presume upon the Law; for he came not to call the righteous, but sinners.

21. How then has the Lord loved righteous deeds, and David has not seen the righteous forsaken, or what equity is this, if the righteous is left and the sinner is taken up, unless you understand that he calls those righteous who presume on the Law and do not seek the grace of the gospel? But no one is justified by the Law; rather he is redeemed by grace. There is, then, righteousness in the Law, but righteousness is not through the Law; for the Apostle himself too, a Hebrew of Hebrews, according to the Law a Pharisee, according to the righteousness which is in the Law having lived without complaint, he who glories on the ground of the Law: What things were gain to me he says these I counted loss for Christ, that is: he cast away the righteousness and the glory of the Law; for the righteousness of the Law without Christ is empty, because Christ is the fullness of the Law. And therefore, though there is righteousness in the Law, yet righteousness is not through the Law. For if righteousness is through the Law, then Christ died in vain; for Christ died to fulfil righteousness. Indeed, to John, who said: I ought to be baptized by you, and do you come to me? he answered: Let it be so now; for thus it becomes us to fulfil all righteousness. So Christ did not die in vain, but died for us, that the righteous might shine as the sun in the kingdom of their Father. But the Jews are not the righteous, to whom it is said: When you see the righteous entering into the kingdom of heaven. These are the righteous, who do not strike back the one who strikes them, who love their enemy. If we do not take it so, a contradiction is found:

22. I did not come to call the righteous, but he does not call those who call themselves righteous; for, ignorant indeed of God and seeking to establish their own righteousness, they have not been subject to the righteousness of God. So the usurpers of righteousness are not called to grace; for if grace is from repentance, surely he who disdains repentance renounces grace. These shall be wounded who sanctify themselves, from these the bridegroom is taken away; but from us neither Caiaphas nor Pilate has taken Christ away, nor can we fast, because we have Christ and feast on the flesh and blood of Christ. For how is he seen to fast who does not hunger, how is he seen to fast who does not thirst? And how can he thirst who drinks Christ, since he himself said: Whoever drinks of the water which I shall give him shall not thirst for ever? Indeed what follows will also make clear that this was said of the fasting of the mind.

He spoke indeed a parable to them: that no one puts a patch from a new garment upon an old garment.

23. He had said that the sons of the bridegroom, that is, the sons of the Word, who through the regeneration of the washing have been taken up into the right of divine generation, cannot fast as long as the bridegroom is with them. Surely that fasting is not set aside by which the flesh is worn down and bodily wantonness chastised; for this fasting commends us to God. For how would he forbid his disciples to fast, when the Lord himself fasted, when finally he said that the most wicked spirits are not usually overcome except by fastings and prayers? Indeed in this place too he called fasting an old garment, which the Apostle judged must be put off, saying: Strip off the old man with his deeds, that we may put on him who is renewed by the sanctification of baptism. To the same form, therefore, the series of precepts agrees, that we should not mix the acts of the old and of the new man, since the former, bodily and outward, works the works of the flesh, and this inner one, who is reborn, ought not to have a many-coloured appearance of old and new deeds, but, of one colour with Christ, to imitate with zeal of mind him to whom he was reborn in the washing. Away then with the discoloured coverings of the mind, which displease the bridegroom; for one who has not a wedding garment displeases him. But what can please the bridegroom except peace of soul, purity of heart, love of mind?

24. The Lord Jesus is a good bridegroom; he initiated nature by a new birth; she who is betrothed to him is set free from the corruptions of the flesh. She does not seek mortal sons — she takes no delight in the pains of Eve —, nor a husband liable to guilt, nor the inheritance of a condemned father. For she has seen the sores of the flesh which she formerly desired; she has noticed that that is not true beauty which has the deformity of vices. And therefore what have you to do with such a bridegroom, woman? Search carefully, and in the whole body you will find a scar. Recognize rather another bridegroom, who is surrounded with light, whose beauty cannot perish. Take him up into your mind, consecrate him in your temple, take him up in your body, as it is written: Take up God in your body. Enter his new bridal chambers, behold his foreign beauty, put him on, see him at the right hand of the Father, and rejoice that you have such a bridegroom. He will clothe you with blessing, lest any rent of sin should harm you.

25. Let us keep, then, the garment with which the Lord clothed us as we came up from the sacred font. Quickly the garment is torn if deeds do not match it; quickly it is worn away by the moths of the flesh and stained by the error of the old man. So here we are forbidden to join new things to old, and in the Apostle even to put old things on over new, but rather to put off the old, to take up the new, that we may be found stripped, not naked. For we are stripped in order to take up better things, but we are made naked when the covering is pulled off us by another's fraud, not laid aside by our own will.

26. And no one puts new wine into old wineskins. The frailty of the human condition is laid open, when our bodies are compared to the cast-off skins of dead animals. And would that we could fulfil the use of good wineskins, so as to preserve the sacrament we have received! Diligence relieves the injury, if new wines are entrusted to renewed skins. And therefore we ought always to keep these skins full; for when empty moth and rust soon consume them, while grace preserves them filled.

27. Beautifully do such precepts suit this work; for it is the sixth work, in which Levi is figured in a certain new form. And on the sixth day man was made; by the sixth work of Christ, then, is reformed a creature no longer old but new, a certain foreign form. And therefore, as a new creature, he holds a banquet for Christ, because both Christ delighted in him and he deserved to have a share of delight with Christ. To shape him, then, the Lord gives precepts; for he was already following, glad, eager and exulting, saying: Now I no longer play the tax collector, now I no longer bear Levi. I have put off Levi, since I put on Christ. I hate my stock, I flee my life; you alone I follow, Lord Jesus, who heal my wounds. Who indeed separates me from the love of God, which is in you? Tribulation, or distress, or famine? I am bound by faith as by certain nails, and I am tied with the good fetters of love. Every commandment of yours I will hold like a cautery pressed in. Though the cautery of the commandment burns, yet it burns out what is putrid in the flesh, lest the contagion creep to the living flesh. Though the medicine stings, yet it takes away the vice of the sore. Take away then, Lord Jesus, with your mighty sword the putrefaction of my sins. While you hold me bound with the bonds of love, cut whatever is diseased. Come quickly, cutting into the hidden and lurking passions of every kind; open the wound, lest the harmful humour creep on. Cleanse all that is foul with a foreign washing. Hear me, men of earth, who carry thoughts made drunk by your sins. I too, Levi, was wounded by such passions. I have found a physician who dwells in heaven and scatters medicines on earth. He alone can heal my wounds, who knows none of his own; he can take away the pain of the heart, the pallor of the soul, who knows hidden things.

And it came to pass on the second-first sabbath, as he was passing through the sown fields, his disciples were plucking ears of grain and eating, rubbing them in their hands.

28. Not only by the comprehension of words but also by the very practice and appearance of deeds, the Lord Jesus begins to strip man of the observance of the old Law and to clothe him with the new garment of grace. So he now leads him through the sown fields on the sabbath, that is, brings him to fruitful things. What the sabbath means, what the crop, what the ears, is no small mystery. For the field is this whole world; the crop of the field is the numerous fruitfulness of the saints in the sowing of the human race; the ears are the fruits of the Church, which the apostles, threshing them out by their works, fed upon, nourishing themselves on our progress.

29. There stood, then, already the crop fruitful in virtues, with its sprouting ears, to which the fruits of our merit are compared; for they likewise either wither in the rain or are scorched by the sun or are soaked by showers or are beaten down by storms or are stored by the reapers within the receptacles of happy barns. So the earth had already received the word of God, and the kindly field, sown with heavenly seed, had poured forth rich offspring. The disciples hungered for the salvation of men, and, as from the husks of bodies, drew out the fruits of minds to the light of faith by the glorious miracles of their works. But the Jews thought this was not lawful on the sabbath, whereas Christ, by the gift of new grace, was marking out the rest of the Law as the work of grace.

30. Nor do I think it without mystery that in this the evangelist, according to Matthew and Mark, set down the sabbaths without qualification, because the sabbaths are the perpetual holidays of eternal resurrection. So either in this world, while the superstitions of the Jews keep holiday and lie idle, or in the future, ourselves celebrating the holidays in perpetual solemnity, we shall eat the good things of the earth, according to what is written: They shall eat, but you shall hunger.

31. Wonderfully, however, according to Luke he spoke of a 'secundoprimum' [second-first], not a 'primosecundum' [first-second] sabbath — for δευτερόπρωτον [second-first] is what is written —; for that which excels ought to be put first. Second, because the first went before from the Law, in which a penalty too was prescribed if anyone worked; but first, because that sabbath of the Law, which was first, has been dissolved, and this has become first, which was established second. For since it is lawful to work on the sabbath and there is no penalty for one who works, surely not even the name clung to the sabbath of the Law, whose force has been dissolved; but yet, granted that that one was first in order, this one is first in gift, nor is this one the less because it is second. For Adam too was first, yet he is not to be compared with the second Adam; for the first Adam into a living soul, the last Adam into a life-giving spirit; and: The first man from the earth, earthly; the second man from heaven, heavenly. Surely the second was preferred to the first; for the one was the cause of death, this one of life. So also the sabbath is called second-first: second according to number, first according to the grace of its working; for better is the sabbath on which impunity is given than that on which a penalty is prescribed. The Law first, the gospel second; yet fear is inferior to grace. Or perhaps here too it is first in the predestination of the counsel and second in the sanction of the decree.

32. Well too does the Lord show in this place also a type of the future in the Law, and he convicts the defenders of the Law of not knowing what belongs to the Law, adducing the example that David, when he was hungry, he and his companions, entered into the house of God and took the loaves of proposition and ate and gave to those who were with him. A great and truly prophetic example, by which, first, not the empty things of the Law but the solid things of usefulness are set forth for us to follow; next, because in David fleeing with his companions from the face of King Saul, Christ was here prefigured in the Law, who with the apostles was hiding from the prince of the world.

33. But how did that observer and defender of the Law both eat the loaves himself and give them to those who were with him, loaves which it was not lawful to eat except for the priests only, unless to show by that figure that the priestly food would pass to the use of the peoples, either because we all ought to imitate the priestly life or because all the sons of the Church are priests? For we are anointed into a holy priesthood, offering ourselves to God as spiritual victims.

34. So the teaching of Christ now overflows the Law, and he does not destroy the Law but fulfils it, because he did not destroy the sabbath either. For if the sabbath was made for man, and the advantage of man required that hungry man, who had long been disinherited of the fruits of the earth, should escape the fasts of the old famine, surely the Law is not destroyed but fulfilled. How then is that charged as a crime against the Lord which in the servant is not held to be a crime?

35. But what is clearer than this figure, which runs through the whole history? David entered the house of Abimelech the priest; but even with the danger of death set before him, the mind of the holy priest does not refuse the guest, does not turn away the exile. So great is the grace of hospitality, that we willingly transfer to ourselves the dangers of others.

36. But this is moral in the history and prophetic in the mystery: that not even when the destruction of future death was foreseen did the devout priests exclude that true David from the hospitality of their mind. And Christ is taught not only to be about to find hospitality in the house of each prophet, but also, by a figure, to be about to take the spoils and weapons of spiritual wickedness; for he who receives Christ in hospitality despoils that intelligible Goliath of his own weapons.

37. But what is clearer than that in the house of Abimelech David asked for five loaves and received one, the type showing that now not by the five books but by the body of Christ food would be prepared for the faithful, so that Christ would take up a body, lest any of the faithful should hunger? Nor is the figure of Doeg empty, who was keeper of the mules, because no one else but the keeper of an unfruitful herd would fulfil the type of Judas the traitor.

38. Now the fact that, because of the reception of David, Saul persecuted the whole house of Abimelech except Abiathar, at that time chief of the priests, shows us by prophecy that no one can harm the true chief of the priests, who alone is Christ.

39. From here the Lord Jesus goes on to other things. For he who had resolved to make the whole man whole ran through the members one by one, so that he might truly say: Are you angry with me, because I made the whole man whole on the sabbath? So in this place he watered with the saving juices of good deeds that hand which Adam stretched out and plucked the fruits of the forbidden tree, that what had withered through crime might be healed by good works. In this Christ refutes the Jews, who by evil interpretations of the Law violated its precepts, thinking that on the sabbath one must rest even from good works, whereas the Law in present things prefigured the form of things to come, in which surely there will be a holiday from evil works, not from good ones. For although worldly works cease, yet to rest in the praise of God is not an idle act of good work.

40. You have heard, then, the words of the Lord saying: Stretch out your hand. That is a common and general medicine. And you who think you have a sound hand, beware lest it be contracted by avarice, beware lest by sacrilege. Stretch it out often, stretch it out to that poor man who beseeches you, stretch it out to help your neighbour, to bring protection to a widow, to snatch from wrong the man you see subjected to unjust insult; stretch it out to God for your sins. Thus the hand is stretched out, thus it is healed. Thus Jeroboam contracted his hand when he sacrificed with idols, and stretched it out when he besought God.

41. And it came to pass in those days, he went out onto the mountain to pray, and he was spending the night in the prayer of God. Not everyone who prays goes up the mountain — for there is a prayer which makes sin — but he who prays well, advancing from earthly things to higher, goes up to the summit of lofty care. But he does not go up the mountain who is anxious about the riches of the world or about honour; he does not go up the mountain who desires the right to another's estate. He goes up who seeks God, he goes up who asks the Lord's help for his course. All the great, all the lofty go up the mountain; for not to just anyone does the prophet say: Go up onto a high mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion; lift up your voice in strength, you who bring good tidings to Jerusalem. Not by bodily steps but by loftier deeds may you go up this mountain. Follow Christ, that you yourself may be a mountain; mountains indeed are round about him. Search in the gospel: you will find that only the disciples went up the mountain with the Lord.

42. The Lord prays, then, not to beseech for himself but to obtain for me; for although the Father has put all things in the power of the Son, yet the Son, to fulfil the form of man, judges that the Father must be besought for us, because he is our advocate. Do not open treacherous ears, so as to think that the Son asks as one weak, asks so that he may obtain what he, the author of power, could not fulfil. The master of obedience shapes us by his own example to the precepts of virtue. We have an advocate he says with the Father. If he is an advocate, he ought to intercede for my sins. Not then as one weak, but as one full of loving-kindness does he beseech. Do you wish to know how he can do all that he wills? He is both advocate and judge. In the one is the office of loving-kindness, in the other the mark of power.

43. And he was he says spending the night in the prayer of God. A pattern is given you, a form is prescribed which you ought to emulate. For what ought you to do for your own salvation, when Christ spends the night in prayer for you? What befits you to do when you wish to undertake some office of devotion, when Christ, about to send the apostles, prayed first and prayed alone?
