Nor is he found anywhere else, if I am not mistaken, to have prayed with the apostles: everywhere he beseeches alone; for human prayers do not grasp the counsel of God, nor can anyone be a sharer with Christ in his inner things. Do you wish to know how he prayed for me, not for himself?

44. He called he says his disciples and chose twelve of them, whom he would appoint as sowers of faith throughout the world, to spread the help of human salvation. At the same time note the heavenly counsel: not some wise men, not rich men, not nobles, but fishermen and tax collectors he chose to send, lest he should seem to have led some over to his grace by prudence, to have redeemed them by riches, to have drawn them by the authority of power and nobility; so that the reason of truth, not the charm of disputation, might prevail.

45. Judas too is chosen, not through imprudence but through providence. How great is truth, which not even a hostile minister weakens! How great the moral goodness of the Lord, who preferred that his judgment rather than his affection be endangered in our eyes! For he had taken on the frailty of man, and therefore he did not refuse even this part of human weakness. He willed to be deserted, he willed to be betrayed, he willed to be handed over by his own apostle, so that you, deserted by a companion, betrayed by a companion, may bear with moderation that your judgment has erred, that your kindness has been lost.

46. And he came down he says with them and stood on a place of level ground. Note everything carefully, how he both goes up with the apostles and comes down to the crowds; for how would the crowd see Christ except in a low place? It does not follow to the heights, it does not go up to lofty places. Indeed, where he comes down, he finds the sick; for on the heights there can be no sick. So too Matthew teaches that the weak were healed in the lower places; for each one is first healed, so that little by little, as his virtues advance, he may be able to go up to the mountain. And therefore he heals each one in the lower places, that is, he calls him back from lust, turns away the injury of blindness, comes down to our wounds, so that by a certain intercourse with himself and his abundance he may make us partakers of the heavenly nature. And he was healing these indeed, but leaving them in the lower places.

47. But seeing the crowds, as you have read, he went up the mountain. And when he had sat down, his disciples came up to him. For as he was about to preach the gospel and to bring forth from the treasure of divinity the oracles of the blessings, he begins to be on higher ground. Here, however, although he was standing in a low place, he yet lifted up his eyes. For so also, when he was raising Lazarus, he groaned in spirit; so also he lifted up his head when he forgave the adulterous woman her sins. For what is it to lift up the eyes but to open the inner light?

48. Indeed Saint Matthew: he opened he says his mouth, that is, the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God; with the inner shrines of his temple unbarred, he unlocked his mouth. So you too, open your mouth, but first implore that it may be opened. For if Paul implores help for the opening of his mouth, much more does it befit you to implore it. The prophet also shows you the key of knowledge with which you should open your mouth, saying: Open your mouth to the word of God. The word of God is the key of your mouth; the key of knowledge is the key of your mouth, by which, the chains of silence loosed, the bars of ignorance are unlocked.

49. Blessed are the poor in spirit, for yours is the kingdom of God. Blessed are those who now hunger and thirst, for they shall be filled. Blessed are you who now weep, for you shall laugh. Blessed shall you be when men hate you. Saint Luke set down only four beatitudes of the Lord, but Saint Matthew eight. But in those eight are these four, and in these four those eight. For the one embraced four as it were cardinal virtues; the other in those eight unlocked the mystical number. For many psalms are written for the eighth, and you receive the commandment to give a portion to those eight, perhaps the blessings; for as the eighth is the perfection of our hope, so the eighth is the sum of virtues.

50. But first let us look at the things that are fuller. Blessed he says are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. Both evangelists set this down as the first blessing. For it is first in order, and a kind of parent and begetting of the virtues, because he who has despised worldly things will himself merit eternal ones; nor can anyone attain the merit of the heavenly kingdom who, possessed by desire for the world, has no means of rising out of it.

51. The second blessing: blessed he says are the meek. The third: blessed are those who mourn. The fourth: blessed are those who hunger. The fifth: blessed are the merciful. The sixth: blessed are the pure in heart. The seventh: blessed are the peacemakers. And fittingly the seventh, since on that day God rested from all the works of the world; for it is the day of rest and of peace. The eighth: blessed are those who suffer persecution for righteousness' sake.

52. Come, Lord Jesus, teach us the order of your beatitudes; for not without order did you say first, blessed are the poor in spirit, second, blessed are the meek, and third, blessed are those who mourn. Even if I know something, yet I know in part. For if Paul knew in part, how much can I know, who am as inferior to Paul in word as in life? For life demands the word and acquires it, but speech without life is not the speech of God. How much wiser than I is Paul! He glories in dangers, I glory in successes; he glories because he is not lifted up by revelations, I, if any revelations came to me, would glory. But yet God can raise up men from stones and bring forth a word from what is shut, draw out a voice from the dumb. And if he opened the eyes of a she-ass so that she saw the angel, he is able to open our eyes too, so that we may see the mystery of God.

53. Blessed he says are the poor. Not all the poor are blessed; for poverty is a thing indifferent. The poor can be both good and bad, unless perhaps that poor man is to be understood as blessed whom the prophet described, saying that better is a righteous poor man than a lying rich man. Blessed is the poor man who cried, and the Lord heard him, poor in crime, poor in vices, the poor man in whom the prince of the world found nothing, the poor man who imitates that poor man who, though he was rich, became poor for our sake. Hence Matthew disclosed it fully, saying: Blessed are the poor in spirit; for the poor in spirit is not puffed up, is not lifted up by the mind of his flesh.

54. This, then, is the first blessing, when I have laid aside all sin and put off all malice and am content with simplicity, destitute of evils. It remains that I temper my character. For what does it profit me to lack worldly things unless I am meek and gentle? For he who follows the right way — he surely follows him who said: Learn of me, for I am meek and humble of heart. Lay aside, then, what is wicked, be in want of vices according to a good poverty, soften your disposition, so that you are not angry, or at least, if angry, do not sin, according to what is written: Be angry and do not sin. For it is a noble thing to temper an impulse by counsel, and it is held no lesser virtue to check anger and restrain indignation than not to be angry at all, since commonly that is reckoned the gentler, this the braver.

55. When you have done this, remember that you are a sinner, and mourn your sins, mourn your offences; and fittingly the third blessing is that of one who bewails his sins, because it is the Trinity which forgives sins. Wash yourself, then, with your tears, bathe yourself with weeping. If you weep for yourself, another will not weep for you; for if Saul had bewailed his sins, Samuel would not have bewailed him. Each one has his own dead to weep for. We are dead when we sin, when we are filled with dead men's bones. The evil word which comes out of the mouth is dead. For it comes out of an evil sepulchre; an open sepulchre indeed is their throat. Therefore the Apostle says: Be imitators of me. He wants us to remember our offences. Paul had nothing to mourn from the time when he believed in Christ, and yet he bewailed his former deeds, saying that I am not worthy to be called an apostle, because I persecuted the Church of God. He, then, was a sinner before faith, but we still sin after faith. Let him who is a sinner bewail himself and accuse himself, that he may become righteous; the righteous man indeed is the accuser of himself.

56. Let us follow out the order, then, because it is written: Set love in order in me. I have laid aside sin, I have tempered my character, I have bewailed my offences; I begin to hunger, and to hunger for righteousness; for a sick man, when he is in grave illness, does not hunger, because the pain of the sickness shuts out hunger. But what is this hunger for righteousness? What are those loaves for which the righteous man hungers? Are they perhaps those loaves of which it is said: I have been young and have grown old, and I have not seen the righteous forsaken, nor his seed seeking bread? He who hungers surely seeks an increase of strength. But what greater increase of virtue is there than the rule of righteousness?

57. After these, blessed he says are the merciful; for mercy follows righteousness. Hence it is said: He has scattered, he has given to the poor, his righteousness remains for ever. But even he who bestows mercy loses his reward unless he shows mercy with a pure heart; for if he seeks boasting, there is no fruit. Cleanse, then, the inner parts of your mind, and, if you have carefully cleansed the secret places of your breast, have compassion on those who are assailed, and understand how many men, how many of your brothers, seek your help.

58. But unless you first empty your inner parts of every stain of sin, so that dissensions and quarrels do not proceed from your disposition, you cannot bring medicine to others. Begin peace, then, from yourself, so that, when you are yourself a peacemaker, you may bring peace to others. For how can you cleanse the hearts of others unless you have first cleansed your own?

59. You have profited others, then, you have brought help to many: hasten. Press on to the end. Though there were many ways of departing from life, one befitted the Lord; for what had been born according to the flesh had also to die according to the flesh. He chose the Passion, to die for us; and do you say, concerning all that he has rendered to you: I will take the saving cup and call upon the name of the Lord, that is, the passion. Hence also to those who desired to sit at his right hand or his left he says: Can you drink the cup which I am about to drink? He leads you to the end, he accompanies you to martyrdom, and appoints the palm of the beatitudes.

60. See, then, the order. You must become poor in spirit; for humility of spirit is the riches of the virtues. Unless you are poor, you will not be able to be meek. He who is meek can mourn present things; he who mourns lower things can desire better things; he who seeks higher things avoids lower things, so that he too may be helped by those above; he who shows mercy cleanses his own heart. For what is it to cleanse the soul but to abolish the filth of death? Almsgiving indeed delivers from death, while patience belongs to perfect love. And he who suffers persecution, set in the final contest, is proved by adversities, so that, when he has striven lawfully, he may be crowned. Some hold these to be steps of the virtues, by which we may ascend from the lowest to the higher.

61. Indeed, as there are increases of the virtues, so also are there increases of the rewards; for it is more to be a son of God than to possess the earth and to merit consolation. But since the first reward is the kingdom of heaven and the last reward is the kingdom of heaven, is the reward equal for beginners and for the perfect? Are we not perhaps taught in the mystical sense that the first kingdom of heaven is that of the Apostle: to be dissolved and to be with Christ? You have the first kingdom when the saints are caught up in the clouds to meet Christ in the air; many indeed of those who sleep shall rise, these to eternal life, those to reproach. The first kingdom of heaven, then, is set before the saints in the release from the body; the second kingdom of heaven is to be with Christ after the resurrection. When you are in the kingdom of heaven, then there is the advance through the mansions. Though the kingdom is one, yet there are diverse merits in the kingdom of heaven. After the resurrection you will begin to possess your earth, released from death. For he to whom it is said: Earth you are and into earth you shall go does not possess his own earth; for he cannot be a possessor who does not take the fruit. Released, then, through the Lord's cross, if only you are found within the yoke of the Lord, you will find consolation in the possession itself. Delight follows consolation, divine compassion follows delight. And him on whom the Lord has compassion he also calls. He who is called sees him who calls; he who has seen God is taken up into the right of divine generation. Then at last, as a son of God, he delights in the riches of the heavenly kingdom. The one, then, begins, the other is filled. For within this world too many are in the Roman Empire, but those who are nearer to the emperor obtain greater favour in the empire.

62. Now let us say how Saint Luke has embraced eight blessings in four. And indeed we know that there are four cardinal virtues: temperance, justice, prudence, fortitude. He who is poor in spirit is not avaricious; he who weeps is not proud, but is meek and peaceable; he who mourns is humbled; he who is just does not deny what he knows was given to all in common for their use; he who shows mercy bestows of his own; he who gives his own does not seek another's, nor lay a snare for his neighbour.

63. The virtues, then, are joined to one another and linked together, so that he who has one is seen to have several; and one virtue befits the saints, but the reward of the virtue that was the more abundant is the more abundant. How great the hospitality in Abraham, how great the humility, how great the holiness, that he saved his brother's son from the enemy, and how great the abstinence, that he sought nothing from the spoil! But because he excelled in faith, he merited the primacy of faith above the rest. So each has several rewards, because he has several incentives of virtues, but what is more copious in any merit is also more overflowing in the reward.

64. Blessed then are the poor in spirit. You have temperance, which abstains from sin, tramples on the world, does not seek what allures.

65. Blessed are those who hunger and thirst; for he who hungers has compassion on the hungry, by having compassion he bestows, by bestowing he becomes righteous, because his righteousness remains for ever. Hence in Matthew we understand a spiritual thirst and hunger, by which the food or the drink of righteousness is desired, because virtue is as it were a kind of material of the virtues, so that the righteous man shows himself equal to his inferiors, shuts out deceit, seeks truth.

66. Blessed are you who now weep, for you shall laugh. You have prudence, whose part it is to weep for perishing things and to seek those that are eternal, to mourn worldly things which fight against themselves, to seek the God of peace, who chose the foolish things of the world to confound the wise, and who destroys the things that are not, that he may obtain the things that are.

67. Blessed shall you be when men hate you. You have fortitude, but that which does not merit hatred for a crime, but suffers persecution for faith; for thus one comes to the crown of the passion, if you disregard the favour of men and follow the divine. Indeed, that you may know that the passion is the consummation of fortitude, in the same way he says their fathers did to the prophets, because the Jews persecuted the prophets even to the death of the body. It belongs also to fortitude to conquer anger and restrain indignation, and thereby fortitude strengthens mind and body alike and does not allow them to be disturbed by any fear or grief, by which, as by depraved interpreters, we are mostly struck down.

68. So temperance has purity of heart and of mind, justice has mercy, prudence peace, fortitude meekness.

69. Woe to you rich, who have your consolation! Although in an abundance of money there are many allurements to offences, yet there are also many incentives to virtues. Although virtue requires no aids, and the contribution of the poor man is more commendable than the liberality of the rich, yet by the authority of the heavenly sentence he condemns not those who have riches, but those who do not know how to use them. For as that poor man is more praiseworthy who bestows with a ready disposition and is not held back by the bars of threatening want and does not think himself needy since he has what is enough for nature, so this rich man is the more guilty, who ought at least to have returned thanks to God for what he received, and not to hide away unused the wealth given for common use and to brood over treasures buried in the earth. Not wealth, then, but the disposition is at fault. And although the avaricious mind keeps anxious watch all its life over a pitiable custody — and no punishment is heavier than this —, guarding with anxious fear what will profit the gains of successors, yet, since the pursuits of avarice and the desires of heaping up are fed by a certain empty pleasure, those who have had the consolation of the present life have lost the reward of the everlasting.

70. Here, however, we can understand by the rich the people of the Jews, or the heretics, or certainly the philosophers of the world, who, delighted with an abundance of words and a certain patrimony of ambitious eloquence, overstepping the simplicity of true faith, have laid up useless treasures. Does not some heretic seem so to you, when you hear him disputing about the generation of the Lord according to the usage of the world, rich in words, poor in strength? He thinks he has abundant riches at this time, but in the future he will recognize the poverty of his faith, and, wasted away by the eternal fast of faith, he will know that the food of unbelief which he belched forth in the present age is the cause of so great a punishment. And the time will come when those who now laugh at our words will mourn their laughter. To them it is well said: Woe, when all men speak well of you!

71. Does he not seem to you to say this to those who lately at the Council of Ariminum, authors of an unbelieving betrayal, while they followed the favour of the emperor, lost the grace of God? While they hunt for ways to please the powerful, they have subjected themselves to a perpetual curse.

72. So Saint Matthew called the peoples to virtue and faith by rewards; this one also deterred them from crimes and sins by the announcement of future punishments, he who, advancing not idly through the enumeration of many heavenly deeds, came later to the place of the blessings, that he might teach the peoples, strengthened by the miracles of divine things, to advance beyond the path of the Law in the footsteps of the virtues. There caution may have been needed, where the hearts of a still weak people were wavering; here virtue had to be roused by the blast of the trumpet. This is taught by the discourse of this book, which is laid open in its prefaces and in the progress of its speech. There those who are weak are still given to drink a kind of milk of the Law, and therefore they are led along the path of the Law to grace — they hear the things that are of the Law, so that by following the Law they may advance beyond the Law —; here the Church, more firmly founded, is not nourished with milk but eats food; for the stronger food is love. Indeed among the three greatest, hope, faith, love, the greater is love.

73. Love, then, is set in order when it is said: Love your enemies, so that that word of the Church which has already been spoken before may be fulfilled: Set love in order in me; for love is set in order when the precepts of love are formed. See how he began from the higher things and cast the Law behind the back of the gospel blessing. The Law commands the requital of vengeance; the gospel bestows love for enmities, kindness for hatreds, prayers for curses, help for persecutors, patience for the hungry, and the grace of reward. How much more perfect is the athlete who does not feel the injury!

74. And lest the Lord should seem to destroy the Law, he keeps requital in benefits, which he disregards in injuries. But yet, by saying: As you wish that men should do to you, do likewise to them, the requital itself is more abundant, when the deed is made equal to the wishes. For virtue knows no measure in gratitude, and, not content to return what it has received, it wishes to heap up what it has taken, lest it be inferior in the benefit, though it be equal in the duty. For benefits are weighed not only by their heap but by order and time, since in an equal benefit he is first who began first; for he is the benefactor who began the kindness, he the debtor who returned it. So the precedence of a benefit is a further benefit; for if someone has returned money, he has not discharged the gratitude, and he remains a debtor of gratitude, even if he is no longer a debtor of the money. Why should we think that gratitude can be emptied by returning it, when its return is a testimony of gratitude received rather than of gratitude paid off?

75. So the Christian is instructed by a good example, so that, not content with the law of nature, he seeks its grace. For if it is common to all, even to sinners, to love in return, he whose kind of profession is loftier ought also to have a richer zeal for virtue, so as to love even those who do not love. For even if the lack of any merits for loving excludes the practice of love, yet it will not have excluded the practice of virtue. For as in the case of one who loves you it is shameful not to return gratitude, and from love of returning gratitude love grows in you for him whom you did not love before, so too in the case of one who does not love you, you ought to love virtue, so that, while you love virtue, you may begin to love him whom you did not love, since the recompense of one who loves is rare and perishable, that of virtue everlasting.

76. But what is so wonderful as to offer the other cheek to the one who strikes? Is not every impulse of the indignant man broken, his anger calmed? Does it not come about through patience that you strike back the striker more fully by his own repentance? So it will be that you both repel the injury and seek favour. And often the greatest causes of love arise, when patience is returned for insolence, kindness for injury. Indeed, as I remember hearing, we think that the haughty brow of philosophy is pressed down by this alone, that it seems to itself to have divided justice into three parts: one toward God, which is called devotion, another toward parents or the rest of the human race, the third toward the dead, so that the due rites of burial may be paid to them. But the Lord Jesus, going beyond the oracle of the Law and the summit of philosophy, extended the office of devotion even to those who have done us harm. For if an enemy who has fought with you with war and arms, on laying down his arms, obtains mercy for his life, and this is commonly granted either from regard for nature or by the very law of war, that life be given to the subdued, how much more must it be granted from the better regard of religion! For since the fact that his own life was assailed does not move the warrior, what ought it to do to the soldier of peace?

77. So that saying of the Apostle, that love is patient, is kind, is not envious, is not puffed up, we see brought to completion in these precepts. If it is patient, it owes patience to the striker; if it is kind, it ought not to answer curses; if it does not seek its own, it ought not to resist the plunderer; if it is not envious, it ought not to hate an enemy. And yet the precepts of divine devotion overflow those of the Apostle; for it is more to give than to yield, it is more to love enemies than not to be envious. All of which the Lord both said and did. Who, when he was cursed, did not curse in return; when he was struck, he did not strike back; when he was stripped, he did not resist; when he was crucified, he asked pardon for his very persecutors, saying: Father, forgive them their sin, for they know not what they do. He was excusing his accusers from the charge.

78. They were preparing the cross; he was repaying salvation and grace. And yet, since the very pursuits of the virtues grow sluggish without reward, he both brought us an example and pledged a reward from heaven, promising that those who were his imitators would be sons of God. For he who hastens to the reward ought not to disdain the example, because the more excellent the reward, the more earnest the duty. And how great is the reward of mercy, which is taken up into the right of divine adoption! Follow mercy, then, that you may merit grace.

79. Wide extends the kindness of God; he rains upon the ungrateful, the fruitful earth does not deny its produce to the wicked, the same sun of the world lights the sacrilegious and the religious alike. Or, to weigh these things in the mystical sense, the Lord watered the people of the Jews with prophetic rains and shone upon them with the rays of the eternal sun, even though they did not deserve it; but since they were soaked with worldly dew, the Church of God is taken up to the heavenly light, yet in such a way that for them too, if they believe, the prerogative of mercy is kept.

80. He added that one must not judge rashly, lest, while you are yourself conscious of your own offence, you be forced to pass sentence on another.

81. It is a great discipline of virtue too, that you should not seek fruitful things from the unfruitful, nor expect the produce of fertility from the untilled. For to each its own cultivation gives fruits: in the thorns of this world that fig cannot be found which, because it is better in its fruitful fruits, is fittingly given the likeness of the resurrection, either because, as you have read, the fig trees have put forth their green figs, since the unripe and useless and falling fruit in the synagogue went before, or because our life is unripe in the body, ripe in the resurrection. And therefore we ought to renounce, far from us, worldly cares, which gnaw the soul and scorch the mind, so that we may obtain the ripe fruits of diligent cultivation. This we cannot find in the untilled places of this world, because they do not gather figs from thorns, nor do they harvest the grape from thistles. The one refers to the world and the resurrection, the other to the soul and the body: either because no one gains by sins fruit for his soul, which, like a grape, is spoiled near the earth and ripens on higher ground, or because no one can escape the condemnation of the flesh except him whom Christ has redeemed, who hung like a grape on the tree. Far, then, from that flesh which was commanded to bring forth thistles for condemned man, let us lift up our spiritual eyes, let us stretch out our hands, so that we may harvest Christ.

82. And he teaches that the foundation of all the virtues is obedience to the heavenly precepts, through which this house of ours cannot be shaken, neither by the flood of pleasures, nor by the onset of spiritual wickedness, nor by the rain of the world, nor by the cloudy disputations of the heretics.

83. Beautifully, when he has completed the precepts, he teaches us to carry out the form of his precepts; for immediately the servant of a Gentile centurion is presented to the Lord to be cured, in whom the people of the nations, which was held by the bonds of worldly slavery, sick with deadly passions, is portrayed as about to be healed by the Lord's kindness. And in saying that he was about to die the evangelist did not deceive; for he was about to die, had he not been healed by Christ. He fulfilled the precept, then, with heavenly love, he who so loved enemies as to snatch them from death and take them up into the hope of everlasting salvation.

84. How great a mark of divine humility, that the Lord of heaven did not at all disdain to visit a centurion's little slave! Faith shines in works, but humanity works more in affections. This he certainly did not do because he could not cure him while absent, but to give you a form of humility to imitate, by which he taught that deference is due alike to the humbler and to the higher. Indeed elsewhere he says to the nobleman: Go; your son lives, that you may know both the power of divinity and the grace of humility. There he would not go, lest in the nobleman's son he should seem to have deferred rather to riches; here he went himself, lest in the centurion's servant he should seem to have despised the condition of a slave; for we are all, slave and free, one in Christ.

85. But see that faith is the prerogative of medicine. Note also that in the Gentile people itself there is discernment of the mystery. The Lord goes on; the centurion excuses himself, and, laying aside military pride, takes up reverence, both easy to faith and ready to do honour. And well does Luke say that friends too were sent by the centurion to meet him, lest by his own presence he should seem to burden the Lord's modesty and to have provoked courtesy with courtesy. So much in the moral sense.

86. But in the mystical sense the people of the nations desires that he whom the people of the Jews crucified should remain inviolate from injury, and, as regards faith, it believed the word, that is, inferring that health is given to men by Christ not by the power of a man but by that of God. But as regards the mystery, it saw that Christ could not yet enter into the breasts of the Gentiles. And therefore he who had not yet washed away the stains of his past thinking thought himself burdened rather than helped by the Lord's condescension. So the widow of Zarephath judged herself unworthy of the prophet's hospitality. And therefore the Lord in this one man sets forth the faith of the Gentiles.

87. And indeed, if you read thus: In no one have I found such faith in Israel, the meaning is simple and easy; but if according to the Greeks: Not even in Israel have I found such faith, this man's faith is preferred even to those more elect and to those who see God.

88. But see the economy: the faith of the master is approved, and the health of the servant is strengthened. So the merit of the master can avail even for his servants, not only by the merit of faith but also by zeal for discipline. Behold also another economy of the Lord's humility. What he does not promise, he performs; for although he had not yet commanded the healing, yet the servants who had been sent found the little slave healthy.

But when he drew near to the gate of the city, behold, a dead man was being carried out, the only son of his mother, and she was a widow, and a great crowd with her. And when the Lord saw her, moved with mercy he said to her: Do not weep. And he came near and touched the bier.

89. This place too overflows toward both graces, so that we both believe that the divine mercy is quickly bent by the lamentation of a widowed mother, especially of one who is broken either by the suffering or by the death of an only son, though the crowd at the funeral wins for her the merit of a widow's dignity; and that this widow, surrounded by a crowd of peoples, seems to be more than a woman, she who by her tears merited to obtain the resurrection of her only son, a young man, because holy Church, in regard for her tears, calls back to life from the funeral procession and the last rites of the grave the younger people, she who is forbidden to weep for him to whom resurrection is due.

90. This dead man, indeed, was being carried to the tomb in a coffin by the four material elements, but he had the hope of rising again, because he was carried on wood. And although this had not profited us before, yet after Jesus touched it, it began to avail for life, so that it was a sign that salvation would be poured back upon the people through the gibbet of the cross. So, when the word of God was heard, those bitter bearers of the funeral stood still, who were pressing on the human body with the deadly flux of material nature. For what else is it but that we lie lifeless as on a kind of bier, that is, the instrument of the last funeral, when either the fire of immoderate desire boils up, or cold humour overflows, or the vigour of souls is dulled by some sluggish habit of body, or our spirit, empty of pure light, nourishes the mind with a clotted stain? These are the bearers of our funeral.

91. But although the last extremities of death have abolished all hope of life, and the bodies of the dead lie near the tomb, yet at the word of God corpses already on the point of perishing rise again, the voice returns, the son is given back to his mother, he is called back from the tomb, snatched from the sepulchre. What is this tomb of yours but evil morals? Your tomb is unbelief, your sepulchre is your throat; an open sepulchre indeed is their throat, from which dead words are brought forth. From this sepulchre Christ frees you; from this tomb you will rise again, if you hear the word of God.

92. Even if there is a grave sin which you cannot yourself wash away with the tears of your repentance, let mother Church weep for you, who intercedes for each one as a widowed mother for her only sons; for she suffers with them by a certain spiritual pain of nature when she sees her children driven to death by deadly vices. We are inward parts of her inward parts; for there are also spiritual inward parts, which Paul has, saying: Yes, brother, may I enjoy you in the Lord: refresh my inward parts in Christ. We, then, are the inward parts of the Church, since we are members of her body, of her flesh and of her bones. Let the loving mother grieve, then; let the crowd too stand by — not only a crowd, but a great one — let it suffer with the good parent. Now you will rise again at your funeral, now you will be freed from the sepulchre; those ministers of your funeral will stand still, you will begin to speak words of life, all will fear; for by the example of one very many are corrected. They will praise God too, who has granted us such great remedies for escaping death.

And John called two of his disciples and sent them to him, saying: Are you he who is to come, or do we look for another?

93. The meaning in these simple words is not simple; otherwise what came earlier conflicts with the present. For how is it that John here does not know him whom earlier he recognized when God the Father pointed him out? How did he there acknowledge him whom before he did not know, and here not know him whom he already knew? I did not know him he says, but he who sent me to baptize, he said to me: He upon whom you see the Holy Spirit descending from heaven. And he believed the word, and acknowledged him when he was pointed out, and adored him when he was baptized, and prophesied him as he came. Indeed I he says saw and bore witness that this is the chosen one of God. How then could it be that so great a prophet should so err as still not to believe that he of whom he had said: Behold him who takes away the sins of the world was the Son of God? For either it is insolence to ascribe divine things to one whom you do not know, or it is unbelief to have doubted about the Son of God. So the suspicion of so great an error does not fall upon such a prophet.

94. So, if the form of the simple meaning conflicts, let us seek a spiritual figure. And since we said above that in John there is a type of the Law, which was the herald of Christ, rightly the Law, which was held bodily shut up in the breasts of the unbelieving as in prisons emptied of eternal light, which the womb fruitful in punishments and the doors of madness confined, cannot carry through the full outcome of its testimony to the Lord's dispensation without the endorsement of the gospel.

The Law indeed prophesied in Exodus the grace of baptism through the cloud and the sea, foretold spiritual food in the lamb, signified the perennial fountain in the rock; it revealed the remission of sins in Leviticus, announced the kingdom of heaven in the psalms, most clearly declared the land of promise in Joshua son of Nun — all these things agree also with the testimony of John —

95. but yet it is held shut up by the tyrannical powers of this world, so that it may not pour forth the light of the Lord's resurrection. So John sends his disciples to Christ, that they may obtain the supplement of knowledge, because Christ is the fullness of the Law; so that, since words without deeds commonly waver, and faith is shown more fully by the testimony of deeds than by the promises of words, the faith which then wavered in the hearts of the Jews, as though the Law were locked up, might be laid open by the very spectacle of the Lord's cross and by the full testimony of the resurrection.

96. And perhaps these disciples are the two peoples, one of which believed from the Jews, the other from the Gentiles, which believed because it heard. So these wished to see, because of that word: But blessed are your eyes which see, and your ears which hear. But we too have seen in John, we have looked upon him with our eyes in the apostles, and we have searched with our hands in the fingers of Thomas; that indeed which was from the beginning, which we have heard and which we have seen, we have looked upon with our eyes, and our hands have searched, concerning the word of life: and the life appeared. When did it appear? When we saw. It did not so appear before it was seen. Thanks then to the Lord, who was crucified for our faith, was crucified for our lusts. My mind is crucified in him.

97. So now too those who review the Old Testament before they know the gospel, and read as it were certain footprints of the Lord's body, think that he is to come, and ask whether he himself is the Christ, the Son of God, who is to come. And when they read of when he spoke with Abraham, or when he showed himself as captain of the heavenly host, they surely say: Are you he who is to come, or do we look for another? But when they have come to the gospel and learned that the blind are given light, the lame walk, the deaf have heard, the lepers have been cleansed, the dead have risen, then they say: We have seen him and have looked upon him with our eyes, and we have put our fingers into the prints of his nails. For we seem to ourselves to have seen him of whom we read, to have beheld him hanging, and to have touched his wounds as the spirit of the Church searches them; for if demons are cast out by the finger of God, faith too is found by the finger of the Church. Or perhaps in a certain working part of our body we all seem to have traced the course of the Lord's Passion; for faith comes through the few to the many. The Law, then, announces that Christ will come; the scripture of the gospel confirms that he has come.

98. Some also understand it thus of John himself: that he was indeed so great a prophet as to recognize Christ and to announce the remission of sins to come, but yet, not as one in doubt but as a devoted prophet, he did not believe that he whom he had believed was to come was to die. He doubted, then, not in faith but in devotion. Peter too doubted, saying: Be merciful to yourself, Lord! This shall not be. He, the prince of faith, to whom Christ had not yet called himself the Son of God, and yet he had believed, did not believe even Christ about the death of Christ. It is a slip of devoted affection, not of lack of devotion. Indeed elsewhere he refuses to have his feet washed, where he does not recognize the mystery, while he is burdened by the Lord's service. So not even the saints believed that Christ would die; what indeed eye has not seen nor ear heard nor has entered into the heart of man, these things God has prepared for those who love him. So in the religious a slip of love does not hinder faith.

99. Indeed the Lord, knowing that no one can fully believe without the gospel, because as faith begins from the Old Testament so it is completed in the New, when he was asked about himself, showed who he was not by any word, but by deeds. Go he says tell John what you have seen and heard. The blind see, the lame walk, the deaf hear, the lepers are cleansed, the dead rise, the poor have the gospel preached to them.

100. A full testimony, surely, by which the prophet might recognize the Lord; for of him and not of another it had been prophesied that the Lord gives food to the hungry, the Lord raises up those dashed down, the Lord looses the fettered, the Lord enlightens the blind, the Lord loves the righteous, the Lord keeps the strangers, he will receive the orphan and the widow and will destroy the way of sinners. He who will do these things, he says: the Lord shall reign for ever. So these are the marks not of human but of divine power: to open to the blind the darkness of perpetual night and to heal with infused light the wounds of sunken eyes, to bring hearing into the ears of the deaf, to restore loosened limbs whose joints have slackened, and also to call back the dead into the light, pouring back the vigour of life.

101. These things before the gospel were either rare or none. Granted that Tobias received his eyes: he is a single example, and yet that medicine was an angel's, not a man's. Granted that Elijah raised a dead man: yet he prayed and wept, this one commanded. Granted that Elisha caused a leper to be cleansed: yet there it was not the authority of the command that availed, but the figure of the mystery. Granted that the meal, multiplying itself at the prophet's commands, did not fail for the food of the hungry widow: yet that meal kept one widow, or rather the figure of the sacrament, foreshadowed, kept that same widow too. But yet these are still small examples of the Lord's testimony: the fullness of faith is the Lord's cross, his death, his burial. And therefore, when he had said those earlier things, he added: Blessed he says is he who shall not be scandalized in me.

102. For the cross could bring scandal even to the elect, but there is no greater testimony than this to the divine person. There is nothing that seems more to be beyond human things than that one should have offered himself for the whole world; by this alone the Lord is fully declared. Indeed so he was designated by John: Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world. But this answer is given not to those two men, John's disciples, but to us all, that we may so believe in Christ, if the deeds agree. For one will come who will usurp this name for himself, whom, though you cannot distinguish him by the appellation of the name, you may yet discern by consideration of his deeds.

103. What did you go out into the desert to see? A reed shaken by the wind? When he had forewarned John's disciples that one must believe in the Lord's cross, as they were going back he turned to the crowds and began to call the poor to virtue, lest, exalted in heart, unstable in mind, weak in counsel, they should prefer the showy to the useful, the perishable to the eternal; but rather that with humble spirit they should take up the cross rather than lift up the trappings of this world, and, as blessed poor who had nothing of the world to lose, should willingly exchange the life of the body for immortal glory. Not idly, then, is the person of Saint John praised here, who, setting aside the love of life, did not change the form of righteousness even for terror of death.

104. What he says did you go out into the desert to see? This world seems to be compared to a desert, still untilled, still barren, still unfruitful, into which the Lord forbids us to go forth in such a way as to think that men puffed up in the mind of the flesh and empty of inner virtue, boasting themselves of the frail loftiness of worldly glory, are to be imitated by us as a kind of exemplar and image: men whom a fickle life disturbs, exposed to the storms of this world, rightly to be compared to a reed, in whom there is no fruit of solid righteousness, who, tufted with the trappings of the forum, covered with knots, resounding with empty noise, of no use in themselves, giving frequent offence too, pursue what is empty within and showy without. We are reeds, founded on no root of a stronger nature, and if a light breeze of more prosperous success has breathed upon us, with wandering motion we strike our neighbours, helpless to support, ready to harm. Reeds love rivers, and the gliding, perishable things of the world delight us.

105. Yet if anyone plucks this reed from the plantings of the earth and strips it of superfluities, stripping himself of the old man with his deeds, and tempers it with the hand of a scribe who writes swiftly, it begins to be not a reed but a pen, to imprint the precepts of the heavenly scriptures on the inner chambers of the mind, to write them on the tablets of the heart. Of this pen you have it said: My tongue is the pen of a scribe who writes swiftly, which others wish to refer to Christ. In one place, then, are read the Word, the pen and the scribe: the Word, because he proceeded from the begetting secret of the Father — my heart has brought forth a good word —; the pen, because the flesh of Christ expressed the sequence of the Father's will and fulfilled the commands of the divine tongue by the shedding of the sacred blood; the scribe, because with his pen, by a certain indivisible distinction of the New and the Old Testament, or of divinity and flesh, he revealed to us the mysteries of the Father's disposition.

106. Imitate this pen by the tempering of your flesh; dip this pen of yours, that is, your flesh, not in ink but in the Spirit of the living God, that what you write may be eternal. With such a pen Paul wrote that letter of which he says: You are our letter, written not with ink but with the Spirit of the living God. Dip your flesh in the blood of Christ, as it is written: that your foot may be dipped in blood. And so do you too dip the footstep of your soul and the gait of your mind in undoubting confession of the Lord's cross. You dip your flesh in the blood of Christ if you wash away vices, wipe away sins, carry about the death of Christ in your flesh, as the Apostle taught, saying: carrying about the mortification of Jesus Christ in our body. Do not bend down to earthly things, lest you break your reed. And therefore it was prophesied of Christ, because he was not to be bent down to earthly things: A bruised reed he will not break, since the flesh which sins had bruised he made solid by the power of the resurrection. A good reed is the flesh of Christ, which fixed the head of the serpent devil and the allurements of worldly desire to the gibbet of the cross.

107. But what did you go out into the desert to see? A reed shaken by the wind? What did you go out to see? A man clothed in soft garments? Here the Lord's word is not about clothes, although care for softer clothing makes very many effeminate, who, as if they could not bear the weight of wool, sweep silk garments along the ground over their hidden feet, and by habit bring it about that their covering is a burden. But yet he seems here to signify other garments, and, if I am not mistaken, human bodies, with which our soul is clothed. Indeed Joseph's tunic too was bloodied as a figure of the Lord's body; and that saying of the Apostle: stripping off from himself the flesh, he made a show of all principalities and powers, what else does it show but that the body was in place of a garment, which the Lord so put off in the Passion that the divinity remained free and immune from injury? This whole passage, then, by a prophetic example exhorts us to the virtue of undergoing the passion.

108. Indeed he added: Behold, those who are in costly clothing are in the houses of kings. Soft garments are also luxurious actions and morals, on account of which the Apostle exhorts us that, stripping off the old man with his deeds, we should put on the new, in whom there is not sweet allurement and the play of wantonness, but the practice and fruit of labour, because the heavenly court by no means receives those softened by tender care of the body and by luxury and the desire of wantonness; to it one ascends by the hard steps of laborious virtue. But those whose limbs, made fluid by pleasures, are relaxed, exiles from the heavenly kingdom, grow old within the dwellings of this world, and the rulers of this world and of darkness — for these are the kings who rule by a certain worldly power — have received them as imitators of their own works.

109. But what did you go out to see? A prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet is this one. How then did they desire to see John in the desert, who was shut up in prison? Him the Lord sets before us to imitate, who prepared the way for the Lord not only by the order of birth according to the flesh and by the message of faith, but also by a kind of going before in a glorious passion. A greater prophet indeed, in whom is the end of the prophets. A greater prophet, because many desired to see him whom this one prophesied, whom this one beheld, whom this one baptized.

110. But yet is he greater even than that one of whom Moses said that The Lord our God will raise up a prophet for you, of whom he said: And it shall be: every soul which does not hear that prophet shall be destroyed from the people? If then Christ is a prophet, how is this one greater than all? Do we deny that Christ is a prophet? On the contrary, I assert that the Lord of the prophets is also a prophet, and I say that this one is greater than all, but among those born of a woman, not of a virgin. For he was greater than those to whom he could be equal by the lot of birth. That other nature is different and is not to be compared with human generations. There can be no comparison of man with God; for each is preferred to his own kind. Indeed, so far could there be no comparison of John with the Son of God that he is reckoned even below the angels.

111. For he who is less he says in the kingdom of heaven is greater than he. For since he had called him an angel, he is rightly preferred to men; and since he had declared him the greater among those born of women, therefore he added: for he who is less in the kingdom of heaven is greater than he, that he might know that he must yield to the angels. And rightly is John preferred to his equals, from whose days the kingdom of heaven is pressed. This expression seems somewhat obscure, and therefore we thought that it should be drawn from another book of the gospel. For if we follow the letter, surely the inferior is pressed by the stronger, but the kingdom of heaven excels human things. But since there are some things which are said to be thickened by pressing, it is not absurd that the kingdom is pressed, when it is thronged by many.

112. And those who press plunder it. If we recall that word of the Lord which is written, when the Son of God says: The kingdom of God is within you, we observe that the heavenly kingdom is made solid in us when Christ, the kingdom of the worldly prince having been driven out and worldly delights put to flight, reigns within the secret places of our souls. Force, then, is brought to bear on the human mind, which, soothed by various allurements, flees labour and seeks pleasure, when, either restrained by fear of punishment or provoked by reward, it strives to conquer itself, and, as though its labours had been endured to the end, strives to snatch the palm which is being withdrawn from it by many adversaries. For we snatch from this world the palm of salvation, and with watchful labour we pluck fruits besieged as it were by watching serpents, yet so that it is not a stealthy taking away but a triumphal plundering. There is also another kind of plundering, when we plunder what was offered to others. Who these robbers are, then, we do not labour to understand, since we know that we have sprung from the race of Benjamin, the ravening wolf. John had gone before, to justify the people of the Jews; the Lord himself had come to the lost sheep of the house of Israel; he had appointed the apostles to found the faith of the people of the Jews either by disputation or by signs and miracles; but since they fled from the gifts offered to them, tax collectors and sinners began to believe in God, to come together into the faith. In these, then, by the apostolic preaching the kingdom of heaven is pressed and is made solid by the breath of the faithful people.

113. She who was troubled by the flow of blood plunders the kingdom; for when the Lord was on his way to the daughter of the ruler of the synagogue, by a kind of stealthy touch she took a foretaste of the remedy of health. That Canaanite woman plunders the kingdom, who, going out from her borders, cried out, saying: Have mercy on me, Lord, son of David; my daughter is grievously troubled by a demon. Truly she pressed the kingdom, persistent in prayers, wise in replies, faithful in words. She calls him back as he passes by, asks him when he is silent, adores him when he excuses himself, bends him when he refuses. Does she not seem to you to snatch, when she draws out what is refused, to snatch beforehand what is reserved for others? For the Lord had said that the children's bread ought not to be given to dogs; but she agreed, and by agreeing plundered it, saying: Yes, Lord; for even the little dogs eat of the crumbs which fall from the table of their masters.

114. You have learned how the heavenly kingdom is seized. Let us too, then, press it, let us plunder it; for no one eats the Passover except in haste. But what is it that seizes the kingdom? Not shamelessness, not wantonness, not pleasure, but that of which it is said: Great is your faith; be it done to you as you will. Behold, she seized it, who obtained what she wished, extorted what she asked. That widow too seized it, who by praying often extorts that she be heard, if not because of innocence, at least because of importunity.

115. So the Church seized the kingdom of the synagogue. My kingdom is Christ; I seize him, sent to the Jews under the Law, born in the Law, nourished according to the Law, that he might save me who was without the Law. Christ is seized when he is promised to some and predestined for others; Christ is seized when he is born for some and gives his support to others; Christ is seized when he is killed by some and buried by us; he is seized from those who lie in wait, he is seized from those who sleep. You have a place where they themselves confessed that we seized him, saying that they had slept: Say that his disciples came by night and stole him while we were asleep. Rise, then, you who sleep, lest you too, while you sleep, lose Christ. Rise, you who sleep, and arise from the dead. You see that those who sleep are dead. And therefore we do not envy others, but provide for ourselves; for the dead could not keep the living one.

116. Let those who have slept rise, even if late, even those who have lost Christ. Christ is not so lost that he does not return, if only he is sought; rather he comes back to those who watch and is at hand for those who rise, nay, he is present to all, he who is everywhere always, because he fills all things. For he fails no one; we fail; he fails no one, I say, he superabounds for all; for sin superabounded, that grace might superabound. Christ is grace, Christ is life, Christ is the resurrection. He who rises, then, finds him present.

117. The kingdom of heaven, then, is seized when Christ is denied by those of his household and adored by the Gentiles; it is seized when he is rejected by them and worshipped by us; it is seized when he is not acknowledged through inheritance and is acquired through adoption.

Book Six

And all the people hearing, and the tax collectors, justified God, being baptized with the baptism of John; but the Pharisees and the lawyers spurned the counsel of God against themselves, not being baptized.

1. Saint Luke, by adding particulars, has made plain what Saint Matthew, speaking as it were in general terms, had left somewhat obscure; for he says: and wisdom is justified by her children. Here, first, we see it expressed what that wisdom is; for he says: they justified God. God, then, is wisdom, because the Son of God is wisdom: wisdom by nature, not by progress. One wisdom is the power of God the Father, another wisdom is a power of the soul: the former is begotten, the latter created. One wisdom is the author of works, the other a work; for it is a work of the mind to be prudently wise, to perceive keenly. These are gifts of nature, but the worker is not a creature but the Creator, that is, not a gift of nature but the giver of nature's gifts.

2. And so God himself is justified through baptism, when men justify themselves by confessing their own sins, as it is written: tell your iniquities, that you may be justified; and he is justified in this, that he is not rejected through stubbornness, but his gift is acknowledged through righteousness; for righteous is the Lord, and he has loved righteousness. In this, then, lies the justification of God: if he is seen to have bestowed his gifts not on the unworthy and guilty, but on those made innocent through the washing, and righteous. Let us therefore justify the Lord, that we may be justified by the Lord.

3. Let us inquire more earnestly what it is for God to be justified. The Apostle says: but let God be true, and every man a liar, as it is written: that you may be justified in your words and may overcome when you are judged; David too says: against you only have I sinned and done evil before you, that you may be justified in your words and may overcome when you are judged. So he who sins and confesses his sin to God justifies God, yielding to him as the victor and hoping for grace from him. In baptism, therefore, God is justified, for in it there is both confession and pardon of sins.

4. Let us not, then, despise the counsel of God as the Pharisees did. The counsel of God is in the baptism of John; who then would doubt that the counsel of God is in the washing of Christ? This is the counsel which the Angel of great counsel found, which no one has known; for who has known the mind of the Lord? No one despises the counsel of a man: who would reject the counsel of God? So let us, as children, justify our mother; let us follow our mother. We know that a mother offers herself to danger for her children. Let us obey the counsel of wisdom our mother, the command of our mother.

5. We have sung to you, and you have not danced; we have lamented, and you have not wept. Although these words may seem not unfitting to the character of children, who through the thoughtlessness of an age not yet of riper gravity indulge in light movement of the body, yet I venture to think they can be referred to a higher sense, in that the Jews seem to have believed neither the psalms at first nor the prophetic laments afterwards: by the psalms they were summoned to rewards, by the laments called back from error. David sang, that we might hang our instruments upon the willows; he himself sang, and danced before the ark of the Lord not for wantonness but for religion. So what is meant is not the leaping of a body swaying with an actor's movements, but the eager agility of the mind and the devout agility of the body. But no amendment followed, neither in the triumphs nor in the downfalls of the Jews, who, summoned by the gifts of divine indulgence, ought to have lifted up the mind, raised the body, forsaken earthly things, sought heavenly ones, and, worn down by the injuries of captivity, ought to have bewailed their sin, because guilt was the author of the injury.

6. So wisdom is justified by all her children. Well does he say by all, because righteousness is kept in store toward all, so that there may be the receiving of the faithful and the rejection of the unbelieving. Hence many of the Greeks also have it thus: wisdom is justified by all her works, because it is the work of righteousness to keep the measure according to each one's merit. Well, then, does he say: we have sung to you, and you have not danced.

7. For Moses sang, when in the Red Sea the waves stiffened for the passage of the Jews, the water stood round about, and the same water, flowing back, drowned the horses of the Egyptians and their riders. Isaiah sang a song for his beloved vineyard, signifying that the people, once fruitful with fertile virtues, would become harsh with crimes. The Hebrews sang, when their footprints grew moist at the touch of a dewy flame, and while all things within and without were burning, the fire nonetheless licked them alone harmlessly and did not scorch them. Habakkuk too, taught to soothe public sorrow by a song, prophesied that the Lord's Passion would be sweet to the faithful. So the prophets sang, resounding with spiritual melodies the oracles of public salvation; the prophets wept with mournful laments, seeking to soften the hard hearts of the Jews.

8. Scripture has taught us to sing with gravity, to sing psalms spiritually; it has taught us also to dance wisely, when the Lord says to Ezekiel: clap with your hand and stamp with your foot; for God, the censor of morals, would not demand the actor's movements of a loose body, nor would he command of men unseemly clatterings and womanish clappings, so as to bring so great a prophet down to the mockeries of the stage and the softness of women. The revealed mysteries of the resurrection do not agree with the disgraces of dancing exacted. There is indeed, there is a clapping proper to good deeds and acts, whose sound may go out into the world and the glory of things well done may resound; there is an honorable dancing, in which the mind leaps for joy and the body is lifted up by good works, when we hang our instruments upon the willows.

9. The prophet, then, is bidden to clap with his hand and stamp with his foot, he is bidden to sing psalms, because he already saw the wedding of the Bridegroom, in which the Church is betrothed and Christ is loved. And good is the wedding, when the soul is wedded to the Word, the flesh to the Spirit. At this wedding the prophet David wished us to make merry; to it he invited us, because he was joining his own descendants in union; and therefore, more joyful than the rest, as though set in the very act of the wedding, he exhorts us to the celebration of the festal gift, saying: rejoice to God our helper, shout for joy to the God of Jacob. Take up a psalm and bring the timbrel, the pleasant psaltery with the harp. Do you not notice a certain act of the prophet dancing? Then elsewhere: I will sing psalms to you, O God, on the harp, Holy One of Israel. My lips shall rejoice when I sing to you, and my soul, which you have redeemed. Do you hear the voices of harpers, do you hear the clatter of dancers? Believe it is a wedding.

10. Take up the harp, you too, so that the strings of your inward veins, struck by the plectrum of the Spirit, may give back the sound of good work. Take up the psaltery, so that the harmony of your words and deeds may sound in concord. Take up the timbrel, so that the inner spirit may set to measure the instrument of your body, and the sweet pleasantness of your conduct may be expressed by your working deeds. So the prophet sang psalms, when he said: come hither from Lebanon, bride, come hither from Lebanon.

11. This song the children sang, and they were not heard. What children? Those of whom he says: behold, I and the children whom you have given me. But this song was not sung in the marketplace, not in the streets, but in Jerusalem; for she is the Lord's marketplace, in which the statutes of the heavenly precepts are laid up.

12. And behold, a woman who was in the city, a sinner. At this place many seem to feel a scruple and to raise questions, whether the two evangelists seem to have disagreed as to the truth of the matter, or rather wished to signify by the diversity of their words some diversity of mystery. For you have in the Gospel according to Matthew that when Jesus had come to Bethany into the house of Simon the leper, a woman came to him having an alabaster box of precious ointment, and as he reclined she poured it over his head. Then here the tax collector says within himself that, if he were a prophet, he would know she was a sinner and ought to avoid her ointment; there, when the ointment has been poured out, the disciples complain. Both, therefore, must be explained; but let what comes first in the order of the writings also receive the first place in the order of interpretation.

13. The Lord Jesus, then, came into the house of Simon the leper. The economy is apparent, in that he does not flee the leper, does not avoid the unclean, so that he may be able to wipe out the stains of the human body. Now the leper's house was in Bethany; Bethany by interpretation means house of obedience. So the whole place is Bethany, and a portion of the whole place is the house of Simon. Does it not seem to you that Bethany is this world, in which we owe the service of obedient servitude, and that the house of Simon the leper is the earth, which is a portion of the world? And the prince of this world is a kind of leprous Simon. So the Lord Jesus Christ came from those higher places into this world and descended to earth. He was not in this world, but by obedient devotion he was sent into this world. He himself says: as he has sent me into this world. So this woman heard that Christ had come, and entered the house of Simon; for this woman could not have been healed unless Christ had come to earth. And perhaps she too enters the house of Simon for this reason, that she bears the likeness of some higher soul, that is, or of the Church, which descended to earth to gather a people to herself by a good odor.

14. This woman, then, Matthew brings in pouring ointment over the head of Christ, and perhaps for that reason he did not wish to call her a sinner; for according to Luke the sinner poured ointment over the feet of Christ. So she may not be the same woman, lest the evangelists seem to have said things contrary to each other. The question can also be resolved by a difference of merit and of time, so that the one is still a sinner, the other already more perfect. For even if the Church or the soul does not change its person, yet it changes its progress. And so, if you set before you a soul drawing near to God faithfully, not with base and obscene sins, but devoutly serving the Word of God, having the confidence of unstained chastity, you notice that she ascends to the very head of Christ; and the head of Christ is God, and she spreads the odor of her merits. For we are the good odor of Christ to God; for the life of the righteous, fragrant with a good odor, honors God.

15. If you understand these things, you will see this woman, you will see her plainly blessed, named wherever this gospel shall be preached, and her memory never fading, because she poured upon the head of Christ the ointment of the fragrance of good conduct and righteous deeds. He who approaches the head does not know how to be puffed up, as that one is, vainly puffed up by the mind of his flesh and not holding the head. But let him who does not hold the head of Christ at least hold his feet, because the one body, joined together and supplied, grows unto the increase of God.

16. That other woman is different, either a different person or different in progress, and she is near to us; for we have not yet renounced our sins. Where are our tears, where our groaning, where our weeping? Come, let us worship and fall down before him and weep before the Lord our God, who made us, so that we may at least be able to come to the feet of Jesus. For we are not yet able: the sinner comes to the feet, the righteous to the head.

17. Yet she too who has sinned has ointment. Bring me, you too, repentance after your sins. Wherever you hear that the Righteous One has come, whether into the house of an unworthy man or into the house of a Pharisee, hasten, snatch the grace before the host, snatch the kingdom of heaven; for from the days of John the Baptist the kingdom of heaven is pressed hard, and those who press tear it away. Wherever you hear the name of Christ, run to him: into whosesoever inner house you learn that Jesus has entered, hasten there yourself too. When you have found Wisdom, when you have found Righteousness reclining in someone's inner chambers, run to the feet, that is, seek at least the last part of wisdom. Do not disdain the feet; she touched the fringe and was healed. Confess your offences with tears, so that that heavenly Righteousness may say of you too:

18. she has watered my feet with her tears and wiped them with her hair. And perhaps Christ did not wash his own feet for this reason, that we might wash them with tears. Good tears, which can not only wash away our offence but also water the footprint of the heavenly Word, so that his steps may abound in us. Good tears, in which there is not only the redemption of sinners but also the refreshment of the righteous; for it is the voice of the righteous man: my tears have been my bread.

19. Even if you cannot approach the head of Christ, let Christ touch your head with his feet. Both his fringe heals and his feet heal. Spread out your hair, lay down before him all the dignities of your body. No ordinary hair is that which can wipe the feet of Christ. He bears witness to this who, as long as he had his hair, could not be overcome. And then, it is not fitting for a woman to pray shaven. Let her by all means have hair with which to wrap the footprints of Christ, with which, the little locks of her beauty and of the comeliness of wisdom, she may wipe his feet, so that they may grow moist at least with the outermost dew of divine power; let her fix her kisses on the feet of Righteousness. She is of no ordinary merit, of whom Wisdom can say:

20. since I entered, she has not ceased to kiss my feet, so that she may know how to speak nothing but wisdom, may know how to love nothing but righteousness, may know how to taste nothing but chastity, may know how to kiss nothing but purity. For a kiss is the token of mutual love, a kiss is the pledge of charity.

21. Blessed is he who can anoint the feet of Christ even with oil. Indeed, Simon had not yet anointed them; but more blessed is she who anoints with ointment; for the grace of many flowers gathered into one spreads varied sweetnesses of odors. And perhaps no one could bring this ointment but the Church alone, which has countless flowers of diverse fragrance, and which rightly takes on the likeness of the sinful woman, because Christ too took on the form of a sinner.

22. And therefore no one can love as much as she who loves in many. Not Peter himself, who said to him: Lord, you know that I love you; not Peter himself, who grieved because he was asked: do you love me? For he did not wish what was manifest to be asked about as though it were unknown. So not Peter himself, because the Church loved in Peter; nor Paul himself, because Paul too is a portion of her. And do you too love very much, so that very much may be forgiven you too. Paul sinned much, for he was even a persecutor; but he loved much, for he persevered even to martyrdom. His many sins were forgiven him, because he too loved much, he who did not spare his own blood for the name of God.

23. See the economy: in the house of the Pharisee the sinful woman is glorified; in the house of the Law and the prophet it is not the Pharisee but the Church that is justified; for the Pharisee did not believe, she believed. Indeed, he said: if he were a prophet, he would surely know who and what sort of woman this is who touches him. Now the house of the Law is Judaea, which is written not on stones but on the tablets of the heart. In this the Church is justified, being now greater than the Law; for the Law does not know the remission of sins, the Law does not have the mystery by which hidden things are cleansed; and therefore what is lacking in the Law is completed in the Gospel.

Two he says debtors there were to a certain moneylender: one owed five hundred denarii, the other fifty.

24. Who are these two debtors but two peoples, one from the Jews, the other from the Gentiles, indebted to that moneylender of the heavenly treasury? One he says owed five hundred denarii, the other fifty. No ordinary coin is this denarius, on which the image of the King is formed, which bears the Emperor's trophy stamped upon it. It is not material money that we owe this moneylender, but merits tried in the balance, the coin of virtues, whose worth is weighed out by the heaviness of gravity, by the appearance of righteousness, by the ring of confession. Woe to me if I do not have what I received; or, since it is hard to find anyone who can pay this moneylender the debt in full, woe to me if I do not ask: 'Forgive me the debt!' For the Lord would not have taught us to pray so, that we should ask that our debts be forgiven us, unless he knew that few would be solvent debtors.

25. But who is this people that owes more, if not we, to whom more has been entrusted? To them were entrusted the oracles of God; to us is entrusted the childbearing of the Virgin. You have as your talent the Virgin's childbearing, you have the hundredfold fruit of faith. Emmanuel, God with us, has been entrusted to us; the Lord's cross, death and resurrection have been entrusted to us. Although Christ suffered for all, yet he suffered especially for us, because he suffered for the Church. And so there is no doubt that he owes more who has received more. And according to men he who owed more perhaps offends more; but through the mercy of the Lord the case is changed, so that he loves more who owed more, if only he obtains grace. For grace is both had by him who repays it, and he who has it pays it in the very fact that he has it; for it is both had by repaying and repaid by having.

26. And therefore, since there is nothing that we can worthily render to God — for what are we to render for the injury of the flesh he took on, what for the scourges, what for the cross, the death, the burial? — woe to me if I do not love! I dare to say: Peter did not repay, and therefore he loved more; Paul did not repay; he repaid indeed death for death, but other things he did not repay, because he owed much. Hear him saying that he did not repay: who has first given to him, and it shall be repaid him? Though we repay cross for cross, burial for burial, do we repay anything, seeing that from him and through him and in him we have all things? Let us therefore repay love for the debt, charity for the gift, grace for the money; for he loves more to whom more is given.

27. But let us return to that earlier woman, whose purpose not even the apostles yet understand, because it was hidden from the ages in God; for who has known the mind of the Lord? The disciples, then, were complaining because the woman poured ointment over his head, and they grumbled, saying: why is this wasted so? For it could have been sold for a price and given to the poor. What exactly was displeasing in their words you cannot detect unless you understand the mystery; for to smell of ointment is the mark of a luxurious man, or rather of one who is no man. Certainly even those who smell of it are wont to anoint themselves, not to drench themselves. What is displeasing in its being said: this could have been sold for a price and given to the poor? Certainly he himself had said earlier: whatever you have done to one of these least, you have done to me; but he himself was offering his own death for the poor.

28. It is not, then, a simple figure; and therefore the Word of God answered them: why do you trouble the woman? The poor you have always with you, but me you do not have always. You always have the poor man with you, then, and therefore do good. You ought not, then, to put off the poor man because he is always with you, since the prophet says to you: do not say to the poor man: tomorrow I will give. But he spoke of mercy alone, while here he has put faith before mercy, for mercy has merit only if it is bestowed with faith going before; for in putting this ointment on my body she did it for my burial. So it was not the ointment that the Lord loved, but charity; he accepted faith, he approved humility.

29. You too, if you desire grace, increase charity; put upon the body of Jesus faith in the resurrection, the odor of the Church, the ointment of common charity. And so, going further, you will give to the poor. That money will profit you more if you do not hand it out as something overflowing, but bestow it in Christ's name as something that will profit; if you so give it to the poor man that you bring it to Christ. Do not, then, take the ointment poured over the head according to the letter only — for the letter kills — but according to the spirit, because the spirit is life.

30. What, then, is this woman's ointment? Who can hear this? Who has such ears that, when Jesus speaks the word which he received from the Father, or rather since he himself is the Word, he can grasp so great a depth of mystery? Even the disciples understand in part, although they do not understand the whole. Hence some think that the disciples said that the faith of the Gentiles ought to have been bought with the price of the ointment, faith which was due only to the price of the Lord's blood; and this seems likely. Indeed, John the evangelist brings in the saying of Judas Iscariot, that the ointment was valued at three hundred denarii, as you have it: for it could have been sold for three hundred denarii and given to the poor. Now the coins of three hundred declare the sign of the cross; but the Lord seeks not a perfunctory foreknowledge of the mystery, but rather wishes the faith of believers to be buried together in him.

31. This, however, we understand of the words of the other apostles; but Judas is condemned for avarice, for he preferred money to the Lord's burial, and even if he had some sense of the Passion, he erred in so costly a sale. Christ wishes to be valued cheaply, that he may be bought by all, so that no poor man may be deterred. Freely he says you have received, freely give. The depth of riches seeks not money, but grace. He himself bought us with precious blood; he did not sell us. Of this we would speak more fully, did we not remember that we have treated it ourselves elsewhere.

32.
