So, according to the sayings of the Lord, in whom are hidden the treasures of wisdom and knowledge, which no one could foresee, I must work at his burial, so that his flesh may be believed to have rested and not to have seen corruption, and the death of his body may so fill our house with its odor that we believe that he commended his spirit into the hands of the Father, and that the divinity, set apart from death, did not undergo fellowship in bodily sufferings.

33. Understand how the body of the Son smells of ointment. That body is one which was put off, not one which was lost. His body is the traditions of the Scriptures, his body is the Church. We are the odor of his body, and therefore it is fitting that we adorn the death of his body, even if that death does not need our adornments; but the poor need them. I will adorn his body if I become a preacher of his words and can open to the Gentiles the mystery of the cross. He adorned it who said: we preach Christ crucified, to the Jews indeed a stumbling block, to the Gentiles foolishness, but to those who are called, Jews and Greeks, Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God. The cross is adorned when that which through ignorance is thought foolish is through the Gospel judged to be wiser, so that we can teach how the adversary power is destroyed through the cross of the Lord. I have put ointment upon the Lord's body: what was thought dead begins to breathe fragrance.

34. Therefore let each one undertake to buy, by his own labor and the striving of virtues, an alabaster box of ointment, not some cheap and common one, but precious ointment of alabaster and pistic ointment. For if anyone gathers the flowers of faith and preaches Jesus Christ crucified, he pours the ointment of his faith into the whole Church, which is the body of Christ, dead to the world, resting in God. The whole house begins to smell of the Lord's Passion, begins to smell of his death, begins to smell of his resurrection, so that each one of this number of the holy people can say: but far be it from me to glory except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ. The odor breathes, the ointment smells in the body, if anyone can — and would that I could! — say with confidence: to me however the world is crucified. Not to one who loves riches, not to one who loves the honors of the world, not to one who loves the things that are his own, but to one who loves the things of Jesus Christ; not to one who loves the things that are seen, but the things that are not seen; not to one desirous of life, but to him who hastens to be dissolved and to be with Christ, is the world crucified. For this is to take up the cross and follow Christ: that we too may die with him and be buried with him, so that we may give off the odor of the ointment which this woman put on him for his burial. No ordinary ointment is this, by which the name of Christ is spread far and wide. Hence it was also said prophetically: your name is ointment poured out; poured out for this reason, that faith might be fragrant with a greater odor.

35. From this woman, then, we understand what that saying of the Apostle means: sin abounded exceedingly, that grace might abound exceedingly. For if sin had not abounded exceedingly in this woman, grace would not have abounded exceedingly; for she acknowledged her sin and brought grace. And therefore the Law is necessary; for through the Law I have known sin. If there had been no Law, sin would lie hidden. By acknowledging sin I ask for pardon. Through the Law, then, I recognize the kinds of sins and also the crime of transgression; I run to repentance, I obtain grace. The Law, therefore, which sends us on to grace, is the author of good.

36. My mother and my brothers are these who hear the word of God and do it. The teacher of morals, who offers others an example from himself, is both the one who gives the precepts and himself the one who carries out his own precepts. For being about to prescribe to others that he who has not left his father and his mother is not worthy of the Son of God, he first subjects himself to this sentence; not that he rejects the dutiful services of a mother's love — for it is his own precept: he who does not honor his father and mother shall die the death — but because he knows that he owes himself more to his Father's mysteries than to his mother's affections. Nor yet are parents rejected injuriously; but we are taught that the bonds of minds are more sacred than those of bodies.

37. So those who were seeking to see Christ ought not to have stood outside; for the word is near in your heart and in your mouth. The Word, then, is within, the light is within. Hence he too says: draw near to him and be enlightened. For if even his own parents, standing outside, are not acknowledged — and perhaps are not acknowledged for the sake of an example to us — how shall we be acknowledged if we stand outside?

38. Nor let anyone think that there is a stumbling block to dutiful love where the commandment of the Law is fulfilled; for if a man shall leave father and mother and shall cleave to his wife and the two shall be in one flesh, rightly is this sacrament kept in Christ and in the Church. And therefore it was not fitting that parents should be preferred to his own body. So here his mother, who is acknowledged even from the cross, is not denied, as certain heretics, setting their little snares, try to catch at; but the pattern of the heavenly precepts is preferred to bodily kinship. It is not unreasonable to understand this also: that by the figure of his parents he shows that the Church which has believed is to be preferred to the Jews, from whom Christ is according to the flesh.

39. And so he who understood that he had come to earth for the sake of the divine mystery and the gathering of the Church left his parents and went up into the ship. For no one could sail through this world without Christ, since even those with whom the Lord is present are often troubled by the storm of worldly temptations. And the economy is kept in the apostles, so that you may notice that no one can depart from this course of life without temptation, because temptation is the exercise of faith.

40. We are subject, then, to the storms of spiritual wickedness; but like watchful sailors let us awaken the helmsman. Yet even they are wont to be in danger. What helmsman, then, shall I seek? Surely him who does not serve the winds but commands them, of whom it is written:

41. but he, rising, rebuked the wind. What is rising? He was at rest, but he was resting in the sleep of the body, while he was intent on the mystery of his divinity; for where there is wisdom, where there is the Word, nothing is done without the Word, nothing without prudence.

42. You have it above that he spent the night in prayer: how would he sleep here in the storm? But the security of his power is expressed, in that while all were afraid he alone rested unafraid. So he who is not a sharer in the danger is not a sharer in the nature. Even if the body sleeps, the divinity works, faith works. Indeed he says: you of little faith, why did you doubt? And rightly are they reproved who were afraid when Christ was present, since surely he who clings to him cannot perish.

43. So he strengthened their faith, poured back the calm, commanded the wind to cease — not the north wind, not the south-west wind, but surely that wind to which it was said by the angel Michael in the epistle of Jude: may the Lord command you. Hence Saint Matthew also: he commanded he says the wind and the sea. And would that he may deign to restrain its harsh blasts in us, so that calm poured back upon our tossing life may take away the fear of shipwreck! Even if he no longer sleeps with the sleep of his own body, let us nevertheless beware lest, through the sleep of our body, he sleep and rest for us.

44. And since it is enough to have touched lightly on what we briefly treated in an earlier place, now, because in the book according to Matthew we have learned that two men possessed by demons met Christ in the region of the Gerasenes, while here Saint Luke brings in one, and him naked — for naked is whoever has lost the covering of his nature and of virtue — I think we must not idly pass over but inquire for what reason the evangelists seem to be at variance about the number. And indeed, though the number disagrees, yet the mystery agrees; for this man who had a demon is a figure of the Gentile people, covered with vices, naked to error, open to crime. Those two likewise also take on the figure of the Gentile people, since, although Noah begot three sons, Shem, Ham, Japheth, the family of Shem alone was taken into the possession of God. From those two sprang the peoples of diverse nations, of whom one was cursed because he had not covered his naked father, the other blessed because, though turned away so as not to take in the shame of his naked parent, he had yet covered his father, with devotion as his guide, and had washed away the curse of his brother's seed.

45. Who for a long time he says was driven, inasmuch as he was harassed from the flood until the coming of the Lord, bursting the bonds of nature with mad fury. And not without reason did Saint Matthew also indicate that they dwelt in the tombs, since the souls of such men seem to dwell in certain mounds of sepulchres. For what else are the bodies of unbelievers but certain sepulchres of the dead, in which the words of God do not dwell? He was driven, then, into desert places, that is, barren of spiritual virtues, a fugitive from the Law, cut off from the prophets, far removed from grace.

46. For he was suffering the assault not of one demon but of a whole legion; and this legion, when it had seen the Lord and recognized, foreknowing it, that at the time of the Lord's coming it was to be thrust into the abyss, began to beg that it might receive leave to enter into the swine. And first we ought to notice the clemency of the Lord, in that he himself condemns no one first, but each one is the author of his own punishment; the demons are not sent into the swine, but they themselves ask it, because they could not bear the brightness of the heavenly light, just as those whose eyes are sore cannot bear the sun's ray, but choose dark places and forsake bright ones. So let the demons flee the splendor of the eternal light and dread before the time the torments due to them, not divining what is to come but remembering what has been prophesied; for Zechariah said: and it shall be in that day, the Lord will destroy the names of the idols from the earth, and there shall be no more remembrance of them. And I will burn up the unclean spirit from the earth. We are taught, then, by this that they will not abide forever, nor can their malice be perpetual. Now, therefore, fearing that punishment, they say: you have come to destroy us. But because they still desire to exist, as they now withdraw from men, on whose account they know they must undergo punishment, they ask to be sent into the swine.

47. Who are these swine, unless perhaps those of whom it was said: do not give what is holy to the dogs, nor cast your pearls before swine, lest perhaps they trample them under their feet? Those, namely, who after the manner of unclean animals, devoid of speech and reason, have defiled the ornaments of the natural virtues by the muddy acts of their life; who are borne headlong by their impulse, since they are called back by regard for no merit, but, thrust down as it were from higher to lower along the slope of wickedness, they are choked in the waters amid the floating things of this world, perishing like men strangled, with the passage of their breath shut off; for in those who are carried uncertain hither and thither by the flowing tide of pleasures there can be no life-giving exchange of any spirit.

48. We see, then, that man himself is the author of his own misery. For had someone not lived after the manner of a swine, the devil would never have received power over him, or, had he received it, it would have been not to destroy him but to test him. Or perhaps, because after the Lord's coming he could no longer deprave the good, he now seeks the destruction not of all men but of the fickle, as a robber lies in wait not for the armed but for the unarmed, and assails the weak with injuries, knowing that he is either despised by the strong or condemned by the powerful.

49. But someone says: why is this permitted to the devil by God? And I say: that the good may be proved and the wicked punished; for this is the punishment of sin. Indeed, read that the Lord sends fever and trembling and evil spirits and blindness and every scourge according to the deserts of sins. But let us return to the reading.

50. When the masters of the herds saw he says this, they fled. For neither the professors of philosophy nor the rulers of the synagogue can bring any medicine to perishing peoples. Christ alone it is who takes away the sins of the peoples, if only they do not refuse the patience of being healed. For the rest, he does not deign to cure the unwilling, and quickly leaves the weak to whom he sees that his presence is a burden, as with the peoples of the Gerasenes, who, coming out of the city, in which the likeness of the synagogue seems to appear, asked him to depart, because they were held by great fear.

51. For a weak mind does not grasp the word of God, nor can it bear the weight of wisdom; it gives way and is undone.

52. And therefore he was not troublesome to them any longer, but went up and returned. He went up, surely, from the lower to the higher, that is, from the synagogue to the Church, and returned across the lake, as he says here, or, as Matthew says, across the strait; for between us and them there is a great strait. And so no one passes from the Church to the synagogue without danger to his salvation; but he too who desires to pass from the synagogue to the Church, let him take up his cross, that he may escape the peril.

53. But why is the man set free not taken with him, but bidden to return home, unless both that occasion for boasting may be avoided and that he may be an example to unbelievers — although that home is a natural lodging — and therefore, because he had obtained the remedy of health, he is commanded to return from the mounds and sepulchres to that spiritual home, so that a temple of God may come to be where there was a sepulchre of the mind?

54. And behold, there came a man whose name was Jairus, and he was a ruler of the synagogue, and he fell at the feet of Jesus, begging him to enter his house, because he had an only daughter about twelve years old, and she was dying. Christ had left the synagogue among the Gerasenes, as we have said, and we welcomed him whom his own had not received, him whom we were awaiting. Neither, then, was he long absent from us, by whom he was awaited, nor does he disdain to return to them, if he is asked. For this man, the ruler of the synagogue, who had an only daughter, was praying for the healing of the perishing synagogue, which was being pressed on toward death because it was being deserted by Christ. Whom do we take the ruler of the synagogue to be but the Law, out of regard for which the Lord did not utterly abandon the synagogue, but kept in store the medicine of salvation for those who believe? So while the Word of God was hastening to this daughter of the ruler, to save the children of Israel, the holy Church gathered from the Gentiles, which was perishing through the fall into baser crimes, snatched by faith the healing prepared for others.

55. We thought this place needed to be expounded sufficiently in the moral sense, and therefore we do not go back over what has been said. But we wish to touch briefly on the mystical sense. Did it not so come about that the Word of God, when he had come to the Jews, was taken up by the Gentiles, and was believed in grace first by those who had not believed under the Law?

56. For just as that woman had spent all her substance on physicians, so too the assembly of the Gentiles had lost all the gifts of nature and squandered the patrimony of life: holy, modest, devout, readier in faith, slower through modesty; for this belongs to modesty and faith, to acknowledge infirmity and not to despair of pardon. Modest, then, she touched the fringe; faithful, she approached; devout, she believed; wise, she knew that she had been healed. So the holy people of the Gentiles, which believed God, blushed for sin so as to forsake it, brought faith so as to believe, showed devotion so as to entreat, put on wisdom so as itself to perceive its healing, took confidence so as to confess that it had snatched what belonged to another.

57. But why is Christ touched from behind? Is it because it is written: you shall walk after the Lord your God? What, too, is the meaning of this, that the ruler's daughter of twelve years was dying and this woman had suffered from a flow of blood for twelve years, unless it be understood that as long as the synagogue flourished, the Church was afflicted? The failing of the one is the strength of the other, because by their offence, salvation for the Gentiles; and the completion of the one is the beginning of the other, not the beginning of nature but of salvation, because blindness in part has befallen Israel, until the fullness of the Gentiles should come in. Not in time, then, but in the appearance of health the synagogue was older than the Church, because as long as the synagogue believed, the Church did not believe, and through the various sufferings of body and soul lay sick and languishing, with a remedy that could not heal. She heard that the people of the Jews was sick, she began to hope for the remedy of her own salvation, she knew that the time had come when the Physician would be present from heaven; she rose to go to meet the Word, she saw that he was being pressed by the crowds; for those who press do not believe, those who touch believe. By faith Christ is touched, by faith Christ is seen; he is not touched with the body, he is not grasped with the eyes; for he does not see who seeing does not see, nor does he hear who does not understand what he hears, nor does he touch who does not touch with faith. Indeed, to express the faith of the one who touched, he says: someone touched me; for I perceived that power had gone out from me.

58. This is a mark of clear evidence that wisdom was not shut up within the possibility of the human condition and within the confinement of the body, that the divinity was not constrained; it is not contained by bodily narrowness, it is not held, but the eternal power overflows beyond the bounds of our mediocrity. For the people of the Gentiles is not set free by human help, but the gathering of the nations is the gift of God, and even by a brief faith it bends eternal mercy. Now if we consider how great our faith is, and understand how great the Son of God is, we see that in comparison with him we touch only the fringe, but cannot reach the upper part of his garment. If, then, we too wish to be healed, let us touch the fringe of Christ by faith.

59. Whoever touches the fringe, whoever touches him from behind, does not escape his notice; for God does not need eyes in order to see, nor does he perceive bodily, but he has in himself the knowledge of all things. Blessed, then, is he who has touched even the last part of the Word; for who can grasp the whole?

But to return to her who is still sick, lest she be believed to have died through our delay rather than through her own, there came he says servants saying to the ruler: do not trouble him; your daughter is dead.

60. And first let us consider this, that when he was about to raise the dead girl, he first healed the woman with the flow of blood, to produce faith. And that you may know that the flow of blood was stanched for the sake of an example: while the one is being sought, the other is healed. So too a temporal resurrection is celebrated at the Lord's Passion, that the perpetual one also may be believed. So too a barren woman is shown to Mary as about to give birth, that the Virgin may be believed about to conceive. Indeed, she heard that Elizabeth was to give birth, and did not doubt concerning her own childbearing.

61. There came he says servants saying to the ruler: do not trouble him. These too are still unbelieving toward the resurrection, which Jesus foretold in the Law and fulfilled in the Gospel. And so, when he had come into the house, he took a few as witnesses of the resurrection to come; for the resurrection was not believed by many at once.

62. Indeed, when the Lord said: the girl is not dead but sleeps, they laughed he says at him. For whoever does not believe mocks. Let them weep for their dead, then, who think them dead; where there is faith in the resurrection, there is the appearance not of death but of rest. Nor is that statement of Matthew out of keeping, where he says that there were trumpeters in the ruler's house and a crowd making a tumult, either because by ancient custom trumpeters were said to be employed to kindle and stir up mourning, or because, through the chanting of the Law and the letter, the synagogue could not grasp spiritual exultation.

63. So Jesus, holding the girl's hand, healed her, and ordered that she be given something to eat. This is a testimony of life, so that it might be believed to be not a phantom but the truth. Blessed is he whose hand Wisdom holds. Would that she might hold my acts too, that Righteousness might hold my hand, the Word of God hold it, lead me into his inner chambers, turn away the spirit of error, turn toward me the spirit of salvation, and order that I be given to eat! For the Word of God is the heavenly bread. Hence too that Wisdom, which has filled the most holy altars with the nourishment of the divine body and blood: come she says eat my loaves and drink the wine which I have mixed for you.

64. Yet what is the reason for so great a difference? Above, the widow's son is raised publicly; here the many witnesses are removed. But I think that in this too the Lord's compassion is shown there, because the widowed mother of an only son could not bear delays, and therefore speed is added, lest she be afflicted further. There is also a pattern of wisdom: in the widow's son, that the Church would believe quickly; in the daughter of the ruler of the synagogue, that the Jews would indeed believe, but out of many only a few.

65. And whoever does not receive you, when you go out of that city shake off even the dust from your feet for a testimony against them. What manner of man he ought to be who preaches the gospel of the kingdom of God is marked out by the gospel precepts: that without staff, without bag, without shoes, without bread, without money, that is, not seeking the props of worldly support, and safe in faith, he should think that the less he seeks those things, the more they can be at hand for him. Those who wish can turn these words to that interpretation, so that this place seems to shape only a spiritual disposition, which seems to have put off, as it were, a kind of garment of the body, not only with power rejected and riches despised, but even with the allurements of the flesh itself renounced.

66. To them, first of all, is given the general command of peace and constancy: that they bring peace, keep constancy, guard the rights of the bond of hospitality; for he declares that it is foreign to a preacher of the heavenly kingdom to run about from house to house and to change the rights of inviolable hospitality. But just as the favor of hospitality is held to be something to be offered, so too, if they are not received, they are commanded to shake off the dust and to go out of the city. By this is taught the reward of the no small good of hospitality: that not only do we grant peace to our hosts, but also, if any offences of earthly fickleness overshadow them, these are taken away when the footsteps of apostolic preaching are received. And not idly, according to Matthew, is it decreed that the house which the apostles enter is to be chosen, so that there may be no cause for changing lodging and violating the bond. Yet the same caution is not enjoined on the one who gives hospitality, lest, while a guest is being chosen, hospitality itself be lessened.

67. But as, according to the letter, this is a venerable form of precept concerning the sacredness of hospitality, so also concerning the mystery a heavenly meaning smiles on us. For when a house is chosen, a worthy host is sought. Let us see whether perhaps the Church is meant as the one to be preferred, and Christ. For what house is more worthy of the entrance of apostolic preaching than the holy Church? Or who seems more to be preferred to all than Christ, who is wont to wash the feet of his guests, and does not allow whomever he has received into his house to dwell with soiled footsteps, but deigns to cleanse their steps, though stained by their former life, for the rest of their way? He alone, then, is the one whom no one ought to forsake, no one to exchange, to whom it is well said: Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the words of eternal life, and we believe. You see the performer of the heavenly precepts, who, because he did not change his lodging, earned fellowship in heavenly consecration.

68. We are commanded, then, first of all to seek out the faith of the Church; if Christ is going to dwell in her, she is without doubt to be chosen. But if an unbelieving people or a heretical teacher disfigures the dwelling, we are told that the communion of heretics is to be avoided, the synagogue fled. The dust of the feet must be shaken off, lest the footprint of your mind be defiled by the crumbling droughts of barren unbelief, as by dry and sandy ground. For just as the preacher of the Gospel ought to take upon himself the bodily infirmities of the faithful people, and, as with his own footprints, to lift up and wipe out their empty deeds, comparable to dust, according to what is written: who is weak, and I am not weak? — so, if there is any church which rejects the faith and does not possess the foundations of apostolic preaching, it must be forsaken, lest it be able to sprinkle on us any stain of unbelief. This the Apostle too clearly asserted, saying: avoid a heretical man after one correction.

69. And he said to them: you give them to eat. But they said: we have no more than five loaves. What is the reason that, although the passion of John is described later on, here he is already shown to be dead by the words of Herod? Is it perhaps this, that after the failing of the Law the gospel food begins to feed the fasting hearts of the peoples? Indeed, after she who took on the type of the Church was healed of the flow of blood, after the apostles were appointed to preach the gospel of the kingdom of God, the nourishment of heavenly grace is imparted. But notice to whom it is imparted. Not to the idle, not to those who sit in the city, as it were in the synagogue or in worldly rank, but to those who seek Christ in the desert places; for those who are not disdainful are themselves received by Christ, and the Word of God speaks with them not of worldly things but of the kingdom of heaven. And if any bear the sores of bodily suffering, to them he gladly grants his medicine.

70. It followed, then, that those whom he had healed of the pain of their wounds he should free from fasting with spiritual nourishment. And so no one receives the food of Christ unless he has first been healed, and those who are called to the supper are first healed by being called. If one was lame, he received the power of walking so that he might come; if deprived of the light of his eyes, he surely could not enter the house of the Lord unless his light were poured back.

71. Everywhere, then, the order of the mystery is kept: first medicine is given to wounds through the remission of sins, afterwards the nourishment of the heavenly table abounds; although this crowd is not yet refreshed with stronger food, nor are hearts empty of a more solid faith fed with the body and blood of Christ. With milk he says I gave you to drink, not with meat; for you were not yet able. But not even now are you able. The five loaves are after the manner of milk; but the more solid food is the body of Christ, the stronger drink is the blood of the Lord. We do not feast on all things at once from the first, nor drink all things. 'This first' he says 'drink'. There is, then, a first thing for you to drink, there is also a second. There is a first thing for you to eat, there is also a second, there is a third. First there are five loaves, second seven, the third is the very body of Christ.

72. Let us by no means, then, forsake such a Lord, who deigns to impart nourishment to us according to each one's strength, lest either stronger food overwhelm the weak or meagre nourishment fail to satisfy the strong; for let him who is weak eat herbs. And let him who already seems to escape the snares of infirmity eat of these five loaves and two fishes. Surely, if he is afraid to ask for food, let him himself leave all that is his and hasten to the Word of God. As he begins to hear, he begins to hunger. The apostles begin to see him hungering. Even if they do not yet understand what he hungers for, Christ understands. He knows that he hungers not for worldly food but for the food of Christ. Let him say: I do not wish to send them away fasting, lest they faint on the way: the good Lord requires zeal, he supplies strength.

73. Would, Lord Jesus, that you would be willing not to send away these fasting ones with me, but refresh them with a banquet imparted by you, so that, made stronger by your nourishment, they may not fear the weakness of fasting. Would that you would say of us too: I do not wish to send them away fasting! And tell me the reason why you do not wish to send them away fasting: nay, you have already said that if you send anyone away fasting, he faints on the way, that is, he faints either in the course of this life or before he reaches the head of the way, before he comes to the Father and understands that Christ is from the Father, understands that Christ is from heaven, and understands that Christ who descended is himself the one who ascended; lest perhaps, when he has learned that he was born of a virgin, he begin to reckon his power not God's but a man's.

74. So, lest they faint, he says: you give them to eat. But they said: we have no more than five loaves and two fishes, unless we go and buy food for all this crowd. The apostles had not yet understood that the food of the believing people is not for sale. Christ knew, he himself knew that we rather were to be redeemed, but that his banquet was free. So the disciples did not yet have the food that could redeem us. Yet they already had food that could satisfy us, they had food that could strengthen us; for bread strengthens the heart of man. So the Lord has compassion, lest anyone faint on the way. If anyone, then, has fainted, he faints not through the Lord Jesus but through himself, and you have nothing to ascribe to the Lord, who overcomes when he is judged. For what would you say to him who has conferred on you all the supports of virtue? Did he not himself beget you, did he not himself feed you? His food is virtue, his food is fortitude. But if through your own negligence you have lost the virtue you had received, what failed you was not the support of the heavenly nourishment but that of your own mind. Indeed, just as the Lord rains upon the righteous and the unrighteous, so too he feeds the unrighteous and the righteous.

75. Did not holy Elijah, in the strength of food, when he was already fainting on the way, walk forty days, and was it not an angel who gave that food? But if Jesus has fed you and you have kept the food you received, you will walk not forty days and forty nights, but — I dare to say it, relying on the examples of Scripture — forty years, going out from the borders of Egypt, until you come into a large land, into a land flowing with honey and milk, which the Lord swore he would give to our fathers. It is the power of this land that you must seek, the land which the meek man possesses. I do not mean this land which is dry, but that which is strengthened by the nourishment of Christ, which, set under the rule of the eternal King, is thronged with the sojourning saints.

76. So the Lord Jesus divides the food. And he indeed wishes to give to all, he refuses no one; for he is the steward of all. But when he breaks the loaves and gives them to the disciples, if you do not stretch out your hands to receive food for yourself, you will faint on the way, and you will not be able to lay your fault upon him who has compassion and divides it; but he divides it to those who remain with him even in the desert, who do not withdraw on the first day nor the second nor the third. For so you have it elsewhere: I have compassion on this crowd, because it is now three days that they continue with me. How great a condescension, how great a kindness is breathed upon men in him! He does not wish to send them away fasting, he does not wish them to faint on the way.

77. Do not, then, fail in the discipline of God, nor grow weary when you are rebuked by him. Do not grow weary now, lest you grow weary hereafter. For what will you answer him, or how will you excuse yourself, if you have lost the strength of the food which he supplies? You cannot say that he did not give food, for he gives to all. You cannot say that he did not wish you to do good, he who set before you good and evil, so that your good might not be of necessity but voluntary. For there is a great difference between him who does something unwillingly by necessity and him who chooses of his own will what is good; for if I do this unwillingly, a stewardship has been entrusted to me; if willingly, I have a reward. Let us consider, then, that we shall stand before the judgment seat of Christ, and if our work has burned, we shall have nothing to plead in excuse. For he will say what he has already spoken through the prophet: my people, what have I done to you, or in what have I been troublesome to you? Answer me. He will say to him who has fainted on the way: for what reason did you faint on the way? Did I not make loaves, did I not bless them, did I not order them to be given? But why were you unwilling to receive?

78. How many, too, of those present will faint on the way even after these sermons — which, though they are ours, are yet to be reckoned loaves, because no one says Lord Jesus except in the Holy Spirit — how many, I say, will faint and go off into the ways of the Gentiles, committing fornication by crooked paths! And would that it were one and not very many! But Jesus is not the author of the fainting, even if someone has fainted; for he distributes to all who follow, whether they be five thousand or four thousand.

79. Not idle is the number, not idle the order, not idle the leftovers of those who ate. For why are the more, that is, five thousand, satisfied with five loaves, that is, with a smaller number, while the fewer, who are four thousand, are fed with seven loaves, that is, with a more abundant number? For if we follow only the miracle of the deed, that seems more divine which from a smaller measure abounded for more people. Why, then, was what is less added to what is more, as though it surpassed it? For we read that first five thousand were satisfied with five loaves, and afterwards four thousand also with seven loaves. Let us therefore seek the mystery, which surpasses the miracle.

80. And so those five thousand, as the five senses of the body, seem to have received from Christ nourishment still nearest to bodily things; but the four thousand, although they are still in the body and in this world, which consists of four elements, are yet not idly said to have received the food of mystical rest; for they are now on a par with the world, being destined to be above the world. For though they are in this world, yet those to whom the nourishment of mystical rest is imparted are not within the world; for in six days the world was made, on the seventh day there was rest, and the Lord sanctified it. Beyond the world, then, is rest; beyond the world also is the fruit of rest. Indeed, blessed are the peacemakers; for they shall be called children of God. For since God is above the world, surely no one can see God unless he first transcends the world with spiritual gaze. And so the seventh blessing is that of the peaceable; seven baskets of fragments, too, remain over for those four thousand. No ordinary bread is this bread of the sabbaths, bread sanctified, bread of rest. And perhaps, if you have first eaten the five loaves with the senses, I will dare to say it too: after the five loaves and the seven, the third time you will not eat bread on earth, but above the earth you will eat eight loaves, like those who are in the heavenly places; for as the seven loaves are loaves of rest, so the eight loaves are loaves of the resurrection. So these who are fed with seven loaves persevered for three days, and perhaps attained an entire faith and constancy in the resurrection to come. Indeed, it is the voice of the saints: we will walk three days, that we may feast to the Lord our God. But those things in their place.

81. But concerning the five loaves Saint John taught me what I did not know, what Saint Matthew had not taught, what Saint Luke had not taught — for a different grace was imparted to each one — he taught me, I say, that these five loaves were of barley. And therefore we said not unfittingly that this food is suited to those who are of the body. For why barley first? Because he who comes to faith must be nourished first with milk, then with solid food; for we were not yet able, and perhaps many of us are not able even now. For since there are contentions and dissensions among us, are we not carnal and walking according to man? Each food is suited to its own strengths; and therefore first barley, then wheat is given us for nourishment, while to the stronger, as to Elijah, bread of meal from the inner part of the grain is supplied. But whom can sinners not defile, but only feed?

82. And there is a difference not only of bread and of number, but also of reclining; for those sit down upon the grass, these recline upon the ground; upon the grass the five thousand, upon the ground the four thousand. It is more to press the ground than to lie upon the grass; for those who still have bodily senses delight in softer things, and therefore recline upon the grass — for all flesh is grass — but these upon the ground, which brings forth wheat and wine and the olive, obtain the food of grace. Those sit down, these recline; for there is a certain greater rest in one who reclines. There two fishes, here without number; hence many have thought that the grace of the sevenfold Spirit is to be understood as defined in the loaves, and in the fishes too the figure of the twofold Testament.

83. We are not reluctant, then, to think that the four thousand, gathered from the four parts of the world, in which the Church is figured, take food of greater grace, according to what is written: because they shall come from the east and the west and from the north and the south, and shall recline with Abraham and Isaac and Jacob in the kingdom of heaven. Hence here thanks are given to the Father by Christ; for not in vain is there only a blessing in the one place, but here also a giving of thanks. For the Lord is wont to give thanks to the Father for his Church, because what he hid from the wise he has revealed to little ones. The blessing, then, is upon us, who are lower; the thanksgiving upon those who by sacred sufferings have triumphed over the infirmities of their bodies. We have already touched on this above, that whereas they are bidden to sit down by fifties, though with a sacred number defined, here, however, there seems to be the people of a more firmly founded Church, which reclines without any defining of number.

84. It is mystical too that the people eat and are satisfied, and the apostles serve; for in the being satisfied is marked the sign of hunger driven off forever, because he will not hunger who has received the food of Christ; and in the ministry of the apostles the future distribution of the Lord's body and blood is foreshadowed. Now that is divine, how the five loaves overflowed for five thousand people; for it is clear that the people was satisfied not with scanty food but with food multiplied.

85. You would have seen, by a kind of incomprehensible watering, pieces they had not broken bearing fruit between the hands of those who were dividing, and fragments untouched by the fingers of those who were breaking springing up of their own accord. How should one who reads these things marvel at the perpetual courses of the waters, and be amazed that unbroken successions flow from liquid springs, when even bread overflows and a watering of a more solid nature abounds? These things, then, were done so that we might discern those things too which we do not discern. By these he clearly showed that he is also the author of those things and the creator of the whole of material nature, which — not found, as the philosophers would have it, but made — supplies flowing successions for the generating of all things.

86. This indeed is a wonder, that whatever you draw from rivers is marked by no sign of loss, that whatever you take from springs is restored by a kind of usurious flow. But even in rivers, if nothing seems to be lost, still nothing seems to be added; and in springs, as the heaped-up streams are seen, so, when the streams are diminished, this is betrayed. But this bread which Jesus breaks is in the mystical sense the word of God and discourse about Christ, which increases while it is divided; for from a few words he has supplied nourishment overflowing for all peoples. He has given us words like loaves, which are doubled in our mouth while they are tasted. Visibly too this bread, in an incredible manner, while it is broken, while it is divided, while it is eaten, is heaped up without any perceptible loss.

87. Nor should you doubt whether the food grows in the hands of those serving or in the mouth of those eating, since everywhere the testimony of our work is enlisted to strengthen belief. So at the wedding wines took their color from the springs while the servants were at work, and those very men who had filled the water jars with water drew out wine which they had not brought. Grasp, if you can, such great wonders of things. Here, as the peoples eat, the fragments grow by their own loss, and from five loaves greater leftovers are gathered than the whole amount; there the elements are turned into an appearance not their own, and nature neither suffers its own diminishing nor recognizes its own origins, yet recognizes its proper uses. Nay more, the nature of wine that is changed is better than that of wine that is born, because it lies in the will of the Creator both what uses he will assign to natures and what natures he will impart to things to be produced. See with how many works he builds up the work. While the servant pours in water, the fragrance poured into it intoxicates, the changed color gives it form, and the taste, once drunk, heaps up faith as well.

88. Let the pagans compare, if they please, with the benefits of Christ, not the deeds but the fictions of their gods. Their fables certainly tell that there was a certain king for whom whatever he touched became gold. But even his very banquets were deadly; for the very napkins stiffened when grasped by his fingers, and the food crackled in his mouth, bringing not nourishment but wounds, and the drink stuck in his throat, able neither to go down nor to come back. Benefits worthy of the prayers, gifts worthy of so great a petitioner, liberality worthy of the giver! Such are the benefits of idols, that when they seem to help, they harm the more. But the gifts of Christ seem small and are very great, and they are bestowed not on one man but on peoples; for the food of those who ate grew in their mouths, and it seemed to be bodily nourishment, but it was taken as the nourishment of eternal salvation.

89. But why is there more left over for the five thousand men, less for the four thousand? Because those four thousand were three days with Christ, and therefore received more of the heavenly fodder.

90. And not idly are the things left over from the crowds gathered by the disciples, because the things that are divine you can find more easily among the chosen than among the peoples. Would that it might be my lot to hear: gather what is left over! If I hear and do it, I shall have many things which the crowds, I shall have many things which the children and the women could not eat. Blessed is he who can gather what is left over even by the learned.

91. Let us see how one gathers. The Law said: you shall not commit adultery. Christ broke this bread, divided this word, not adding from elsewhere, but sharing out from what was his own. He who has looked he says at a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her. You have a fragment from his own. He added: if your right eye causes you to stumble, pluck it out. You have another: and if your right hand causes you to stumble, cut it off; and: he who marries a woman put away by her husband commits adultery. You see how many fragments from one. Moses says that Abraham had two sons, one by the bondwoman and one by the free woman. Paul said: these are the two testaments. He divided this word and found a mystery. Blessed, then, is he who gathers what Christ divides.

92. For what reason did Christ fill twelve baskets, unless to undo that word about the Jewish people, that his hands served in the basket, that is, the people who formerly gathered clay in baskets now, through the cross of Christ, works the nourishment of heavenly life and heaps up the food of faith where before there was the filth of Gentile unbelief? Nor is this gift for a few, but for all. For through the twelve baskets there overflows, as it were, the strengthening of the faith of each tribe; for bread strengthens the heart of man.

93. And he said to them: whom do you say that I am? Simon Peter answered: the Christ of God. Not even the crowd's opinion is idle, because some believed that Elijah, whom they thought was to come, others that John, whom they knew had been beheaded, or one of the earlier prophets, had risen. But to inquire into this is beyond us: it belongs to another's judgment, to another's wisdom. For if it is enough for Paul the Apostle to know nothing but Christ Jesus and him crucified, what more ought I to desire to know than Christ? For in this one name is the expression both of the divinity and of the Incarnation, and faith in the Passion. And therefore, although the other apostles know, yet Peter answers before the rest: you are the Christ, the Son of the living God. And so he embraced all things, who expressed both the nature and the name, in which is the sum of powers. Do we even raise questions about the generation of God, when Paul judged that he knew nothing but Christ Jesus and him crucified, and Peter thought that nothing else was to be confessed but the Son of God? We, with the regard of human weakness, pry into both when and how he was born and how great he is. Paul knew that in these things there was more of a stumbling block of questioning than of progress in edification, and therefore he judged that he knew nothing but Christ Jesus. Peter knew that in the Son of God are all things; for all things the Father has given to the Son. If he gave all things, he poured into him the eternity which he has, and majesty. But whither am I slipping further? The end of my faith is Christ, the end of my faith is the Son of God. It is not permitted me to know the sequence of the generation; yet it is not permitted me to be ignorant of faith in the generation.

94. Believe, then, as Peter believed, so that you too may be blessed, so that you too may deserve to hear: because flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, but my Father who is in heaven. For flesh and blood can reveal only the things that are earthly; but he who speaks mysteries in the spirit is grounded not by the teaching of flesh and blood but by divine inspiration. Do not, then, give your assent to flesh and blood, lest you drink in the precepts of flesh and blood and yourself become blood and flesh. For he who clings to the flesh is flesh, and he who clings to God is one spirit. My spirit shall not remain he says in these men forever, because they are flesh.

95. But would that those who hear were not flesh and blood, but that, withdrawn from the desires of flesh and blood, each could say: I will not fear what flesh may do to me! For he who has conquered the flesh is a foundation of the Church, and if he cannot equal Peter, he can imitate him; for great are the gifts of God, who has not only restored to us what had been ours, but has also granted us what is properly his own.

96. It matters, however, what is meant by the fact that the crowd supposed him to be no other than either Elijah or Jeremiah or John the Baptist. Elijah perhaps, because he was caught up to heaven. But Christ is not Elijah. The one is caught up, this one returns; this one did not think it robbery to be equal with God; the one is avenged by fires called down, this one chose to heal his persecutors rather than destroy them. But why did they think him Jeremiah? Perhaps because he was sanctified in the womb. But this is not Jeremiah. The one is sanctified, this one sanctifies; the sanctification of the one began from the body, this one is holy from the Holy. Why, too, does the people think him John, unless perhaps because, placed in his mother's womb, John perceived the presence of the Lord? But this is not John. The one worshipped in the womb, this one was worshipped; the one baptized in water, Christ in the Spirit; the one urged repentance, this one forgave sins.

97. And therefore Peter did not wait for the people's opinion, but brought out his own, saying: you are the Christ, the Son of the living God. He who is, is always; he neither began in order to be, nor ceases to be. Great, moreover, is the grace of Christ, who has given almost all his own names to his disciples. I am he says the light of the world, and yet he granted to his disciples the very name in which he himself glories, saying: you are the light of the world. I am the living bread; and: we all are one bread. I am the true vine; and he says to you: I have planted you a fruitful vine, all true. The rock is Christ — for they drank of the spiritual rock that followed, and the rock was Christ —: he did not deny even to his disciple the grace of this name, so that he too should be Peter, because he has from the rock the solidity of constancy, the firmness of faith.

98. Strive, then, that you too may be a rock. And so seek the rock not outside yourself but within yourself. Your rock is your action, your rock is your mind. Upon this rock your house is built, so that it can be battered by no storms of spiritual wickedness. Your rock is faith; faith is the foundation of the Church. If you are a rock, you will be in the Church, because the Church is upon the rock. If you are in the Church, the gates of the underworld will not prevail against you. The gates of the underworld are the gates of death, and the gates of death cannot be the gates of the Church.

99. And what are the gates of death, that is, the gates of the underworld, but each and every sin? If you have committed fornication, you have gone in at the gates of death. If you have violated faith, you have passed through the gates of the underworld. If you have committed a mortal sin, you have entered the gates of death. But God is mighty, who can lift you up from the gates of death, that you may proclaim all his praises in the gates of the daughter of Zion. But the gates of the Church are the gates of chastity, the gates of righteousness, which the righteous man is wont to enter, saying: open to me the gates of righteousness, and having entered into them I will confess to the Lord. But as the gate of death is the gate of the underworld, so too the gate of righteousness is the gate of God; for this is the gate of the Lord; the righteous shall enter by it. And therefore flee from persistence in offences, lest the gates of the underworld be able to prevail against you; for if sin has gained dominion in you, the gate of death has prevailed. Flee, then, contentions, dissensions, the din and tumults of discord, lest you fall into an entrance through the gates of death. For the Lord Jesus Christ did not wish at first to be preached, lest any din should arise. He rebukes his disciples, that they should tell no one these things: that the Son of Man must suffer many things and be rejected by the chief priests and the elders and the scribes and be killed and rise again on the third day.

100. And perhaps he added this because the Lord knew that even the disciples would hardly believe in the Passion and the resurrection. And therefore he preferred to be himself the one who bore witness to his Passion and resurrection, so that faith might arise from the deed, not discord from what was heard. So Christ did not wish to glory, but preferred to seem ignoble, that he might undergo the Passion: and do you, who were born ignoble, glory? Must you not go by the same way by which Christ walked, the way by which he himself walked? This is to know him, this is to imitate him, through ill repute and good repute, that you may glory in the cross, as he himself gloried. So Paul walked, and therefore he glories, saying: but far be it from me to glory except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ.

101. But let us see why according to Matthew we find that the disciples were warned to tell no one that he was the Christ, while here it is written that they were rebuked, that they should tell no one these things: that he was to suffer many things and to rise again. You see that in the one name of Christ are all things. For he is the Christ who was born of a virgin, he it is who did wonders among the people, he who died for our sins and rose from the dead. If you take away one of these, you have taken away your salvation. For even heretics seem to have Christ for themselves; for no one denies the name of Christ, but he denies Christ who does not confess all the things that are Christ's. For many reasons, then, he commands the disciples to be silent: to deceive the prince of the world, to shun boasting, to teach humility, and at the same time lest the disciples, still untrained and imperfect, be overwhelmed by the weight of the greatest preaching.

102. Now let us consider for what reason he commands even the unclean spirits to be silent. But this too Scripture has opened to us, for God said to the sinner: why do you declare my righteous acts? Lest anyone, while he hears him preaching, follow him in his error; for the devil is a wicked teacher, who often mixes false things with true, so as to cover the testimony of fraud with the appearance of truth.

103. Let us consider this too: whether he here commanded the disciples for the first time to tell no one that he was the Christ, or whether he had commanded it before as well, when he appointed the twelve apostles, declaring to them: do not go away into the ways of the Gentiles, and do not enter into the cities of the Samaritans; but go to the sheep that have perished of the house of Israel. Cure the sick, cleanse the lepers, cast out demons; and: learn who in it is worthy, and stay there. And so not even here does it seem to have been commanded that they should preach Christ the Son of God.

104. The order of the discussion, then, is an order of teaching; and therefore we too, when any from the Gentiles are called to the Church, ought so to arrange the sequence of precepts that we first teach that there is one God, author of the world and of all things, in whom we live and are and move, whose offspring too we are, so that he is to be loved by us not only for the gifts of light and life but also for a certain kinship of race; then let us destroy that opinion which there is about idols, that the matter of gold and silver or wood cannot have divine power in itself. When you have persuaded them that there is one God, then by his evidence you will establish that salvation was given us through Christ, beginning from the things which he did in the body and describing them as divine, so that he may be seen to have been more than man: death conquered by the power of one, a dead man raised from the dead; for faith grows little by little, so that when he is seen to have been above man he may be believed to be God. For unless you prove that he could not have done those things without divine power, how can you prove that there was divine power in him?

105. But perhaps too little authority and credit may be granted to us: read the speech the Apostle delivered before the Athenians; if he had wished from the first to destroy the ceremonies of the idols, the ears of the Gentiles would have spurned his speech. He begins, then, from the one God, the maker of the world, saying: God who made the world and all things that are in it. They could not deny that there is one maker of the world, one God, one creator of all things. He added to this that the Lord of heaven and earth does not deign to dwell in things made with hands; then that it is in no way likely that the power of divinity is shut up by the craft of man within the empty matter of gold and silver, teaching that the remedy for error is the zeal of repentance. Then he came to Christ, and yet he preferred to name him not God but man, saying: in the man by whom he has determined faith for all, raising him from the dead. For he who teaches ought to consider the persons of his hearers, lest he be mocked before he is heard. For when would the Athenians believe that the Word was made flesh and that a virgin conceived of the Spirit, they who mocked because they had heard of the resurrection of the dead? Yet Dionysius the Areopagite believed, and others believed in the man, so that they might believe in God. What difference does it make in what order each one believes? Perfect things are not sought at the beginnings, but from the beginnings one comes to the things that are perfect. With such a precept, then, he instructed the Athenians; such an order must be kept by us among the Gentiles.

106. But when the apostles spoke to the Jews, they said that he was the Christ who had been promised to us by the prophetic oracles; they did not at first, on their own authority, name him the Son of God, but a man approved, a righteous man, a man raised from the dead, that man of whom it was said in the prophets: you are my Son, today I have begotten you. So do you too, for the things that are hard to believe, call in the authority of the divine word, and point out his coming as promised by the voice of the prophets; teach also, by the testimonies of the Scriptures, the resurrection, known long beforehand, not one usual and common with others, so that in the very assertion of the body raised you may gain a testimony of eternal divinity. For when you have proved that the bodies of others underwent corruption after death, you demonstrate that this one, of whom it is said: you will not give your Holy One to see corruption, had no part in human frailty; you prove that he surpassed the deserts of the human condition, and is to be compared with God rather than with men.

107. But if a catechumen who is approaching the sacraments of the faithful is to be instructed, he must be told that there is one God, from whom all things, and one Jesus Christ, through whom all things; that we must not speak of two Lords; that the Father indeed is perfect and the Son also is perfect, but that the Father and the Son are of one substance; that the Word of God is eternal, not one which is uttered but one which works, begotten of the Father, not produced by the voice. The apostles, then, are forbidden to preach him as the Son of God, so that they might afterwards preach him crucified. This is the glory of faith, if you truly understand the cross of Christ. Other crosses profit me nothing; the cross of Christ alone is useful to me, and truly useful, through which the world is crucified to me and I to the world. If the world is crucified to me, I know that it is dead: I do not love it; I know that it passes away: I do not covet it; I know that corruption will consume this world: I avoid it as foul, I beware of it as a plague, I leave it as something that will do harm.

108. But some cannot at once believe that salvation was poured back upon the world through the cross. Establish, then, through the history of the Greeks that this was possible. So at times the Apostle too persuades unbelievers, and he does not shun the verses of the poets, so as to destroy the fables of the poets. For if it is called to mind that the histories of the Greeks assert that legions, and often great peoples, were set free by the offered deaths of certain persons; if they remember that the commander's daughter was summoned to sacrifice so that the armies of the Greeks might be carried across; if we consider that word of ours, that the blood of goats and bulls and the ashes of a heifer, sprinkled, sanctify the defiled to the cleansing of the flesh, as is written to the Hebrews; if a pestilence of regions, contracted through the sins of certain men, is asserted to have been dispelled by the death of some one man — something which either prevailed by reason or gained strength by arrangement, so that the cross of the Lord might be believed the more easily — it will be an easy step for those who cannot deny their own things to confirm ours.

109. But since no man could be so great as to take away the sins of the whole world, neither Enoch nor Abraham nor Isaac — who, though he offered himself to death, was yet preserved, because he could not abolish all sins; for what man is so great that in him the sins of all should die? — therefore not one from the people, not one from the multitude, but the Son of God was chosen by God the Father, who, since he was above all, could offer himself for all. It was fitting that he should die, so that, being stronger than death, he might set others free, having become free among the dead without help, that is, free without the aid of man or of any creature, free from death; and rightly free, he who repelled the slavery of the desires and knew not the bonds of death.

Book Seven

1. But I say to you truly: there are some standing here who shall not taste death until they see the kingdom of God. Just as the Lord always raises us toward the future rewards of the virtues and teaches that contempt for the things of the world is profitable, so too he sustains the weakness of the human mind with a reward in things present. For it is hard to take up the cross and to offer one's life to dangers and one's body to death, to deny what you are when you would wish to be what you are not; and rarely does virtue, however lofty, exchange present things for future ones. For it seems difficult to men to buy hope with dangers and to purchase the gain of a future age with the loss of present things. Therefore the good and moral Master, lest anyone be broken by despair or weariness, since the allurement of sweet life soothes even a steadfast disposition, promises the faithful an unbroken continuance of living; for consolations grow cold under the fear of death, and a great love of life barely sets the blandishments of hope against the terror of the safety it has sought. You have nothing, then, to complain of, nothing to excuse yourself with: the Judge of all has given both a reward to virtue and a remedy to weakness, so that weakness may be sustained by things present, virtue by things to come. If you are strong, despise death; if you are weak, flee it. But no one can flee death unless he follows life. Your life is Christ; he is the very life that knows not how to die.

2. So if we wish not to fear death, let us stand where Christ is, so that he may say of us too: truly there are some standing here who shall not taste death. It is not enough to stand, unless one stands where Christ is; for they alone cannot taste death who are able to stand with Christ. Here one may weigh from the very nature of the word that those who are seen to have deserved the fellowship of Christ will not have even a slight sense of death. Certainly the death of the body is tasted by sipping, but the life of the soul is held by possessing.

3. But what is it to taste death? Can it be that, just as bread is life, so bread is also death? For there are those who eat the bread of sorrow; there are also the peoples of the Ethiopians, who have received the dragon for food. Far be it from us to devour the venom of the dragon; for we have the true bread, that bread which came down from heaven. He eats that bread who keeps the things that are written. There are, therefore, those who do not taste death until they see the kingdom of God. There are also those who do not see death, as it is written: who is the man who shall live and shall not see death?

4. But who is the man who does not die, since there can be no resurrection except of the dead? Although we have heard of no death of the body concerning Enoch and Elijah, and the Lord said of John the evangelist: if I will him to remain until I come, yet since we think that here it was not expressed of John alone, but that this was laid down generally of many, here it is not the death of the body but that of the soul that is denied. For there are those who live though dead, since there are those who are dead though living, like her who living is dead, as it is written: let death come upon them, and let them go down living into hell. If, then, anyone goes down living into hell — for by sin he goes down into hell, the dwelling of death — there are surely those for whom the course of living has not been broken off even in the death of the body, like Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, whom we learn by the authority of the divine sentence to be living, since, as he is the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, he is certainly not the God of the dead but of the living.

5. So he speaks not of one but of very many; for neither did Peter die, against whom, according to the Lord's sentence, the gate of the underworld could not prevail, nor did James and John die, the sons of thunder, over whom, taken up for the use of heavenly glory, earthly things do not prevail but lie beneath them. Be you too, then, a Peter, devout, faithful, peaceable, so that you may open the gates of the Church and escape the gates of death. Be a son of thunder. You say: how can I be a son of thunder? You can be, if you recline not on the earth but on the breast of Christ. You can be a son of thunder, if earthly things do not move you, but you rather shake the things that are earthly by the virtue of your mind. Let the earth tremble at you, not take you captive; let the flesh fear the power of your soul and, being shaken, be subdued. You will be a son of thunder if you are a son of the Church. Let Christ say to you too from the gibbet of the cross: behold your mother, let him say to the Church: behold your son; for then you will begin to be a son of the Church, when you see Christ victorious on the cross. For he who thinks the cross a stumbling block is a Jew, he is not a son of the Church; he who thinks the cross foolishness is a Greek. But he is a son of the Church who thinks the cross a triumph, if he recognizes the voice of Christ triumphant.

6. So that you may know, then, that Peter, John and James did not taste death, they were found worthy to see the glory of the resurrection; for he took these three alone after these words, within about eight days, and led them up a mountain. What is it that he says: within eight days after these words? Can it be because he who hears and believes the words of Christ will see the glory of Christ at the time of the resurrection? For the resurrection took place on the eighth day, whence many psalms too are inscribed for the eighth. Or perhaps it was to show us that he had said that he who loses his life for the word of God shall save it, since he restores his promises in the resurrection.

7. But Matthew and Mark recorded that these were taken after six days. Of this we could say: after six thousand years — for a thousand years in the sight of God are as one day —, but more than six thousand years are counted, and we prefer to understand the six days as a symbol, because the works of the world were created in six days, so that through the time we understand the works, and through the works the world. And therefore it is shown that the resurrection will come when the times of the world are fulfilled; or else because he who has ascended above the world and passed beyond the moments of this age, set as it were on high, will await the eternal fruit of the future resurrection.

8. Let us pass beyond the works of the world, then, so that we may see God face to face. Go up into the mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion. If he who brings good tidings to Zion goes up into the mountain, how much more he who brings the good tidings of Christ, and of Christ rising in glory? For perhaps many may see him in the body; for many of us have known Christ according to the flesh, but now we know him no longer.

9. Many of us have known him, because many of us have seen him — we saw him, and he had no form nor comeliness —, but three alone, and three chosen, are led to the mountain. I would think that in the three the human race is comprehended in the mystical sense, since the whole human race flowed down from the three sons of Noah, did I not see that they were chosen. Or perhaps because only those who have confessed Christ will be worthy to come to the grace of the resurrection, since the ungodly do not rise again in judgment, but are punished as though the judgment had already been held. Three, therefore, are chosen to go up the mountain, since two also are chosen to be seen with the Lord. On both sides a sacred number. And this perhaps for this reason, that no one can see the glory of the resurrection except one who has kept the mystery of the Trinity whole with the incorrupt sincerity of faith. Peter goes up, who received the keys of the kingdom of heaven; John, to whom his mother is entrusted; James, who first mounted the priestly throne;

10. then Moses and Elijah appear, that is, the Law and the prophet with the Word; for neither can the Law be without the Word, nor is anyone a prophet except one who prophesied of the Son of God. And those sons of thunder indeed beheld Moses and Elijah too in bodily glory, but we too see Moses with the Son of God every day; for we see the Law in the Gospel when we read: you shall love the Lord your God; we see Elijah with the Word of God when we read: behold, a virgin shall conceive in the womb.

11. Hence Luke rightly added that they spoke of his departure which he was to accomplish in Jerusalem. For the mysteries teach you of his departure. Today too Moses teaches, and today Elijah speaks, and today we can see Moses in greater glory. For who cannot, when even the people of the Jews could see him, indeed did see him? For it saw the face of Moses in glory, but it received a veil, but it did not go up the mountain, and therefore it went astray. He who saw Moses alone could not at the same time see the Word of God.

12. Let us, then, unveil our face, so that, beholding the glory of God with unveiled face, we may be re-formed into the same image. Let us go up the mountain, let us beseech the Word of God to appear to us in his form and beauty, and to be strengthened and to proceed prosperously and to reign. For these too are mysteries and are referred to a higher sense; for according to your capacity the Word is either lessened for you or grows, and unless you climb the summit of a loftier wisdom, wisdom does not appear to you, the knowledge of the mysteries does not appear to you, it does not appear to you how great is the glory, how great the beauty in the Word of God; but the Word of God appears as it were in a kind of body, not having his form nor comeliness, and he appears as a man under a stroke, who could bear our infirmities; he appears to you as a sort of speech born of man, covered with the wrappings of letters, not shining with the power of the Spirit. But if, while you consider the man, you believe him to have been begotten of a virgin, and little by little faith breathes on you that he was born of the Spirit of God, you begin to go up the mountain. If you see him set on the cross triumphing over death, not destroyed; if you see that the earth trembled, the sun fled back, darkness was poured over the eyes of the unbelieving, the tombs were opened, the dead rose again, so that it might be a sign that the Gentile people, which had been dead to God, as though the tombs of its body had been unsealed, rose again when the light of the cross was poured in; if you see this mystery, you have gone up the lofty mountain, you behold the other glory of the Word.

13. His garments are some below, others above. And perhaps the garments of the Word are the sayings of the Scriptures and, as it were, certain clothings of the divine understanding; for just as he appeared to Peter and John and James in another form and his clothing shone white, so too to the eyes of your mind the sense of the divine readings now grows white. The divine words, therefore, become like snow, the garments of the Word exceedingly white, such as no fuller on earth can make.

14. Let us seek this fuller, let us seek this snow. We read that Isaiah went up to the fuller's field. Who is this fuller, unless perhaps he who is wont to wash away our sins? Indeed he himself said: if your sins be as scarlet, I will make them white as snow. Who is this fuller, unless he who is wont to offer to the divine Sun the garments of our understanding, the garments of the virtues, with their bodily stains washed away?

15. I have heard too — that we may take an example from contraries to refute them — that ⟨...⟩ compared the eloquence of two wise men to snow and to bees. I have found too that David said: how sweet are your words to my throat, above honey and the honeycomb to my mouth! And below: your word is a lamp to my feet, O Lord, and a light to my paths. The Word of God is light, the Word of God is snow. The Word of God is also above honey and the honeycomb, because from the divine mouth speech flowed sweeter than honey, and clear words fall after the manner of snow with gentler meanings. Truly this speech alone is to be compared to the snows, which, sent from heaven to the earth, has made fruitful the barren fields of our hearts. That this is not presumed by usurpation but derived from the sequence of the reading, God himself testifies, saying: let my speech be awaited as the rain, and let my words come down as the dew, as the shower upon the grass and as the snow upon the hay.

16. O that my mind, Lord Jesus, might grow green with the moisture of your rain; O that you would sprinkle my earth with the whiteness of that snow, lest the fields of the body run riot with the over-hasty heat of springtime, but rather that the seeds of the heavenly Word, pressed down by the snowy warmth, may make them fruitful! When snow falls, the birds of the air have nowhere to dwell, and the harvest of wheat abounds more richly than usual.

17. Peter saw this grace, and those who were with him saw it too, even though they had been heavy with sleep. For the incomprehensible splendor of the divinity overwhelms the senses of our body. For if the bodily eye cannot bear a ray of the sun striking straight into the eyes of those who gaze at it, how could the corruption of human limbs bear the glory of God? And so the condition of the body is formed purer and finer in the resurrection, when the concretion of vices has been drained away. And perhaps they were heavy with sleep for this reason, that they might see the form of the resurrection after rest. And so, waking up, they saw his majesty; for no one sees the glory of Christ unless he is awake. Peter was delighted, and he whom the allurements of this world could not capture was enticed by the grace of the resurrection.

18. It is good he says for us to be here — hence that other also says: for to be dissolved and to be with Christ is far better — and, not content to have praised it, more excellent not only in affection but also in devotion of deeds, a ready workman, he promises the service of common obedience to build three tabernacles. And although he did not know what he was saying, yet he promised a duty by which not thoughtless forwardness but premature devotion heaps up the fruits of dutiful love. For that he did not know belonged to his condition; that he promised belonged to his devotion. But the human condition does not permit, in this corruptible, in this mortal body, the making of a tabernacle for God. Whether in the mind or in the body or in some other place, shun seeking what it is not lawful to know. If Peter did not know, how can you know? If he who made the promise, and who by the greatness of his mind knew nothing of the limits of the body, did not know, how can we know, shut in within the enclosure of the flesh by a certain sloth of mind? In the end, such great devotion pleased God.

19. And as he was saying these words a cloud came and overshadowed them. This overshadowing is of the divine Spirit; it does not darken men's affections but reveals hidden things. It is also found in another place, when the angel says: and the power of the Most High shall overshadow you. Its effect is shown when the voice of God is heard, saying: this is my beloved Son, hear him,

20. that is: not Elijah the Son, not Moses the Son, but this is the Son, whom alone you see; for they had withdrawn when the Lord began to be pointed out. You see that this is the perfect faith not only of beginners but also of the perfect, indeed even of the heavenly ones: to know the Son of God. But since we have already spoken of these things above, know that this cloud is not one congealed from the misty moisture of smoking mountains, nor the pitchy gloom of condensed air which veils the sky with the horror of darkness, but a bright cloud, which does not drench us with rain waters and the flood of a soaking shower, but from which, when the voice of almighty God was sent forth, the dew of faith watered the minds of men.

21. And when the voice was made, Jesus was found alone. So, although they were three, they became one. Three are seen at the beginning, one at the end; for in perfect faith they are one. Indeed the Lord also asks this of the Father, that we may all be one. And not only are Moses and Elijah one in Christ, but we too are one body of Christ. Therefore they too are, as it were, received into the body of Christ, because we too shall be one in Christ Jesus; or perhaps because the Law and the prophets come from the Word, and the things that began from the Word end in the Word; for the end of the Law is Christ for righteousness to everyone who believes.
