22. The foxes have holes and the birds of the air nests, where they may rest; but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head. It does not seem consistent with reason that we should judge this man simple and faithful who is rejected from the Lord's favor, though he had pledged the obedience of untiring service; but the Lord seeks not the appearance of obedience but purity of disposition. Indeed he says above: whoever receives this child in my name.

23. In this place the Lord teaches that there ought to be simplicity without arrogance, charity without envy, devotion without anger; for we are urged that the mind even of a child is to be received with the disposition of one more advanced, because the child, while he claims nothing for himself, carries out the pattern of virtue and, if he does not know reason, knows nothing of guilt. Yet because to most people simplicity without reason seems not virtue but weakness, you are admonished to receive the true simplicity, that is, to carry out by diligence the gift of nature.

24. And therefore he says: whoever receives this child in my name receives me. And he who receives me receives him who sent me. For he who receives an imitator of Christ receives Christ, and he who receives the image of God receives God. But since we could not see the image of God, he was made present to us through the Incarnation of the Word, so that the divinity which is above us might be reconciled to us.

25. But as for John, who, more inclined through zeal of charity, loving very much and therefore very much loved in return, thinks that he who does not follow in obedience should be shut out from the benefit, he is rightly not rebuked, but taught; he is not rebuked, because he acted out of love; he is taught, so that he may know that there is a difference between the weak and the strong. And therefore the Lord, even though he rewards the stronger, yet does not shut out the weak.

26. Let them be and do not forbid them; for he who is not against you is for you. Well said, Lord; for Joseph and Nicodemus too, disciples in secret for fear, yet in due time did not deny you their service. But since you said elsewhere: he who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters, open it to us, lest it seem contradictory. And I think that if anyone considers the Searcher of minds, he ought not to doubt that each man's deed is judged by his mind. Indeed to one he says: follow me, to another: the foxes have holes. The one is urged on, the other is put away, so that you may know that the devout man is admitted, the undevout is shut out.

27. But as for his rebuking the disciples because they longed for fire to come down upon those who had not received Christ, it is shown us that vengeance must not always be taken on those who have sinned, since sometimes clemency profits more, you for patience, the fallen for correction. Indeed the Samaritans, from whom fire is warded off in this place, believed the sooner. At the same time learn that he did not wish to be received by those whom he knew had not turned with a simple mind; for had he wished, he would have made the undevout devout. But why they did not receive him the evangelist himself has recorded, saying: because his face was that of one going to Jerusalem. The disciples, however, longed for him to be received within Samaria, but God calls whom he deigns to and makes religious whom he wills. Nor do the disciples sin, who follow the Law; for they knew both that it was reckoned to Phinehas for righteousness that he had slain the sacrilegious, and that at the prayers of Elijah fire came down from heaven, so that the wrong done to the prophet might be avenged. But let him who fears be avenged; he who does not fear does not seek vengeance. At the same time it is shown us that the merits of the prophets were in the apostles, when they presume for themselves, by right of asking it, the same power which the great prophet earned. And rightly do they presume that fire would come down from heaven at their word, since they are sons of thunder.

28. But the Lord does all things wonderfully. He neither receives that man who offers himself rashly, nor is he moved on his own account against these who undevoutly reject the Lord, to show that perfect virtue has no zeal for revenge, and that there is no anger where there is the fullness of charity, and that weakness is not to be shut out but helped. Far from religious men be indignation, far from the great-souled the desire for revenge, far too from the prudent an inconsiderate attachment and an incautious simplicity. Hence it is said to this man too: the foxes have holes, and the obedience of him whose service is not approved is not admitted. For the hospitality of faith ought to be circumspect, lest, while we unbar the inner rooms of our house to unbelievers, we slip by improvident credulity into the snares of another's unbelief.

29. But lest we seem to have improvidently passed over the question why here he says that those who can command unclean spirits in the name of Jesus through the laying on of hands are not to be forbidden, whereas according to Matthew he says to them: I do not know you. Depart from me, all you workers of iniquity, we ought to observe that there is no difference of sense or discord of sayings, but that the latter is to be judged by this, that not only the duties of office but also the works of virtue are required in a cleric, and that the name of Christ is so great that it helps even those who are not very holy to protection, even if it does not help them to grace. Hence no one ought to boast or claim for himself the credit for a man cleansed, in whom the power of the eternal Name has worked, not any capacity of human frailty; for the devil is conquered not by your merit but by hatred of him.

30. Let a man, as far as he can, show a sincere faith and keep the observance of the commandments with a religious mind, lest it be said to him: the foxes have holes. For it is a deceitful animal, always intent on ambushes, and it practices the plunder of fraud. It allows nothing to be safe, nothing to be at rest, nothing to be secure, since it seeks its prey among the very dwellings of men.

31. Now he compares heretics to foxes. Indeed, when he calls the Gentiles, he shuts out the heretics; for the fox is an animal full of fraud, preparing a hole and always desiring to lurk in the hole. So are the heretics, who do not know how to prepare a house for themselves, but try to deceive others with their trickeries. Jacob dwells in a house; the heretic is in a hole, like the fraudulent fox always plotting a snare for that hen of the Gospel, of which it is written: how often would I have gathered your children as a hen her chickens, and you would not? Behold, your house will be left to you desolate. Rightly, therefore, do they have holes, because they have lost the house they had had. This animal is never tamed, whence the Apostle too says: avoid a heretic after one rebuke; nor is it of any use or good for food; for it is not of this that Christ says: my food is to do the will of my Father who is in heaven. Indeed he even banishes them from his fruits, saying: take for us the little foxes that destroy the vineyards, that is, those that destroy the lesser vineyard, not the greater. And therefore Samson tied torches to their tails and let them loose into the harvests of the foreigners, because heretics try to burn up the fruits of others, more resonant with barking than polished in speech — for he who denies the Word has no voice either —, free of mouth for the present, but for the future, their end already bound, signifying by the torches of the tail the burning of their own end.

32. The birds of the air too, which are often drawn into a likeness of spiritual wickedness, build as it were certain nests in the hearts of the wicked; and therefore the Son of Man, because iniquity has abounded, has nowhere to lay his head. For when cunning holds sway, since there is no room for simplicity, the divinity can have no possession in the affections of individual men. For the head of Christ is God, who, when he has found a mind innocent, in a manner lays upon it the weight of his majesty, which seems to be a sign that a more abundant grace is implanted in the hearts of the good.

33. And so, that you may observe that God spurns not service but fraud, he who had rejected the deceitful man chooses the innocent, saying: follow me. But he says this to one whose father he already knew to be dead, that father, certainly, of whom it was said to him: forget your father's house. See, then, that the Lord calls even the unwise whom he pities, and to the one asking leave for his father's burial he answered: let the dead bury their dead, but you go and proclaim the kingdom of God.

34. So, since we have received the duty of burying as a religious one, how is it that here even the burial of a father's corpse is forbidden, unless that you may understand that human things are to be put after divine? A good pursuit, but a greater hindrance; for he who divides his zeal draws off his affection, he who divides his care delays his progress. Therefore the greatest things must be undertaken first; for the apostles too, so as not to tie up their zeal for discoursing, ordained ministers for the poor, and they themselves, when they were sent by the Lord, were instructed to greet no one on the way, not because the duty of kindness displeased him, but because the intention of pursuing devotion pleased him more.

35. But how can the dead bury the dead, unless you understand here a twofold death, one of nature, the other of guilt? There is also a third death, in which we die to sin and live to God, like Christ, who died to sin; for in that he died, he died to sin once, but in that he lives, he lives to God.

36. There is, then, one death, by which the bond of body and soul is severed, not dreadful, not to be feared, since it seems to be a kind of departure of ourselves, not a punishment; not to be feared by the brave, to be desired by the wise, to be sought by the wretched, of which it was said: men shall seek death and shall not find it.

37. There is also another, which brings the destruction of worldly pleasures, in which not nature but sins die. This death we undergo when we are buried together in baptism and have died with Christ from the elements of this world, when we suffer oblivion of our former conduct. This death Balaam wished to die when he prophesied, that he might live to God, and therefore he said: let my soul die among the souls of the righteous, and let my seed be as their seed.

38. There is also a third death, when Christ, who is our life, is not known; but to know Christ is eternal life, which is now at hand for the righteous in shadow, but in the future will be face to face; for the Spirit before our face is Christ the Lord, of whom he said: in his shadow we shall live among the nations. In the shadow of his wings David hoped; in his shadow the Church desired and sat down.

39. If your shadow, Lord Jesus, confers so much, how much, surely, will the truth bring? How shall we live when we are no longer in the shadow but in life itself? For now our life is hidden with Christ in God; but when Christ, our life, appears, then we too he says shall appear with him in glory. Sweet is that life which has no death; for this life of the body has death by the lot of nature, a death which is often even desired. The soul itself too often has death through the stain of sin — for the soul that sins shall itself die —, but when, strengthened by the firmness of blessedness, it has begun not to be subject to sin, it will no longer be mortal, but will enjoy eternal life.

40. Toward this life, brethren, let us hasten, sorrowful in the world, because we are exiles from the Lord; for he who is not an exile from the body is an exile from the Lord. But it is far better to be dissolved from the body and to cleave to God, so that we too may be one in the presence of almighty God and, taken up through the glory of the resurrection into the brightness of nature and imitating, in the inviolable concord of souls, by an everlasting covenant, the unity of lasting peace, may see the only-begotten Son of God, so that what the Son of God promised concerning us in praying to the Father may be fulfilled. That they too may be one, as we also are one.

41. It is not, then, the burial of a father's corpse that is forbidden, but the dutiful love of divine religion is set before the bond of kinship; the one is left to his fellows, the other is enjoined on the chosen. Or else, because the throat of the ungodly is an open sepulchre, it is prescribed that the memory of those whose merit perished together with their body is to be blotted out; and the son is not called away from his duty to his father, but the faithful man is separated from communion with the unbelieving.

42. For there is a burial proper to the righteous, such as that of which it was said: for she, in pouring this ointment on my body, has done it for my burial; and therefore he who in good faith buries Christ within himself, so as to rise again with him, ought not to bury within himself the unbelief of the devil.

43. There is also that prophetic burial, that we should place certain things upon the tombs of our forebears, which you, reader, recognize, and which the unbeliever ought not to understand: not that food or drink is commanded, but that the venerable communion of the sacred oblation is revealed. It is not, then, a prohibition of the gift, but a mystery of religion, that there is to be no communion for us with the Gentile dead; for since the sacraments belong to the living, those who have life are not seen as dead.

44. Behold, I send you as lambs among wolves. He says this to the seventy disciples, whom he appointed and sent two by two before his face. For what reason did he send them two by two? Because the animals were sent into the ark two by two, that is, male with female, unclean by the number but made clean by the sacrament of the Church. This was fulfilled by the oracle which Saint Peter received, when the Holy Spirit said to him: what God has cleansed, do not call common. And he perceived that it was said of the Gentiles, who followed the succession of bodily generation rather than of spiritual grace. These the Lord cleansed and made heirs of his Passion.

45. So, sending the disciples into his harvest, which, although it was sown by the Word of God, still required the labor of cultivation and a careful service of tending, lest the birds of the air scatter the sown seeds, he says: behold, I send you as lambs among wolves.

46. These animals are opposed to one another, so that some are devoured by others. But the good shepherd does not know how to fear wolves for his flock, and therefore these disciples are sent not to be a prey but for grace; for the care of the good shepherd brings it about that the wolves can dare nothing against the lambs. He sends lambs, therefore, among wolves, so that that word might be fulfilled: then the wolves and the lambs shall feed together.

47. And since our recent discourse on the fox turned out not unwelcome, if I have found your judgment faithful in the figure of a very small animal, I presume that with the support of your zeal I may open the deep mysteries that are veiled under the form of wolves. We have set out above that the heretics are signified under the form of foxes, who promise in name that they follow Christ, but forswear him by their zeal for fraud. These the Lord does not receive, but keeps away and forbids from his nest. We must observe what the wolves seem to signify.

48. They are, of course, beasts that lie in wait for sheepfolds, prowl about the shepherds' huts, do not dare to enter the dwellings of houses, watch for the sleep of the dogs, the absence or sloth of the shepherd, and fly at the throat of the sheep so as to strangle them quickly. Fierce and rapacious, and also by the nature of their body rather stiff, so that they cannot easily turn themselves, they are carried along by a kind of headlong rush of their own and are therefore often tricked. Moreover, if they see a man first, they are said by a certain force of nature to snatch away his voice; but if the man sees them first, he is said to put them to flight. And therefore I must take care lest, if in today's discourse the grace of the spiritual mysteries has not been able to shine forth, the wolves be thought to have seen me first and to have wrung from me the accustomed support of my voice.

49. Are not the heretics to be compared to these wolves, who lie in wait for the sheepfolds of Christ and growl around the folds by night rather than by day? For it is always night for the unbelieving, who try to cover the light of Christ with the clouds of a perverse interpretation and, as far as in them lies, to darken it. They prowl, then, around the folds, yet they do not dare to enter the inns of Christ, and therefore they are not healed, because Christ does not wish to bring them into his inn, in which that man was cured who, going down from Jerusalem, fell among robbers. Him, when his wounds had been bound up and oil and wine poured in, that Samaritan placed on his own beast, brought to the inn and left to the innkeeper to be healed. They do not, then, receive medicine who do not seek the physician; for if they sought him, they would not disparage him.

50. They watch for the absence of the shepherd, and therefore they strive either to kill the shepherds of the churches or to drive them into exile, because while the shepherds are present they cannot attack the sheep of Christ. The robbers, then, try to despoil the Lord's flock, being hard and stiff with a kind of bodily bent of mind, and they are by no means wont to turn aside from their error. And therefore the Apostle says: avoid a heretical man after one rebuke, knowing that he who is such is perverted. These Christ, the true interpreter of Scripture, tricks, so that they may pour out their empty rushes to no purpose and may be unable to do harm.

51. If they forestall anyone with the cunning trickery of their disputation, they strike him dumb; for he is dumb who does not confess the Word of God in the same glory in which he is. Take care, then, lest the heretic take away your voice, if you have not first detected him yourself. For his unbelief creeps on while it lies hidden; but if you have recognized the fabrications of his ungodliness, you will have no cause to fear the loss of your devout voice. Beware, then, the poisons of cunning disputation; they seek the soul, they fly at the throat, they fix a wound in the vital parts. Grievous are the bites of the heretics, who, more grievous and more rapacious than the beasts themselves, know no end of greed and ungodliness.

52. Nor let it move you that they seem to put forward a human form; even if a man is seen outwardly, within a beast growls. And therefore there is no doubt that they are wolves, according to the divine sentence of the Lord Jesus, who says: beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly they are ravening wolves. By their fruits you shall know them. If, then, anyone is moved by appearance, let him question the fruit. You hear someone called a priest, you learn of his plunderings: he has the clothing of a sheep, the deeds of a robber; outwardly a sheep, inwardly he is a wolf, who knows no measure in plundering, who, as though hardened in limb by Scythian frost in the night, bloody of jaws, prowls around seeking whom he may devour. Does he not seem to you a wolf, who, insatiable in the rawness of human slaughter, desired to sate his madness with the death of faithful peoples?

53. He howls, he does not discourse, who denies the Author of the voice and with sacrilegious speech breaks in with a bestial growl, who does not confess the Lord Jesus as the ruler of eternal life. We heard his howlings when the sword was sent forth throughout the world. He displayed harsh teeth, swollen jaws, and thought that he had taken away from all the voice which he alone had lost. And therefore, so that we may escape these wolves, the Lord teaches what we are to follow, saying:

54. carry no purse nor bag nor shoes. What it means that a purse is not to be carried he has expressed plainly elsewhere; for Matthew wrote that the Lord said to the disciples: do not possess gold nor silver. If we are forbidden to possess gold, what of snatching it, what of carrying it off? If you are commanded to give away what you have, how do you heap up what you did not have? You who preach that one must not steal, do you steal? You who say that one must not commit adultery, do you commit adultery? You who abhor idols, do you commit sacrilege? You who glory in the Law, do you dishonor God by transgression of the Law? For the name of God is blasphemed through you.

55. Not so the apostle Peter, who, the first to carry out the Lord's sentence, in order to show that the Lord's commands had not been poured out in vain, when he was asked by a poor man to bestow some money on him, said: silver and gold I have none. He glories that he has no silver and gold; is it a shame to you to have as yet less than you desire? There is, then, a glorious poverty too, because there is also a blessed poverty, as it is written: blessed are the poor in spirit; yet Peter glories not so much in this, that he has no silver and gold, as in this, that he keeps the command of the Lord, who enjoined: do not possess gold; that is to say: you see that I am a disciple of Christ, and do you ask gold of me? He has given us something else far more precious than gold, that we should work in his name. So I do not have what he did not give, but what he gave I have: in the name of the Lord Jesus rise and walk.

56. Just as, then, he who wishes to build barns for storing up grain is reproved by the authority of the Lord's sentence, so he who wishes to provide a purse for hoarding gold incurs the blemish of reproof.

57. No bag nor shoes. Both are usually made of the hide of a dead animal; but the Lord Jesus wishes nothing mortal to be in us. Indeed he says to Moses: loose the shoe from your feet; for the place on which you stand is holy ground. He is bidden, then, to loose the mortal and earthly shoe when he was being sent to free the people; for the minister of this task ought to fear nothing nor be held back from the duty he has undertaken by the danger of death. For he himself, when he had willingly taken up the defense of his brethren, that is, of the Jews, was called back from what he had begun by the terror of a false charge and fled from Egypt. And therefore, since the Lord had approved his affection but had seen that his condition was weak, he judged that the footstep of his soul and mind should be stripped of mortal bonds.

58. But if it troubles anyone for what reason in Egypt they are bidden to eat the lamb with shoes on, whereas the apostles are sent without shoes to preach the Gospel, he ought to consider that one who is set in Egypt must still beware of the bites of the serpent — for there are many poisons in Egypt —, and he who celebrates the Pasch in a type can lie open to a wound, but he who is a minister of the truth blunts poisons and does not tremble. Indeed a viper bit Paul on the island of Melita, and the inhabitants of that place, seeing the viper hanging from his hand, thought that he was going to die; but when they saw him standing unharmed, they said that he was a god, whom the poison could not hurt. And that you may know that this is true, the Lord himself says: behold, I have given you power to tread upon serpents and scorpions and upon all the power of the enemy, and they shall do you no harm.

59. The apostles are bidden not to take staffs in their hand; for so Matthew thought it should be written. What is a staff but the badge of power displayed and the instrument for avenging pain? So the disciples of the humble Lord — for in humility his judgment was taken away —, of the humble Lord, I say, carry out his precept by the duties of humility; for he sent them to sow the faith, men who would not compel but teach, nor put forth the force of power, but exalt the doctrine of humility. In this place he thought that patience too should be joined to humility, because he himself also, according to the testimony of Peter, when he was reviled, did not revile in return; when he was struck, did not strike back. This, then, is to say: be imitators of me; lay aside the pursuit of revenge; beat back the insolence of those who strike you not by returning the injury, but by the magnanimity of patience. No one ought himself to imitate what he blames in another; gentleness returns heavier wounds upon the insolent. With this fist the Lord struck back at the one who strikes, by saying: to him who strikes you on the cheek, offer the other also. For it comes about that he who notices that kindness has been returned to him in exchange for the injury he did condemns himself by his own judgment and is pricked in his feelings by a kind of goad.

60. Yet he has apostles whom he sent even with a rod, as Paul testifies, saying: what do you wish? Shall I come to you with a rod, or in charity and the spirit of gentleness? This rod the Apostle also gave to Timothy, saying: reprove, entreat, rebuke. Or perhaps before the Lord's Passion, which made firm the hearts of wavering peoples, gentleness alone was needful, and after the Passion correction. Certainly let the Lord soothe, let Paul rebuke; let him persuade who can soften even hard hearts, let him reprove who cannot carry all things by persuasion. Paul, then, had taken up the rod from the teaching of the Law. For he had read: he who spares the rod hates his son. He had read too that those who ate the lamb were bidden by a prophetic command to have a staff in their hands. And therefore the Lord says in the Old Testament: I will visit their iniquities with the rod; but in the New he offered himself, so that he might pardon all, saying: if you seek me, let these go. And elsewhere you have it that, when the apostles wished to ask for fire from heaven to consume the Samaritans, who would not receive the Lord Jesus within their city, he turned and rebuked them and said: you do not know of what spirit you are; for the Son of Man came not to destroy men's souls but to save them.

61. The more perfect, then, are sent without a rod; the weaker eat with a staff. But Paul too threatens a rod indeed, yet visits offenders in the spirit of gentleness. Indeed, that you may know that he is a mild teacher, he consults the will of the very people whom he reproves. What do you wish? He says shall I come to you with a rod, or in charity and the spirit of gentleness? Once he spoke of the rod; twice he added milder things, joining gentleness to charity. Yet at first he threatened, but he showed gentleness; for writing in the second epistle to the same Corinthians he says: I call God as witness upon my soul that it was to spare you that I did not come to Corinth. Hear for what reason he thought he should spare them: that I might not he says come to you again in sorrow. He cast away the rod and took up the affection of charity.

62. And greet no one on the way. Perhaps to some this may seem hard and proud, and not in keeping with the precept of the gentle and humble Lord, that he who commanded that one should yield even the place at table should in this place charge his disciples: greet no one on the way, though this is a common practice of courtesy. In this way inferiors are accustomed to win for themselves the favor of superiors; the Gentiles too have such exchanges of civilities with Christians. How does the Lord tear away this practice of human kindness?

63. But consider that it is not only this: greet no one, but that not for nothing was on the way added. Indeed Elisha too, when he sent his servant to lay his staff upon the body of the dead boy, charged him not to greet anyone on the way; for he bade him hurry in haste, so that he might carry out the duty of bringing about the resurrection, lest by conversing with someone who met him he be turned back from the task he had been charged with. So here too it is not the courtesy of greeting that is taken away, but the obstacle that would hinder devotion that is removed, so that, when divine things are commanded, human things may be set aside for a little. A greeting is a fair thing, but fairer, the more prompt it is, is the carrying out of divine things, which through delay has often incurred offense. And for this reason even honorable things are forbidden, lest a customary courtesy creep in and hinder the ministry of the duty, whose delay is a fault.

64. Next, this is another virtue: that no one should move from house to house with roving levity, so that we may keep constancy even in the love of hospitality and not easily dissolve a bond of friendship once grown together; that we should bring forward the message of peace, so that our very first entrance may be hallowed by the blessing of peace; that we ought to be content with the food and drink offered; that the banners of faith are not to be kept down and the Gospel of the heavenly kingdom is to be preached; that the dust is to be shaken off the feet, if anyone has thought that we are not to be received in the hospitality of his city.

65. He teaches also that those who have judged that the Gospel is not to be followed will be liable to a heavier punishment than those who have judged that the Law is to be broken, because Tyre and Sidon, had they seen such great wonders of heavenly works, would not have despised the remedy of repentance. And that this worldly affluence or insolence is neither to be compared with the heavenly gift nor, again, abandoned without remedy, since each has the help of repenting. Lastly he opens a heavenly mystery, that it pleased God to reveal his grace to little ones rather than to the wise of this world; which the apostle Paul has set out with a fuller pen, saying: has not God made foolish the wisdom of this world? For since, in the wisdom of God, this world did not know God through wisdom, it pleased God through the foolishness of preaching to save those who believe.

66. Let us take, then, the little one to be him who does not know how to exalt himself and to vaunt the art of his own wisdom with decked-out words, as most philosophers do. He was a little one who said: Lord, I did not exalt my heart, nor were my eyes lifted up, nor did I walk in great things nor in wonderful things above me. And that you might know that this one was little not in age, not in understanding, but by his humility and a certain turning away from boasting, he added: but I exalted my soul. Do you see how lofty this little one was, on how high a summit of the virtues? Such little ones the Apostle wishes us to be, when he says: if anyone among you seems to be wise in this world, let him become a fool, that he may be wise; for the wisdom of this world is foolishness with God.

67. A most beautiful passage on the faith is joined on, when he says that all things have been delivered to him by his Father. When you read all things, you recognize the Almighty, not faded in hue, not degenerate from the Father; when you read delivered, you confess the Son, to whom by nature all things are his own by right of the one substance, not conferred as a gift through grace. He added: no one knows who the Son is except the Father, and who the Father is except the Son, and he to whom the Son wills to reveal him.

68. I recall that this passage was not passed over in the books which I wrote on the faith. But that you may know that, just as the Son reveals the Father to whom he wills, so also the Father reveals the Son to whom he wills, hear the Lord Jesus himself speaking, when he praised Peter because he had confessed that he was the Son of God: blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jona, for flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, but my Father who is in heaven.

69. To these is joined the reading in which those are exposed who think themselves lawyers, who hold the words of the Law but are ignorant of the force of the Law. And from the very first chapter of the Law he teaches that they are ignorant of the Law, proving that at the very beginning the Law proclaimed both the Father and the Son and announced also the sacrament of the Lord's Incarnation, saying: you shall love the Lord your God and: you shall love your neighbor as yourself.

70. Hence the Lord says to the lawyer: do this and you shall live. But he, who did not know his neighbor because he did not believe in Christ, answered: who is my neighbor? So he who does not know Christ does not know the Law either. For how can he know the Law when he is ignorant of the truth, since the Law announces the truth?

71. A certain man was going down from Jerusalem to Jericho. That we may resolve more plainly the passage set before us, let us recall the ancient history of the city of Jericho. We remember, then, that Jericho, as we read in the book entitled of Joshua son of Nun, was a great city enclosed by walls of masonry, such as could not be breached by iron nor pierced by the ram; that in it lived Rahab the harlot, who received with hospitality the spies whom Joshua sent, laid a plan, answered the citizens when they demanded them that they had gone away, hid them on the roof, and, so that she might rescue herself and her own from the destruction of the city, tied a scarlet cord in the window; and that the impregnable walls of the city itself collapsed at the sound of seven priestly trumpets and the shout of the people in jubilation sounding with them.

72. See how each keeps his own duty: the spy his watch, the harlot the mystery, the victor his faith, the priest religion. These do not fear danger for the sake of praise; she does not betray those she has taken in, even in dangers; he, anxious to keep faith rather than to conquer, commands the safety of the harlot before the destruction of the city; and the insignia of religion are the weapons of the priest. Further, who would not think this full of wonder, that out of the whole city no one was saved except the one whom the harlot set free?

73. This is the history of the simple truth. But if it is considered more deeply, it signifies mysteries to be marveled at. For Jericho is a figure of this world, into which Adam, cast out of paradise, that is, from that heavenly Jerusalem, came down by the fall of his transgression, passing, that is, from the living to the underworld, he whom a change not of place but of conduct made an exile from his nature. For far changed from that Adam who enjoyed unharmed blessedness, when he turned aside to the sins of the world, he fell among robbers, among whom he would not have fallen, had he not, straying from the heavenly command, made himself liable to them. Who are these robbers but the angels of night and darkness, who sometimes transform themselves into angels of light but cannot persevere? These first strip off the garments of spiritual grace that we have received, and so they are wont to inflict wounds; for if we keep undefiled the garments we have put on, we cannot feel the blows of the robbers. Take care, then, that you are not stripped first, as Adam was first stripped, deprived of the keeping of the heavenly command and despoiled of the garment of faith, and so received a deadly wound, in which the whole human race would have perished, had not that Samaritan, coming down, healed his bitter wounds.

74. No ordinary man is this Samaritan, who did not himself despise the one whom the priest, whom the Levite had despised. And do not despise for the name of his sect one whom you will marvel at for the interpretation of the word; for by the word Samaritan a guardian is signified. This is what its interpretation holds. Who is the guardian but he of whom it was said: the Lord guarding the little ones? So, just as there is one Jew in the letter, another in the spirit, so too there is one Samaritan outwardly, another in secret. This Samaritan, then, coming down — who is he who came down from heaven, if not he who ascended into heaven, the Son of Man who is in heaven? — seeing the man half-dead, whom no one had been able to cure before, like that woman with an issue of blood who had spent all her substance on physicians, came near him, that is: he was made our neighbor by taking upon himself compassion for us, and near to us by the bestowal of mercy.

75. And he bound up his wounds, pouring in oil and wine. This Physician has many medicines with which he is wont to heal. His speech is a medicine. One word of his binds up wounds, another soothes with oil, another pours in wine: he binds up wounds with a sterner precept, he soothes with the remission of sin, as with wine he stings by the announcement of judgment.

76. And he set him he says on his own beast. Hear how he sets you on it. He bears our sins and grieves for us. And the shepherd set the weary sheep on his shoulders. For man was made like a beast of burden, and therefore he set us upon his own beast, so that we might not be as the horse and the mule, so that by taking up our body he might do away with the infirmities of our flesh. Then he brought us to the inn, us who were beasts of burden.

77. Now an inn is a place into which those wearied by a long journey are wont to turn aside. So the Lord brought him into the inn, he who raises the needy from the earth and lifts the poor from the dunghill, and he took care of him,

78. lest the sick man should fail to keep the precepts he had received. But this Samaritan had no leisure to stay long on earth: he had to return to the place from which he had come down.

79. And so on the next day — what is this next day, unless perhaps that of the Lord's resurrection, of which it was said: this is the day which the Lord has made? — he thus brought out two denarii and gave them to the innkeeper and said: take care of him.

80. What are these two denarii, unless perhaps the two Testaments, which bear stamped upon them the image of the eternal King, by whose price our wounds are healed? For we have been redeemed by precious blood, so that we might escape the sores of the final death.

81. These two denarii, then — although it is not absurd to understand in them also the forms of these four books —, the innkeeper received. Who? Perhaps he who says: and I count them as dung, that I may gain Christ, so that he might take care of the wounded man. The innkeeper, therefore, is he who said: Christ sent me to preach the Gospel. The innkeepers are those to whom it is said: go into the whole world and preach the Gospel to every creature, and he who believes and is baptized shall be saved; saved, that is, from death, saved from the wound inflicted by the robbers.

82. Blessed is that innkeeper who can heal another's wounds; blessed is he to whom Jesus says: whatever you spend over, I will repay you when I return. A good steward, who even spends over. A good steward is Paul, whose sermons and epistles overflow, as it were, beyond the account he had received. He carried out the Lord's measured command with an almost measureless labor of mind and body, so that by the dispensing of weighty spiritual address he might relieve many from sickness. A good innkeeper, then, of that inn in which the she-ass knew the manger of her master and in which the flocks of lambs are shut in, lest, while the rapacious wolves growl around the folds, there be an easy attack upon the sheepfolds.

83. He pledges, then, that he will repay the reward. When will you return, Lord, unless on the day of judgment? For although you are always everywhere and, standing in our midst, are not perceived by us, yet there will be a time when all flesh will behold you returning. You will repay, then, what you owe. Blessed are those to whom you are debtor. O that we may be fit debtors, O that we may be able to discharge what we have received, and that neither the office of the priesthood nor that of the ministry may puff us up! How will you repay, Lord Jesus? You have indeed promised that there is a plentiful reward in heaven for the good; yet you will repay also when you say: well done, good servant, since you have been faithful over a few things, I will set you over many things; enter into the joy of your lord.

84. Therefore, since no one is more a neighbor than he who healed our wounds, let us love him as Lord, let us love him also as neighbor; for nothing is so near as the head to the members. Let us love also him who is an imitator of Christ, let us love him who suffers with another's need through the unity of the body. For it is not kinship that makes a neighbor but mercy, because mercy is according to nature; for nothing is so much according to nature as to help one who shares our nature.

85. Mercy, then, has been spoken of, but virtue has not one form only. There follows next, by the example of Martha and Mary, the active devotion of the one in works, the religious attention of the other's mind to the Word of God; and this, if it accords with faith, is preferred even to works themselves, according to what is written: Mary has chosen for herself the best part, which shall not be taken away from her. Let us too, then, strive to have what no one can take away from us, so that not a perfunctory but a diligent hearing may be given; for even the seeds of the heavenly Word itself are wont to be taken away if they are sown along the way. Let the desire for wisdom drive you, as it did Mary; for this is the greater, this the more perfect work; and let not the care of ministering turn you away from the knowledge of the heavenly Word, nor should you reproach and judge as idle those whom you see devoting themselves to wisdom; for that peaceable Solomon took her to himself to dwell with him.

86. Yet Martha is not reproved in her good ministry, but Mary is preferred because she chose the better part for herself; for Jesus abounds in many things and bestows many things. And therefore the wiser chose what she perceived to be the principal thing. Indeed the apostles did not think it best to leave the Word of God and serve tables. But both are tasks of wisdom; for Stephen too, full of wisdom, was chosen as a minister. And therefore let him who ministers defer to the teacher, and let the teacher invite and encourage the one who ministers; for the body of the Church is one, though the members are diverse: each needs the other. The eye cannot say to the hand: I have no need of your work, or again the head to the feet, nor can the ear deny that it is of the body; for even if some members are principal, yet others are necessary. Wisdom is in the head, action in the hands; for the eyes of the wise man are in his head, because he is truly wise whose mind is in Christ and whose inner eye is raised to the things above. And therefore the eyes of the wise man are in his head, but those of the fool in his heel.

87. Which of you having a friend, and he will go to him at midnight and says to him: friend, lend me three loaves. This is another passage of precept, that prayer should be offered at every moment, not only by day but also by night; for you see that this man who went at midnight, asking three loaves of his friend and persisting in the very earnestness of asking, is not cheated of what he prayed for. What are these three loaves, if not the nourishment of the heavenly mystery? And if you love the Lord your God, you will be able to earn it not only for yourself but also for others. But who is more a friend to us than he who gave up his body for us? From him David asked loaves at midnight and received them; for he asked when he said: at midnight I rose to confess to you; therefore he earned these loaves which he has set before us to eat. He asked when he says: every night I will wash my bed; for he did not fear to wake as a sleeper one whom he knows to be ever astir and watchful.

88. And therefore, mindful of what is written, persisting in prayers by night and by day, let us ask pardon for our sins. For if that man, so holy and occupied with the necessities of a kingdom, spoke praise to the Lord seven times a day, always intent on the morning and evening sacrifices, what ought we to do, who ought to ask the more, the more often we fall through frailty of flesh and mind, so that to those weary from the way and heavily fatigued by the course of this age and the windings of this life the bread of refreshment, which strengthens the heart of man, may never be lacking?

89. And the Lord teaches that we must keep watch not only at midnight, but at almost every moment; for he comes both in the evening watch and in the second and in the third, and is wont to knock. Blessed, therefore, are those servants whom the lord, when he comes, shall find watching. If, then, you desire that the power of God should gird itself and serve you, you must always keep watch; for many are the snares laid for us, and heavy is the sleep of the body; if the mind begins to sleep that sleep, it will lose the vigor of its virtue. Shake off your sleep, then, that you may knock at the door of Christ, which Paul too begs to have opened to him, beseeching that he may be helped not only by his own prayers but also by those of the people, so that a door may be opened to him to speak the mystery of Christ. And perhaps that is the door which John saw opened; for he saw and said: after these things I saw, and behold, a door opened in heaven, and the first voice which I heard, as of a trumpet speaking with me and saying: come up hither, and I will show you the things that must come to pass. The door, then, was opened to John, the door was opened to Paul, so that he might receive for us loaves to eat. For he persevered, knocking at the door in season, out of season, so that he might refresh with the abundance of heavenly nourishment the nations, wearied by the toil of the world's road.

90. This, then, is a passage of precept about praying often, about the hope of obtaining, about the way of persuading, first in a precept, afterwards in an example. For he who promises something ought to bring hope of what is promised, so that obedience may be paid to admonitions and faith to promises; and faith, by contemplating human loving-kindness, gains a greater hope of eternal loving-kindness, provided, however, that just things are asked, lest prayer be turned into sin. Nor was he ashamed to ask for something rather often, lest he should seem either to distrust the Lord's mercy or arrogantly to resent that he had not obtained something at the first prayer — for this he says I asked the Lord three times —; and he shows that God often does not grant what is prayed for, because he judges useless the things that we believe will profit us.

91. Every kingdom divided against itself shall be made desolate. The reason for this saying is that he was said to cast out demons by Beelzebub, the prince of the demons; it was to show that his own kingdom is undivided and everlasting. And rightly he also answered Pilate: my kingdom is not of this world. And therefore those who place their hope not in Christ, but suppose that demons are cast out by the prince of the demons, he declares not to belong to the everlasting kingdom. This concerns the people of the Jews, which in afflictions of this kind summons the help of a demon to cast out a demon. For how can the kingdom, when faith is torn apart, remain, being divided? For since the people of the Jews is under the Law, and Christ too according to the flesh was begotten from the Law, how can the kingdom of the Jews, which is from the Law, be everlasting, when the people itself divides the Law, when Jesus, who is due from the Law, is denied by the people of the Law? Thus in part the faith of the Jewish people fights against itself, and by fighting is divided, and by being divided is dissolved. And therefore the kingdom of the Church will remain for ever, because its faith is undivided, its body is one; for there is one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one God and Father of all, who is above all and through all and in all.

92. How great is the madness of sacrilegious fury here, that, when the Son of God took flesh to crush unclean spirits and to carry off the spoils of the prince of the world, and gave to men too the power of destroying spiritual wickedness, dividing his spoils, which is the mark of one who triumphs, some should take to themselves the aid and protection of diabolical power, when demons are driven out by the finger of God, or certainly, according to Matthew, by the Spirit of God! From this it is understood that the kingdom of the divinity is as it were a kind of undivided body, since Christ is the right hand of God, while the Spirit, under the likeness of a finger, seems to express the joining of what is, as it were, one body according to the divinity. Does not the kingdom seem to be undivided, since it is as it were an undivided body? For there dwells, as you have read, bodily in Christ the fullness of the divinity. And this you certainly cannot deny of the Father, nor ought you to deny it of the Spirit. Nor let it seem to you that, by comparison with our members, some apportioning of power is to be made; for there can be no division of an undivided thing, and therefore the naming of the finger is to be referred to the form of unity, not to a distinction of power, since the right hand of God says: I and the Father are one; but although the divinity is undivided, the person is distinct.

93. Yet when the Spirit is called a finger, a working power is signified, because the Holy Spirit, just like the Father and the Son, is the worker of the divine works. For David says: for I shall see the heavens, the works of your fingers; and in Psalm 32 (33): and by the Spirit of his mouth all their power. And Paul says: but all these things one and the same Spirit works, dividing to each as he wills. And when he says this:

94. but if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God, surely the kingdom of God has come upon you, he shows at the same time that the power of the Holy Spirit, in whom is the kingdom of God, is a kind of imperial power, and that we too, in whom the Spirit dwells, are a royal house. Hence he also said later on: the kingdom of God is within you. And therefore we ought to regard the Holy Spirit as sharing in the divinity and dominion and imperial majesty, because the Lord is the Spirit, and where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty.

95. When the unclean spirit has gone out of a man, he walks through places that have no water, seeking rest and not finding it. It cannot be doubted that this was said of the people of the Jews, which the Lord, in what went before, separated from his kingdom. Hence understand that all heretics and schismatics too are separated from the kingdom of God and from the Church. And therefore it is clearly evident that every assembly of schismatics and heretics is not of God but of the unclean spirit. So in one man there is a comparison of the whole Jewish people, from which the unclean spirit had gone out through the Law. But since among the nations and the Gentiles it could not find rest, through the faith of Christ — for Christ is a burning to unclean spirits, he who in the hearts of the Gentiles, which before were dry and afterwards through baptism grew moist with the dew of the Spirit, had quenched the fiery darts of the adversary —, therefore it went back to the people of the Jews, which, adorned with an outward and perfunctory appearance, remains more polluted in its inner mind. For it neither washed away nor quenched its burning with the stream of the sacred font, and rightly the unclean spirit returned to it, bringing with it more wicked spirits, since with a sacrilegious mind it had offended against both the week of the Law and the mystery of the eighth day. So, just as for us the grace of the sevenfold Spirit is multiplied, so for them every injury of the unclean spirits is heaped up; for totality is sometimes comprehended in this number, because on the seventh day, the works of the world being completed, God rested. Therefore also the barren has borne seven, and she who was populous in sons has been weakened. Lastly, that you may know that the people of the Synagogue is disfigured where the blessedness of the Church is praised, he added:

96. this generation is a wicked generation; it seeks a sign, and no sign shall be given it but the sign of Jonah. For as Jonah was a sign to the Ninevites, so will the Son of Man be also. In this too, when the people of the Jews is condemned, the mystery of the Church is plainly expressed, which, in the Ninevites through repentance and in the queen of the south through zeal for acquiring wisdom, is gathered from the ends of the whole world, so that it may come to know the words of the peaceable Solomon. A queen indeed, whose kingdom is undivided, rising up into one body out of diverse and distant peoples. So that sacrament is great concerning Christ and the Church, but yet this is greater, because that went before in figure, while now the mystery is full in truth; for there was the type of Solomon, but here is Christ in his own body. The Church, then, consists of two things, that you should either not know how to sin or cease to sin; for repentance blots out sin, wisdom guards against it. So much in the mystical sense.

97. For the rest, the sign of Jonah, as it is a type of the Lord's Passion, so also is a testimony to the grievous sins which the Jews committed. At the same time one may observe both an oracle of majesty and a token of loving-kindness; for by the example of the Ninevites both punishment is announced and a remedy is shown. Hence the Jews too ought not to despair of pardon, if they are willing to do penance.

98. No one lights a lamp and puts it in a hidden place nor under a bushel, but upon a lampstand. So, since in what went before he preferred the Church to the Synagogue, he exhorts us to transfer our faith rather to the Church; for faith is a lamp, according to what is written: your word is a lamp to my feet, O Lord; for the Word of God is our faith, the Word of God is light, faith is a lamp. He was the true light, which enlightens every man coming into this world. But a lamp cannot give light unless it has received its light from elsewhere. This is the lamp which is lit, namely the virtue of our mind and understanding, so that that mina which had been lost may be found. Let no one, then, set his faith under the Law; for the Law is within measure, grace beyond measure: the Law overshadows, grace makes bright. And therefore let no one shut up his faith within the measure of the Law, but let him bring it to the Church, in which shines the grace of the sevenfold Spirit, which that chief of priests illumines with the brightness of the divinity on high, lest the shadow of the Law extinguish it.

99. Indeed that lamp which the chief of the priests was wont to light in the morning and in the evening by the ancient rite of the Jews has vanished, set as it were under the bushel of the Law; and that city of Jerusalem which is on earth, which killed the prophets, lies hidden, set as it were in a valley of weeping; but that Jerusalem which is in heaven, in which our faith serves as a soldier, set on that highest mountain of all, that is, in Christ, the Church, cannot be hidden by the darkness and ruins of this world, but, shining with the brightness of the eternal Sun, illumines us with the light of spiritual grace.

100. Now you Pharisees cleanse the outside of the cup and the dish first. You see that our bodies are signified by the representation of earthen and fragile things, which, thrown down by a short fall, are broken. And you see that the inner things the mind has willed are easily betrayed through the senses and the acts of the body, just as the things a cup holds within shine through outside. Hence too there is no doubt that later on the Passion of the body is declared by the word cup, when the Lord says: the cup which my Father has given me, do you not wish me to drink it? For he drinks his body who absorbs bodily frailty with a spiritual disposition and, as it were, pours it over into the mind and soul, so that the weakness of the outer things is swallowed up by the inner. You perceive, then, that it is not the outside of this cup or dish that defiles us, but the inside. And therefore, like a good teacher, he taught how we ought to cleanse the defilement of our body, saying:

101. give alms, and behold, all things are clean to you. Do you see how great are the remedies? Mercy cleanses us, the word of God cleanses us, according to what is written: now you are clean because of the word which I have spoken to you. And not in this place only, but in others too, you have it expressed how great a grace it is: for almsgiving frees from death; and: shut up alms in the heart of the poor, and it will pray for you in the evil day.

102. So the whole of this most beautiful passage from here on is directed to this end: since he invites us to the pursuit of simplicity, he condemns the superfluous and earthly things of the Jews, who, by understanding the things of the Law according to the body, are not undeservedly compared to a glass and a dish because of their own fragility, and who observe the things of which we have no use, but neglect those in which lies the fruit of our hope, and therefore commit a great sin while they despise the better things; and yet the blotting out of that very sin is promised, if mercy follows.

103. Briefly, too, he touches on their many vices, who bend all their zeal to paying tithes of the cheaper produce, and have neither any fear of the judgment to come nor any love of God, since works without faith are vain; for they pass over the judgment and the love of God: the judgment, because they do not refer all that they do to the judgment; the love, because they do not love God from the heart.

104. But lest, on the other hand, he make us zealous for faith and neglectful of works, he sums up the perfection of the faithful man in a brief saying, so that he may be approved both by faith and by works, saying: these things you ought to have done, and not to leave those undone.

105. He also rebukes the arrogance and boastfulness of the Jews, in that they seek the first places at table at banquets. Against the lawyers themselves too a sentence of condemnation is pronounced, who, like tombs that do not appear, mislead by their appearance and deceive in their use, so that, while outwardly they promise what is fair, within they enclose what is full of stench. This most teachers do, who demand from others what they themselves cannot emulate. And therefore they themselves are tombs, as it is also said elsewhere: their throat is an open sepulchre.

106. A good passage too against the most empty superstition of the Jews, who by building the tombs of the prophets condemned the deeds of their fathers, but by emulating their fathers' crimes turned the sentence back upon themselves. For by building the prophets' tombs they convicted of crime those who had killed them, and by emulating like deeds they declared themselves too heirs of their fathers' iniquity. It is not the building, then, but the emulation that is reckoned as the crime. For they cannot be acquitted of inherited iniquity who, by crucifying the Son of God, which is more grievous, added a crowning heap to their fathers' crimes. And therefore he rightly added elsewhere: fill up the measure of your fathers, because there is nothing beyond the wrong done to God in which they could sin more grievously.

107. Therefore Wisdom sends to them apostles and prophets. Who is Wisdom but Christ? Indeed in Matthew you have: behold, I send to you prophets and wise men.

108. They are reproved further under the name of the Jews and are declared liable to future punishment, because, while they usurp for themselves the teaching of divine knowledge, they hinder others and do not themselves acknowledge what they profess.

109. Are not five sparrows sold for two pennies, and not one of them is in forgetfulness before the Lord? But even the hairs of your head are all numbered. Do not fear: you are more than many sparrows. The Savior has woven in a most beautiful passage on holding fast to simplicity and emulating faith, lest after the manner of Jewish unbelief we press down some things in our affection and feign others with our voice, since at the last time the hidden things of our thoughts, accusing or even defending, will be seen to lay open the secret of our mind. For what greater enticement to simplicity is there than that each one should know that there can be no hiding place for fraud?

110. But since the cause of unbelief is twofold, because it springs either from ingrained malice or from a fear that befalls, lest anyone, terrified by fear of a power, be compelled to deny God whom he knows in his heart, he fittingly added that only the punishment of the soul is to be dreaded, that the penalty of the body is not to be feared — for death is the end of nature, not a penalty — and therefore that death is the ending of bodily punishment, but the penalty of the soul is perpetual, and that God alone is to be feared, to whose power nature sets no bounds, but that same nature is subject to it; and that death is not terrible, which immortality will redeem with a richer interest.

111. The Lord had breathed in the disposition of simplicity, he had raised up the strength of the mind; faith alone was wavering: well did he strengthen it from meaner things; for if God does not forget sparrows, how can he forget men? But if the majesty of God is so great and so eternal that not one of the sparrows nor the number of our hairs is beyond God's knowledge, how unworthy it is to think that the Lord is either ignorant of the hearts of the faithful or despises them, he who knows meaner things!

112. Perhaps someone may say: how did the Apostle say: Is God concerned for oxen, when surely an ox is more precious than a sparrow? But care is one thing, knowledge another. Indeed the number of the hairs is understood not in the act of counting but in the ease of knowing; for God does not keep the anxious watch of one who counts with care, but for him to whom all things are known all things are as it were numbered. Yet they are well said to be numbered, because we number the things that we wish to keep.

113. We can, however, discuss here some hidden meaning of spiritual understanding, especially since this seems absurd, that men should be compared to men rather than to sparrows. For these five sparrows seem to be the five senses of the body, touch, smell, taste, sight, hearing, which, if after the manner of sparrows they rummage in the filth of earthly dirt and seek food from untilled and fetid places, are caught in the snares of their own sins and cannot fly back to the fruits of higher works on which minds feast. For there is a certain snare of seductive pleasure, which fastens certain bonds to the footsteps of our minds, so that, if the sense of earthly matter has blunted the fiery vigor and purity of our nature, it sets it, knocked down for the price of worldly luxury, under a kind of auction of vices.

114. There are also certain markets of our sins. And so, captured by the enticements of various pleasures, we are either sold under sin or redeemed from sin. Christ redeems us, the adversary sells; the one auctions us off to death, the other redeems us to salvation. Hence Matthew fittingly set down two sparrows, signifying the body and the soul, since the flesh too, if, consenting to the law of God and stripping itself of the law of sin, it has passed over in sense by purity into the nature of the soul, is lifted up to heaven on spiritual wings. And so we are taught that nature gave us the grace of flying, pleasure took it away, which weighs down the soul with the foods of evils and bends it into the nature of bodily mass.

115. And rightly he set down that none of them falls without the will of God; for what falls sinks toward the earth, and what flies is borne up to the height of immortality. But lest anyone should be in doubt about what Matthew said, Luke has plainly resolved it, that the will of God is knowledge; for it is not that whoever falls falls by the will of God, but that he who has collapsed under the weight of his own sins cannot escape God's notice; for Job too is tempted by his will. He gave you an adversary, but he set out a prize. And do not plead your frailty as an excuse: because you have the image, you have received a defense; whence this too profits for salvation, that you know that the devil cannot harm without God's permission, so that you may not fear the power of the devil more than offending the divinity.

116. Now it is not doubtful that the soul is compared to a sparrow, since you read: our soul has been rescued like a sparrow from the snare of the hunters. And elsewhere: how do you say to my soul: move to the mountains like a sparrow? We read also that man himself is compared to a sparrow, for it is written: but I am like a solitary sparrow in the building, that is, formed out of two sparrows into one sparrow, that is, into the fineness of a spiritual substance, by the joining of both wings in concord. So the sparrow is good to whom nature supplies the power of flight. There is also a bad sparrow, which has abolished the use of flying by the fault of an earthly stain, such as are these sparrows which are sold for two pennies. In one place they are sold for a penny, in another for two pennies.

117. How great the cheapness of sins! For death is cheap, but virtue precious. For the adversary knocks us down at a cheaper valuation like captive slaves, but the Lord, a fit appraiser of his own work, has redeemed us at a great price as comely servants whom he made after his own image and likeness, as the holy Apostle said: for you have been bought with a great price. And well great, for it is valued not with bronze but with blood, because Christ died for us, who freed us with precious blood, as Saint Peter also records in his epistle, writing to us: for not with corruptible things, silver or gold he says were you redeemed from your vain manner of life of the tradition of the fathers, but with the precious blood as of a lamb undefiled and spotless, Christ Jesus. And well precious, because it is the blood of a spotless body, because it is the blood of the Son of God, who redeemed us not only from the curse of the Law but also from the perpetual death of ungodliness.

118. In sum, then, this is the sense: if the Lord has provided for cheap birds and for unbelieving men, either in the rising of the sun or in the fruitfulness of the earth, if he imparts the gift of his mercy to all, there is no doubt that regard for the merits of the faithful will avail with him. Excellently, moreover, he has both woven in what sharpens faith and laid under faith itself the foundations of the virtues: for as faith is the incentive of fortitude, so fortitude is the support of faith.

119. And everyone who says a word against the Son of Man, it will be forgiven him, but whoever has spoken against the Holy Spirit, it will not be forgiven him. Surely by the Son of Man we understand Christ, who was begotten of the Holy Spirit and of a virgin, since his only parent on earth is a virgin. Is the Holy Spirit then greater than Christ, that those who sin against Christ obtain pardon, while those who offend against the Holy Spirit do not deserve to obtain remission? But where there is unity of power, there is no question of comparison nor dispute about greatness, since the Lord is great and there can be no end of his greatness. If then, as we believe, there is unity of the Trinity, the greatness is surely undivided, as the operation is undivided, which is shown in what follows; for while it has been said elsewhere: the Father gives you what you are to speak, here he has added:

120. for the Holy Spirit will teach you in that hour what you ought to say. So if the operation is one, the insult also is one. But let us return to our subject. Here it seems to some that we should understand the same Christ as both the Son of Man and the Holy Spirit, saving the distinction of persons and the unity of substance, since the one Christ, both God and man, is likewise Spirit, as it is written: a Spirit before our face is Christ the Lord, and likewise holy, because just as the Father is God and the Son is Lord, and the Father is Lord and the Son is God, so also the Father is holy and the Son is holy and the Spirit is holy. Indeed the cherubim and seraphim cry out with unwearied voices: Holy, holy, holy, so that the Trinity may be signified by the title repeated a third time. If then Christ is both, what is the difference, except that we should know that it is not lawful for us to deny the divinity of Christ? Indeed in persecution what is sought except that we deny that Christ is God? And so whoever does not confess God in Christ, and Christ from God and in God, does not deserve pardon. But also every spirit that does not confess that Christ has come in the flesh is not of God; for he who has denied the man has denied God, because Christ is God in man and man in God.

121. Most, however, hold this view, saying that the blasphemy is not pardonable if anyone says that Christ casts out demons by Beelzebub, not by divine power.

122. And someone from the crowd said: Master, tell my brother to divide the inheritance with me. But he said to him: Man, who has appointed me judge or divider over you? This whole passage is arranged to train us to undergo suffering for the confession of the Lord, either through contempt of death or through hope of reward or through the announcement of a lasting punishment for which pardon will never be granted. And since avarice is usually wont to tempt virtue, a precept and an example for abolishing this thing too are added, the Lord saying: who has appointed me judge or divider over you? Well does he decline earthly things who had come down for the sake of divine things, and he does not deign to be judge of lawsuits and arbiter of property, he who has the judgment of the living and the dead and the award of merits. So one must consider not what you ask, but from whom you request it, and you should not think that a mind intent on greater things is to be distracted by lesser ones. Hence not undeservedly is this brother rebuffed, who was eager to occupy the dispenser of heavenly things with corruptible ones, since between brothers it is not a judge in the middle but dutiful love as trustee that ought to divide the patrimony, although it is the patrimony of immortality, not of money, that men should seek; for in vain does he gather wealth who does not know whether he will use it, like that man who, when his full barns were bursting with new harvests, was preparing storehouses for his overflowing fruits, not knowing for whom he was gathering. For all things that are of the world remain in the world, and whatever is gathered for heirs passes away from us; for those things are not ours which we cannot take away with us. Virtue alone is the companion of the dead, mercy alone follows us, which, as guide and forerunner to the heavenly dwellings, acquires eternal tabernacles for the dead through the use of cheap money, as the precepts of the Lord testify, who says to us: make for yourselves friends of the unrighteous mammon, that they may receive you into their eternal tabernacles. Good therefore and salutary and fitting is the precept, which could rouse even the avaricious, so that they may be eager to exchange corruptible things for everlasting, earthly things for divine. But since the devotion of faith is often called back by weakness, and those ready to bestow their patrimony are drawn back by thought of their own livelihood, for this reason the Lord added, saying:

123. do not be anxious for your soul, what you shall eat, nor for your body, what you shall put on. The soul is more than food and the body than clothing. For nothing is more moral for producing the faith that all things can be conferred by God on those who believe, than that that airy spirit, without our labor, keeps perpetual the living partnership of soul and body, bound together in one dwelling, and the use of saving nourishment does not fail, except when the last day of dying has come. Since then the soul is clothed with the garment of the body and the body is animated by the vigor of the soul, it is absurd for us to think that an abundance of sustenance will fail us, who obtain the continuous substance of living.

124. Consider he says the birds of the sky. A great example truly, and a fitting one for us to follow by faith. For if divine providence bestows unfailing nourishment on the birds of the sky, which have no practice of tilling, no yield from the fruitfulness of harvests, it is true that avarice appears to be the cause of our want. For they have the use of unlabored fodder in abundance for this reason, that they do not know how to claim by a special ownership the fruits given to them for common food; we have lost the common things while we claim things as our own; for nothing is one's own where nothing is perpetual, nor is plenty certain where the outcome is uncertain. For why should you set value on your riches, when God willed that even your sustenance should be common with the other living creatures? The birds of the sky claim nothing as their special own, and therefore they do not know how to lack food, because they know not how to envy others.

125. Consider the lilies, how they grow; and below: but if God so clothes the grass, which is today and tomorrow is cast into the oven. A good and moral discourse indeed, in that the Lord's word, by the comparison of the flower and the grass, has called us forth to faith in the bestowal of the divine mercy, either according to the letter, because we can add nothing to the stature of our body, or spiritually, because beyond the measure of our stature we can add nothing without God's favor. For what is so moral for persuasion as that, when you see that even things which are irrational are so clothed by God's providence that they lack nothing of use either for grace or for adornment, you should believe much more that man, who is rational, if he places all his use in God and does not violate faith by a bent for wavering, can never be in want, because he has rightly presumed on the divine favor?

126. But yet, that we may weigh these things more deeply as well, since it does not seem idle that a flower is either compared to a man or indeed, in Solomon, is preferred to what was almost more than men, who merited so much as either to found a temple to God in outward form or to figure the Church of Christ in a mystery, it does not seem foreign to think that by the brightness of the color the glory of the heavenly angels is expressed, who are truly the flowers of this world, because the world is adorned by their brightness, and they breathe forth the good odor of sanctification. Supported by their protection we can say: we are the good odor of Christ to God in those who are being saved; they, hindered by no anxiety, exercised by no practice of labor, preserve in themselves the grace of the divine liberality and the gifts of a heavenly nature. Hence it is well that Solomon is shown here clothed with his glory and elsewhere covered, because he clothed with the glory of works the weakness of bodily nature, covered over as it were by a certain virtue of mind. But the angels, whose more divine nature remains free of bodily injury, are rightly preferred to a very great man, yet in consideration of our injury. Therefore, since through the resurrection men will be as the angels in heaven, the Lord, who granted it to them also, has bidden us too, by the example of the angels, to hope for an increase of heavenly glory, until this mortal be swallowed up by life; it is fitting for this corruptible to put on incorruption and this mortal to put on immortality.

127. Many also think this comparison more apt according to the nature of the flower and the habit of the plant set before us, because lilies require the cultivation of no yearly practice, nor, as for the yield of the other fruits, so also for the generation of this flower does labor return in its round, driven on by anxious farmers. For in whatever droughts of the field, all that is nourished toward the flower is quickened by a certain generative power, out of itself and within itself, of a sap that always abides. So, when you see the stalk of full-grown leaves dried up, the nature of the flower still lives on; for the greenness is hidden, not lost. But when it has been called forth by the caresses of spring, the clothing of the plant takes up again the tresses of the flower or the adornment of the lily. Since we remember that this passage has been treated more fully elsewhere, it is enough to have touched on it now, lest there be a return to the same things.

128. It is a delight to notice that lilies grow not in the roughness of the mountains and the wild places of the woods, but in the pleasantness of gardens. For there are certain gardens bearing the fruits of diverse virtues, according to what is written: a garden enclosed, my sister, my bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed, because where there is integrity, where chastity, where religion, where the faithful silence of secrets, where the brightness of angels, there are the violets of confessors, the lilies of virgins, the roses of martyrs. Nor let anyone think it incongruous that angels are compared to lilies, since Christ himself has called himself a lily, saying: I am the flower of the field and the lily of the valleys. And well is Christ a lily, because where the blood of martyrs is, there is Christ, who is the lofty flower, spotless, harmless, in whom no roughness of thorns gives offense, but grace poured around him shines forth; for roses have thorns, because martyrs have torments. The divinity that cannot be struck has no thorns, for it did not feel torments.

129. So if the lilies, like the angels, are clothed above the glory of men: neither ought we to despair of God's mercy toward us, to whom the Lord promises, through the grace of the resurrection, a form like that of the angels. In this place he seems also to touch on that question which the Apostle too did not pass over, which the nations of this world ask, how the dead rise and with what body they come.

130. For when he says: seek the kingdom of God and all these things will be added to you, he shows that grace will be lacking to the faithful neither in the present nor in the time to come, provided that those who desire divine things do not seek earthly ones. For it is unbecoming that men who serve as soldiers for a kingdom should be anxious about food. The king knows how to feed, nourish, clothe his household, and therefore he said: cast your thought upon God and he himself will nourish you.

I came to send fire upon the earth, and what do I will but that it be already kindled? I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how I am straitened until it is perfected?

131. In what precedes he willed that we keep watch, awaiting at every moment the coming of the Lord Savior, lest while someone grows slack and neglects, putting off his works from day to day, he be overtaken by the day of the coming judgment or by his own death and lose the merit of his commendation.
