And that indeed is a general form of precept for all, but the course of the following example seems to be set forth for the stewards, that is, for the priests, that they may know that a heavy penalty must be undergone by them in the future, if, intent on worldly delights, they have neglected to govern the Lord's household and the people entrusted to them.

132. But since the progress is slight and the grace of merits small in being called back from error by fear of punishment, and greater is the prerogative of charity and love, for this reason the Lord sharpens our zeal to win his favor and inflames us with the desire of acquiring divinity, saying: I came to send fire upon the earth, surely not that fire which consumes good things, but the author of good will, which improves the golden vessels of the Lord's house, but consumes hay and stubble. And that divine fire burns up all worldly things, grown hard through the pleasure of the world, and the work of perishing flesh, the fire which blazed in the bones of the prophets, as holy Jeremiah says: for it became like a burning fire blazing in my bones. For there is the fire of the Lord, of which it has been said: a fire will burn before him. And the Lord himself also is fire, as he himself said: I am a fire burning and not consuming; for the fire of the Lord is eternal light, with which fire those lamps are lit of which he spoke above: let your loins be girded and your lamps burning. And therefore, since the day of this life is night, a lamp is necessary. Ammaus and Cleopas also testify that this fire was sent into them by the Lord, saying: was not our heart burning on the way, when he opened the Scriptures to us? Clearly therefore they taught what is the working of this fire, which illuminates the secrets of the heart. Perhaps for this reason the Lord will come in fire, that at the time of the resurrection he may consume all vices, and by his sight fulfil the desires of each, and kindle the light of merits and of mysteries.

133. So great therefore is the Lord's condescension that he testifies that there is in him a zeal to pour devotion into us, to consummate perfection in us, and to hasten his Passion for us. Though he had nothing in himself to grieve over, yet he was straitened by our distresses, and at the time of death he showed a sadness which he had taken on not from fear of his own death but from the delay of our redemption, according to what is written: and how I am straitened until it is perfected? Surely he who is straitened until perfection is secure about the perfection. But also elsewhere: My soul he says is sorrowful unto death. Not because of death, but unto death is the Lord sorrowful, because it was the condition of bodily affection, not the dread of death, that struck him. For he who took up a body had to undergo all things that belong to the body, to hunger, to thirst, to be straitened, to be saddened, but the divinity cannot be changed through these affections. At the same time he shows that in the contest of the Passion the death of the body is a release from anxiety, not a heaping up of pain.

Do you think that I came to give peace on the earth? No, I tell you, but separation. For from now on there will be five in one house divided, three against two and two against three will be divided. Father against son and son against father will be divided, mother against daughter and daughter against mother, mother-in-law against her daughter-in-law and daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law.

134. While in almost all passages of the Gospel the spiritual understanding is at work, it is so most of all in the present one: lest the harshness of a bare exposition offend anyone, the course of the sense must be tempered by spiritual depth, especially since our most holy religion, by moral disciplines and gentle examples of devotion, graciously invites even those exiled from faith to reverence for itself, so that discipline, going before faith, may by its gentleness dissolve the hardness of superstitions, and may compel minds subject to errors to believe also by faith, which it was able to soothe by devotion. For when the deep things of faith are not grasped by weak hearts, the things that are to be venerated are judged through the things that are commanded, so that just things from the Just One, holy things from the Holy One, may bear witness that the author of their goods is good.

135. Shall we then suppose that the Lord, who at once embraced in his counsel reverence for the divinity and the grace of dutiful love, saying: you shall love the Lord your God and you shall love your neighbor, has now so changed as to abolish the names of kinship and to dash the affections of dutiful love? Are we to believe that he commanded separations from our dearest ones? And how is he himself our peace, who has made both one? How does he himself say: my peace I give you, my peace I leave you, if he came to separate parents from children and children from parents by severing the bond of kinship? How is cursed he who does not honor his father, and religious he who deserts him?

136. But if we observe that the first cause is that of religion, the second that of dutiful love, we shall consider this question also untangled; for you must place human things after divine ones. For if duty is to be shown to parents, how much more to the author of parents, to whom you owe thanks even for your parents! Or if they do not acknowledge the Parent at all, how are you to acknowledge them? He does not say, then, that we must renounce our dear ones, but that God must be preferred to all. Indeed you have in another book: he who loves father or mother more than me is not worthy of me. You are forbidden not to love your parents, but to prefer them to God; for the dear ones of nature are benefits of the Lord, and no one ought to love the benefit he has received more than God, by whom the benefit received is preserved. So even according to the letter a religious exposition is not lacking for those who understand with devotion; yet there lies beneath it something for which we may think this sense should be referred higher, since he added:

137. from now on there will be five in one house divided, three against two and two against three will be divided. For who are the five, when a list of six persons seems to have been set down, father and son, mother and daughter, mother-in-law and daughter-in-law? Although the same woman who is mother can be taken as mother-in-law; for she who is mother of the son is mother-in-law of his wife, so that even according to the letter the reckoning of the number is not absurd, and it shines out clearly that faith is not tied by the chains of nature, because, though they are bound by the duty of dutiful love, yet in faith they are free.

138. Nor does it seem incongruous if we untangle this sense by a mystical interpretation. One house is one man; for each man is the house either of God or of the devil. Thus the spiritual house is the spiritual man, as we have in the epistle of Peter: and you, like living stones, are built up a spiritual house into a holy priesthood. In this house, then, two are divided against three, three against two. Two, we frequently read, are the soul and the body. But if the two have agreed on earth, he has made both one. And elsewhere: I chastise my body and bring it into servitude. That which serves is one thing, that to which it is subjected another.

139. If we have recognized the two, let us recognize the three as well, which is easy to understand from those two. For there are three affections of the soul in the body, one rational, another concupiscible, the third irascible, that is, λογιστικόν, ἐπιθυμητικόν, θυμικόν. So not two against two, but two against three and three against two will be divided; for through the coming of Christ man, who was irrational, has been made rational. Before, we were like the beasts, which know not reason; we were carnal, we were earthly, according to the sentence: earth you are and into earth you shall go. The Son of God came, he sent his Spirit into our hearts, we were made spiritual sons.

140. We can say that in this house there are another five, that is, smell, touch, taste, sight, hearing. So if, in accordance with the things we hear or read, setting apart the sense of sight and hearing, we shut out the superfluous pleasures of the body which are produced by taste and touch and smell, we divide two against three, because the disposition of the mind is not caught by the enticements of the vices, but through emulation of virtue withdraws itself from the allurements of pleasure, and there is not one consent of all, which would hurl it headlong into error, but by dividing, the desires of the heart and the duties of virtue separate themselves. Or if we take the five senses of the body, then the vices of the body and its sins separate themselves. And perhaps these are the five whom that rich man in the Gospel, given to luxury, who is described as tormented in the underworld, calls his brothers, to whom he asks that word be sent, that they may know that in this world delights must be renounced, so that the pursuits of virtue may have rest after this world.

141. The flesh and the soul can also be seen, separated from the smell, touch and taste of luxury, dividing themselves in one house against the vices that assail them, flesh and soul subjecting themselves to the law of God, withdrawing from the law of sin. Although their dissension through the transgression of the first man turned into nature, so that they in no way agreed with equal zeal for virtue, yet through the cross of the Lord Savior, with both the enmities and the law of commandments made void, they came together into the concord of fellowship, after Christ, our peace, coming down from heaven, made both one and broke down the middle wall of partition, the enmities in his flesh, making void the law of commandments in decrees, that he might create the two in one new man, making peace, and reconcile both to God in one body. Who are the two, except the one inner, the other outer? The one looks to the vigor of the soul, the other relates to the sense of the body, although they agree in the harmony of an inseparable affection, when the flesh, subjected to the better, obeys its saving commands; not that it migrates into the nature of the soul, its matter poured over into fineness. But because, having renounced delights and been cleansed from every stain of the vices, it walks the path of heavenly conduct with a disposition of obedience, no longer, as before, fighting against the law of the mind, but freed through the law of the mind and the spirit of life from the law of sin, so that the flesh becomes an appendage of the soul, no longer the procuress of the vices but a kind of emulator and, as it were, handmaid of virtue,

142. and again, when the soul does not connive at the enticements of the body nor is overcome by delight in carnal pleasures, but the mind, pure and stripped of servitude to this world, entices and draws the senses of the body into its own pleasures, so that through the practice of hearing and reading it feasts on the increase of virtue and is satisfied with the spiritual nourishment of an inner sap, never to know hunger; for reason is the food of the mind and an excellent nourishment of sweetness. It does not burden the members, nor does it turn them into the shameful things of nature, but into ornaments, when the wallowing-place of lusts is changed into a temple of God and the lodging-house of vices begins to be a sanctuary of virtues. This surely happens when the flesh, returned to its nature, recognizes the nurse of its vigor and, laying aside the audacity of contumacy, is joined to the judgment of the governing soul, such as it was when it received the secret places of paradise to dwell in, before, infected with the venom of the pestilent serpent, it came to know a sacrilegious hunger and, through eagerness of gluttony, passed by the memory of the divine precepts which clung to the senses of the soul.

143. From here sin is said to have flowed, as from the body and the soul as its parents. While the nature of the body is tempted, the soul, in ill health, suffers with it. Had it reined in the appetite of the body, the origin of sin would have been extinguished at its very birth; but the soul, steeped with it as though it had been poured in by a virile motion of the body, its own vigor also corrupted, heavy with burdens not its own, brought it forth. For that more violent and stronger sex is borne along by a kind of powerful impulse, like a virile affection; she strives to hold to a reason gentler rather than more vehement. From these, therefore, the motion of diverse desires grew up.

144. But when the soul returns to herself, confronted with shame at her misshapen offspring, she abjures her degenerate heirs, disowning the motions, loathing the sin. The flesh too, bankrupted by the principal of heavy labors and wearied by the interest of a miserable injury, when it grieves that it has been pierced by its own desires, which it begot for itself, as by the briars of the world, hastens to put off the old man, so as to take itself away from itself, lest a succession doomed to perish leave its improvident parent destitute. Again, the irrational motion of the desires, enticing her with the allurements of pleasure as with the painted comeliness of some beauty, took her to itself as if for partnership. Thus pleasure, as a kind of daughter-in-law of the body and the soul, marries the motion of depraved desire.

145. So, as long as an undivided and inseparable consent remained in one house, with the vices conspiring together, there seemed to be no division. But when Christ sent upon the earth the fire with which to burn up the sins of the flesh, or the sword which signifies the edge of drawn power, by which the secrets of the spirit and of the marrow are pierced, then the flesh and the soul, renewed by the mysteries of regeneration, forgetting what they were, begin to be what they were not, and separate themselves from the fellowship of the old vice, though it was dear to them before, and cast out, as it were, the union with a prodigal posterity, so that the parents are divided against the children, while the intemperate motion is disowned by the temperance of the body, and the soul declines partnership in guilt, and no place remains for that pleasure, alien and taken in from outside.

146. The children also are divided against the parents, while the old vices shun the aged censure of the renewed man, and the more youthful pleasure flees the rule of dutiful love as it would the discipline of a serious household. Nor is it incongruous if we think that these too divide themselves so that they may become better than their parents, especially since he has said in what follows: if anyone comes to me and does not hate father or mother and children and brothers and sisters, even his own soul, he cannot be my disciple. And therefore, according to the simple form of understanding, the son who follows Christ is put before Gentile parents; for religion surpasses the duties of dutiful love.

147. That too according to a higher interpretation, because sin is born of the flesh and works as if in the womb of the flesh, of which the Apostle says: but if I do what I do not will, it is no longer I that work it, but the sin that dwells in me. When the blood of the Lord, poured out for the life of this world, has abolished the vices, it is turned from offense into grace — for sin abounded, that grace might abound the more — and it comes about that repentance, arising from sin, drives on to conversion of purpose and to the desire of spiritual grace. And so what was for me unto death will be unto salvation. So sin, washed away by the flowing water of the font, is separated from the flesh from which it was begotten, and, while each one desires to redeem his offense, out of the chain of guilt there comes a zeal for discipline.

148. The concupiscence of evil things also, and a certain more fervent motion of desire, is poured over by the word of God into an appetite for divine charity and love, and in the same nature there comes to be a different discipline, and that appetite of body and soul acquires for itself a pleasure in the heavenly mysteries much better than before. For the mind is nourished by the knowledge of things, and, having learned the promise of things to come, raised higher, it disdains the old works of the soul; the natural man indeed does not perceive the things that are of the Spirit of God, for it is foolishness to him; but the spiritual man judges all things, and he himself is judged by no one.

149. While you are going with your adversary to the magistrate, on the way take pains to be freed from him, lest perhaps he condemn you before the judge, and the judge hand you over to the exactor, and the exactor send you to prison. I tell you, you will not come out from there until you pay even the last farthing. Matthew too set this down, but he in a particular sense, this one in a general; for he thought it was said of restoring peace between brothers at variance, this one of repentance and the amendment of every offense. Let us then discuss who the adversary is, who the magistrate, who the judge, or who the exactor, or what we should think the farthing is, which, unless one has paid it, he is sent to prison.

150. And according to Matthew the adversary is one with whom you seem to have been least of all at peace in this life, who pursues you before the judge to come of the dead and the living with the charge of an abiding enmity; but according to Luke our adversary is above all that one who sows the enticements of sins, that he may have as partners in punishment those whom he had as companions in error; and therefore he seeks associates in guilt, that he may betray them as liable to punishment. The apostle Peter warns us to beware of him, saying: your adversary the devil, like a lion ravening and roaring, seeks whom he may devour.

151. According to Matthew our adversary is every practice of virtue and the apostolic and prophetic word, which binds us to harsher precepts and to the teachings of a harder life, with which it befits us to agree, so that we emulate it by works, lest by some stubbornness of ours we be shown to have fallen away from it. But according to Luke nothing is more our adversary than our own lapse, which accuses us by the evidences of our life; not that any judge to come seeks the service of an accuser, but because before him who takes cognizance of all, our own conduct accuses us, when it is found a stranger to the practice of virtue and to the apostolic precepts.

152. So every practice of the vices is an adversary to us, lust is an adversary, avarice an adversary, all wickedness an adversary, every unjust thought an adversary, every bad conscience, in short, which both afflicts us here and in the future will accuse and betray us, as the Apostle testifies, saying: their conscience bearing them witness and their thoughts among themselves accusing or even defending. But if conscience betrays each one, how much more is the work of our deeds present before God, which, expressed in our body, will be reviewed at the last time, and the secret of our thought, written in our hearts, will be read! Let us therefore take pains that, while we are set in this course of life, we may be freed from wicked action as from an evil adversary, lest, while we go with the adversary to the magistrate, he condemn our error on the way.

153. Therefore also according to Matthew he says: be agreeing with your adversary, while you are with him on the way; but the Greek said εὐνοῶν, that is, well-wishing. For if, while we are in this life, we release ourselves from the bonds of the devil, neither will he be condemned on our account, and we shall be strangers to his bonds. Hence also Psalm 79 (80) is written for the Assyrian. For you counsel well for the adversary and act for that Assyrian, that is, the vain one, if, freed from his snares, you show him this good will, so that he escapes the penalty of your lapse and of death. But if you stick fast in his bonds, he will hand you over as a defendant to the magistrate, he who is both accuser and betrayer.

154. Who is the magistrate except him with whom is all power and who claims for himself the lofty dignity of full and perfect time, to whom the holy prophet, relying on the consciousness of good works and not fearing the adversary, hastens, saying: my soul has thirsted for the living God; when shall I come and appear before the face of God? For this magistrate will hand the defendant over to the judge, that is, to him to whom he gave power over the living and the dead, and gave it by nature, not by grace; for he did not receive a power that he did not have, but that which he took from the substance of the Father when he was begotten. He who showed you the accuser shows you this magistrate and judge, and shows when he is to be revealed. In the day, he says, when God will judge the secrets of men according to my Gospel through Jesus Christ our Lord. This then is the judge, Jesus Christ, through whom secrets are convicted and the penalty of wicked work is ordered.

155. Do you wish to know that Christ is the judge, who hands over to the exactor and sends to prison? Ask him himself, or rather read him in the Gospel, saying: take him and send him into the outer darkness. He himself also showed his exactors in another place, where he says: so it will be at the consummation of the world; the angels will go out and separate the wicked from the midst of the righteous and send them into the furnace of fire: there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth.

156. It remains now for us to understand what the figure of the farthing means. And the hidden meaning of a spiritual understanding seems to be expressed by the name of an everyday thing. For just as those who pay out money render a debt, and the name of the loan is not cancelled before the whole amount of the entire principal, down to the last coin, is paid by whatever kind of payment, so by the compensation of charity and of other acts, or by any satisfaction, the penalty of sin is discharged.

157. It is not idle either that in this place he set down not two mites as elsewhere, not a penny, not a denarius, but a farthing; for exchange in farthings is like a kind of compensation, when one thing is rendered and another is signified as paid. So here too either the injury is redeemed by the price of charity, or the penalty is relaxed by the assessment of the injury.

158. We remember that a farthing is usually given in the baths, by the offering of which, as there each one receives the means of bathing, so here he receives that of washing clean, because each one's sin is washed away by the kind of condition written above, when the guilty man is exercised by penalties so long that he pays the punishments of the error he committed.

159. Concerning those Galileans, however, whose blood Pilate mixed with their sacrifices, there seems to be a certain figure touching those who, under the compulsion of the devil, do not offer sacrifice purely. Theirs is prayer unto sin, as was written of Judas the betrayer, who, placed amid the sacrifices, was planning the betrayal of the Lord's blood.

160. A certain man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard. What does it mean that the Lord in his Gospel frequently brings in the parable of the fig? For you have elsewhere that at the Lord's command all the greenness of this leafy tree withered. From which you understand the Creator of all, who can command natures either to wither suddenly or to grow green. Elsewhere he mentions that from the tenderness of this tree and from its leaves the coming of summer is usually inferred, signifying by both that the empty glory which the Jewish people displayed fell like a flower at the coming of the Lord, because it continued unfruitful in works, and that the day of judgment, like the coming of summer, in which the ripe fruits of all lands are stored away, is to be measured by the fullness of the Church, in which the Jews too are to believe.

161. Here too, then, let us seek some hidden meaning of a higher sense. The fig tree is in the vineyard, and the vineyard was the Lord Sabaoth's, which he gave over to be plundered by the nations. And so he who caused his vineyard to be plundered here also ordered the fig tree to be cut down; and the comparison with this tree is apt for the synagogue, because just as this tree, abounding with flowing leaves, disappoints the hope of its owner by a vain expectation of hoped-for yields, so also in the synagogue, while its teachers, unfruitful in works, yet glory in their words as in abounding leaves, the empty shadow of the Law overflows. And the hope of a false yield, once looked upon, mocks the prayers of the believing people.

162. There is also something in the nature of the tree by which you may the more believe that the appearance of the synagogue is expressed by comparison with it. For if you inquire carefully, you will find the habit of this nature distinct from the manner of the other trees. For the others bear a flower before the fruit, and by the flower as their herald mark the fruits to come; this alone from the beginning puts forth fruits instead of flowers. In the others the flower is shaken off and the fruits are born; in this the fruits fall so that fruits may succeed. So those first ones, empty of fruit, come forth in place of a flower; thus those that do not know the order of nature, through a kind of premature habit of birth, cannot keep the benefit of nature. For where the bud is wont to push itself out from the middle of the bark, there all the tiniest fruits of this kind burst out. Of these we read in the Song of Songs: the fig tree puts forth its green figs. And so, while the other shrubs whiten in early spring, the fig alone does not know how to grow white with a flower of its own, perhaps because no riper use is found in these fruits. For when others succeed, these are rejected as though degenerate and, weak because of a withering root, are cast off in favor of the renewed ones, to which the sap is more useful. Yet some few remain and do not fall, on which this yield has smiled, that they come forth from between two twigs, a short little shoot breaking out, so that, covered by twin protections, as in the bosom of nature their parent, they grow in by the warmth of a fuller sap. These, called forth by the mild temperature of a gentler breeze and grown riper with longer seasons, when they have put off the wild temper of their former sap, are preferred to the others in appearance and in the grace of ripeness.

163. Look now at the worship and the minds of the Jews, who, like the first fruits of the ill-bearing synagogue, fell down in the likeness of a dropping green fig, so that the fruits of our race, which will remain beyond the age, might succeed. For the first people of the synagogue, weak as it were with a root of withering works, could not draw up the richness of natural wisdom, and therefore fell like a useless fruit, so that, as from the same shoots of the fruitful tree, the new people of the Church might come forth from the fatness of the ancient religion. So that which was ceased to be, that this which was not might begin. Yet the first were from Israel. Those whom a branch of stronger nature had brought forth, under the shadow of the Law and of the cross, colored in the bosom of both with a twofold sap after the example of the ripening green fig, surpassed the others by the grace of most beautiful fruits, those to whom it is said: you will sit upon twelve thrones judging the twelve tribes of Israel.

164. Nor is this foreign, that Adam and Eve, those first parents of our race as of our error, who covered themselves with the leaves of this tree, deserved to be exiles from paradise, when, conscious of their transgression, they shunned the presence of the Lord as he walked about, so as to signify what was to come: that in the last times the people of the Jews, at the coming of the Lord Savior, who came to call them, recognizing that it had been stripped of its virtues through the temptations of the devil and dreading the uncovered disgraces of its conscience, would, straying from religion and confounded by its transgression, withdraw from the Lord, covering the shameful parts of its deeds with the veils of flowing words as with leaves.

165. So those who plucked leaves from the fig tree, not fruits, were cast out of the kingdom of God; for they were a living soul. The second Adam came and sought no longer leaves but fruits; for he was a life-giving spirit. And in the spirit the fruit of virtue is acquired, the Lord is adored. And the Lord sought it, not because he did not know that fruit was lacking to the fig tree, but to show in a figure that the synagogue ought already to have had fruit.

Then from what follows he teaches that he did not come before the time, he who came for three years; for so you have: behold, it is three years since I came seeking fruit on this fig tree and I do not find it. Cut it down therefore; why does it even occupy the ground?

166. He came to Abraham, he came to Moses, he came to Mary, that is, he came in the seal, he came in the Law, he came in the body. We recognize his coming from his benefits: in one place there is purification, in another sanctification, in another justification. Circumcision purified, the Law sanctified, grace justified: one in all, and all one. For no one can be cleansed except one who fears the Lord. No one deserves to receive the Law unless purified from guilt; no one approaches grace unless he has known the Law. So the people of the Jews could neither be purified, because it had a circumcision not of the mind but of the body, nor be sanctified, because it did not know the power of the Law, following carnal things rather than spiritual — the Law, however, is spiritual — nor be justified, because it did not repent of its sins and therefore did not know grace.

167. Rightly therefore no fruit was found in the synagogue, and so it is ordered to be cut down. But the good husbandman, and perhaps he in whom the foundation of the Church lies, foreseeing that another was to be sent to the Gentiles and he himself to those who are of the circumcision, devoutly intervenes that it be not cut down, trusting in his calling that the people of the Jews too can be saved through the Church, and so he says: let it alone this year also, until I dig around it and put in a basket of dung.

168. How quickly he recognized that the hardness and pride of the Jews were the causes of the barrenness! And so he who knows how to detect vices knows how to cultivate. He promises that the hard places of their heart must be dug with the apostolic mattocks, so that the twice-sharpened word may turn over the mind's crust, laid over it by long neglect, and, the heart being broken up, may rouse the sense, now thriving with the breath of the air, lest a heap of earth overwhelm and hide the root of wisdom. He says also that a basket of dung must be put in. Great indeed is the force of dung, so great that it makes fruitful things from unfruitful, green from withered, productive from barren. On it Job sat when he was tempted, and he could not be overcome; and Paul reckons things as dung, that he may gain Christ. Indeed, whereas Job had earlier lost very many things, after he sat on the dung he had nothing that the devil could take away from him. So the earth is good that is dug, good the dung that is put in. Indeed the Lord raises the needy from the earth and lifts up the poor from the dung.

169. And so, through the exercise of spiritual understanding and a disposition of humility, that good husbandman judges that the Jews too will be fruitful in the Gospel of Christ. For he remembered that the Lord spoke through Haggai, that on the twenty-fourth of the ninth month, from the day on which the temple of the Lord Almighty was founded, the vine he says and the figs and the pomegranates and the olive trees, which will not bear fruit, from this day I will bless. By this it is revealed that just at the setting of the declining year, that is, at the sunset of an age already growing old, the holy temple of God, which is the Church, was to be founded, by whose grace, through the sanctification of the washing, the peoples of the Jews and of the nations can have the fruit of their merits.

170. So through the nature of the tree the appearance of the synagogue is indicated, fruitful in its second yields — for we are from the seed of the fathers — and rightly are the Jews compared to dropping green figs, because with a fattened heart and a very hard neck they cannot come to their own perpetuity. If they die and as it were drop off from this world, so as to be reborn into the inner man through the grace of the washing, they will surely be fruitful. But the unbelief of obstinate men made the synagogue useless, and therefore, as barren, it is ordered to be cut down.

171. What was said of the Jews I think all must beware of, and we most of all, lest, empty of merits, we occupy the fertile ground of the Church, we who, like pomegranates, ought to bear blessed fruits within, fruits of modesty, fruits of union, fruits of mutual charity and love, enclosed in the one womb of mother Church, lest the wind harm them, lest the hail shake them off, lest the heat of desire scorch them, lest the shower of moisture crush them.

172. Some, however, think that this fig tree is by allegory a figure not of the synagogue but of malice and wickedness. These, however, differ in nothing except that they choose the genus instead of the species; and they say that they are guarding against this, because the Lord said to the fig tree: may fruit never be born from you forever, whereas we surely know that very many of the Jews have believed and will believe. But he who believes is no longer the fruit of the synagogue but of the Church, and he is not born of the synagogue who is reborn in the Church. For as there are those who went out from us, but were not of us, for if they had been of us, they would have remained with us, so we say of those of the Jews who believe that, if they were of the synagogue, they would remain in the synagogue, but they went out from the synagogue for this reason, that they may be believed not to have been of the synagogue. Moreover, he would otherwise be interceding for malice and saying that it should be cultivated so as to bear fruit, when the Lord came for this very purpose, to abolish the seeds of wickedness.

And he was teaching in their synagogue on the sabbaths. And behold, a woman who had a spirit of infirmity for eighteen years and was bent over.

173. How quickly he showed what he was saying about the synagogue! He shows that he had come to that very tree, since he was preaching in it. Then in the infirm woman there follows a figure as of the Church, which, when it has fulfilled the measure of the Law and of the resurrection, raised up to the lofty summit in that perpetual rest, will not be able to feel the bending down of our infirmity. Nor could this woman have been cured otherwise than because she fulfilled the Law and grace, the Law in the commandments, grace in the washing, through which, dead to the world, we rise again to Christ; for in the ten words is the perfection of the Law, in the number eight is the fullness of the resurrection. The work of the sabbath, then, is a sign of the future, that each one who has fulfilled the Law and grace will through the mercy of Christ be stripped of the troubles of bodily frailty. And therefore sanctification was sent ahead earlier in a sign through Moses, that there might be a training for the future sanctification and for the spiritual observance of abstaining from worldly works. Indeed God too rested from the works of the world; not from works, for his operation is everlasting and unceasing, as the Son says: my Father works until now and I work, so that, after the likeness of God, our worldly works, not our religious ones, might cease.

174. But the ruler of the synagogue, not understanding this, was forbidding anyone to be healed on the sabbath, though the sabbath is a type of the rest to come — and therefore it is a rest from evil work, not from good — and for this reason it was prescribed that, carrying no burden of sins and not empty of good works, we should celebrate the sabbaths that are to come after death. Hence the Lord seems to answer spiritually, when he says: hypocrites, does not each one of you on the sabbath loose his ox or his ass and lead it to water?

175. For why did he point to no other animal, except to show that, though the rulers of the synagogue opposed it, it would yet come to pass that the Jewish and the Gentile people would lay down the thirst of the body and the heat of this world by the abundance of the Lord's fountain? The ox indeed has known its owner and the she-ass its master's manger. And so that people, which before was fed on the nourishment of cheap hay, which withers before it is plucked up, has received that bread which came down from heaven. And therefore through the calling out of two peoples he says that the Church will be saved, which, when the time of the Law was fulfilled and in the age of the Lord's resurrection, had received the time of its release. How merciful then is the Lord, how kind in both, whether he pities or punishes! In the type of the synagogue he orders the tree to be cut down, in the type of the Church he saves the woman. How sweet the parable, and how easy the solution. He compares bond with bond, so that the charge of the Jews may be refuted by their own action. For while they themselves loose the bonds of animals on the sabbath, they reproach the Lord, who freed men from the bonds of sins.

To what is the kingdom of God like, and to what shall I reckon it to be like? It is like a grain of mustard, which a man took and cast into his garden, and it grew and became a tree, and the birds of the sky rested in its branches.

176. The present reading teaches us that the nature of comparisons is to be looked at, not their appearance. So let us see why the lofty kingdom of heaven is compared to a grain of mustard; for I remember that in another place too a grain of mustard is read, where it is compared to faith, the Lord saying: if you have faith like a grain of mustard, you will say to this mountain: be lifted up and cast into the sea. And this faith is not slight but great, which can command a mountain to remove itself; for the Lord does not require a middling faith of the apostles, who, he knows, must contend against the height of spiritual wickedness that exalts itself. Do you wish to know that great faith is required? Read in the Apostle: and if I have all faith, so that I move mountains.

177. So if the kingdom of heaven is like a grain of mustard and faith is like a grain of mustard, faith surely is the kingdom of heaven and the kingdom of heaven is faith. So he who has faith has the kingdom of heaven, and the kingdom is within us and faith is within us; for we read: the kingdom of God is within you; and elsewhere: have faith in yourselves. Indeed Peter, who had all faith, received the keys of the kingdom of heaven, that he might open it to others also.

178. Now let us consider, from the nature of mustard, what the force of the comparison is. Its grain is certainly a cheap and simple thing: if it begins to be ground, it pours out its strength. Faith too at first seems simple, but if it is ground by adversities, it pours out the grace of its power, so as to fill others too, who either hear or read, with its odor. Our martyrs Felix, Nabor and Victor are a grain of mustard: they had the odor of faith, but they lay hidden. Persecution came, they laid down their arms, they bent their necks, and, crushed by the sword, they spread the grace of their martyrdom through the bounds of the whole world, so that it may rightly be said: their sound has gone out into all the earth.

179. But faith is in one place ground, in another pressed, in another sown. The Lord himself is a grain of mustard. He was untouched by injury, but the people, which had not touched him, did not know him, as one does not know a grain of mustard. He chose to be ground, so that we might say: for we are the good odor of Christ to God; he chose to be pressed, whence Peter too said: the crowds press you; he chose to be sown like a grain, which a man took and cast into his garden. For in a garden Christ was both taken and buried, in a garden he grew, where he also rose again, and he became a tree, as it is written: as the apple tree among the trees of the wood, so is my brother in the midst of the sons.

180. So you too, sow Christ in your garden — a garden is surely a place full of flowers and of diverse fruits — in which the grace of your work may blossom and the manifold odor of varied virtue breathe forth. There then let Christ be, where the fruit is. Sow the Lord Jesus: he is a grain when he is seized, he is a tree when he rises again, a tree overshadowing the world; he is a grain when he is buried in the earth, he is a tree when he is raised up to heaven.

181. Press, too, with Christ, and sow faith. Faith is pressed when we believe Christ crucified. Paul pressed faith when he said: and I, coming to you, brothers, came not with eminence of speech or of wisdom, preaching to you the mystery of God; for I did not judge myself to know anything among you except Christ Jesus and him crucified. And because he taught how to press faith, he taught also how to lift it, saying: now indeed we no longer know Christ crucified. And we sow faith when, from the Gospel and the readings of the apostles and prophets, we believe in the Lord's Passion. We sow faith, then, when we cover it, as with softened and worked soil, with the Lord's flesh, so that, as it were by the warmth and pressure of the sacred body, faith may sow itself abroad. For he who has believed that the Son of God was made man believes that he died for us, believes that he rose again for us. I sow faith, then, when I plant his burial among it.

182. Do you wish to know that Christ is a grain and that Christ was sown? Unless a grain of wheat falling into the earth dies, it remains alone; but if it has died, it will bring much fruit. So we have not erred, since we said what he himself had already said. And he is both a grain of wheat, because he strengthens the heart of man, and a grain of mustard, because he kindles the heart of man. And although both suit all things, yet he seems a grain of wheat when his resurrection is treated; for he is the bread of God who came down from heaven, because the Word of God and the example of the resurrection nourish minds, sharpen hope, strengthen affection; but a grain of mustard, because the discourse about the Lord's Passion is more bitter and sharper, more bitter for weeping, sharper for moving. And so, when we hear and read that the Lord fasted, the Lord thirsted, the Lord wept, the Lord was beaten, the Lord said at the time of the Passion: watch and pray, lest you enter into temptation, then, rebuked as by the juice of a more bitter word, we temper the sweetnesses of bodily pleasures that are sweeter than is right. So he who sows a grain of mustard sows the kingdom of heaven.

183. Do not despise this grain of mustard; it is indeed the least of all seeds, but when it has grown it becomes greater than all herbs. If Christ is the grain of mustard, how is Christ either least, or how does he grow? Truly not by nature, but by appearance he grows out again. Do you wish to know him as least? We saw him, and he had no form nor comeliness. Learn him as greatest: fair in form above the sons of men. For he who had no form nor comeliness was made more excellent than the angels, beyond all the glory of the prophets, whom weak Israel had eaten like herbs; for of the bread by which hearts are strengthened, it had refused the one and had not received the other.

184. And Christ is a seed, because he is the seed of Abraham; for the promises were spoken to Abraham and to his seed. He does not say: 'and to seeds', as of many, but as of one: 'and to your seed', which is Christ. And not only is Christ a seed, but even the least of all seeds, because he came not in kingship, not in riches, not in the wisdom of this world. But suddenly, like a tree, he spread out the lofty crown of his power, so that we say: under his shadow I desired and sat down. And often at the same time. I think, he seemed both a tree and a grain. He is a grain when it is said: is not this the son of Joseph the carpenter? But amid these words he suddenly grew, as the Jews themselves testify, since they could not, as it were, reach the branches of a spreading tree, saying: whence has this man this wisdom?

185. The grain, then, is the figure, the tree is wisdom, in whose leafy branches that night-raven in the dwelling, that solitary sparrow in the building, that man caught up into paradise and to be caught up into the air and the clouds, now rest in a safe seat. There rest also the powers and angels of heaven and all who have deserved to fly up by spiritual deeds. Saint John rested when he reclined on the breast of Jesus; nay rather, he himself too is extended like a branch by the sap of that tree. Peter is a branch, Paul is a branch, forgetting the things that are behind and reaching out to those that are before. Into their bosoms and certain recesses of their discourses we who were far off, that is, we who have been gathered from the nations, whom the storm and whirlwind of spiritual wickedness long tossed through the void of the world, fly, taking up the oars of the virtues, that the shade of the saints may defend us from the heat of this world, flourishing now in the security of a sure anchorage,

186. because our soul, before bent down by heavy sins like that woman, rescued like a sparrow from the snare of the hunters, has migrated into the branches and the mountains of the Lord. And so we who before were vain with superfluous observances and flitting about with empty levity, now, through faith in Christ, our hands loosed and free from the bonds of the sabbaths, give ourselves to good works, and in our very feasts we have liberty, we shut out intemperance, lest, free from the Law, we be slaves to desires; for the Law bound men to itself, that it might release them from desires. Grace, which took away the lesser servitude, has commanded much weightier things: all things for us are lawful, but not all things are expedient: for it is a grave thing to use a power so as to fall back under a power. Cease to be under the Law, that by virtue you may be above the Law.

To what shall I reckon the kingdom of God to be like? It is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in flour, until the whole was leavened.

187. This comparison belongs among the questions to be inquired into, since it spreads so much ambiguity that it has drawn the differing opinions of very many. Fittingly, therefore, we said above that Christ was a grain of wheat, because a spiritual leaven lay beneath for us; and for that reason many think that Christ is the leaven, because it raises up the power it has received. And because leaven, made from flour, surpasses its own kind in force, not in appearance, so Christ too, sprung from the fathers and equal to them in body, yet stood out incomparable in divinity. Let the holy Church, therefore, which is figured in the type of this woman of the Gospel, whose flour we are, hide the Lord Jesus in the inner parts of our mind, until the color of heavenly wisdom covers the secret inmost places of our soul.

188. And since we read in Matthew that the leaven was hidden in three measures, it seemed fitting that we should believe the Son of God hidden in the Law, covered in the prophets, fulfilled in the preachings of the Gospel, so that he may acquire perfect faith for us out of them all, and, formed by the comparison of the writings in us who are his body, may be fulfilled all in all; for he was the Word of God and the mystery which has been hidden from the ages and from the generations, and nothing can be said that bears more witness to the expression of his everlastingness. For he surely was; for he was in such a way that, hidden from the sacrilegious, made manifest in the saints, predestined before the ages, he was kept for glory. And this is glory, brothers, that we can search out the mystery hidden from the ages in God. Which is surely in God and from God; for God cannot admit an alien nature.

189. Nor am I in doubt that some think this was said of this world, until it be leavened in the Law, the prophets, the Gospel, so that every tongue may confess the Lord. So let us discuss all things, let us inquire more diligently. No one finds except him who has first sought. Let us build the tower, let us reckon the cost of the Scriptures, let us compare the expenses, lest anyone say even of any one of ours: this man wished to build and could not complete it. He who builds ought to lay a good foundation. Faith is a good foundation, a good foundation that of the apostles and prophets; for our faith rises up out of the two testaments, and he does no wrong who has said that the measure of perfect faith is equal in both, since the Lord himself says: if you believed Moses, you would believe me, because the Lord spoke in Moses too. Well then is the measure perfect in both, because he is perfect in both and the faith of both is one, because the oracle and the response are of one power and sense.

190. Yet I am glad to follow what the Lord himself taught, that the leaven of the Church is spiritual doctrine; for since it is written: beware of the leaven of the Pharisees, and since the Apostle has said: not in the leaven of malice and wickedness, it is shown that doctrine is leaven. But the leaven of tares is one thing, the leaven of wheat another; hence we also assent to good authors, so as to say that the Church sanctifies with a spiritual leaven the reborn man, who is in body and soul and spirit; for the body and the soul are sanctified, and the spiritual grace itself has received an increase of sanctification, when, through the office of the Church, as of something that leavens, and through the teaching of the Scriptures, which grows together as it were by the comparison and abundance of heavenly words, its use, poured into the whole man and mixed through him, has become ingrained, so that they themselves too become one leaven. This surely happens when these three agree with one another in an even balance of desires and an equal concord of wills breathes upon them.

191. This, then, is the work of the Church, not hasty nor fortuitous, but brought together by long meditation, that these three may be one, whole from the law of sin. Of this view we have the Apostle as champion, saying: may the Lord himself sanctify you in all things, that your spirit and soul and body may be kept whole without complaint in the day of our Lord Jesus Christ. This indeed does not come about through worldly temptations, unless that woman of the Gospel hides that leaven, to which the kingdom of heaven is compared, in three measures of flour, until the whole is leavened; for there are three measures, as I said, of flesh, soul and spirit, but the spirit, by which all of us set in this body live, is this more truly when the wantonness of the flesh does not overflow and the soul is not bent by bodily errors and the measure of living is kept unharmed in the whole man. But because the equality of the measures hardly persists without the aid of the Church and of doctrine, for this reason that woman, who bears the figure of the Church, mixes the power of spiritual doctrine into them until that whole inner, hidden man of the heart is leavened and rises into the grace of the heavenly bread. For well is the doctrine of Christ called leaven, because Christ is bread, and the Apostle says: for we, many, are one bread, one body.

192. So it becomes one leaven when the flesh does not lust against the spirit nor the spirit against the flesh, but we mortify the deeds of the flesh, and the soul, conscious that it has received the breath of life from the breathing of God, turns away from the earthly contagions of worldly ties. Hence the Apostle too has bidden us walk not in the flesh but in the spirit, so that, sanctified through the washing of regeneration, putting off the old man with his desires and having put on the new, who is created according to Christ, we may walk not in the oldness of the letter but in the newness of the spirit, so that even at the time of the resurrection an incorrupt communion of body and soul and spirit may remain for us, and that now we may obtain all that we ask. Many think that the Lord also signified this when he says: if two of you agree on earth, concerning anything whatever that you ask, it will be done for you by my Father, who is in heaven.

193. And so some take the two as the soul and the body, others as the soul and the spirit, so that, if on earth, that is, in the body, the soul and the spirit agree with each other and do not fight one another with diverse desires, all things prayed for may be seen to be capable of being brought to effect. This comes about when both have become one, if, the enmities being removed or dissolved, the two, that is, the soul and the spirit, are founded into one new man, so that we pray with the spirit, and pray also with the mind. Although many take them as the two peoples, from Israel and from the Gentiles, to be gathered into one at the time of the resurrection, so that there may come a perfection to remain forever, and that which is in part may be destroyed, many again take them as husband and wife agreeing with one another in the zeal of charity.

194. So if in this life the three measures have remained in the same leaven until they are leavened and become one, so that there is no difference between them in their equality and we do not seem compounded of the diversity of three, there will be in the future for those who love Christ an incorrupt communion, and we shall not remain compound; for we who are now compound shall be one and shall be transformed into one substance. For in the resurrection one part will not be inferior to another, as now the weakness of the flesh in us is frail, and the bodily habit of our nature either lies open to wounds or is subject to injuries or, pressed down by the mass of its own burden, cannot lift its step higher and raise it above the earth; but we shall be shaped into the grace of a simple creature, when what was said by John has been fulfilled: Beloved, now we are sons of God, and it has not yet been revealed what we shall be, but we know that when it has been revealed, we shall be like him. So since the nature of God is simple — God is indeed spirit — we too shall be shaped into the same image, so that as is the heavenly, such are the heavenly also. Therefore, as we have borne the image of that earthly one, let us bear also the image of this heavenly one, which our mind ought to put on.

195. And therefore, in the first place after this, the man with dropsy is cured, in whom the overflowing flux of the flesh weighed down the duties of the soul and quenched the ardor of the spirit. Next humility is taught, when at that wedding feast the seeking of a higher place is checked, yet gently, so that the kindness of persuasion might shut out the harshness of coercion, reason might advance to the effect of persuasion, and correction might amend the affection. To this, as at the next threshold, kindness is joined, which by the definition of the Lord's sentence is marked out thus, if it is bestowed on the poor and the weak; for to be hospitable to those who will repay is an affection of avarice.

196. Last, as to a man whose service is completed, the pay of despising possessions is prescribed, since neither can he who, intent on lower pursuits, buys up earthly possessions for himself obtain the kingdom of heaven, since the Lord says: sell all your goods and follow me, nor he who buys oxen, since Elisha killed those that he had and divided them among the people. And he who takes a wife thinks of the things that are of the world, not those of God; not that marriage is blamed, but because integrity is called to a greater honor, since the unmarried woman and the widow thinks of the things that are of the Lord, that she may be holy in body and spirit. For she who is married thinks of the things that are of the world, how she may please her husband.

197. But that we may now return to favor with the married too, as above with the widows, we do not shun the opinion which many follow, so that we consider three kinds of men to be excluded from the fellowship of that great supper: Gentiles, Jews, heretics.

198. And therefore the Apostle says that avarice must be fled, lest, hindered after the manner of the Gentiles by iniquity, malice, impurity, avarice, we be unable to come to the kingdom of Christ; for everyone indeed who is avaricious or unclean, which is the service of idols, has no inheritance in the kingdom of Christ and of God.

199. The Jews, however, by a bodily ministry place on themselves the yokes of the Law, and therefore, according to the prophet, let us break their bonds and cast away their yoke from us; for we have received Christ, who has hung the gentle yoke of his kindness upon our necks. And the five yokes are those of the ten words, or the five books of the old Law, concerning which he seems to say to the Samaritan woman in the Gospel: for you have had five husbands.

200. But heresy, like Eve, tempts the firmness of faith with a womanish affection, and, slipping with slippery ease, courts the allurements of a false comeliness, neglecting the inviolate beauty of truth. For this reason, then, they make excuses, because the kingdom is closed to no one except him whom the profession of his own voice has shut out; but the Lord graciously invites all, and it is either our sloth or our error that turns us away.

201. So he who bought a farm is a stranger to the kingdom — for, as you have read, the floods swallowed up those who were buying and selling in the time of Noah — and so is he who chose the yoke of the Law rather than the gift of grace, and he who excuses himself because he is taking a wife; for it is written: if anyone comes to me and does not hate his father and mother and wife, he cannot be my disciple. For if for your sake the Lord renounces his mother, saying: who is my mother or who are my brothers? Why should you wish to put anyone before your Lord? But the Lord bids us neither to ignore nature nor to be slaves to nature, but so to indulge nature that you revere its Author and do not fall away from God through love of parents.

202. And so, after the supine disdain of the rich, he betook himself to the Gentiles; he bids the good and the bad come in, to increase the good, to change the disposition of the bad for the better, that what was read today might be fulfilled: then wolves and lambs will feed together. He invites the poor, the weak, the blind, by which we are shown either that no weakness of body shuts anyone out of the kingdom, and that he sins more rarely who lacks the enticement to sin, or that the infirmity of sins is remitted through the mercy of the Lord, so that he who has been redeemed from crime, not by works but by faith, if he glories, may glory in the Lord.

203. So he sends to the outlets of the ways; prudence indeed is sung in the outlets. He sends to the streets, because he sent to sinners, that from the broader ways they might come to the narrow one which leads to life. He sends to the ways and around the hedges, because those surely are fit for the kingdom of heaven who, occupied with no desires for present things, hasten to things to come, set on a kind of path of good will, and who, after the manner of a hedge, which separates cultivated land from uncultivated and keeps off the incursions of beasts, know how to distinguish good and evil and to hold up the defense of faith against the temptations of spiritual wickedness. Indeed the Lord, to show that his vineyard had been fortified, said: and I put a hedge he says around it and dug around it. And the Apostle says that the middle wall of the hedge has been taken away, which had broken the continuity of the defense. Faith therefore and reason are sought, and sought in the streets, that is, in the passages of the inner affection. Because it is written: let your waters be poured out in your streets.

204. Yet this is not enough, that someone who is called should come, unless he has the wedding garment, that is, unless he has faith and charity. And therefore he who has not brought peace and charity to the altars of Christ will be taken up by feet and hands and sent into the outer darkness. There will be weeping, and gnashing of teeth. What is the outer darkness? Is there some prison there too, and quarries to be endured? By no means. But whoever are outside the promises of the heavenly commandments are in the outer darkness, because the commandments of God are light, and whoever is without Christ is in darkness, because Christ is the inner light.

205. So there is neither any gnashing of bodily teeth, nor any perpetual fire of bodily flames, nor is the worm bodily. But these things are said because, just as from much indigestion both fevers and worms are born, so if anyone does not digest his sins by interposing, as it were, a certain sobriety of abstinence, but, mixing sins with sins, contracts as it were a kind of indigestion of old and recent offenses, he will be scorched by his own fire and consumed by his own worms. Hence Isaiah also says: walk in the light of your fire and in the flame which you have kindled. The fire is that which sorrow for sins begets; the worm is so called because the irrational sins of the soul prick the mind and the sense of the guilty man and eat away certain inward parts of the conscience, sins which are born like worms out of each one, as out of the body of the sinner. Indeed the Lord declared this through Isaiah, saying: and they will see the limbs of the men who have transgressed against me; and their worm will not die and their fire will not be quenched.

206. The gnashing of teeth also betrays the disposition of one who is indignant, because each one repents too late, groans too late, is angry with himself too late, that he has sinned with so obstinate a wickedness.

207. What man of you he says who has a hundred sheep, and if one of them has strayed, does not leave the ninety-nine in the desert and go after the one that strayed? You had learned in what went before to banish negligence, to avoid arrogance, to take up devotion, not to be held by worldly occupations, not to prefer perishable things to everlasting ones; but since human frailty cannot keep a firm footing amid such slipperiness of the world, the good physician has also shown you remedies against error, the merciful judge has not denied you the hope of pardon. And so it is not idly that Saint Luke set down three parables in order: the sheep which was lost and was found, the drachma which was lost and was found, the son who was dead and came to life again, so that, called forth by a threefold remedy, we may heal our wounds;

208. a threefold cord indeed will not be broken. Who are these, the father, the shepherd, the woman? Are they not God the Father, Christ and the Church? Christ carries you on his body, he who took your sins upon himself; the Church seeks, the Father receives. As a shepherd he carries back, as a mother she searches, as a father he clothes. The first is mercy, the second intercession, the third reconciliation. Each fits each: the Redeemer comes to aid, the Church intercedes, the Author is reconciled. The mercy of the divine work is the same, but the grace differs according to our merits. The weary sheep is called back by the shepherd, the drachma which was lost is found, the son returns to his father by his own footsteps and comes back with full repentance for the error he condemns. Hence it is well written: men and beasts you will save, O Lord. What are these beasts? The prophet said: I sow Israel with the seed of men and Judah with the seed of animals. And so Israel is saved as a man, Judah is gathered as a sheep. I would rather, then, be a son than a sheep; for a sheep is found by the shepherd, a son is honored by the father.

209. Let us rejoice, then, since that sheep which had perished in Adam is lifted up in Christ. The shoulders of Christ are the arms of the cross. There I laid down my sins; on the neck of that noble gibbet I rested. That sheep is one in genus, not in species; for we all indeed are one body, but many members, and therefore it is written: you, however, are the body of Christ and members from his members. There came therefore the Son of Man to save what had perished, that is, all, because as in Adam all die, so in Christ all are made alive.

210. Rich, then, is the shepherd, of whom we all are a hundredth part. He has innumerable flocks of angels, of archangels, of dominions, of powers, of thrones and of others, which he left on the mountains. And since they are rational, not undeservedly do they rejoice at the redemption of men. Yet this too may serve as an incentive to uprightness, if each one believes that his conversion will be pleasing to the companies of the angels, whose patronage he ought to seek, or whose offense he ought to fear. So you too, be a joy to the angels; let them rejoice at your return.

211. It is not idle either that that woman rejoices at the drachma found. This is no ordinary drachma, on which is the figure of the prince. And therefore the image of the king is the wealth of the Church. We are sheep: let us pray that he deign to set us beside the water of refreshment; we are sheep, I say: let us seek pasture; we are drachmas: let us have value; we are sons: let us hasten to the Father.

212. Nor let us fear because we have squandered on earthly pleasures the patrimony of spiritual dignity that we received. As for the treasure which the father bestowed on the son, the treasure which he had, the wealth of faith is never exhausted: though he has given all, he has all, for he does not lose what he has given. Nor should you fear that he may not receive you; God does not indeed delight in the destruction of the living. Already, running to meet you as you come, he will fall upon your neck — the Lord indeed raises up those who are dashed down — he will give you a kiss, which is the pledge of devotion and love, he will order a robe, a ring, shoes to be brought out. You still fear an injury, he restores your dignity; you dread punishment, he offers a kiss; you fear reproach, he prepares a feast. But now let us discuss the parable itself.

A certain man had two sons, and the younger said to him: give me the portion of the substance.

213. You see that the divine patrimony is given to those who ask, and do not think it the father's fault that he gave to the younger. No age is weak for the kingdom of God, nor is faith weighed down by years. He certainly judged himself fit, he who asked. And would that he had not withdrawn from his father: he would not have known the hindrance of age. But after he set out abroad, — rightly, then, did he squander his patrimony, he who withdrew from the Church — after, he says, leaving his father's house, he set out abroad into a far country.

214. For what is farther than to withdraw from oneself and to be separated not by regions but by morals, to be set apart by pursuits, not by lands, and, as though the tide of worldly luxury flowed between, to be divorced in one's deeds? For he who separates himself from Christ is an exile from his fatherland, a citizen of the world. But we are not strangers and sojourners, but we are fellow citizens of the saints and members of the household of God; for we who were far off have been made near in the blood of Christ. Let us not begrudge those who return from a far country, because we too were in a far country, as Isaiah teaches. Thus you have: those who sat in the region of the shadow of death, light has risen for them. The far country, then, is that of the shadow of death; but we, for whom Christ the Lord is spirit before our face, live in the shadow of Christ. And therefore the Church says: in his shadow I desired and sat down. So he, by living luxuriously, consumed all the adornments of nature. Hence you, who have received the image of God, who have his likeness, do not consume it by irrational foulness. You are God's work: do not say to wood: you are my father, lest you receive the likeness of wood, for it is written: let those who make them become like them.

215. There came a famine through that region: a famine not of feasts but of good works and virtues. What fasts are more pitiable? For he who withdraws from the word of God hungers, because man lives not by bread alone but by every word of God. He who withdraws from the fountain thirsts, he who withdraws from the treasure is in want, he who withdraws from wisdom is dulled, he who withdraws from virtue is dissolved. Rightly, therefore, did this one begin to be in want, who abandoned the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God and the depth of the heavenly riches. He began, then, to be in want and to suffer famine for this reason, because nothing is enough for prodigal pleasure. He who does not know how to be filled with everlasting food always suffers hunger for himself.

216. So he went and clung to one of the citizens; for he who clings is in a snare. And this citizen seems to be the prince of this world. Then he is sent to his farm, the one that he bought who excuses himself from the kingdom; and he feeds swine, those surely into which the devil asks to enter, which he hurls headlong into the sea of this world, as they live in filth and stench.

217. And he longed he says to fill his belly with the husks. For the luxurious have no other care but to fill their belly, whose god is the belly. And what food is fitter for men of this sort than that which, like a husk, is empty within and soft without, by which the body is not refreshed but filled, so that it is more a burden than a use?

218. There are those who take the swine for the herds of demons, the husks for the slender virtue of empty men and the boastfulness of their discourses, men who can profit nothing, displaying, by a kind of empty seduction of philosophy and a kind of applause for sonorous eloquence, pomp rather than any usefulness; but these delights cannot be lasting, and therefore no one gave to him;

219. for he was in the region of him who has no one, because he does not have those who are. For all the nations are counted as nothing, but God alone is he who gives life to the dead and calls the things that are not as the things that are.

220. But coming to himself he said: how many loaves the hired servants of my father have in abundance! Well does he return to himself who withdrew from himself. For he who goes back to the Lord restores himself to himself, and he who withdraws from Christ disowns himself. And who are the hired servants except those who serve for wages, who are of Israel, not pursuing what is good from zeal for uprightness, nor called forth by the grace of virtue, but by zeal for profit? But the son, who has the pledge of the Holy Spirit in his heart, does not seek the petty gains of worldly wages, for he keeps the right of an heir. There are also hired servants who are hired for the vineyard. Good hired servants are Peter, John, James, to whom it is said: come, I will make you fishers of men. These abound not in husks but in loaves. Indeed they gathered twelve baskets of fragments. O Lord Jesus, would that you might take the husks from us and give us loaves — for you are the steward in the Father's house — O that you would deign to hire us too as hired servants, though we come late! For you hire even at the eleventh hour and deign to pay an equal wage, an equal wage of life, not of glory; for not for all is laid up the crown of righteousness, but for him who can say: I have fought the good fight.

221. I thought that this should not be passed over, because I know that some say that they are keeping the grace of the washing, or repentance, for themselves until death. First, how do you know whether your soul will be required of you this very night? Next, why should you think that everything can be brought to you while you sit idle? Grant that the grace is one, that the wage is one, yet the prize of victory is different, toward which Paul not in vain stretched himself out, who after the wage of grace still pursued that he might lay hold of the prize, because he knew that, even if the wage of grace was equal, the palm yet belongs to few.

222. And since we have come into the vineyard of the Lord, let us not go away empty; for it is a delight to gather its fruits, to see its hired servants. For what does it mean that the laborers are hired on one day at different hours, except that a thousand years in the eyes of the Lord are as yesterday which has passed, and an hour in the night? What is the night but that which went before, that the day might draw near? And well an hour in the night, because a thousand years are as one day. He knew the force of this day who said: Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today and for ever. He too knew that the day is manifold who wrote: this is the day of the generation of heaven and earth, when they were made; on which day God made heaven and earth and every green thing of the field. For when he had described seven days above, he afterwards embraced in one day all that had been made, showing the whole time of the world in the sight of the Lord as one day, because out of what was formless and dark the form of this world came forth by the brightness of the divine work. So if the day is the whole time of the world, it surely has its hours also in the ages, or the ages themselves are hours. And the hours of the day are twelve. Hence well, in a mystery, Christ is the day, whose apostles are twelve, who shone with heavenly light, the turns of grace within them being marked out.

223. So the householder came and at the first hour hired laborers, perhaps those who from the beginning of the world until the flood deserved to be righteous, of whom he says: and I spoke to you before the light and sent to you my servants the prophets before the light. The third hour begins after the flood, embracing the times of Noah and of the rest, who as good laborers are appointed to the vineyard. That is why Noah was made drunk, as at the midday meal. The sixth and the following hours are marked out by the merits of the patriarchs Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. At the ninth, when the age was already declining and the light of virtue was, as it were, growing pale, the Law and the prophets censured the faded morals of men. The eleventh, and what remains of the day, the holy advent brings forth. Hence he himself also says in the Gospel: walk, while you have the light.

224. But now let us return to the father. Although I do not fear that, through the example of this penitent, we may seem to have been long absent — for we were never absent, we who were busy in the vineyard, in which, if he too had remained, he would not have withdrawn from the father — yet we must beware lest we cause a delay in his reconciliation, which the father did not cause. He is easily reconciled when he is earnestly asked. And so let us learn with what kind of entreaty the father is to be approached. Father he says. How merciful, how kind is he, who even when offended does not disdain to hear the name of father! Father he says I have sinned against heaven and before you.

225. This is the first confession, before the Author of nature, the guardian of mercy, the arbiter of guilt. But even though God knows all things, yet he awaits the voice of your confession. With the mouth indeed confession is made unto salvation, since whoever burdens himself lightens the weight of his error, and he who forestalls the accuser by confessing shuts out the odium of accusation; the righteous man indeed in the beginning of his speech is his own accuser. But in vain would you wish to hide from him whom you can deceive in nothing, and you may without danger disclose what you know is already known. Rather confess, so that Christ, whom we have as advocate with the Father, may intercede for you, the Church may pray for you, the people may weep for you. Nor should you fear that you will not obtain it. The advocate pledges pardon, the patron promises grace, the champion promises you the reconciliation of the Father's paternal devotion. Believe, because he is truth; acquiesce, because he is power. He has reason to intercede for you, lest he have died for you in vain. The Father too has reason to forgive, because what the Son wills, the Father wills.

226. I have sinned against heaven and before you. Surely it is not the element that is meant, but it is signified that by the sin of the soul the heavenly gifts of the Spirit have been diminished, or else that one ought not to have strayed from the bosom of that mother, the Jerusalem which is in heaven.

227. I am no longer worthy to be called your son; for he who has been cast down ought not to exalt himself, so that by the merit of his humility he may be lifted up.

228. Make me as one of your hired servants. He knows that there is a difference between sons, friends, hired servants, slaves. A son through the washing, a friend through virtue, a hired servant through labor, a slave through fear. But even from slaves and hired servants friends are made, according to what is written: you are my friends, if you do what I command you; I no longer call you slaves.

229. This within himself; but it is not enough to say it unless you come to the father. Where are you to seek him, where to find him? Rise first, that is, you who before were sitting and sleeping. And therefore the Apostle says: rise, you who sleep, and arise from the dead. Iniquity sits on a talent of lead. But to Moses too it is said: but you, stand here. Christ chose those who stand. Arise therefore, run to the Church; here is the Father, here is the Son, here is the Holy Spirit.

230. He runs to meet you, he who hears you pondering within the secret places of your mind. And while you are still far off, he sees and runs up. He sees into your heart, he runs up lest anyone hinder you, he embraces you too. In the running to meet there is foreknowledge, in the embrace clemency and, as it were, a kind of affection of fatherly love. He falls upon your neck, to raise you up as you lie, and to turn back toward heaven, in which you may seek your Author, the one burdened with sins and bent down toward earthly things. Christ falls upon your neck, to strip your nape of the yoke of servitude and to hang his sweet yoke upon your neck. Does he not seem to you to have fallen upon John's neck, when John was in the bosom of Jesus, reclining with his neck bent back? And therefore he saw the Word with God, because he was raised up toward things above. He falls upon the neck when he says: come to me, you who labor, and I will refresh you; take my yoke upon you. He falls, then, in this way, if you turn back.
