231. And he orders a robe, a ring, shoes to be brought out. The robe is the cloak of wisdom, with which the apostles cover the nakedness of the body, in that each one wraps himself in it. And therefore they receive the robe, that they may clothe the weakness of the body with the power of spiritual wisdom. For of wisdom it is said: he will wash his robe in wine. So the robe is the spiritual clothing and the wedding garment. What is the ring but the seal of sincere faith and the impress of truth? And the shoe is the preaching of the Gospel. And therefore he received the first wisdom — for there is also another, which does not know the mystery — he received the seal of his words and deeds, and a kind of defense of his good intention and of his course, lest anywhere he strike his foot against a stone and, tripped up by the devil, abandon the office of preaching the Lord. This is the preparation of the Gospel, which directs those who are prepared to the course of heavenly things, so that we walk not in the flesh but in the spirit.

232. The fatted calf too is killed, so that, restored to the fellowship of the mysteries through the grace of the sacrament, he may feast on the flesh of the Lord, rich with spiritual power. For no one ought to take part in the heavenly sacraments unless he has feared God — which is the beginning of wisdom — unless he has either kept or received the spiritual seal, unless he has preached the Lord. And he who has the ring has both the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, because God has sealed. Whose image is Christ; and he gave the Spirit as a pledge in our hearts, so that we may know that this is the seal of that ring which is given on the hand, by which the inner places of the heart and the ministries of our deeds are sealed. So we have been sealed, as we also read: believing he says you have been sealed with the Holy Spirit. And well is it the flesh of the calf, because that is a priestly victim, which used to be offered for sins,

233. that the Son brings in the father as feasting on, to show that our salvation is the Father's food and the redemption of our sins is the Father's joy. And here indeed, if you refer it to the Father, it is because the Son is the victim for sins. The Father delights in the return of the sinner; above, the Son delights in the sheep found, that you may know that the delight of the Father and of the Son is one, one the operation in the foundation of the Church.

234. And the father rejoices, because the son had perished and is found, had been dead and has come to life again. He perishes who was; for he cannot perish who was not. And so the Gentiles are not, the Christian is, according to what was said above, that God chose the things that are not, to destroy the things that are. Yet here too the image of the human race can be taken in one man. Adam was, and in him we all were: Adam perished, and in him all perished. Man, then, is re-formed in that man too who had perished, and he who was made after the likeness and image of God is restored by the divine patience and magnanimity. What then does this mean: God chose the things that are not, to destroy the things that are? That is: he chose the people of the Gentiles, which was not, to destroy the people of the Jews.

235. It can also be seen as said of one who repents, because no one dies except one who once lived. And therefore the Gentiles do not die, but are dead; for he who has not believed in Christ is always dead. And the Gentiles, when they have believed, are made alive through grace, but he who has lapsed comes to life again through repentance.

236. There follows the passage teaching that we ought to favor the remission of sins after repentance, lest, while we begrudge another his pardon, we ourselves not deserve it from the Lord. For who are you, to contradict the Lord, so that he may not relax guilt for whom he wills, when you forgive whom you will? He wills to be asked, he wills to be entreated. If righteousness belonged to all, where would the grace of God be? Who are you, to begrudge God?

237. And therefore this brother is censured, so far that he is said to come from the farm, that is, busied with earthly works, ignorant of the things that are of the Spirit of God, so that in the end he complains that not even a kid was ever killed for him; for not for envy but for the pardon of the world was the lamb sacrificed. The envious man seeks a kid; the innocent desires a lamb to be sacrificed for him. That is why he is also called the elder, because a man quickly grows old through envy. That is why he also stands outside, because the malevolence of a livid mind shuts him out. That is why he cannot hear the chorus and the symphony, that is, not those theatrical incentives to wantonness nor the harmony of courtly sounds, but the concord of the people singing together, which resounds with the sweet delight of joy over a sinner saved.

238. Set before me someone of those who seem righteous to themselves, who do not see the beam in their own eye and cannot bear the speck of another's fault. How indignant he is when pardon is granted to someone who confesses his sin and has long wept to obtain indulgence! How his ears cannot bear the spiritual symphony of the people! For this is the symphony, when in the Church, with the undivided concord of different ages and virtues, as of various strings, the psalm is sung together, the response is made, Amen is said. This is the symphony which Paul too knew. And therefore he says: I will sing with the spirit, I will sing also with the mind. These things we thought should be treated concerning the present parable.

239. Nor do we begrudge it if anyone wishes to refer these two brothers to the two peoples, so that the younger is the people from the Gentiles, as Israel was, whom the elder brother begrudged the benefit of the father's blessing. This the Jews were doing when they complained that Christ was eating with the Gentiles, and therefore they asked for a kid, the sacrifice of evil odor. The Jew asks for a kid, the Christian for a lamb, and therefore Barabbas is released to them, the lamb is sacrificed for us. Hence with them is the stench of crimes, with us the remission of sins, sweet in hope, delightful in fruit. He who seeks a kid awaits the antichrist; for Christ is a victim of good odor.

240. In the kid there also seems to be a complaint that the Jews lost the rite of the old sacrifice, or that no one's blood profited them as the blood of Christ profited the Church; for the blood of the prophets could not redeem them. But shameless he is, and like that Pharisee who justified himself with an arrogant prayer, who thought that he had never transgressed the commandment of God because he kept the Law in the letter; ungodly, he who accused his brother of having squandered his father's substance with harlots, when he ought to have noticed that it was said to him: harlots and tax collectors go before you into the kingdom of heaven,

241. and he stands outside, and he is not shut out, but he does not go in, ignorant of the will of God concerning the calling of the Gentiles, having now become a slave instead of a son; the slave indeed does not know what his lord is doing. When he came to know it, he envies and is tormented at the goods of the Church and stands outside. For outside, Israel hears the chorus and the symphony and is angry, because here the grace of the faithful people and the harmonious jubilation of the people sing together.

242. But the good father wished to save this one too, saying: you have always been with me, either as the Jew in the Law or as the righteous man in communion; but also, if you cease to envy: and all that is mine is yours,

243. either as the Jew possessing the sacraments of the old testament, or, as one baptized, those of the new as well.

244. No slave can serve two lords, not because there are two, but because there is one Lord. For even if there are those who serve mammon, yet he knows no rights of lordship, but they themselves lay the yoke of servitude upon themselves; for it is not a just power, but an unjust servitude.

245. And therefore he says: make for yourselves friends of the unrighteous mammon, so that by giving generously to the poor we may procure for ourselves the favor of the angels and of the other saints. Nor is the steward blamed, in whom we learn that we ourselves are not lords, but rather stewards of the property of others. And therefore, although he sinned, yet because he sought help for himself for the future from the indulgence of his lord, he is praised. And finely did he call mammon unrighteous, because avarice tempted our affections with the various enticements of riches, so that we wished to be slaves to riches.

246. Hence he says: if you have not been faithful in what is another's, who will give you what is yours? Riches are another's to us, because they are outside nature, and neither are they born with us nor do they pass on with us; but Christ is ours, because he is life. Indeed he came to his own, and his own did not receive him. So no one will give you what is yours, because you have not believed what is yours, you have not received what is yours.

247. So the Jews seem to be convicted of both fraud and avarice; and therefore those who were not faithful in their possessions — which, since they knew them to be another's, for the fruit of the lands has been given to all by the Lord God for common use, they surely ought to have shared with the poor — did not deserve to receive Christ either, to acquire whom Zacchaeus offered half of his goods.

248. So let us not serve what is another's, because we ought to know no lord but Christ; for there is one God the Father, from whom are all things and we in him, and one Lord Jesus, through whom are all things. What then? Is the Father not Lord, or the Son not God? But the Father too is Lord, because by the word of the Lord the heavens were made firm, and the Son is God, who is over all, God blessed for ever. How then is it that no one can serve two lords? For the Lord is one, because God is one. Indeed you shall worship the Lord your God and him alone shall you serve. From which it is clear that the lordship of the Father and of the Son is one. And it is one if it is not divided, but is whole in the Father, whole in the Son. And so, because we assert a Trinity of one Godhead and lordship, we confess one God and one Lord. But those who speak of one power of the Father, another of the Son, another of the Spirit bring many gods and many lords into the Church by the fault of Gentile error.

Book Eight

1. The law and the prophets until John, not because the law failed, but because the preaching of the Gospel begins; for the lesser things seem to be fulfilled when the more powerful succeed them. And therefore let us do violence to the kingdom of heaven; for everyone who does violence hastens with vehement zeal, and does not grow slack with a sluggish affection. There is therefore a devout violence of faith; sluggishness is culpable. The Law allowed many things according to nature, so that, being more indulgent to natural desires, it might call us to the pursuit of righteousness; Christ cut into nature, because he cuts away even natural pleasures. And therefore let us do violence to nature, so that it may not sink down to earthly things, but raise itself up to the things above.

2. Everyone who puts away his wife and marries another commits adultery, and he who marries a woman put away by her husband commits adultery. I think we must first speak of the law of marriage, so that afterwards we may discuss the prohibition of divorce. For some think that every marriage is from God, chiefly because it is written: What God has joined, let man not separate. So if every marriage is from God, no marriage may be dissolved. And how did the Apostle say: But if the unbeliever departs, let him depart? In this he wonderfully both refused to let any ground for divorce rest with Christians and showed that not every marriage is from God; for Christian women are not joined to Gentiles by God's judgment, since the law forbids it.

3. But there meets us what Solomon says: Fathers divide house and substance among their sons, but a wife will be prepared for a man by God. He who reads it in Greek does not think it contrary. For the Greek well said ἁρμόζεται [is fitted together]; for 'armonia' [harmony] is the name given to the fitting and apt joining of all things put together. It is harmony when the pipes of an organ, joined in order, keep the grace of a regular melody, and the apt order of the strings preserves concord. So a marriage does not have its harmony when a Gentile woman is unlawfully joined to a Christian husband. Therefore, where there is marriage, there is harmony; where there is harmony, God joins; where there is no harmony, there is strife and dissension, which is not from God, because God is love.

4. Do not, then, put away your wife, lest you deny that God is the author of your union. For if you ought to bear with and amend the ways of others, much more those of your wife. Hear what the Lord said: He who puts away a woman makes her commit adultery. For she who may not change her marriage while her husband lives may be stolen upon by the desire to sin. So he who is the author of the error is also guilty of the fault, when she is put away, a mother with little children, when she is thrust out in her old age with tottering step. It is hard if you shut out the mother and keep the children, so that to the insult against love you add also an injury against dutiful affection; harder, if on the mother's account you drive out the children together with her, when the children ought rather to redeem the mother's fault in their father's sight. How dangerous, if you expose the fragile age of a young girl to error! How impious, if you desert the old age of one whose youth you deflowered! Shall an emperor, then, dismiss a veteran without glory, his service unhonored, and thrust him from his holding under the empire, and shall a farmer drive from his field the rustic worn out by his labor? Or is what is unlawful towards subjects lawful towards an equal?

5. You put away your wife, then, as if by right and without crime, and you think it is lawful for you because human law does not forbid it. But divine law forbids it; you who obey men, revere God. Hear the law of the Lord, which even those who make laws obey: What God has joined, let man not separate.

6. But here not only a heavenly precept is broken, but also a certain work of God. Will you endure, I ask, that your children should be under a stepfather while you live, or live under a stepmother while their mother is unharmed? Suppose the repudiated woman does not marry: ought she, too, to have displeased you as a husband, she who keeps faith with you, an adulterer? Suppose she marries: her necessity is your crime, and the marriage you think a marriage is adultery. For what difference does it make whether you commit adultery with open confession of the crime or under the guise of a husband, except that it is graver to have made a law of the crime than a theft?

7. But perhaps someone says: How did Moses command to give a bill of divorce and to put away a wife? He who says this is a Jew; he who says this is not a Christian. And therefore, since he objects what was objected to the Lord, let the Lord answer him: Because of the hardness he says of your heart Moses permitted you to give a bill of divorce and to put away wives; but from the beginning it was not so. Moses permitted, he says, God did not command; but from the beginning is the law of God. What is the law of God? A man shall leave father and mother and shall cling to his wife, and the two shall be in one flesh. So he who puts away his wife tears his own flesh, divides his body.

8. This passage shows that what was written because of human frailty was not written by God. Hence the Apostle too says: I charge he says not I, but the Lord, that a wife should not depart from her husband, and below: To the rest he says I say, not the Lord: if any brother has an unbelieving wife and he leaves her. So where the marriage is unequal, he added: But if the unbeliever departs, let him depart. At the same time the same Apostle denied that it belongs to the divine law that a marriage of whatever kind should be dissolved; he neither commanded it himself nor gave authority to the one who deserts, but took away the blame from the one deserted. So much in the moral sense.

9. Yet because above he set forth that the kingdom of God is preached, and when he had said of the law that not one tittle can fall, he added: Everyone who puts away his wife and marries another commits adultery. Rightly the Apostle admonishes us, saying that this sacrament is great, concerning Christ and the Church. You find, then, a marriage which no one would doubt to be joined by God, since he himself says: No one comes to me unless my Father, who sent me, has drawn him; for he alone could join this marriage. And therefore in the mystical sense Solomon said: A wife will be prepared for a man by God. The man is Christ, the wife is the Church: a wife in love, a virgin in integrity. So let persecution not separate him whom God has drawn to the Son, let luxury not turn him away, let philosophy not plunder him, let Mani not defile him, let the Arian not turn him away, let the Sabellian not taint him. God has joined, let the Jew not separate. All are adulterers who desire to adulterate the truth of faith and wisdom.

10. What is this he says bill of divorce of your mother, by which I put her away? You have heard of divorce: believe in the marriage. You have heard what the husband himself says to the people of the Jews: Behold, in your iniquities you have been sold, and for your sins I have put away your mother. Remain, then, in your father's house, remain with the bridegroom, strive to please your husband. O mind, you who have believed God, be a strong woman, such as she is, whether the soul that belongs to the Church or the Church herself, of whom Solomon says: Who will find a strong woman? More precious than precious stones is she who is such: her husband trusts in her. Let us see what she does for her husband, what her work is, what her service, why Christ trusts in her.

11. A good wife clothes her husband. Let our faith clothe Jesus with his body, let it clothe his flesh with the glory of his divinity, as she too made double garments for her husband, so that she might honor him both in the present age and in the age to come. No ordinary woman is this, whose weaving-room is such, whom her husband finds not plucking the soft threads of wool, but handling the weighed-out tasks of precious virtue; who lifts up her hands in the nights and directs her work by the balance, and weighs the weight of her own character, and knows also how to keep the measure of her deeds, weaving in the woof of glorious labor; anxious as to when her husband will return, troubled and sighing and already longing to be with her husband, saying: My husband delays to come, I myself will hasten to him; I will meet him face to face, when he begins to come into his glory.

12. Come, Lord Jesus, that you may find your bride, not defiled, not adulterated, one who has not violated your house nor neglected your commands. Let her say to you: I have found him whom my soul loved; let her bring you into the house of wine — for wine, indeed, gladdens the heart of man — let her be inebriated in the spirit, recognize the mystery, speak the oracle.

13. Now a certain rich man was clothed in purple. It seems a narrative rather than a parable, since even the name is expressed. Not idly here did the Lord represent the rich man, who had enjoyed the delights of this world, as set in the misery of perpetual hunger in the underworld, and not without reason did he have five brothers. That is, the five senses of the body seem to have been associated in a kind of natural brotherhood, which seethed with immoderate and innumerable desires; but Lazarus he placed in Abraham's bosom, as in a kind of harbor of rest and retreat of holiness, lest, enticed by the pleasure of present things, we should remain in vices, or, overcome by weariness of labors, flee from hardships. Whether, then, Lazarus is one poor in this world but rich to God, or some apostolic man, poor in speech, wealthy in faith — for neither is all poverty holy nor are riches criminal, but as luxury defames riches, so holiness commends poverty — whether, then, it is an apostolic man who holds the true faith and does not seek the fillets of words, the paint of arguments, the showy veils of sentences: he receives a reward with interest as he attacks the heretics, Mani, Marcion, Sabellius, Arius and Photinus — for these are nothing other than brothers of the Jews, to whom they are bound by a brotherhood of unbelief — and as he checks also the desires of the flesh, which, as I said, smoke up through those five senses; he receives, I say, a reward with interest, whose compensation is overflowing abundance and whose interest is perpetuity.

14. Nor do we think it foreign to suppose that this discourse concerns faith as well, which Lazarus gathers when it is thrown away from the rich man's table. His sores, according to the letter, the fastidious rich man would surely shudder at, nor amid precious banquets and perfumed guests would he bear the stench of sores licked by dogs, he to whom the smell of the air and nature itself would be an object of disgust; although the insolence and swelling pride of the rich is expressed by fitting indications, in that they are so forgetful of the human condition that, as though set above nature, they take from the miseries of the poor the incentives of their own pleasures, laugh at the needy, insult the destitute, and take away from those on whom it befits them to have pity.

15. Let him, then, who will, gather both, like Lazarus. I think he is like him who, often beaten by the Jews, for the patience of believers and the calling of the Gentiles, offered the sores of his body to be licked by certain men, as it were by dogs, because it is written: They will return at evening and suffer hunger like dogs. That Canaanite woman recognized this mystery, to whom it is said: No one takes the children's bread and casts it to the dogs. She recognized that this bread is not the bread which is seen, but that which is understood, and therefore she answered: Yes, Lord; for even the little dogs eat of the crumbs that fall from their masters' table. These crumbs are from that bread. And because the bread is the word, and faith is of the word, the crumbs are as it were certain doctrines of the faith. Hence the Lord answered, to show that it was faithfully spoken: O woman, great is your faith.

16. O happy sores, which shut out perpetual pain! O rich crumbs, which drive away everlasting hunger, which fill the poor man who gathers you with eternal nourishment! The ruler of the synagogue cast you from his table when he rejected the inner mysteries of the prophetic scriptures and of the law; for the crumbs are the words of the scriptures, of which it is said: And you have cast my words behind you. The scribe cast you away, but Paul gathered you up most diligently, gathering a people amid his injuries. They licked his sores who saw him undaunted at the serpent's bite when the serpent was shaken off, and believed. That keeper of the prison licked them, who washed Paul's wounds and believed. Blessed dogs, upon whom the moisture of such sores drips, so that it fills their heart and fattens their throats, whereby they grow accustomed to guard the house, keep the flock, beware of wolves.

17. Now set before your eyes the Arians, intent on worldly pursuits, who court alliance with royal power so as to attack the truth of the Church with military arms: do they not seem to you to be lying in a kind of purple and fine linen on piled-up couches, they who defend the counterfeit in place of the true? And to abound in rich words, when they boast that the earth trembled under the Lord's body, that the sky was overcast with darkness, that the seas were stirred up or calmed by a word, while yet they deny the true Son of God? Set beside them also that poor man who, knowing that the kingdom of God is not in word but in power, expressed in few words what he believed, saying: You are the Son of the living God: do not those riches seem to you to be in want, and this poverty to overflow? Rich heresy has composed many gospels; poor faith has held this one Gospel only, which it received. Rich philosophy has made itself many gods; the poor Church has known one God.

18. Between this rich man and this poor man, then, there is a great chasm, because after death merits cannot be changed; and so the rich man is brought before us in hell, longing to draw from the poor man something of a refreshing spirit; for water is the refreshment of a soul set in pains, of which Isaiah says: And water will leap with delight from the fountains of salvation. But why is he tormented before the judgment? Because for the luxurious man to lack delights is punishment. For the Lord too says: There will be weeping and gnashing of teeth, when you see Abraham, Isaac and Jacob and all the prophets in the kingdom of heaven.

19. Late does this rich man begin to be a teacher, when now he has the time for learning, and not for teaching. In this place the Lord most clearly declares that the Old Testament is the foundation of faith, beating back the unbelief of the Jews and shutting out the wickedness of the heretics, by whom the weaker mind is tripped up; for they are the little ones, who do not yet know the increases of virtue.

20. One may notice, moreover, that in the comparison of that steward earlier and of this rich man now there is an incentive to mercy: there, perhaps, he teaches that we should give to the saints, whom he calls friends and to whom he grants tabernacles; but here he teaches that we should give to the poor.

21. If your brother sins against you, rebuke him. How well, after the rich man who is tormented in punishments, he added the precept of granting pardon, to those surely who turn back from error, lest despair should fail to call anyone back from his fault! And how moderate he is, so that pardon is neither difficult nor indulgence lax, lest either harsh invective strike anyone down or connivance invite him to fault! So also elsewhere: If your brother sins against you, go and correct him between you and him; for friendly correction profits more than turbulent accusation: the one strikes shame into a man, the other stirs indignation. Let what the one admonished fears may be made public rather be kept secret. For it is good that he who is corrected should think you a friend rather than an enemy; for one more easily assents to counsels than submits to injuries. Hence the Apostle too says: Correct him he says as a brother, that he may blush, do not regard him as an enemy. For fear is a weak guardian of permanence, but shame is a good teacher of duty; for he who fears is restrained, not amended, but in him who is ashamed to do wrong it turns into nature.

22. He put it beautifully: if he sins against you; for to sin against God and to sin against man is not an equal case. Accordingly the Apostle, who is a true interpreter of the divine oracle, says: A heretic he says after one correction avoid, because unbelief is not as pardonable as a fault. And since error often creeps in through inexperience, he commanded that it be corrected, so that either obstinacy may be avoided or a lapse set right.

23. But what does this mean: If he turns to you seven times, forgive him? Is a number fixed beforehand for pardon, or is it rather because, since on the seventh day God rested from all his works, after the week of this world a lasting rest is promised to us, so that, just as the evils of this world will cease and keep holiday, so also the severity of vengeance may come to rest? Now the sabbath is not only of days but also of months, and therefore on the tenth day of the seventh month there is the sabbath of sabbaths; and not only of months but also of years, and not of years only but also of generations, and finally of the world itself, of which the great sabbath is a type, as in the law there is the seventh week, after which the year of jubilee is celebrated. This mystery the Lord willed to reveal to us when he says: Not only seven times, but also seventy times seven, because in the seventh generation, as you have it according to Luke, Enoch was carried off, lest wickedness should change his heart, and in him the sting of sorrow came to rest. But in the seventy-seventh generation the Lord was born of Mary, and taking upon himself the sins of the human race he granted remission of all offenses.

24. So although according to the letter you learn to forgive often and not to hold on to indignation — for there is nothing by which he can be offended who has the habit of forgiving — yet recognize the mystery. For not idly did the Lord say to the woman on the sabbath: You are released from your infirmity, showing his people, who like that woman would follow when called, that by his coming he had remitted their sins. So Lamech is condemned seventy-seven times, because he sins more gravely who commits a crime while punishing one. But the sacraments of baptism remit every enormity of crime. Learn, then, to forgive the injuries done to you, because Christ forgave his persecutors.

25. Nor is it idle that he suffered on the great sabbath, signifying that there would be a sabbath on which death would be destroyed by Christ. And if the Jews so celebrate the sabbath that they keep both a month and a whole year as a sabbath, how much more ought we to celebrate the resurrection of the Lord! And therefore our forebears handed down to us that all the fifty days of Pentecost are to be celebrated as Easter, because the beginning of the eighth week makes Pentecost. Hence the Apostle too, as a disciple of Christ who knew that there is a diversity of times, writing to the Corinthians says: With you he says perhaps I shall remain and spend the winter; and below: But I shall remain at Ephesus until Pentecost; for a great door has been opened to me. So with the Corinthians, by whose errors he was distressed, he spends the winter, because their affection towards the worship of God had grown cold; with the Ephesians he celebrates Pentecost and hands on the mysteries to them, and relaxes his mind, because he saw them glowing with the ardor of faith. So through these fifty days the Church knows no fasting, just as on the Lord's day, on which the Lord rose again, and all the days are as the Lord's day.

26. There will be another Lord's day too, on which the Lord's body will rise again. Paul knew it, who says: But you are the body of Christ and members from members. For this body of the Lord, and bones from his bones, will cleave to the head; and the head, moreover, of the Church is Christ. Then, therefore, fasting will cease, since in perpetual joy weariness, care and fatigue will cease. Then death will be destroyed; for last of all, indeed, death will be destroyed. For although it ceased in Enoch and was not found in him, yet it was not destroyed; for he was carried off so that he might escape, Christ was sacrificed so that he might destroy it. And therefore he well said: Where, O death, is your victory? Where, O death, is your sting? So in this resurrection Christ will rise a second time, as it were, in his own body. Blessed, then, is he who has a part in the first resurrection; for as Christ is the firstfruits of those who sleep, so then the firstfruits of those who rise again will be his saints of the Church.

27. This mystery Peter could not know. Perhaps he knew about Enoch: yet who could grasp with a human mind the mystery hidden in God? Let the Lord come, then, into my soul, into my mind, and subject it to himself, so that when my mind has been subjected, I may say: I will not fear evils, for you are with me.

28. If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, you will say to this mulberry tree: 'Be uprooted and be cast into the sea', and it will obey you. Of the grain of mustard seed we have spoken above; now we must discuss the mulberry tree. I read of a tree, yet I do not believe it a tree. For what reason is there, what profit is there here for us, that a tree which bears fruit for toiling farmers should be uprooted and cast into the sea? Although we may judge this possible by the power of faith, that insensible nature should obey the commands of sensible beings, what yet does the very kind of tree mean? I have read indeed: I was a goatherd, plucking mulberries, and I think the prophet signified to us that he himself, a sinner, was converted from a flock of sinners; although it fits that the future prophet of the Gentiles should seem to have sought fruit among brambles, to have dug out food from brambles, as one who would place the discolored and fetid flocks of the Gentiles, the peoples of the nations, in the pastures of his writings, so that they might grow fat with spiritual refreshment, while he himself would milk spiritual milk from the converted sinner.

29. But since in another book of the Gospel it was said of a mountain — whose appearance, bare of growing things, starved of vines and olives, barren of harvests, fit for the lairs of beasts and restless with the coursing of wild animals, seems to express the self-exalting height of spiritual wickedness, as it is written: Behold, I am against you, corrupt mountain, which corrupts the whole earth — it is fitting that in this place too we should think the same is meant, since faith shuts out the unclean spirit, especially as the nature of the tree agrees with this opinion. For its fruit is first white in the flower, then, when formed, it reddens, and at maturity it turns black. The devil too, cast down by transgression from the white flower of the angelic nature and from his glowing power, grew foul with the black stench of sin. Behold him, then, saying to the mulberry tree: Be uprooted and be cast into the sea, when he casts the legion out of a man and permits it to pass into the swine, which, driven wild by the devilish spirit, plunged themselves into the sea.

30. This passage, then, is an exhortation to faith, teaching in the moral sense that even things that have been made solid can be dissolved by faith. From faith comes love, from love hope, and in turn they flow back into one another in a kind of holy circuit.

31. It follows that no one should glory in his works, because by right we owe obedience to the Lord. For if you do not say to a servant who is plowing or feeding sheep: Pass over, recline — where it is understood that no one reclines unless he has first passed over; thus Moses too first passed over, so that he might see a great sight — if, then, you not only do not say to your servant: Recline, but require another service of him and do not thank him, so neither does the Lord allow in you the practice of one work or labor only, because, while we live, we must always work.

32. So acknowledge that you are a servant, bound in debt to very many services. Do not set yourself above others because you are called a son of God — grace must be acknowledged, but nature must not be forgotten — nor boast if you have served well, for you did what you ought to do. The sun obeys, the moon complies, the angels serve. The vessel of the Gentiles chosen by the Lord says: I am not worthy he says to be called an apostle, because I persecuted the Church of God. Then elsewhere, showing himself conscious of no fault, he added: But I am not justified by this. Let us too, then, not demand praise from ourselves, nor snatch at the judgment of God beforehand and forestall the sentence of the judge, but reserve it for its own time and its own judge. After this the ungrateful are rebuked, and so at last he comes to treat of the future judgment.

In that hour he who is on the housetop and his vessels in the house, let him not go down to take them, and he who is in the field, let him likewise not turn back. Remember Lot's wife.

33. When the Lord was asked by the disciples when the kingdom of God would come, he said: The kingdom of God is within you, that is, through the truth of grace, not through the slavery of fault. So let him who wishes to be free be a servant in the Lord; for in the measure in which we share in the servitude we share also in the kingdom. He said, then: The kingdom of God is within you; but when it would come he would not say, though he did say that the day of judgment would come, so as to strike all with the terror of an imminent judgment and not to bring the security of delay.

34. And lest he seem to sadden the disciples if he denied them anything, he says in another book: But of the day and the hour no one knows, neither the angels of heaven nor the Son. He did well to set down 'the Son' neutrally; for the same Son of Man is Son of God, so that we may judge it to have been said rather according to the Son of Man, because he knows the end of the times not by the nature of man but by the nature of God. Yet it is not foreign to the faith if you take it of the Son of God. For what is there that the good Father would have hidden from the Son, to whom he gave all things? Or how did he not give the knowledge of the time, who gave the power of the judgment itself? And how can the Son not know what the Father knows, since the Son is in the Father and the Spirit searches even the depths of God, since the Son himself is the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God? But why he would not say it, he shows in another place: It is not yours to know the times and years which the Father has set in his own power.

35. Do you see where he is heading who denies that the Trinity is of one power, so that there should be something the Son does not know? For why would the Father hide it from his own Son? For either from envy we are unwilling to make known to others what we know, or else lest we perhaps be betrayed; but neither does the suspicion of envy fall on the Father nor that of betrayal on the Son. Therefore they are of one knowledge, because they are of one power. Then too, he who knows the signs of the future judgment surely knows the end as well.

36. For what is there that he does not know who is like the flashing lightning, in that he, the light, the Son of God, illuminates the inner depths of the heavenly mystery? In that, he says, hour. So he knows the hour too, but he knows it for himself; for me he does not know it. And he rightly asserts that the cause of the flood and of the burning and of the judgment proceeded from our sins, because God did not create evil, but our own deserts found it for themselves.

They were eating, indeed, and drinking, they were taking wives and being given in marriage,

37. not that marriages are condemned — for neither is food condemned, since in the one lies the support of succession, in the other the support of nature; otherwise we should have to go out of this world — but in all things measure is sought, and whatever, moreover, is more abundant is from evil. Let there be some consent for a time, that we may be free for prayer; let there be, amid the cares of the world and the surfeit of intemperance, some sobriety of religion and some truce of chastity.

38. Therefore, since because of the wicked the good must suffer contrition of heart and mind in this age, so that they may receive a richer reward in the future, they are furnished with remedies, so that those who are in Judea should flee to the mountains. What is this Judea? For I know another Judea too, according to the spirit, not according to the letter; for known, indeed, in Judea is God. And what sort of mountains are these, that can check the movement of the future judgment, since it is written: Trembling, moreover, will seize the mountains? Heaven and earth will pass away: how will a portion of the earth remain immune, or be able to protect me, when it does not preserve itself? Where, then, shall I hide myself from the wrath of him who troubles the depth of the sea? If I ascend into heaven, he is there; if I descend into hell, he is present. He who is everywhere, then, cannot be deceived, but he can be appeased.

39. The day of judgment, then, is at hand. If you do not wish to be seized, fear daily, flee daily. Do you ask how you should flee? Go up onto a mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion, so that you may stand out on the summit of lofty merits; for God, indeed, is of the mountains and not God of the valleys. Go up to where Christ sits at the right hand of God, whose foundations are on the holy mountains, and mountains are round about him. Paul is your mountain, Peter is your mountain. Set the footstep of your mind upon their faith. Those established in the law of God and in the inheritance of faith the day of judgment finds not for punishment but for glory.

40. If anyone too is placed on the housetop, that is, has already climbed to the upper parts of his house and to the summit of eminent virtues, let him not fall back to the earthly works of this world. For I know a housetop on which Rahab — a harlot in type, the Church in mystery, joined to the Gentile peoples by fellowship in the sacraments — hid the spies whom Joshua sent; and if they had gone down to the lower parts of the house, they would have been killed by the searchers who had been sent to seize them. The housetop, then, is the office of a sublime mind and the summit of the soul, by which the naked infirmity of the body is covered. Hence it seems to me that that paralytic too, who was let down from the roof by four young men, was holy for this reason, that with the support of four virtues, prudence, fortitude, temperance and justice, he laid himself at the feet of Christ by a certain lofty reasoning. For nothing is more exalted than humility, which, being as it were higher, knows not how to be lifted up, because no one aspires to what he judges to be beneath him.

41. But since we are dealing with the judgment, let us not step down from the housetop, lest, while we desire to carry off the vessels that are in the house, we be caught. For not in every house are there vessels of gold and silver, but in most there are wooden ones; nor is every house full; for there are empty ones too, which the prophet knew, who said: What has happened to you now, that you have gone up into empty houses? The city is filled with those crying out. And he added: All your princes have fled, all who were wounded in you and have fallen from faith into unbelief. Sabellius was wounded, Valentinus was wounded, Arius was wounded; for they were found in an empty house.

42. Do you wish to see a full house? Follow Peter, when he was hungry, going up to the upper parts of the house. There he recognized the mystery of the Church that was to be gathered, so that he should not judge unclean the Gentile people whom faith can cleanse from every contagion. Now the vessels are of clay; the vessel, therefore, is the body. And so beware lest from desire of the body you abandon the noble pursuits of the mind. If Peter did not receive the mystery while placed in the lower parts, how will you receive it? He received it because he went up, so that he might preach the Lord and not fear the suffering of the body.

43. So he who is on the housetop, let him not go down, and he who is in the field, let him not turn back. Whence am I to understand what the field is, unless Jesus himself teaches me, saying: No one who puts his hand to the plow and looks back is fit for the kingdom of heaven? The idle man sits in the farmhouse, the laborious man sows in the field: the weak man at the hearth, the strong man at the plow. Good is the smell of the field; for the smell of Jacob is the smell of a full field. The field is full of flowers, full of various fruits. Plow your field, then, if you wish to be directed to the kingdom of God. Let a fruitful crop of good merits flower for you. Let there be an abundant vine on the sides of your house and young olive shoots round about your table. Let your soul, now conscious of its own fertility, sown with the word of God and plowed also with spiritual tillage, say to Christ: Come, my brother, let us go out into the field; and let him answer: I have entered my garden, my sister, my bride: I have gathered the vintage of my myrrh. For what vintage is better than that of faith, by which the fruit of the resurrection is stored up, by which the fountain of perpetual joy is watered?

44. So, since you are forbidden to look back, much more are you forbidden to return and take your tunic; for you have learned that to him who asks you for your tunic you ought to let go your cloak as well. So, directed towards the kingdom of God, do not seek after riches and patrimony. I know of another tunic too, written of, concerning which the Apostle exhorts us that, putting off the old man with his deeds, and having put on the new, we should not seek again the tunic of former error. Hence she too says: By night I have put off my tunic; how shall I put it on? That is, you ought not only to renounce sins, but also to abolish all memory of your former conduct. Accordingly Paul, forgetting the things that are behind, put off his guilt, but did not neglect repentance.

45. And therefore Remember says the Lord Lot's wife, who, because she looked back, lost the gift of her nature; for behind is Satan, behind is Sodom. Therefore flee intemperance, shun luxury. And that you may know that not all can flee to the mountain, recall that he who did not turn back to his former pursuits — for he had earlier chosen Sodom — escaped for this reason, that he reached the mountain; while she who was weaker, because she looked back to what was behind, could not reach the mountain even with the help of her husband's support, but remained behind.

46. On that night there will be two in one bed: one will be taken and the other will be left. He did well to say night, because the Antichrist is the hour of darkness, in that he pours darkness over the hearts of men, when he says that he is the Christ, as false prophets arise who assert that Jesus is dwelling now in the deserts, so as to mock men with the error of a wandering opinion, now in the inner chambers, so that those who hear may be held fast by the name of an exalted power. But Christ, like flashing lightning, scatters the globes of his light through the whole world, and therefore he neither wanders in deserts nor is shut up in any places, because I fill heaven and earth, says the Lord; but he shines with the light of his lightning, so that in that night we may be able to see the glory of the resurrection. What, then, does it mean that he says:

47. two in one bed, and two women grinding, and two in the field: one will be taken and the other will be left? Is God unjust, that he should separate by the reward of their merits those who are equal in their pursuits and in their fellowship of life, and not distinguished in the quality of their actions? It is not so; but the quality of the reward answers to a man's actions. So the habit of union does not make men's merits equal; for both the father rises against the son and the son against the father in zeal for religion, because not all accomplish what they undertake, but he who perseveres to the end, he will be saved. Then too, the Lord searches out not the outward service but the inner disposition; for if you offer rightly and do not divide rightly, the sacrifice is not acceptable to God. So from one bed — for there is a bed of human infirmity, since it is written: You have turned his whole bed in his infirmity — one is left, the other is taken. He who is taken is caught up to meet Christ in the air; but he who is left is rejected.

48. Two women grinding at the mill. The sense indeed is that those seem to be signified who seek nourishment from hidden things and bring it out from the inner parts into the open. But yet we must inquire what these women grind, unless perhaps it is what we read in Isaiah: If you bring fine flour, it is vain, so that the fine flour is what is offered by those who grind. Let us examine, then, which women grind, and what they grind, and what the mill is. And perhaps this world is the mill; in it, I think more fittingly, the form of the human body is represented, in which our soul is shut up as in a kind of bodily prison, to work heavenly bread, if it takes good counsel. In this mill, then, either the synagogue or the soul that is liable to offenses, grinding wheat soaked and spoiled with heavy moisture, cannot separate the inner parts from the outer, and therefore it will be left, because its fine flour has displeased. But the holy Church, or the soul stained by no contagions of offenses, which grinds such wheat as is parched by the heat of the eternal sun, which God has clothed as he willed and the angels have cleansed from every stain of refuse, offers to God good fine flour from the inner chambers of men and commends the offerings of her sacrifice.

49. And not only two women grinding, but also two men working will be in one field, of whom one will be taken, the good sower, who has not sown upon the roads but upon plowed and tilled ground, so that the earth may multiply the fruit pressed down by humility, not scattered by boasting; but the sower of tares will be left, from which a fine flour fit to be rejected is made. Who these different farmers are we can discover, if we observe for what reason the Apostle said that there are two νοῦς, that is, two minds in us; perhaps for this reason, that one belongs to the outer man, who is corrupted, the other to the inner man, who is renewed through the sacraments. And that one is perhaps the worse which is lifted up, vainly puffed up by the mind of his flesh and not holding the head, because it turns aside from the observance of the saving precepts of our Lord Jesus Christ; for he is the head of all, who is the author of all. The other is the more excellent, which loves humility, seeks wisdom, does not neglect mercy: the good sower, namely; for he has scattered, indeed, he has given to the poor, his righteousness remains forever. This one, then, is spiritual, that one carnal. For as from the Apostle's words we understand that the seducer who exalts himself with a swollen spirit is puffed up by the mind of his flesh, so too we show that the holy man is renewed in the spirit of his mind, as the same Apostle says: But be renewed in the spirit of your mind. He shows, therefore, that there are two minds: one, which becomes the mind of the flesh when it is conquered by sin; the other, which, joined to the spirit, forswears the allurements of the flesh.

50. And not only two minds, but also two laws are in us, both of which the Apostle has explained to us, saying: For I delight in the law of God according to the inner man, but I see another law in my members, warring against the law of my mind and making me captive in the law of sin, which is in my members. There is, then, a law of the inner man, and there is also one of the outer: the former forbids sin, the latter persuades to it; the former condemns error, the latter suggests it; the former instructs the mind, the latter tempts it. There are also two other, more vehement laws in us, one of God, the other of sin, according to the same teacher, who says: So then I myself with the mind serve the law of God, but with the flesh the law of sin. And it is shown that if you speak of the mind alone, you show it to be contrary to the flesh; for when Paul says that he serves the law of God with the mind, he surely shows that the mind in itself, unless it is conquered by the flesh, is good, and created by nature such that it resists error. So when it is conquered, it is the mind of the flesh, having its slipping not from nature but from the flesh, and the conquered passes, as it were, into the name and property of the conqueror; but by nature it is opposed to the flesh. So with the mind we serve God, with the flesh sin. And the mind will be the better if, as a fellow worker with the Holy Spirit, it does not interrupt its devout duty.

51. These, then, are the ones working in our field: of them the one yields good fruit by diligence, the other loses it by neglect, and this the lawgiver calls blood, saying: The soul of all flesh is its blood. To this many also refer what is written: You shall not eat flesh in blood, lest we should regard the pleasures of the body, bloodied by the wounds of the soul, as a refreshment rather than as the guilt of blood, we who ought to be refreshed by the word of God. There is, then, a food of refreshment, and there is a food of blood; for as the flesh of the Lord is truly food, so his blood is truly our drink. Let us, then, supply the Lord with good food from our works, lest, when he comes again and, as on that fig tree, finds no fruit, he, hungry because of the barrenness of our merits, should turn away his loving purpose, saying to that soul, which he will have found bare of fruits and bloodied with blood: Let no fruit ever be born from you forever. The soul, then, of all flesh is its blood. There is also a more excellent soul, of which God said: All souls are mine; as the soul of the father, so also the soul of the son is mine.

52. Nor should we pass over interpreting it of two peoples, that is, that in this world, which is very often compared to a field, there are two peoples, one of believers, the other which does not believe, who will receive the recompense of their merits; and therefore the one which is faithful is taken, the other which is unfaithful is left. And those two women grinding are two souls, or certainly the Church and the synagogue; for a figure in the divine scriptures is accustomed to be not single but manifold, so that one word comprises several meanings. So the mind of the flesh and the soul of the flesh and the synagogue gather that wheat and grind that fine flour which are offered in vain; but the mind which is joined to the soul, and the soul which is the receiver of salvation, or the Church of God, cultivate and grind the spiritual fine flour of the true law. Hence also come the loaves of proposition, which only the priests eat, to whom it is prescribed to eat a purer bread, that, surely, which came down from heaven. And we are all, if our merits allow, priests of righteousness, who are consecrated with the anointing of gladness to kingship and priesthood.

53. So let us so work and cultivate our field, set as we are in the task of this cultivation, that in that higher Jerusalem, where the true keeping of the law is celebrated, we may have fine flour from our sheaves — and blessed are they who have been able to gather them — so that coming they may come with exultation, bearing their sheaves. These, then, are the spiritual fruits and the happy yields of true labor, which are soaked by no useless rain; but the fruit of the flesh is liable to corruption, and therefore he who sows carnal things will reap carnal things. But of the field what shall I say, since it is plain that in the work of the farmer lies either praise or blame?

54. And they answered and said: Where, Lord? This the disciples said; but the Lord, when he had forewarned them both whither they should flee and where they should stay and what they should beware of, embraced the whole in a general definition, saying:

55. Wherever the body is, there the eagles will be gathered. So let us first conjecture what the eagles are, so that we may define what the body is. For the souls of the righteous are compared to eagles, because they seek the heights, leave lowly things behind, and are said to live to a long age. Hence David too says to his soul: Your youth will be renewed like the eagle's. If, then, we have understood the eagles, we can no longer be in doubt about the body, especially if we remember that Joseph received the body from Pilate. Do not Mary of Cleopas and Mary Magdalene and Mary the mother of the Lord and the company of the apostles around the Lord's burial seem to you to be eagles around the body? Do they not seem to you eagles around the body, when the Son of Man will come with the intelligible clouds, and every eye will see him, and those who pierced him?

56. There is also the body of which it was said: My flesh is truly food and my blood is truly drink. Around this body are the eagles which fly around it on spiritual wings. There are eagles around the body also that believe that Jesus has come in the flesh, because every spirit that confesses that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is from God. Where, then, there is faith, there is the sacrament, there is the lodging-place of holiness. The Church too is a body, in which through the grace of baptism we are renewed in spirit, and the declining years of old age are restored to an age come alive again.

57. Let the children come to me and do not forbid them; for of such is the kingdom of God. Yet this age is weak in strength, feeble in understanding, immature in counsel. Age, then, is not preferred to age; otherwise it would harm us to grow up. What need is there of prayers that the maturity of years should follow, if it is to take away from me the merit of the heavenly kingdom? Did God, then, give the progress of life for vices, and not for the growth of virtue? And why did he himself choose apostles not of childish but of more advanced age? And why does he say that children are fit for the kingdom of heaven? Perhaps because they do not know malice, do not know how to defraud, do not dare to strike back, know nothing of searching out wealth, and do not covet honor or ambition. But virtue is not to be ignorant of these things but to despise them, nor is there praise for continence where integrity comes from weakness. So it is not childhood that is meant, but a goodness that emulates childlike simplicity. For virtue is not to be unable to sin, but to be unwilling, and so to hold to perseverance of will that the will imitates infancy, and practice imitates nature. Accordingly the Savior himself expressed this, saying: Unless you are converted and become like this child, you will not enter the kingdom of heaven.

58. Who, then, is the child to be imitated by the apostles of Christ? Is it one of the little ones? Is this, then, the virtue of the apostles? Who, then, is the child? Is it not perhaps he of whom Isaiah says: A child is born to us, a son is given to us? For he, the child, said to you: Take up your cross and follow me. And that you may recognize the child: When he was reviled, he did not revile in return; when he was struck, he did not strike back; for this is perfect virtue. So in childhood too there is a certain venerable old age of character, and in old age an innocent childhood; for venerable old age is not that of long duration, nor reckoned by the number of years; but men's understanding is gray hair, and an unspotted life is the age of old age. Hence it is also written: Praise the Lord, you children, praise the name of the Lord, because no one but the perfect praises the Lord; for no one, indeed, says Lord Jesus except in the Holy Spirit.

59. This seems to have been prophesied of the people of the Church, which, though younger, went before the older people of the Jews in the pursuit of virtue. And therefore that saying: Behold I and my children whom you have given me. These are the children who, accompanying the Lord with prophetic cries as he rode upon the colt of an ass, said that the redemption of the Gentiles had come; these are the children, or infants, who sucked with a fuller draught those breasts of Christ that are better than wine; for out of the mouth, indeed, of infants and sucklings you have perfected praise.

60. So it may seem hard and harsh to some that the disciples were forbidding the little infants to come to the Lord, unless you understand either the mystery or the affection; for they did not do it from the harshness of a mind envious towards children, but they were offering the services of diligent servitude to the Lord, lest he should be crushed by the crowds. Accordingly it is written elsewhere: Master, the crowds are crushing you; for our own advantage must indeed be rejected where there is injury to the divinity. Let us, then, flee pride and follow childlike simplicity, because truth is the adversary of pride, while simplicity runs together with truth, sublime in its very humility. For God does not dwell in an abject heart, but, as the prophets have handed down to us: The throne of virtue is exalted, that is, in him whose wisdom is raised to the height of truth. Nor, like Cain, let anyone hide the treachery of a murderer under the appearance of a brother, but let him be a brother without and within. So much for the affection. But as to the mystery: it was because they were eager that the people of the Jews, from whom they had been born according to the flesh, should be saved first; yet they also made supplication for the Canaanite woman. They knew, then, the mystery, that the calling of both peoples was due, but perhaps they did not yet know the order.

61. Now attend to the difference of the words. Where he bids the infants come to him, so that he might bless them either by preaching or by the laying on of hands, he calls them children; where he commands that the little ones are not to be scandalized, he calls them little ones; for those who are touched by Christ are not scandalized, those who draw near to Christ do not slip; but those slip whom not smallness of age but littleness of virtue has made little ones. At the same time he also teaches that the weak are not to be tempted, lest their offenses be turned back upon us, since their prayers to the Lord, though they themselves be weak in the merits of virtue, are yet raised up by the patronage of the angels. Let no one, then, mock the poor man, because he provokes him who made him; let no one tempt the feeble, lest he injure the angels; let no one cast down the weak, lest he undo the benefit of the Redeemer.

62. And therefore he said: Woe to this world because of scandals, because many have judged the cross of the Lord a scandal, while the humility of the Lord's Passion is the sacrament of our salvation, so that we may take up the duty of virtue and take the example of humility. Woe, then, to him who has not believed in the cross of the Lord, by which the weak are scandalized: It is better for him that an ass's millstone be hung about his neck and that he be sunk into the depth of the sea.

63. In the divine scriptures, indeed, we ought to weigh not the sequence of words but the weight of things; for a kind of monstrous display of a punishment of unheard-of and hideous kind does more to repress sins. Yet lest from this too a scandal should arise for anyone who is weak, we think that the ass's millstone, the man's neck and the depth of the sea are not idly set together. For since the Gentile people has received the type of the ass, does it not seem to you to turn, as it were, an ass's millstone, so long as it is engaged in the error of its ignorance: bound indeed by the chains of nature, so as to grind out the word, to seek God, but overspread with the blindness of a covered mind, so that it does not know how to raise the face of the soul to God, to open the eyes of the heart? And therefore, with no briskness in its course, its footsteps often returning upon themselves, it labors unwillingly for another's use. Yet he who turns the millstone has at some time an end of finishing his work, and bears the hope of putting off his blindness; but on whom is the millstone hung at the neck? He carries a stone who refused to carry the yoke of the Lord. So the ass is to the millstone, the blind man to the stone, the Gentile to the rock, worshiping him whom he neither sees nor recognizes; for God does not dwell in things made by hands, nor is he recognized in a rock, but in spirit.

64. Both peoples, then, the Gentile and the Jew, are set forth by a kind of display in this saying, but a more vehement punishment is decreed against the Jews. For the memory of the Gentiles will be overwhelmed by the waves of this age and abolished in the mire of this world, since they wished to be among the things that are not, and, estranged from the knowledge of God, they have been sunk as it were into the depth of the sea; but the Jews, taken up through the patriarchs, marked by circumcision, instructed through the law, will not perish as unknown, but will be punished as sacrilegious. For the God unknown to the Athenians was known in Judea, but not received. And therefore he who does not know will be unknown, he who transgresses will be condemned; and neither is the one freed from guilt who did not know his own author, and the other is shut out from pardon who did not receive the Lord. Yet it is more tolerable not to have brought faith to Christ than to have laid hands on him.

Now a certain ruler asked him, saying: Good Master, by doing what shall I possess eternal life? And Jesus said to him: Why do you call me good? No one is good but God alone.

65. A crafty question, and therefore a shrewd answer. For this ruler, a tempter, called him a good master, who ought to have called him good God. For although there is goodness in divinity and divinity in goodness — for no one, indeed, is good but God alone; every, moreover, man is a liar, and whatever is a liar is surely not good — yet by adding good Master he called him good in part, not in the whole; for God is good in the whole, man in part. Therefore the Lord says: Why do you call me good, whom you deny to be God? Why do you call me good, when no one is good but the one God? He does not, then, deny that he is good, but indicates that he is God; for what is good but what is full of goodness? But when it is written: There is none who does goodness, there is not even one, surely he said it of men, not of God; for God is one, not one of a number. So too the Son of God is excepted as the only one, not as one of many, and he is only-begotten, not one of the begotten. And therefore no one is good does not prejudge Christ, because no one judges Christ. For no one is said as of what is common to us, but Christ has nothing in common with us.

66. But if anyone is troubled that no one is good but the one God, let him be troubled too by this, that no one is good but God. But if the Son is not excepted from God, surely Christ is not excepted from the good either. But since in God the Son is another in person but one in power — for one, indeed, God, from whom are all things, and one Lord, through whom are all things — and since God and Lord are not two gods but one God, because the Lord your God is one Lord, surely, since according to majesty God is one in both persons, the one Good too is in both. For how is he not good who is born of the good? For a tree, indeed, that is good makes good fruits. How is he not good, when the substance of goodness taken from the Father has not degenerated in the Son, which has not degenerated in the Spirit? And therefore: Your good Spirit will lead me in the right way. But if the Spirit is good, who received from the Son, surely he too is good who handed it on. And since the Father is good, surely he too is good who has all things that the Father has; or, if you deny that the Son has goodness, you deny the Father too.

67. An evident argument needs no examples; yet at least follow the authority of the scriptures; for it is written: The Lord is a good judge to the house of Israel. Does he say it of the Son or of the Father? But the Father judges no one, because he has given all judgment to the Son. So the good Lord is the Son. Take another. Those who come to baptism surely confess the Trinity, because they are baptized in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit: so they confess both to the Father and to the Son and to the Holy Spirit. When, then, it is said: Confess to the Lord, for he is good, surely the Father is good, the Son is good, the Holy Spirit too is good, but one God; and again: that the Lord is good to those who wait for him. Or is he not good who grants good things to the soul that seeks him? Or is he not good who satisfies your soul with good things? Or is he not good who says: I am the good shepherd?

68. But you think God is good for this reason, that he does not have judgment, in which lies the necessity of punishing. Although it has already been set forth that the judge is good to the house of Israel, yet elsewhere you have: How good is the God of Israel to the upright of heart! Of whom, then, do you think it was said, of the Father or of the Son? If of the Father, then he is not good to all. Why, then, do you detract from the Son? If of the Son, then confess that the Son too is good God; for he is the blessed God of Israel, because he has visited and wrought the redemption of his people. He is the king and God of Israel, to whom it is said: Rabbi, you are the Son of God, you are the king of Israel. So, then, here he says: since you who tempt me cannot perceive me as good, why do you call me good? Good indeed am I, but to the upright of heart, I to whom being good comes from nature, not from craftiness. The Son, then, is good; for he is the brightness of eternal light and the spotless mirror of God's majesty and the image of his goodness. How, then, is he not good who is the image of goodness? For as the image of God is God, but one God, so also the image of the divine goodness is good, but one goodness. For me, certainly, it is profitable to believe that God is good, whom I am to have as the judge of my offenses. Let those see to it who are unwilling to believe that he is good. So, because this man who tempts him is a lawyer, as is shown in another book, he did well to say: No one is good but the one God, so as to remind him that it is written: You shall not tempt the Lord your God, but that he should rather confess to the Lord, for he is good.

69. Accordingly he often touches him sharply; for to him who was boasting in the law that he had kept all things from his youth, he showed, so as to expose his empty boasting, that what belongs to the law was still lacking to him. And therefore he is brought, sorrowful, to the precept of mercy, and upon him a kind of natural form of sentence is pronounced.

70. It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God. Great force, great weight in the words. For with what other words could he more vehemently express that a rich man ought not to boast in his riches than with such as these, by which it is defined that a merciful rich man is against nature? Take away from me the allurements and the paint of words, which are wont to enervate thoughts. This man was not to be coaxed but broken, he who rejected mercy. Yet if any are delighted more by the ornaments of words than by a certain manly and natural bearing and form of the meaning, then — as good suitors do in taking a wife, examining her character, not her comeliness, and not being turned back by any offense in her looks when they are drawn by the virtue of her mind — so let these too seek in the words the mystery, which is a kind of mind and soul of the words, and let them not pick over the words in the mystery.

71. The camel, then, is taken in type as the Gentile people, which that lion, seeking whom he may devour, drives, laden with treasure, into the desert in the prophetic visions: in tribulation, indeed, and anguish are the lion and the lion's whelp; to the asps and the offspring of flying asps they carried their riches on asses and camels. And the camel was rightly figured for the Gentile people, because, degenerating through the deformity of superstition before the people of the nations believed, it displayed the appearance of a beastly foulness, absurd footsteps, a misshapen face. So this sinner entered more easily through the narrow way, which is the way of Christ, who, piercing through the way of death by the passion of his own body, like a needle, mended the torn garments, as it were, of our nature, than did the people of the Jews, rich in the law, needy in faith, headlong in fury, shameful in crime.

72. You can also understand it in the moral sense of every sinner and of the arrogant rich man. Does not that tax collector, burdened with the consciousness of his own offenses, when he did not dare to lift his eyes to God, seem to you, like a kind of camel, to enter more easily into the eye of the needle by the remedies of his confession than that Pharisee into the kingdom of God, arrogant in prayer, boastful of innocence, presumptuous of glory, reproacher of mercy, preacher of himself, accuser of another, who rather called the Lord to account than entreated him? If anyone, then, shudders at the camel, let him shudder at the man who is fouler in his deeds than a camel.

73. Honor father and mother. Beautifully for me is the beginning of the law read today, when it is the birthday of my priesthood; for each year the priesthood seems to begin as if anew, when it is renewed with the turning age of the times. It is good, too, that it is read: Honor father and mother; for you are parents to me, you who conferred the priesthood on me; you, I say, are children or parents: children one by one, parents all together. For gladly would I call you my children or my parents, you who hear the word of God and do it: children, because it is written: Come, children, hear me; parents, because the Lord himself said: Who is my mother or my brothers? My mother and my brothers are those who hear the word of God and do it.

74. Well, then, did the law, which had first said: You shall love the Lord your God, and you shall love your neighbor, add: Honor your father and mother. For this is the first step of dutiful love; for God willed these to be your authors. Honor them with services, so that you refrain from insults, because the dutiful love owed to parents must not be wounded even by a look. But it is little not to injure them, for the law has provided that they suffer no wrong — for he who, indeed, curses father or mother shall die the death — but you, honor them, so that you may be good. The benefit of the law is one thing, the duty of dutiful love another. Honor your own, because the Son of God honored his; for you have read: And he was subject to them. If God was subject to his little servants, how much more you to your parents? Christ, then, honored Joseph and Mary, not by the debt of nature but by the duty of dutiful love; he honored also God the Father as no one else could honor him, so as to be obedient unto death: honor, then, your parents, you too.

75. Now honor consists not only in respect but also in generosity. Honor widows who are truly widows; for it is honor to bestow according to merits. Feed your father, feed your mother. And if you have fed your mother, you have not yet repaid the pains, you have not repaid the torments which she suffered for you; you have not repaid the services with which she carried you, you have not repaid the nourishment which she gave with the tender affection of dutiful love, milking her breasts into your lips; you have not repaid the hunger which she endured for you, lest she should eat anything harmful to you, lest she should drink anything that would harm the milk. For you she fasted, for you she ate; for you she did not take the food she wanted, for you she took the food she did not want; for you she kept vigil, for you she wept: and will you let her be in want? O son, how great a judgment you take upon yourself if you do not feed your parent! You owe what you have to her to whom you owe what you are. How great a judgment, if the Church feeds those whom you will not feed! If any believing man he says or any believing woman has widows, let him provide for them, so that the Church be not burdened, so that it may suffice for those who are truly widows. This is said of strangers: what of parents?

76. Not idly have we spoken of this just now, for a mother's complaint has touched us; but we preferred to admonish publicly one who is such rather than to restrain him privately. And if he is not betrayed by our voice, let him at least blush at his own feeling. Do not let it come about, son, that the hunger of others feeds your parents; do not let it come about, son, that the fastings of the poor seek food for your parents. If not for grace and salvation, then for shame's sake feed them, son, you at least. Is it not a shame if, as you enter the church, your aged mother stretches out her hands to strangers and, passing over her daughter, begs alms of others, while you pass by with lofty neck and with winks of the eyes, trailing your robe, wearing earrings and bracelets and rings and the rest of which Isaiah speaks? What if she turns her speech against you, demanding back the debt of nature, the price of her nursing, the service your hand owes to the mother who bore you? What will you answer? Will you give to others?

77. What if they throw it at you: Go, first feed your mother? For even if they are poor, yet they do not seek an impious contribution. Did you not hear above that that rich man reclining in fine linen and purple, from whose table Lazarus gathered crumbs, is tormented with eternal punishments because he did not share his food with a poor man? If not to give to strangers is grave, how much graver to shut out one's parents! But you say that you prefer to give to the Church what you would have given to your parents. God does not seek a gift from the hunger of parents. Hence, when the Jews reproached the disciples of the Lord because they did not wash their hands, the Lord answered: Whoever says: 'Whatever gift is from me will profit you', has not honored his father or mother.

78. Therefore we have not imprudently turned aside to the obscurity of this sense. For the Jews, while they follow the tradition of men, neglect that of God. The disciples, preferring the tradition of God, neglected that of men, so as not to wash their hands when they ate bread, since he who has been washed all over has no need to wash his hands. Jesus had washed them; they did not seek another washing; for by one baptism Christ did away with all baptisms. So he whom the Church has washed has no need to wash again. The disciples, then, were attentive to the mystery, seeking the cleanness not of their body but of their soul. This the Jews reproached, but they are shrewdly refuted by the Lord, because they observe empty things and despise those that would profit. And therefore he says to them: Why do you say to father or mother, whom the law commands to be honored: Whatever gift is from me will profit you? That is: when a needy father or mother asks the son for something for their expenses, the Jew, because, fearing the law, he seeks an excuse for not giving, is wont to say: A gift, whatever you would gain from me, so that the devout father shrinks from accepting money dedicated to God. But this is the tradition of men who weave excuses for their own avarice; the tradition of God, however, is that you feed your parents first. For if, according to the divine oracle, an insult to a parent is atoned for by death, how much more hunger, which is graver than death!

79. In this place the Lord curbs insolent boasting.
