For many, so as to be praised by men, bestow on the Church what they take away from their own, when mercy ought to proceed from the domestic duty of dutiful love. Give, then, first to your parent, give also to the poor, give to that presbyter the earthly things in which you abound, so that you may receive from him the spiritual things which you lack; for he who honors will be honored. See, then, that he, when he receives, gives, and receives not as one in need but as one who will repay with a greater measure. Give to the poor man for his rest, so that you too, sharing your own with the needy, may obtain rest. But as scripture says that parents are to be fed, so too it says that parents are to be left for God's sake, if they hinder the affection of a devoted mind.

80. And it came to pass, when he drew near to Jericho, a certain blind man was sitting by the way. In the book according to Matthew two are brought in, here one; there as he was going out of Jericho, here as he was drawing near. But there is no discrepancy; for since in this one man there is a type of the Gentile people, which by the Lord's sacrament received back the brightness of its lost light, it makes no difference whether it receives healing in one or in two, since, drawing its origin from Ham and Japheth, the sons of Noah, it put forward in the two blind men the two founders of its race.

81. Luke too seems not to have omitted this, when he adds Zacchaeus, who, little in stature, that is, raised by no dignity of inborn nobility, slight in merits, like the people of the nations, having heard of the coming of the Lord Savior, whom his own had not received, longed to see him. But no one easily sees Jesus, no one set on the earth can see Jesus. And because he had neither prophets nor a kingdom, as a grace of natural beauty, he climbed into the sycamore, trampling, that is, the vanity of the Jews under his foot, and correcting also the errors of an earlier age; and therefore he received Jesus into the hospitality of his inner house. And he did well to climb a tree, so that a good tree might bear good fruits, and that he, cut from the natural wild olive and grafted contrary to nature into the good olive, might be able to bear the fruit of the law; for the root is holy, even if the branches are useless, whose fruitless glory the people of the Gentiles has surpassed by faith in the resurrection, as by a kind of lifting up of the body.

82. And behold, a man named Zacchaeus. Zacchaeus in the sycamore, the blind man by the way. The one the Lord waits for, meaning to have mercy on him; the other he ennobles with the splendor of his lodging: the one he questions, meaning to heal him; at the house of the other he invites himself uninvited; for he knew that the reward of his host would be rich; and yet, though he had not yet heard the voice of one inviting him, he had heard the affection.

83. But lest we seem, as men disdainful of the poor, to have quickly left that blind man and passed over to the rich man, let us wait for him, because the Lord too waited; let us question him, because Christ too questioned him. Let us question because we do not know; he questioned because he knew. Let us question so that we may know how this man was cured; he questioned so that in one man we, the many, might learn how we may merit to see the Lord; for he questioned so that we might believe that no one can be healed unless he confesses. And at once he says he saw and followed him, magnifying the Lord. And he was passing through Jericho.

84. For otherwise he would not see, unless he followed Christ, unless he proclaimed the Lord, unless he passed beyond the world. Let us return to favor with the rich as well; for we do not wish to offend the rich. We wish, if it can be done, to heal all, lest, stung by the comparison of the camel and left behind in the case of Zacchaeus sooner than was fitting, they should take the offense of a just resentment.

85. Let them learn that guilt does not cling to possessions, but to those who do not know how to use possessions; for riches, as they are hindrances in the wicked, so in the good they are helps to virtue. Zacchaeus, certainly a rich man, was chosen by Christ; but by bestowing half of his goods on the poor and also restoring fourfold what he had taken away by fraud — for the one is not enough, nor does liberality find favor if injury persists, because not spoils but gifts are sought — he received a richer reward than he was bestowing.

86. And he does well to bring in a chief of tax collectors. For who should despair of himself, when even this man reached salvation, whose wealth came from fraud? And he himself he says was rich, so that you may know that not all the rich are greedy.

87. What does it mean that scripture has expressed the stature of no one else but this man: who was little in stature? See whether perhaps he was little in malice, or still little in faith; for he had not yet promised anything when he climbed, he had not yet seen Christ: rightly, then, he was still little. Accordingly John was great, because he saw both Christ and the Spirit like a dove remaining upon Christ, as he himself says: I saw the Spirit descending like a dove and remaining upon him.

88. And what is the crowd, if not the confusion of an unskilled multitude, which could not see the summit of wisdom? So Zacchaeus, as long as he is in the crowd, does not see Christ; he climbed above the crowd and saw him, that is: having passed beyond the ignorance of the common people, he merited to behold him whom he desired.

89. He added beautifully: because the Lord was to pass by that way, either where the sycamore was, or where one who was to believe was, so that he might both keep the mystery and sow grace; for he had so come as to pass through the Jews to the Gentiles.

90. So he saw Zacchaeus up above; for already by the loftiness of his faith he stood out among the fruits of new works, as on the height of a fruitful tree. And since we have turned from the type to moral matters, it delights me, amid the goodwill of so many believers, to relax the mind on the Lord's day and to mingle festivity with it. Zacchaeus in the sycamore: a new fruit, that is, of a new time, so that in this too that saying might be fulfilled: The fig tree has put forth its green figs; for Christ came for this, that from trees not fruits but men should be born. We read elsewhere: When you were under the fig tree, I saw you. So Nathanael was under the tree, that is, above the root, because he was righteous — for the root is holy — yet Nathanael was under the tree, because he was under the law; Zacchaeus was above the tree, because he was above the law; the one a hidden defender of the Lord, the other a public preacher; the one was still seeking Christ from the law, the other, now above the law, was leaving his own goods and following the Lord.

91. Behold, your mina has gained ten minas. A good order: being about to call the Gentiles and to order the killing of the Jews, who did not want Christ to reign over them, he first set out this comparison, lest it should be said: 'He had given nothing to the people of the Jews by which it could become better', or: 'What is required of one who has received nothing?' This is no ordinary mina, for which above the woman of the Gospel, because she had not found it, lit a lamp, sought it with the light brought near, and rejoiced when it was found.

92. Accordingly, from one mina one man made ten minas, another five. Perhaps these five contain moral things, because the senses of the body are five; those ten contain double things, that is, the mystical things of the law and the moral things of uprightness. Hence Matthew too set down five talents and two talents, so that in the five talents there are moral things, in the two both, the mystical and the moral. Thus what is lesser in number is richer in substance. And here we can understand the ten minas as the ten words, that is, the teaching of the law, and the five minas as the instructions of discipline; but I want the lawyer to be perfect in all things: for the kingdom of God is not in word, but in power.

93. And rightly, since he is speaking of the Jews, only two bring back the money multiplied, not, surely, by the interest of bronze, but by that of disputation. For the interest of money lent at usury is one thing, that of heavenly doctrine another.

94. Accordingly, when the Lord says: Why did you not put my money on the table? he seeks interest not on our money but on his own.

95. One says that he hid it in the ground, because by his zeal for pleasure he buried the reason which was given to us after the image and likeness of God, and hid it as in a pit of the flesh. Of the others nothing is said, who like prodigal debtors lost what they had received. In those two are the few who were sent at two times to the tenants of the vineyard; in the rest, all the Jews. This comparison Matthew wished to fit to us also: that, just like the rich man who does not share his money with the poor, so too he who does not share the grace of his teaching with the unlearned, though he could teach, is guilty of no ordinary fault. Since we have spoken of this in the books written On the Faith, it was better to pass over it quickly.

96. And what can the ten cities be, unless perhaps souls, over which he is rightly set who has lent out at interest to the minds of men the money of the Lord and those chaste words, tried like silver? For as Jerusalem is said to be built as a city, so are peaceful souls. And as the angels preside, so too do those who have merited the life of the angels.

Book Nine

And it came to pass, when he had drawn near to Bethphage and Bethany, to the mount which is called of Olives, he sent two disciples, saying: Go into the village which is opposite, in which, as you enter, you will find the colt of an ass tied, on which no one has sat.

1. Beautifully, having left the Jews, the Lord, who was to dwell in the affections of the Gentiles, goes up to the temple; for this is the true temple, in which the Lord is worshipped not in the letter but in the spirit; this is the temple of God, which the order of faith, not a structure of stones, has founded. So those who hated are forsaken; those who were going to love are chosen.

2. And therefore he comes to the mount of Olives, to plant young olive trees on the height of virtue, whose mother is that Jerusalem which is above. On this mount is that heavenly husbandman, so that all those planted in the house of God may say, each one of them: But I am like a fruitful olive tree in the house of the Lord. And perhaps the mount itself is Christ. For who else would bear such fruits, not of olive trees bowed down by an abundance of berries, but of nations made fruitful by the fullness of the Spirit? He it is through whom we ascend and to whom we ascend. He is the door, he is the way, who is opened and who opens, who is knocked at by those who enter and is adored by those who have finished their service.

3. So the colt was in the village, and it was tied with the ass. It could not be loosed except at the command of the Lord. The apostolic hand loosed it: such the deed, such the life, such the grace. Be such that you can loose those who are bound.

4. Now let us consider who they were who, when their error was discovered, were cast out of paradise and bound in the village. And you see how life called back those whom death had driven out. And therefore according to Matthew we read of both an ass and a colt, so that, since in two human beings each sex had been driven out, in two animals each sex might be called back. So there, in the mother ass, he figured as it were the Eve of error, but here, in the colt, he expressed the generality of the Gentile people.

5. And therefore the colt of the ass is sat upon. And well: on which no one has sat, because no one had, before Christ called the peoples of the nations to the Church. Indeed according to Mark you have it thus: which no one of men has yet sat upon. But it was held tied with the chains of unbelief, made over to an unjust lord, a slave to error; but he could not claim dominion for himself whom not nature but guilt had made lord. And therefore when 'Lord' is said, one is recognized — for there are indeed many gods and many lords, but in a general sense — one God and one Lord. So if the Lord is not specified, he is defined no longer by the addition of a person, but by the universality of nature.

6. But Mark brought in the colt tied before the door, because whoever is without Christ is outside, in the road, but he who is in Christ is not outside. He added in the thoroughfare, where no one has a sure possession, no manger, no food, no stable. Wretched is the servitude whose right is unsettled; for he who does not have one master has many. Strangers tie up so as to possess; this one looses so as to hold; for he knows that gifts are stronger than chains.

7. Nor is it idle that two disciples are sent, Peter to Cornelius, Paul to the rest. And therefore he did not designate persons, but defined a number; yet if there is anyone who demands persons, he can reckon it of Philip, whom the Spirit sent to Gaza, when he baptized the eunuch of Queen Candace and from Azotus to Caesarea sowed the word of the Lord through all the cities. Nor should it be passed over that he declares they are soon to be sent back, because those had to be sent forth who would preach the Lord Jesus in the lands of the Gentiles.

8. So when those who had been sent were loosing the colt, did they use their own words? Not at all, but they spoke as Jesus had told them, so that you may recognize that not by their own speech but by the word of God, not by their own name but by the name of Christ, they poured faith into the Gentile peoples, and that the adverse powers, which claimed for themselves the homage of the nations, gave way to the divine command.

9. Hence also the apostles spread their own garments under Christ, either so that by the preaching of the Gospel they might set forth the glory of the deed — for in the divine Scriptures garments are often virtues — which by their own virtue too might soften somewhat the hardness of the Gentiles, so that with zealous affections they might render the unstumbling service of a blessed carriage. For it was not a delight to the Lord of the world to be carried on the back of an ass for public show, but that by a hidden mystery he might spread a covering over the inner chambers of our mind, and that, as a mystical rider, he might sit in the secret places of souls in an inward seat, infused as it were into a kind of body of the divinity, guiding the steps of the mind, bridling the wantonness of the flesh, so that by the leading of devotion he might tame the accustomed disposition of the Gentile people. Happy those who received such a rider in their inmost reins; happy indeed those whose mouths the rein of the heavenly Word held in, lest they be loosed in much speaking!

10. What is this rein, brethren? Who will teach me how it either holds in or looses the mouths of men? He showed me the rein who said: that speech may be given me in the opening of my mouth. Speech, then, is a rein, speech is a goad, and therefore it is hard for you to kick against the goad. This one, then, taught us to open the heart, to endure the goad, to draw the yoke. Let another teach us also to bear the reins of the tongue; for the virtue of keeping silent is rarer than that of speaking. Let him teach indeed who, like one dumb, did not open his mouth against deceit, ready for the scourges and not refusing the blows, so that there might be a devout seat for God.

11. Learn from the household servant of God to carry Christ, since he first carried you, when as shepherd he brought back the straying sheep; learn to spread beneath him the willing back of your mind, learn to be under Christ, so that you may be above the world. Not just anyone easily carries Christ, but he who can say: I am bowed down and humbled exceedingly; I roared from the groaning of my heart. But if you desire not to be shaken, set a washed footstep upon those garments of the saints. For beware lest you walk with muddy feet; beware lest with sideways steps you abandon the paths of the prophetic ways that have been laid down for you. For so that the advance of the Gentiles who were to come might be safer, those who went before Jesus paved the path with their own clothing as far as the temple of God. So that you might walk without stumbling, the disciples of the Lord, stripping themselves of the garment of their own body, have strewn the way for you by their martyrdom amid the hostility of the crowds. Yet if anyone wishes to take it thus, we do not object: that this colt was already walking upon the garments of the Jews.

12. But what the broken-off branches mean, which are certainly wont to entangle the steps of those who walk, I should indeed be in doubt, had not the good husbandman of the whole world taught me in the earlier passages that now the axe is laid to the roots of the trees, to cut down the barren ones at the coming of the Lord Savior and to lay low on the ground the empty glory of the unfruitful Gentiles, to be trodden down by the feet of the faithful, so that the nations, by the spirit of a renewed mind, might sprout like the shoots of young trees from old stumps.

13. Do not, then, despise this little ass; for as there are ravening wolves in the clothing of sheep, so, on the contrary, the hidden man of the heart is within under a beast's form, because under the cast-off skin of the body, which we have in common with the beasts, a mind full of God is quickened. That this pertains to a figure of men Saint John declared more plainly by adding that they took for themselves the hearts of palm trees; for the righteous will flourish like the palm. And therefore at the coming of Christ the standards of righteousness and the insignia of triumphs were already being raised upon the shoulders of men. Why does the crowd marvel at what the mystery is working? Even if it does not know what it marvels at, yet it marvels, because on that colt wisdom sits, power abides, righteousness clings.

14. Do not despise this she-ass either, which once saw the angel of God whom a man could not see. And it saw, and carried, and spoke, so that you might recognize that in later times, at the coming of the great Angel of God, those Gentiles, asses before, would speak.

15. Beautifully, according to Luke, we read that the crowds praising God met him at the descent of the mount, so that they might signify that the worker of the spiritual mystery had come to them from heaven. The crowd, then, recognizes God, calls him king, repeats the prophecy: Hosanna to the Son of David, that is, it declares that the Redeemer of the house of David, the one awaited, has come, also, according to the flesh, the Son of David — and this was that crowd which was to crucify him after a brief moment. Truly a memorable mark of the divine working, that testimony against themselves is wrung from the unwilling, when they deny in their affections the God whom they confess with their voices.

16. Hence also that saying of the Lord: if these are silent, the stones will cry out. Nor indeed is it strange if, against their nature, rocks should echo the praises of the Lord, whom his slayers, harder than crags, proclaim; or perhaps it is because, when the Jews fell silent after the Lord's Passion, living stones, according to Peter, were going to cry out. So, although its own affections fight against it, the crowd nevertheless leads God with praise to his temple.

17. But God wills his temple to be not a merchant's lodging-house but a dwelling of holiness, and he shapes the practice of the priestly ministry not by a saleable office of religion but by free service. Consider, then, what the deeds of the Lord prescribe to you as an example for living.

18. And he cast out all who were selling and buying in the temple, and overturned the tables of the money-changers and the chairs of those who sold doves. In general, therefore, he taught above that worldly contracts ought to be absent from the temple of God, but in particular he drove out the money-changers. Who are the money-changers but those who seek gain from the Lord's money and do not distinguish good and evil? For the Lord's money is the divine Scripture. For he too distributed denarii to his servants when he was about to set out, and divided talents, and for the healing of the wounded man left two coins with the innkeeper; for by the two Testaments our wounds are cured. But you, like a good money-changer, store up the words of the Lord, pure words, silver tried in the fire, purged by the sevenfold Spirit, and do not by an impious exchange accept a counterfeit image of the king — for even Satan transforms himself into an angel of light — nor mix into your treasure the diminished figure of your prince by the fraud of Arian unbelief, nor tempt the ear of the faithful with the sound of money, so that the jingling of bronze shuts out the hearing of the holy Scriptures, or the desire of possessing is planted in religious affections. So not all the money-changers are to be shut out; for there are good ones too. Indeed, you should have given my money he says to the money-changers, and on coming I would have received it back with interest. But if the coin is of the Scriptures, the interest too is of the Scriptures.

19. But why he overturned the chairs of those who sold doves I do not understand according to the letter; for the dealers in birds could not claim for themselves the dignity of any honorable seat at the market. For what prerogative of dignity is there in doves? Yet the form of the Lord's baptism, when the Holy Spirit descended in a dove, admonishes us, by the type of merchants of this kind cast out of the temple, that those cannot have fellowship in the Church of God who make a traffic of the grace of the Holy Spirit. Freely he says you have received, freely give. Indeed to Simon, who thought that the gift of sanctifying could be conferred on himself for a price, Peter replied: Your money he says be with you unto destruction, since you think that you can obtain the grace of God with money. You have no part or lot in this faith.

20. The sellers of sheep and oxen too I take to be those who, as foul auctioneers, hunt for some market out of another's labor or simplicity; or else, because the sheep and oxen are cast out and the doves are ordered to be taken away, the Jewish people appears to be shut out; for Ephraim is like a dove, in that the Lord hated both their successions and their labors. The bronze is poured out so that grace may be gathered; the table of the money-changers is overturned so that the Lord's may be put in its place; the altar is thrown down so that the altars may be raised up.

21. And he did this not equipped with any band of men or with riches, but with a scourge of cords he struck the crowds, and no one dared to resist. And now he uses a rod, now a scourge — for the rod is upright, the rod of your kingdom — a rod, to correct; a scourge, to persuade. There it is straight. Here it is a moral precept, bent as it were, by which the sinner's conscience is scourged as with a pliant lash. For the terrors of the prophets are one thing, the persuasions of the apostles another: yet in both is the discipline of one Word. And therefore he made a scourge of cords, because the lines have fallen he says to me in splendid places; for my inheritance is splendid to me; for lines is the name of the cords with which surveyors divide the boundaries of fields to be measured out. And therefore as a good surveyor he was marking off the boundaries of the Synagogue, and ordered the profaners of the Church to go out of the temple; for a new measurer of fruitful souls had come, who would measure the kinds of fields, not their extent.

22. Yet beautifully no measure of possession is enclosed by stretched lines, nor are the limits of faith bounded as though of some defined thing, but by a scourge that ranges freely without measure the borders of the Church are extended; the Jews are banished, not into some prescribed exile, but into an unending one, so that nowhere in the world would a place remain for the synagogue.

23. A man planted a vineyard. Many derive various meanings from the name of vineyard, but Isaiah plainly recorded that the vineyard of the Lord of Sabaoth is the house of Israel. Who else but God established this vineyard? He it is, then, who let it out and went abroad: not that the Lord, who is always everywhere, set out from place to place, but because he is more present to those who love him and is absent from those who neglect him. For a long time he was away, lest the demand should seem too hasty. For the more indulgent the generosity, the more inexcusable the obstinacy.

24. Hence according to Matthew you rightly have that he also set a hedge around it, that is, he fortified it with the rampart of divine protection, so that it might not easily lie open to the incursions of spiritual beasts. And he dug in it a winepress. How are we to understand what the winepress is, unless perhaps because psalms bear the title of the winepresses, since in them the mysteries of the Lord's Passion, like must, seethed over more abundantly as the Holy Spirit of the prophets fermented? Indeed, those with whom the Holy Spirit overflowed were thought to be drunk. So here too he dug a winepress, into which the inner fruit of the rational grape might flow down by a spiritual infusion. He built a tower, that is, raising up the summit of the Law; and so he let out this vineyard, fortified, furnished and adorned, to the Jews.

25. And at the time of the fruits he sent his servants. Rightly he put the time of the fruits, not the yield; for there was no fruit of the Jews, no yield of this vineyard, of which the Lord says: I waited for it to make grapes, but it made thorns. So the winepresses overflowed not with the wine of gladness, not with spiritual must, but with the gory blood of the prophets. Indeed Jeremiah was cast into the pit; for these were by now the winepresses of the Jews, filled not with wine but with mire. And although the prophets in general seem to be meant, yet the reading leads us to believe that the one who was stoned is Naboth, of whom, though we have received no prophetic word, we have yet received a prophetic deed, because by his own blood he prophesied that there would be many martyrs for this vineyard. But who is the one who is wounded in the head? Surely Isaiah, the frame of whose body the saw divided more easily than it bent his faith, or wore down his constancy, or cut away the vigor of his mind.

26. So it came about that, when he had sent many others, whom the Jews sent back to him dishonored and empty-handed, since they could gain nothing from them, last of all he sent even his only-begotten Son, whom the unbelievers, desiring to remove him as the heir, killed by crucifying him and cast out by denying him.

27. Briefly, then, how great things, and how many! First, that goodness is natural, which often trusts the unworthy; next, that Christ came as the last remedy for evils; then, that he who denies the heir despairs of the author. But Christ is heir and likewise testator: heir, because he outlived his own death, and obtains in our progress, as an inheritance, the profits of the testaments which he himself drew up.

28. Beautifully, then, he asks, so that they may condemn themselves by their own sentence. But he says that the Lord of the vineyard will come, because in the Son the Father's majesty also is present, or because in the last times he breathes upon human affections with a nearer presence. So they themselves pronounce sentence upon themselves, that the wicked should perish and the vineyard be transferred to other tenants. Let us consider, then, who the tenants are, and what the vineyard is.

29. The vineyard is our type, because the people of God, founded on the root of the eternal vine, rises above the earth and, overspreading the poor soil, now ripens into budding flower, now is clothed in the greenness spread around it, now takes up a gentle yoke, when it has grown up with more mature arms like the shoots of a fruitful vine. For the husbandman is the almighty Father, the vine is Christ, but we are the branches, and unless we bear fruit in Christ we are cut down by the sickle of the eternal Cultivator. Rightly, then, the people of Christ is called a vineyard, either because the sign of the cross is marked on its forehead, or because its fruit is gathered at the last season of the year, or because for all, as for the rows of the vineyard, so for poor and rich, lowly and mighty, slaves and masters, there is in the Church of God an equal measure, no distinction. As the vine is wedded to trees, so the body is joined to the soul, and the soul also to the body. As the vineyard is raised up while it is yoked, as when pruned it is not diminished but increased, so the holy people is set free while it is bound, is lifted up while it is humbled, is crowned while it is cut down. Moreover, as a tender shoot cut from an old tree is grafted into the growth of another root, so this holy people, the scars of the old shoot smoothed away, is cherished on that wood of the cross as in the bosom of a loving parent and grows into it; and the Holy Spirit, as if let down into the deep trenches of the earth, so poured into this prison-house of the body, washes away with the watering of saving water whatever is foul, and raises the bearing of our members to a heavenly discipline.

30. This vineyard the diligent husbandman is wont to dig, to yoke, to prune, and, when the mounds of earthly mass have been cleared away, now to bake the hidden parts of our body with sun, now to drench them with rain, accustomed to clear the field of growth, lest the bud be harmed by brambles, lest the shade of the leaves run riot and the unfruitful boasting of words, overshadowing the virtues, hinder the ripening of our natural disposition. But far be it from us to fear anything adverse for this vineyard, which the ever-watchful guardian of the Lord Savior has fortified with the wall of eternal life against all the allurements of worldly wickedness. He stretched out its branches as far as the sea; the Lord's indeed is the earth. Everywhere God the Father is worshipped, everywhere Christ the Lord is adored.

31. This is our vintage. Glad, then, and secure, let some load their laps with clusters of mellow grapes, let others of us taste the heavenly gifts with our mouths, and let most of us, pressing out the fruit of the divine gift with the steps of good wills, dye our feet, bared with the buskins pulled off, in the flowing must; for the place on which we stand is holy ground, and therefore the shoes must be loosed, so that the spiritual footstep, as it ascends the tribunal of the sacred seat, may be stripped of the knots of bodily bonds; for it is fitting that the vintage of the whole world should be here, where the vineyard of the whole world is.

32. Behold the acceptable time, in which the year is not stiff with the winter frosts of darkening unbelief, nor does the shapeless crust of blasphemy harden under deep snows in a lasting frost, but the earth, freed from the storms of sacrilege, now is in labor with new fruits and brings forth the old; for the storm of all dissensions has subsided; all the fires of worldly greed and every heat, by which the people of Italy were scorched in the blazes of perversity, once Jewish, lately Arian, are now tempered by a serene breeze. The tempest is calmed, concord sails, faith breathes; eagerly the sailors seek again the harbors of faith which they had left, and press sweet kisses on the shores of their homeland, rejoicing that they are freed from dangers, released from errors.

33. Hail, vineyard worthy of so great a guardian: not the blood of one Naboth, but of countless prophets, and, what is more, the precious blood of the Lord has consecrated you. He, though he did not, terrified by royal threats, suppress his constancy through fear, nor, enticed by most lavish rewards, sell his devout affection, but, resisting the king's desire, lest a bed of mallows be sown in his garden once the vine was cut down, quenched with his own blood, since he could do nothing else, the fires prepared for the vines, was yet defending a temporal vineyard; but you the death of many martyrs has planted for us for ever, you the cross of the apostles, emulating the Lord's Passion, has propagated to the ends of the whole world.

34. Whose image and inscription does it have? In this place the Lord teaches that we ought to be circumspect in answering against heretics or Jews. Elsewhere he said: be shrewd as serpents. Many take this place to mean that, since the cross of Christ was foretold when the serpent was lifted up, by which he would abolish the serpentine venom of spiritual wickedness, it appears that one ought to be shrewd in the way Christ is, simple in the way the Spirit is. See for yourself that serpent, which always guards its head and wards off the deadly wound. Asked by the Jews whether he had received authority from heaven, he replied: The baptism of John, whence is it, from heaven or from men? So that, since they did not dare to deny that it was from heaven, they would convict themselves of madness, denying as they did that its author was from heaven. Asked for the didrachma, he asks about the image; for the image of God is one thing, the image of the world another. Hence he too admonishes us: as we have borne the image of that earthly one, let us bear also the image of this heavenly one.

35. Christ does not have the image of Caesar, because he is the image of God. Peter does not have the image of Caesar, because he said: we have left all things and followed you. The image of Caesar is not found in James or John, because they are sons of thunder; but it is found in the sea, where those dragons have their heads crushed upon the water, and the greater dragon himself, his head broken, is given as food to the peoples of the Ethiopians. If, then, he did not have the image of Caesar, why did he pay the tax? He did not give of his own, but returned to the world what was the world's. And you, if you wish not to be liable to Caesar, do not have the things that are the world's. But you have riches: you are liable to Caesar. If you wish to owe nothing to an earthly king, leave all that is yours and follow Christ.

36. And rightly he decrees that the things of Caesar are to be rendered first; for no one can be the Lord's unless he has first renounced the world. But we all renounce in words, we do not renounce in affection; for when we receive the sacraments, we renounce. How heavy the bonds, to promise to God and not to pay! It is better he says that you do not vow than that you vow and do not pay. The contract of faith is greater than that of money. Pay what you promised while you are in this body, before the exactor comes and casts you into prison. Amen I say to you, you will not come out from there until you pay the last farthing.

37. If a man's brother has died. The Sadducees, that is, the more detestable portion of the Jews, test the Lord in this place. Their folly is openly rebuked, but in the mystical sense their argument is turned back on them: what kind of example of chastity they offered from their own, when according to the letter a woman is compelled to marry even against her will, so that the brother may raise up the seed of the deceased. So the letter kills, as a procuress of vices, but the Spirit is the teacher of chastity.

38. So let us see whether this woman may not be the synagogue, which had seven husbands, as it is said to the Samaritan woman: for you have had five husbands, because the Samaritan woman follows only the five books of Moses, the synagogue chiefly seven; and from none of them, because of its unbelief, did it receive the seed of a posterity to inherit. And therefore it will not be able to have a share with its husbands in the resurrection, because it turned a spiritual precept upside down according to the sense of the flesh; for it was not some brother according to the flesh who was enjoined to raise up seed for his dead brother, but that brother who from the dead people of the Jews would take to himself as wife the wisdom of divine worship, and from her raise up seed in the apostles, who, like the remnants of the dead Jews, left still unformed in the womb of the synagogue, deserved, according to the election of grace, to be preserved by the admixture of the new seed.

39. But the synagogue is often shown receiving the robe, which is the mark of marriage, as the author of the faithful; it is often brought in also as repudiated, because it is the mother of the unbelieving. For it the bodily law died, so that the spiritual law might rise again. So the holy people of God, if it has loved the seven books of the Law with a kind of conjugal charity and has obeyed them as it were as a husband's commands, will have in the resurrection a heavenly fellowship, not confounded by the shame of bodily defilement, but enriched with the gifts of divine grace.

Book Ten

1. The Lord said to my Lord. As he is about to complete his precepts, the Lord also closes, at the end of his testament, with the faith and the mercy that lead the way to the Passion: faith, in that we should believe Christ both to be our Lord God and to sit at the right hand of God — not that he who is everywhere sits bodily. Indeed he himself is in the Father, because he is in the substance of God, because there is one power, one majesty. He himself, then, is in the Father and the Father in him, because the Word is in God, God in the Word; he himself is in the Father, he himself is at the right hand of the Father, because he shares with the Father, second to none; he himself is sent by the Father, because he came down from heaven to fulfil the Father's will. Take away from here the questions of unbelief, and religion is complete. He is not set above because he sits at the right hand, nor does he suffer injury because he is sent. No rank of dignity is sought where there is the fullness of divinity.

2. This too must be considered: that he rebukes those who call Christ the son of David; and how then did that blind man merit healing by confessing the son of David? How did the children, saying Hosanna to the son of David, bring to God the glory of a most exalted proclamation? But they are not rebuked in this passage because they confess the son of David, but because they do not believe him the Son of God. For in true faith it is not the one or the other, but both; for although in the beginning we determined to know nothing except Christ Jesus and him crucified, now, however, as men near to the judgment, we no longer know Christ crucified, but await him coming in the clouds. The unbeliever looks upon the wounds; the faithful man, caught up into the air, runs to meet Christ.

3. Let us therefore believe that Christ is both God and man, one in both, not another, he to whom his enemies are made subject by the Father, not through any weakness of his own power, but through the unity of nature, because the one works in the other. For the Son too makes enemies subject to the Father, he who glorifies the Father upon the earth, and the Father gave the Son the name which is above every name. But the Son also says to the Father: I have manifested your name to the men whom you gave me. And in giving the name which is above every name he gave no more than he had, but gave all that he had. And he gave this name so that every tongue should confess to God that the Lord Jesus is in the glory of God the Father.

4. See, then, each point. The Father makes subject to the Son, the Son makes subject to the Father; the Father raises the Son, the Son raises himself. Hence he says: Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up again. Both the Father is Lord and the Son is Lord — The Lord said to my Lord — yet not two lords, but one Lord, because both the Father is God and the Son is God, yet one God, because the Father is in the Son and the Son in the Father. One God, because there is one Godhead — Your throne, O God, is for ever and ever; an upright rod is the rod of your kingdom. You have loved righteousness and hated iniquity; therefore God, your God, has anointed you — yet one God, in whom the teaching of both the New and the Old Testament agrees. For in the Old Testament too it is written: You shall love the Lord your God and You shall worship the Lord your God and him alone shall you serve; in the New: One God and Father of all. So too the Father is Lord and the Son is Lord, yet one Lord. Indeed it is written: do not serve two lords. This is in the New; and in the Old it is written: Hear, O Israel, the Lord your God is one Lord. Hence the Apostle admirably, so as not to speak of two gods, or name two lords, or detract from either the Son or the Father: One he says God the Father, from whom are all things, and one Lord Jesus, through whom are all things. For he who is God is also Lord, and he who is Lord is God. Indeed it is written: Know that the Lord himself is our God.

5. All things, then, that the Father has, the Son also has. God the Father according to the principle of generation, God the Son according to the unity of the image. The Son is Lord, because he subjected all things to himself by reason, though he could have done so by power; the Father is Lord, because he is the root of the Son. Thus for us the Father in the Son is distinguished by the definition of the persons and joined by the unity of power. And so the one is in the other, and they are one in each other. For it is both the glory of the Father, if he has not degenerated in the Son, and the grace of the Son, if the Father is seen in Christ. So he is not degenerate in whom is the supreme majesty of unity, nor alien in whom the property of generation is the expression of the truth.

6. There shall not be left a stone upon a stone that shall not be destroyed. The passage about the widow came next; since we have already praised her in the book we wrote on widows, we now set it aside. Now this was said truly, according to the matter in hand, of the temple which Solomon built, and that it was first to be overthrown by the enemy at the time of judgment; for there is nothing made by work and hand which either age does not consume, or force overthrow, or fire burn up. Yet there is also another temple, built of fair stones and adorned with gifts, whose overthrow the Lord seems to signify, namely the synagogue of the Jews, whose old structure is dissolved as the Church rises. There is also a temple in each one, which falls when faith fails, and above all if anyone falsely holds out the name of Christ as a screen, so as to storm the inner affection.

7. There can also be this exposition, which may profit me more. For what does it profit me to know the day of judgment? What does it profit me, conscious of so many sins, if the Lord comes, unless he comes into my mind, returns into my thoughts, unless Christ lives in me, unless Christ speaks in me? So Christ must come to me, his advent must take place for me. Now the second advent of the Lord takes place at the failing of the world, when we can say: To me, however, the world is crucified, and I to the world.

8. But if such a failing of the world finds this man in the upper parts of the house, so that his way of life is in heaven, then the bodily and visible temple will be destroyed, the bodily law, the bodily Pasch and the visible Pasch, the bodily unleavened bread and the visible unleavened bread — I dare to say, a temporal Christ, such as he was to Paul before he believed — because in him for whom the world fails Christ is eternal. For him there is a spiritual temple, a spiritual law, a spiritual Pasch too, since Christ is slain once; he feasts on unleavened bread not of earthly grain, but of the grain of righteousness. So for him there comes the presence of wisdom, the presence of virtue and of righteousness, the presence of redemption; for Christ, indeed, once died for the sins of the people, but he was to redeem the sins of the people daily.

9. But when you hear of battles and reports of battles. The Lord, asked when the destruction of the temple would come and what would be the sign of his coming, teaches about the signs; about the time he does not think it should be made known. But Matthew added a third question, so that the disciples asked both about the time of the temple's destruction, and about the sign of the coming, and about the consummation of the age; Luke thought that enough was known about the consummation of the age if one learned about the coming of the Lord.

10. Of these heavenly words, however, none are witnesses more than we, whom the end of the world has found. For how many battles, and what reports of battles have we received! The Huns rose up against the Alans, the Alans against the Goths, the Goths against the Taifali and the Sarmatians; us too, in Illyricum, the exile of the Goths has made exiles from our homeland, and the end is not yet. What a famine for all, what a plague of oxen and men alike and of the other cattle, so that pestilence has made even us, who did not endure the war, equal to the vanquished! So, since we are at the setting of the world, certain sicknesses of the world go before. Famine is a sickness of the world, pestilence is a sickness of the world, persecution is a sickness of the world.

11. But there are also other wars which the Christian man endures, battles of diverse desires and clashes of passions, and enemies at home far more grievous than those from outside. Now avarice goads, now lust inflames, now fear terrifies, now anger stirs up, now ambition moves, now the spiritual forces of wickedness which are in the heavenly places try to terrify. And so the shifting disposition of the wavering mind is pressed as if by certain battles and shaken as if by certain earthquakes.

12. But the stronger man says: If camps stand against me, my heart shall not fear; if battle rise up against me, in this will I hope. He stands in the battle line, presenting his breast to meet the enemy; even if some Goliath rises up, savage and towering, yet, while the rest are afraid, he rises up, lowly, like David, casting off the armour of an earthly king, taking up the lighter weapons of faith, and hurling with a threefold cord the javelin of pure confession he wounds the shamelessness of the persecutor, scorning threats, heedless of the powers, fit even to have Christ speak in him. Now Christ, now the Father, now the Spirit of the Father speaks. These do not disagree, but accord. What one speaks the three speak, because the voice of the Trinity is one. To this victor, who has slain Goliath with his own sword while he meets death for Christ, once the foreigners are put to flight, the young maidens run to meet him — those, namely, like eagles — saying: Saul struck among thousands, David among ten thousands. This is a sign that the victors over the world are set before princes. Indeed, when kings are dead, the martyrs succeed for ever to the kingdom in the honour of heavenly grace, and the former become suppliants, the latter patrons.

13. There is also another sword of Goliath, another weapon of the devil, namely the speech of heretics, which the man who knows how to sing psalms seizes in order to overcome the adversary — one who hears of wars here but does not suffer them, not restless, not moved by any wind of doctrine, not knowing the famine of the word and filled with the abundance of heavenly Scripture — one who would not shrink from challenging the heretic as he resounds with empty words. Then let the one who is weak wait, lest by an unequal encounter he create a prejudice against the rest. Let David come, whose mouth Christ opens to declare the mysteries; let that Nazirite come whose hair does not fall, either having nothing superfluous which could fall or being about to lose nothing of his higher virtues, whole in sobriety, strong in peace, one who guards all his senses and his words to the end.

14. Let the Gospel be preached, so that the world may be destroyed. For just as the preaching of the Gospel has gone before into the whole world — the preaching which already both Goths and Armenians have believed, and therefore we see the end of the world — so also the Gospel is preached by the spiritual man, when he pursues all the advances of wisdom and all the virtues with a mind and spirit that sing psalms, destroying death last of all. For then is the end, when Christ shall have delivered up in him the kingdom to God and the Father, and this man shall have been made subject to him who subjected all things to himself, that God may be all in all. And the Gospel will be preached through all the cities, that is, the cities of Judea; known, indeed, in Judea is God. For then the cities of Judea are built, when the foundations of the virtues are laid.

15. When you see Jerusalem surrounded by an army. Truly Jerusalem was besieged and stormed by the Roman army; whence the Jews too thought that the abomination of desolation was then accomplished, because the Romans, mocking the rite of Jewish observance, threw a pig's head into the temple. That I would not say even if I were raving. For the abomination of desolation is the accursed coming of Antichrist, because with ill-omened sacrileges he defiles the inner parts of minds, sitting in the temple according to the historical sense, to claim for himself the throne of divine power. But according to the spiritual interpretation he is fittingly brought in as standing, because he desires to fix the footprint of his unbelief in the affections of each one, arguing from the Scriptures that he is the Christ.

16. Then desolation will draw near, since very many, having fallen away from true religion, will defect through error. Then will be the day of the Lord, which the Apostle too has plainly set forth, saying that we must take heed, as though the day of the Lord were at hand, lest anyone deceive us in any way; for unless the falling away comes first and the man of sin is revealed, the son of perdition, who opposes and exalts himself above all that is called God or that is worshipped, so that he sits in the temple of God, showing himself as though he were God, and the rest.

17. So he will sit in the temple, and in the inner temple of the Jews, who will deny Christ, in a temple not inviolable but subject to corruption, which either the ruin of unbelief entangles, or the force of anger overthrows, or the fire of desires burns up. And well will the day of the Lord come then, and the days will be shortened for the sake of the elect, since, just as the first coming of the Lord was for the redeeming of sins, so the second too is for the repressing of offences, lest more should slip into the error of unbelief.

18. Then false prophets, then famine. Recall to me the times of Elijah, and you will find then prophets of confusion, then Jezebel, then famine, then drought of the land. For what reason? Because iniquity had abounded, charity had grown cold. Indeed the righteous man was in the desert, the wicked on the throne.

19. There is also another antichrist, the author of this one, namely the devil, who strives to besiege with the army of his legion my Jerusalem, my soul, surely the soul of God, the peaceable soul. Ours is not, indeed, a wrestling against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the rulers of this world, of this darkness. Then is the falling away, when the soul defects from itself and, again, trembles and is troubled when it thinks of the Lord. Then, when this antichrist holds, until he be taken out of the midst, righteousness is in exile, iniquity reigns; then faith is rare, as the Lord himself said, as though in doubt: Then, when the Son of Man comes, will he find faith upon the earth? Either, surely, in our own earth or in the whole world. So also elsewhere: The Lord looked down upon the sons of men, to see if there is any that understands or seeks God, not because God doubts, but because faith was so rare among men that, in the opinion of men, there would seem to be room for doubt.

20. So when the devil is in the midst of the temple, there is the abomination of desolation according to the prophet Daniel; but when the spiritual presence of Christ has shone upon each one who labours, the wicked one is taken out of the midst, and righteousness begins to reign, which empties every principality out of the minds of the faithful.

21. There is also a third antichrist, either Arius or Sabellius; indeed, all are antichrists who lead us astray by perverse interpretation. And therefore let him who reads understand; he who understands is not led astray so as to believe false things in place of true, after the manner, surely, of the Jews, who denied the true Christ; whence it will follow that they believe him to be true who is false. So the Arians too will not deny to Antichrist what they deny to Christ.

22. Woe to those who are with child and who give suck in those days! Is conception, then, criminal? Yet children are the rewards of marriage. And how did the Lord bless Sarah, and she bore? How did Hannah pray and give birth? How did Rachel, blessed, receive sons? Did the prophets err, since the Lord could not err? But the Lord spoke in the prophets too, and therefore neither could they err. How then will anyone be able to make the discord of the writings square?

23. But since there is war there, let us turn to the spirit of peace; for Peace has said: A woman, when she is in labour, has sorrow, because her hour has come; but when she has given birth, she no longer remembers the sorrow, and he added: and you now indeed have sorrow, but I will see you again, and your heart will rejoice, to show that it belongs to the perfect to rejoice, and to the weak to waver, as men still afraid of uncertain things. But here too he said above: They were eating and drinking, they were marrying and taking wives, binding themselves, that is, to this life and fettering themselves with worldly cares. These surely are the pregnant women to whom woe is spoken, who stretch out the fat of their flesh and in whom the step of the inner mind grows sluggish, barren of virtues and pregnant with vices.

24. But not even those pregnant women are exempt from condemnation who, set in the labour of good deeds, have not yet yielded any progress in the work they have undertaken. For there are also those who conceive from the fear of God, who say: From your fear we have conceived and been in labour. But not all give birth, not all are perfect, not all can say: We have brought forth the spirit of salvation upon the earth, not all are Marys, to conceive Christ of the Holy Spirit and bring forth the Word. For there are some who cast out the Word as an abortion before they give birth; there are some who have Christ in the womb but have not yet formed him, to whom it is said: My little children, of whom I am in labour again until Christ be formed in you. So those who are still in the womb are being brought forth as imperfect; those are now more perfect to whom it is said: I have begotten you through the Gospel.

25. There are many fathers through the Gospel, and many mothers, who bring forth Christ. Who then will show me the parents of Christ? He himself has shown them, saying: Who is my mother, or who are my brothers? Whoever does the will of my Father who is in heaven, he is my brother and sister and mother. Do the will of the Father, that you may be the mother of Christ. Many have conceived Christ and not brought him forth. So she who brings forth righteousness brings forth Christ, she who brings forth wisdom brings forth Christ, she who is in labour with the Word is in labour with Christ.

26. There is also she who was in labour with injustice and brought forth iniquity. Woe to these pregnant women, who, heavy in body, are the slower to escape danger; woe to these, for whom the pain of the birth to come, by which the whole body is shaken, is still entire: for them the signs of the coming judgment are the beginnings of sorrows.

27. Moses too taught that there is a pregnant woman who, if two men quarrelling trample on her, quickly miscarries. And therefore let the good woman flee strife and follow peace, so that she may complete her childbirth, and let her not wait for the time of nine months: the birth of the Word lies not in the fullness of time but in the fullness of diligence. Made perfect, however, in a short time, the righteous man fulfilled many times. But on the other hand the imperfect soul is quickly trampled and loses the word it has conceived. But woe to him who scandalizes one of these little ones, woe to him who tramples on a pregnant woman! For if he has struck out the pledge while it is still unformed, it is a loss of money; but if it is formed, he shall give soul for soul, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand.

28. But why an eye or a hand, when the aborted child, struck out already formed, has both a hand and an eye? Hence it appears that each one is to be condemned according to the measure in which he did harm. For if anyone, whether a heretic or a schismatic, strikes out a catechumen, as it were, from the womb either of the soul or of the Church in labour, he is punished more lightly; if one of the faithful, more heavily. And therefore one must beware lest by quarrelling with such men you trample on the Church; for avoid foolish, indeed, and undisciplined questions, knowing that they breed quarrels, by which the Church, as if in labour, is harassed by the trampling of the whole body.

29. So quickly fill your belly, that you may the sooner bring forth your offspring. Hear how you are to fill it and with what: With the fruits he says of his mouth a man has filled his belly, and with the fruits of his lips he shall be satisfied.

30. Concerning nursing mothers too, that sublime judgment of Solomon and that dispute of the two women taught me that, even if they have already been freed from the pain of childbirth, they still waver in fear for the pledges that are yet to be made strong, because through the drunkenness of the nurse sleep may creep upon her, the mother may overlay her little one, deny her own, seek another's. But even she who did not kill her son is in peril for her own, because she hangs on the uncertainty of the judgment.

31. So let us too, lest the day either of judgment or of death find us as the parents of imperfect works, make haste to wean our little ones. Isaac, once weaned, was not exposed to his mother's sleep, and therefore Abraham made a great feast when he weaned his son. David, weaned, hopes for the reward of his soul; for he is not the Corinthian who, at the beginning of faith still too weak for stronger food, is given milk to drink, but, strengthened now with solid bread, he had grown up into the fullness of perfect age. So the care of begetting is not enough, unless the power of nursing is at hand.

32. And so in you too, as in Mary, let the Word of God grow, let it advance in wisdom and age. This comes about if you keep all the words of righteousness in your heart and do not wait for the time of old age, but, betrothed in your first age to a righteous husband, quickly conceive wisdom without corruption of your body, quickly bring it forth, quickly nurse it. See for me Paul, persecuting yesterday, believing today, preaching tomorrow.

33. Pray that your flight be not in winter or on the sabbath. Since the Lord will come on the day of judgment, before which fire will burn, and the force of fire is either always the same or scorches more quickly in summer, how does he say that we must pray that our flight be not in winter — unless perhaps because he who flees to the mountains ought not to have to fear the cold and the ice, the storms and the hail of sins, but to wish for the calm of summer light, lest his weak footsteps slip on the slipperiness of the body?

34. Hence that soul too, secure now in her progress, resting now on firm roots, rejoices, saying: Winter is gone, the time of pruning has come; for in winter the wind strips the trees of their beauty, and the harshness of the cold kills the tender leaves into a semblance of death; but in spring the seeds rise again and, as it were, a new summer of greening nature grows up. In spring is the Pasch, when I was saved; in summer is Pentecost, when we celebrate the glory of the resurrection after the likeness of what is to come.

35. We must pray also that the Lord at his coming may not catch you on the sabbath, that is, idle from works. And therefore, according to the law, work day after day, and be fervent in spirit and wakeful in labour. Nor is it without meaning that it is written: On the sabbath you shall humble your soul, and that the people remained seventy years in captivity: then religion was violated, freedom crushed, chastity wounded. So you ought to emigrate from this life at the time when the virtues flourish and the vices are captive, not when the soul is captive, its vigour and virtue lie idle, and the sins of the body hold sway.

36. And there shall be signs in the sun and moon and stars. Both the true sequence of the prophecy and the whole account of the mystery is this: that the Jews too will be led captive a second time into Babylonia and Assyria; those who denied Christ will be captives in the whole world, and the Jerusalem which is seen will be trodden down by a hostile army, the Jews falling by the edge of the sword, and all Judea will be made subject to the nations that are to believe, by the edge of the spiritual sword, which is the two-edged word; and diverse signs will take place in the sun and moon and stars.

37. These signs are expressed more plainly according to Matthew. Then, he says, the sun shall be darkened and the moon shall not give her light and the stars shall fall. For when very many fall away from religion, bright faith will be darkened by the cloud of unbelief, because for me that heavenly Sun is either diminished or increased by my faith. Just as, when very many look at the rays of the sun of this world, the sun seems either paler or brighter according to the capacity of the one who sees, so the spiritual light is poured into each according to the devotion of the believer. And just as the moon, in her monthly courses or by the interposition of the earth when she is opposite the sun, vanishes, so too the holy Church, when the vices of the flesh stand in the way of the heavenly light, cannot borrow the brightness of the divine light from the rays of Christ. For in persecutions it is mostly love of this life alone that shuts out the light of God.

38. The stars shall fall: those, surely, who already gleam with the glory of the resurrection, those men holding the word of life as lights in the world, those men of whom it was said to Abraham that as the heaven and the stars shine, so would his seed also. So patriarchs will fall away from men, prophets will fall away, if the bitterness of persecution grows strong. These things must happen until the fullness of the Church is heaped up in all, and the fullness of the virtues in each; for so the good are proved, so the weak are exposed. So the various surges of minds will be so heavy that, for those of evil conscience through the multitude of their sins, the dew of the sacred fountain in us dries up through fear of the judgment to come; for unbelief is dry, faith is dewy.

39. For the powers in heaven shall be moved, and then they shall see the Son of Man coming in the clouds. Perhaps, just as the advent of the Lord is awaited, so that his presence may come to be in the whole world, whether of men or of the universe — a presence which surely comes about in each one when you have received Christ with all your affections — so also the powers of heaven, at the advent and return of the Lord Savior — for he is the Lord of powers — must obtain an increase of grace and be moved by the fullness of divinity pouring itself more closely into them.

40. There are also powers of the heavens, which declare the glory of God, and which may be moved by a fuller infusion of Christ, spiritual powers, which see Christ. David has taught us how these powers are moved, saying: Come to me and be enlightened. Paul too has taught how you may see Christ: When, indeed, you have turned to the Lord, the veil is taken away, and you see Christ.

41. You see him in the clouds. I do not indeed think that Christ will come in dark gloom and the dreadfulness of rain — for clouds are seen, they veil the sky with a gloomy dread, and how has he set his tabernacle in the sun, if his coming will be rainy? —

42. But there are clouds which, because it must be so, veil the brightness of the heavenly mystery; there are clouds which are moist with the dew of spiritual grace. Look at the cloud in the Old Testament: In the pillar he says of cloud he spoke to them. Through Moses, surely, he spoke, through Joshua son of Nun, who made the sun stand still so that he might receive the brightness of a fuller light. So Moses and Joshua son of Nun are clouds. See that holy men are clouds, who fly like clouds and as doves with their young. Clouds above me are Isaiah, Ezekiel, who show me through the cherubim and seraphim the holiness of the divine Trinity; all are clouds. In these clouds Christ comes; he comes in a cloud in the Song of Songs, in a serene cloud, shining with the joy of the bridegroom; he comes also in a light cloud, incarnate of the Virgin; for the prophet saw as it were a cloud coming from the east. And well did he call it a light cloud, one which earthly vices did not weigh down. See the cloud upon which the Holy Spirit came and which the power of the Most High overshadowed.

43. When therefore Christ has appeared in the clouds, the tribes of the earth shall beat themselves; for there is a certain order of crimes and series of sins which is broken off by the coming of Christ.

44. See the fig tree and all the trees. When they put forth fruit from themselves, you know that summer is near. In a different manner indeed, yet the statements of the evangelists seem to converge into one. For Matthew spoke of the fig tree alone, when its branch has become tender, this one of all the trees. But whether, when fruit grows green on all the trees and the fig tree, now fruitful, blossoms, so that every tongue confesses to God, the people of the Jews confessing too, we ought to hope for the coming of the Lord, at which the fruits of the resurrection will be reaped as in the season of summer; or whether, when the man of iniquity, the branch of the synagogue, has put on a light and fragile boasting like leaves, we ought to infer that the judgment is drawing near: for the Lord hastens both to reward faith and to bring an end to sinning.

45. This fig tree, then, has a twofold figure: either when hard things grow mild or when sins run riot. For through the faith of believers the things which were dry before will blossom, and through the grace of their offences sinners will glory. There is the fruit of faith, here the wantonness of unbelief. The tilling of the Gospel husbandman promises me fruit of the fig tree. We must not despair, if sinners have covered themselves with fig leaves as with a garment of deceit, to veil their conscience; so leaves without fruit are suspect. Such are the garments the exiles of paradise have.

46. Behold, as you enter the city, a man will meet you carrying an amphora of water. It is good to consider where the Lord keeps the Pasch. According to Matthew you have: Go into the city to a certain man. First, mark the majesty of the divinity. He speaks with the disciples and already knows what is going to happen elsewhere. Next, behold his condescension, for it is not the person of a rich or powerful man that is chosen, but a poor man is sought out, and the cramped lodging of a poor man is preferred to the spacious houses of the nobles. Go he says to a certain man. You knew, Lord, his name, whose service you knew; you knew his service, whose meeting you knew; but he is designated without a name, so that he may be reckoned obscure. Nothing here is contrived, where not the person but the cause is expressed. But according to Mark, carrying a flagon of water.

47. This man, then, is commanded to the apostles to be followed. And how is he the master of the house? That you may recognize that the definition is one of holiness, not of wealth. How is it that he has a room spread in the upper part? That you may notice his great merit, in whom the Lord with his disciples would rest, delighting in his lofty virtues. Would that it might be my lot, then, to carry an amphora of water, my lot to carry a flagon of water, which the master of the house carries, who has a large room spread in the upper part. For what is an amphora but a more perfect measure, having no middling measure? Measure, the Lord says, good, shaken together, running over, they will give to you.

48. And of water what shall I say? Above the water, before the very birth of the world, the Holy Spirit, as you read, was borne. O water, which washed the world when it was sprinkled with human blood, so that a figure of the present washings might go before! O water, which merited to be the sacrament of Christ, which washes all things and is not washed! You begin first, you complete the perfect mysteries. From you is the beginning, in you the end, or rather you cause us to know no end. Through you the stench of wasting flesh is done away, and entrails perishing with decay are preserved for a long age, when salt is sprinkled on them. Through you a drink is poured into bodies parched with heat, sweet for delight, saving for life, pleasant for enjoyment; you gave a name to the prophets and apostles, you gave a name to the Savior — they are clouds of heaven, they the salt of the world, he is the fountain of life —; you who, pressed by mountains, are not shut in; dashed upon rocks, are not broken; poured into the earth, do not fail; but, welling up from the inmost veins, either, enclosed, you breathe forth the vital spirit, or, spread abroad, you supply fertile moisture, or, poured over, you furnish fitting irrigation, lest the earth, its vital parts dried up and lifeless, should deny its yearly harvests. The substance of all the elements, heaven, air, sea, earth, give you back. You the rock poured forth, struck by the prophet's touch, to water the hearts of thirsting peoples; you, when you burst forth from the side of the Savior, those who struck him saw, and they believed; and therefore you are one of the three witnesses of our regeneration; three, indeed, are the witnesses, the water, the blood and the spirit: the water for the washing, the blood for the price, the spirit for the resurrection.

49. And I appoint to you, as my Father appointed to me, a kingdom. The kingdom of God is not of this world. So man's emulation toward God is not one of equality, but of likeness; for Christ alone is the full image of God, because of the unity of the Father's brightness expressed in him; but the righteous man is after the image of God if, for the sake of imitating the likeness of the divine way of life, he despises this world through the knowledge of God and scorns earthly pleasures through receiving the Word, by which we are nourished unto life. Hence too we eat the body of Christ, that we may be partakers of eternal life. For it is not food and drink that is promised us by way of reward and honour, but the communion of heavenly grace and life; nor are the twelve thrones some sort of receptacles for bodily sitting; but since Christ, according to the divine likeness, judges by knowledge of hearts, not by questioning about deeds, rewarding virtue and condemning impiety, so the apostles too are formed for a spiritual judgment, by rewarding faith and by execrating unbelief, refuting error by virtue, pursuing the sacrilegious with hatred.

50. Let us turn, then, and beware lest there be among us, to our ruin, any contention about precedence; for if the apostles contended, it is not held out as an excuse, but set before us as a warning. If Peter is converted at length, for having followed the first call of the Lord, who can say that he himself has been converted quickly? Beware, then, of boasting, beware of the world; for he is commanded to strengthen his brothers who said: We have left all things and followed you.

51. Next, it must be observed that humility is not defined by every eagerness to show honour; for you can defer to someone for the sake of worldly favour, out of fear of power and regard for advantage. Your edification is sought, not another's honour, and therefore one form of sentence is given to all, so that there may be no boasting about precedence, but a contention about humility; and for this the Lord sets himself forth to be imitated. We had need of all things, he of nothing, and yet he showed himself the author of humility when he served his disciples. This he surely did not out of eagerness for advantage, but as an exercise of virtue.

52. But Peter, although ready in spirit, yet still weak in the affection of the body, is told beforehand that he will deny the Lord; for he could not equal the constancy of the divine purpose. The Passion of the Lord has rivals; it has no equals. So I do not reproach him for denying; I praise him for weeping. The one belongs to the common condition, the other is peculiar to virtue. And therefore he is instructed to take care, not compelled to deny.

53. He who has he says a purse, let him take it, and likewise a wallet, and he who has not, let him sell his tunic and buy himself a sword. Why do you command me to buy, you who forbid me to strike? Why do you prescribe that I have what you forbid to be drawn? Unless perhaps so that there may be a defence ready, not a vengeance made necessary, so that you may be seen to have been able to avenge yourself, but to have been unwilling. Yet the Law does not forbid me to strike back, and therefore perhaps, to Peter offering two swords, you say It is enough, as though it had been permitted up to the Gospel, so that in the Law there might be instruction in equity, in the Gospel the perfection of goodness.

54. To many this seems unjust, but the Lord is not unjust, who, though he could avenge himself, preferred to be sacrificed. There is also a spiritual sword, so that you may sell your patrimony and buy the Word, with which the inner recesses of the mind are clothed. There is also the sword of the Passion, so that you may put off the body and, with the cast-off covering of the sacrificed flesh, the crown of sacred martyrdom may be bought for you — as you can gather from the Lord's beatitudes, for he proclaimed the highest crown of all, if anyone suffers persecution for righteousness. Indeed, so that you may know that he spoke of the Passion, lest he should trouble the minds of the disciples, he brought forward an example from himself, saying:

55. that this which is written must be fulfilled in me, that he was also reckoned among the unjust. Yet it still troubles us that the disciples brought out two swords: perhaps one of the New, one of the Old Testament, with which we are armed against the snares of the devil. Indeed the Lord says: It is enough, as though nothing were lacking to him whom the teaching of both Testaments has fortified.

56. Father, if it is possible, take this cup from me. Many stick fast at this place, who bend the sadness of the Savior into an argument for a weakness ingrown from the beginning rather than one taken on for a time, and want to twist the sense of the natural meaning. But I not only do not think it needs excusing; nowhere, indeed, do I more admire his dutiful love and his majesty; for he would have conferred less on me, had he not taken on my affection. So he grieved for me, who had nothing for himself to grieve over, and, setting aside the delight of eternal divinity, he is touched by the weariness of my weakness. For he took on my sadness, to bestow his joy on me, and in our footsteps he came down even to the distress of death, to call us back to life in his footsteps. Confidently, then, I name sadness, because I preach the cross; for he took on not the appearance of the Incarnation, but its truth. He had therefore to take on grief too, that he might conquer sadness, not shut it out. For those do not have the praise of fortitude who have borne the numbness rather than the pain of wounds; for a man, indeed, under the stroke and knowing he says how to bear infirmities.

57. He wished to teach us, so that, since in Joseph we had learned not to fear prison, we might learn in Christ to conquer death and, what is more, how we might conquer the sorrow of death to come. For how should we imitate you, Lord Jesus, unless we followed you as a man, unless we believed you dead, unless we had seen your wounds? How would the disciples believe that he had been going to die, had they not learned of the sadness of one about to die? So they still sleep and do not know how to grieve, they for whom Christ grieved; for so we read that he bears our sins and grieves for us. You grieve, then, Lord, not for your own wounds but for mine, not for your own death but for our weakness: and we reckoned you to be in sorrows, when you grieved not for yourself but for me; for you were made weak, but because of our sins, not because that weakness was yours, taken from the Father, but because it was taken on for me, since it profited me that the instruction of our peace should be in you and that by your bruise you should heal our wounds.

58. But what wonder if he grieved for all who wept for one? What wonder if, about to die for all, he is weary, who sheds tears when about to raise Lazarus? But there he is moved by the tears of a loving sister, because human things touched his mind; and here he works with a deep affection, so that, since in his flesh he was destroying our sins, the grief of his soul might also abolish the grief of our soul. And perhaps he is sad for this reason, that after the fall of Adam we had to depart from this world by such a passage that it was necessary to die: God, indeed, did not make death, nor does he rejoice in the perdition of the living, and therefore he loathes what he himself did not make.

59. Indeed he says: Take this cup from me, as a man refusing death, as God keeping his own sentence; for we must die to the world, that we may rise to God, so that, according to the divine sentence, the law of the curse may be undone when the end of our nature has been dissolved into the clay of the earth.

60. But when he says: Not my will, but yours be done, he referred his own will to the man, the Father's to the divinity; for the will of man is temporal, the will of the divinity eternal. The will of the Father is not one, the will of the Son another; for there is one will where there is one divinity. Learn, however, to be subject to God, so that you choose not what you yourself want, but what you know will please God.

61. Next let us consider the proper sense of the words themselves: My soul is sad, and elsewhere: Now my soul is greatly troubled — so it is not the one taking up, but the one taken up that is troubled; for the soul is subject to passions, the divinity is free — and then: The spirit is ready, but the flesh is weak.
