Now it is not he himself who is sad, but the soul. Wisdom is not sad, nor the divine substance, but the soul. He took up my body; he did not deceive me, so as to be one thing and seem another: he seemed sad and he was sad, not for his own Passion, but for our scattering. Indeed he says: I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock will be scattered. He was sad because he was leaving us little ones behind. For the rest, how steadfastly he offered himself to death Scripture declares, since he went to meet those seeking him, strengthened the troubled, roused the fearful, received the traitor with the condescension of a kiss.

62. Nor is this far from the truth, that he was sad for his persecutors, whom he knew would pay the penalties of sacrilege in evils. And therefore he said: Take this cup from me, not because the Son of God feared death, but because he did not wish even the wicked to perish. Indeed he says: Lord, do not lay this sin against them, that his Passion might be saving for all.

63. Judas, do you betray the Son of Man with a kiss? A great indication of divine power, a great lesson in virtue. Both the plan of the betrayal is laid open and yet patience is not withheld. You showed, Lord, whom he was betraying, while you make hidden things manifest. You showed also whom he was handing over, when you say: the Son of Man, because it is the flesh, not the divinity, that is seized. Yet this convicts the ungrateful man the more, that he handed over him who, though he was the Son of God, had nevertheless willed for our sake to be the son of man, as if he were saying: For your sake, ungrateful man, I took on what you hand over. Behold the hypocrisy! So I think it should be pronounced as a question, as though he were rebuking the traitor with the affection of one who loves: Judas, do you betray the Son of Man with a kiss? That is: do you inflict a wound with the pledge of love, and shed blood with the office of charity, and bring death with the instrument of peace? Do you, a servant, betray your master, a disciple your teacher, a chosen one your maker? This is that saying: Useful are the wounds of a friend rather than the willing kisses of an enemy. This is said to the traitor; but what is said to the peaceable one?

64. Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth. And he kissed him, not to teach us to dissemble, but so that he might not seem to shrink from the betrayal, and might move the traitor the more by not denying him the offices of love; for it is written: With those who hated peace I was peaceable.

65. And when the sign had been given, he says, he is seized by those who had come with clubs. But it was mysteries, not arms, that held the Lord of all. Indeed he spoke, and they fell backward. What need have I of legions of angels, what need of the heavenly host? The voice of the Lord alone terrifies more. This, to show a token of the divine majesty, he chose who reclined on the breast of Christ. So the crowd lays hands on him, though he is willing; chains are bound on. O madmen, O unbelievers! Not thus is wisdom seized, not thus is righteousness held.

66. Nor was the zeal of the disciples lacking. Indeed Peter, learned in the Law, ready in affection, who knew that it had been reckoned to Phinehas for righteousness that he had slain the sacrilegious, struck the servant of the prince. But the Lord turned aside the bloody wounds and set divine mysteries in their place, so that the servant of the prince of the world — that is, a slave of the worldly powers, not by the condition of nature but by guilt — received the wound of his ear, because he had not heard the words of wisdom. Everyone, next, who commits sin is the slave of sin. By your sins he says you have been sold. The sale was because of our sins, but the redemption of sinners because of the goodness of God. Or, if Peter struck the ear deliberately, he taught that they ought not to have an ear in appearance which they did not have in mystery. But the good Lord restores hearing to him too, showing, according to the prophetic words, that even those who were wounded in the Passion of the Lord can be healed if they turn, because every sin is washed away by the mysteries of faith.

67. So Peter takes off the ear. Why Peter? Because he it is who received the keys of the kingdom of heaven; for he condemns who also absolves, since he has obtained the same power of binding and of loosing. He takes off the ear of the one who hears ill; he takes off with the spiritual sword the inner ear of the one who understands ill.

68. Let us beware lest anyone's ear be taken off. The Passion of the Lord is read: if we refer the weakness of the bodily Passion to his divinity, the ear is cut off, and cut off by Peter, who did not allow Christ to be reckoned a prophet, but taught that he is to be marked out by a faithful confession as the Son of God. So when we read that Jesus is held, let us beware lest we hear it from anyone and think that he is held according to his divinity, held against his will, held as one weak. He is held indeed and, as John said, bound according to the truth of the body; but woe to those who bind the Word! For they bind him who think Christ only a man; they bind him who do not think him foreknowing, who do not confess him almighty. Evil are the bonds of the Jews, with which they bind not Christ but themselves. He is bound, moreover, not in the house of some devout and righteous man, but in the house of Caiaphas, that is, in an impious house, where it is even prophesied that he will die for all. How mad, then, are they who confess the benefits and persecute the author of the benefits!

69. They lose the ear, therefore, because they have lost the profit of hearing. Many who think they have it do not have it. In the Church all have it; outside the Church they do not have it. Or perhaps he took away the ear lest, hearing, they should sin the more, since they could not keep what they heard. So too once the Lord confounded the tongues of those building the tower, lest they should hear one another and increase the work of their impiety.

70. Grasp, if you can, how at the touch of the saving right hand pain flees and wounds are healed, not sprinkled with medicine but closed over by a touch. The clay recognizes its worker, and the flesh follows the hand of the Lord, its worker; for the Creator restores his work as he wills. So too elsewhere sight is re-formed for that blind man by clay smeared upon his eyes, as if he returned to his nature. He could have commanded, but he preferred to work, so that we might know that he is the one who from the clay of the earth formed the members of our body, fitted for various offices, and gave them life by infusing the vigour of the mind.

71. So they came and seized him, to perish the more grievously by the fruit of their own zeal; and the unhappy men neither understood the mystery nor revered so merciful an affection of dutiful love, in that he did not allow even his enemies to be wounded. They were bringing death upon the Just One; he was healing the wounds of his persecutors.

72. But Peter followed afar off. And rightly did he follow afar off, being already near to denying; for he could not have denied, had he clung close to Christ. But perhaps in this too he ought to be revered by us with the greatest wonder, that he did not leave the Lord, even when he was afraid. Fear belongs to nature, concern to dutiful love. That he fears is foreign to him; that he does not flee is his own. That he follows belongs to devotion; that he denies, to being taken unawares. That he slips is common; that he repents belongs to faith. Already a fire was burning in the house of the chief of the priests; Peter came near to warm himself, because, with the Lord shut in, the warmth of the mind had grown cold even in him.

73. What does it mean that a maidservant first betrays him, though men surely could have recognized him more readily, unless that this sex too should be seen to have sinned unto the death of the Lord, so that this sex too might be redeemed through the Passion of the Lord? And therefore a woman first received the mystery of the resurrection and keeps the commandments, to abolish the old error of transgression.

74. Peter, then, when betrayed, denies — for let us suppose that Peter denied, because the Lord said: You will deny me three times, and I would rather that Peter denied than that the Lord was mistaken —; what did he deny? What he had promised incautiously. He considered his devotion, he did not look to his condition, and he was punished, because he said he would lay down his life, which belongs not to human weakness but to divine power. Since he paid so heavy a fine for an imprudent word, how great is the penalty of unbelief!

75. Where, though, does Peter deny? Not on the mountain, not in the temple, not in his own house, but in the praetorium of the Jews, in the house of the chief of the priests. There he denies, where truth is not; there he denies, where Christ was seized, where Jesus was bound. Why should he not go astray, whom the doorkeeper let in, and the doorkeeper of the Jews questioned? Wrongly did Eve lead Adam on, wrongly did a woman bring Peter in; but the one falls in paradise, where the fault was not pardonable, the other in the praetorium of the Jews, where innocence was hard. To the one the fall was forbidden, to this one the error was foretold. That fall, once committed, prejudged the case for this one; this error undid that one.

76. Let us consider also in what state he denies. It was cold. If we consider the season, it could not have been cold; but it was cold where Jesus was not recognized, where there was no one who saw the light, where the consuming fire was denied. The cold, then, was of the mind, not of the body. Indeed Peter stood at the coals, because he was chilled in affection. Evil is the Jewish flame: it burns, it does not warm. Evil is the hearth which sprinkles a kind of soot of error even on the minds of saints, beside which even Peter's inner eyes grew dim — those eyes not of flesh and blood, but the eyes of the mind, with which he saw Christ.

77. Someone says: Do you condemn even the elements of the Jews? I do not condemn the elements, because they are not the Jews', but it is another flame I condemn, the flame of unbelief. This flame of the Jews I condemn, following the divine oracles; for the Lord says: your silver is reprobate. If the silver of the Jews is reprobate, the hearth of the Jews too is reprobate. Indeed with the hearth and the gold of the Jews the head of a calf was fashioned, that is, the sovereignty of sacrilege.

78. But let us look at the course of the denial, which I see is different among the evangelists. So new a thing was it that Peter could sin that his sin could not be grasped even by the evangelists. And so Matthew put it that Peter, when the maidservant betrayed him as one of those who were with Jesus the Galilean, answered at his first utterance: I do not know what you say. This Mark too has, who followed Peter and could learn it more truly from Peter himself. The first utterance of Peter as he denies is one by which, however, he seems not to deny the Lord, but to have separated himself from the woman's betrayal.

79. Yet consider what he denied. That he was one, surely, of those who were with Jesus the Galilean, or, as Mark put it, with Jesus the Nazarene. Did he deny that he had been with the Son of God? It is as if to say: I do not know the Galilean, I do not know the Nazarene, whom I know as the Son of God. Let men have the names of places; the Son of God cannot be named from a homeland, whose majesty no place encloses. And so that you may know this is true, it is also proved by an example; for elsewhere, when the Lord asked the disciples: Who do men say that I am, the Son of Man? some said Elijah, others Jeremiah or one of the prophets, but Peter said: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. Did he deny there too, because he chose to confess Christ not as the son of man but as the Son of God? Surely, what do we think ambiguous here, which Christ himself approved? Take another point too.

80. For Peter, when asked: And are you one of those who were with Jesus the Galilean? shrinks from the word of eternity — for they 'were' not, who began to be — that is to say: he alone was who was in the beginning.

81. Indeed he says: I am not; for to be belongs to him who always is. Hence Moses too says: He who is has sent me. Again, when he was pressed as being one of them, according to Mark he denied, so that you may know that the evangelist gave more to truth than to favour; but nevertheless he denied that he was one of them, he did not deny Christ. He may have denied the fellowship of men, not the grace of God. He may have denied that he was one of those who were with the Galilean; he did not deny that he was with the Son of God.

82. Indeed, according to Matthew, when betrayed as having been with Jesus the Nazarene, he says: I do not know the man. This same thing at the third utterance too both evangelists whom we have set forth recorded: that he answered with an oath that he did not know the man. And rightly did he deny the man, whom he knew as God. Indeed, where there is an oath, the answer is cautious. For although Peter denied, yet he did not swear falsely, because the Lord had not said that he would swear falsely. But if it is doubtful in Peter, how dangerous an oath is!

83. John, however, put it thus: that Peter, asked by the maidservant whether he was one of the disciples of that man, answered at his first utterance: I am not; for he was not the apostle of a man, he who was Christ's. Indeed Paul too denied that he was the apostle of a man, saying: Paul an apostle, not from men nor through man, but through Jesus Christ and God the Father. But lest he should seem to bring in any ambiguity about the Incarnation, he added: who raised him from the dead, so that you may believe him man too, when you have first believed him God. This he keeps elsewhere also in the same tenor, where he says: For there is one God and one mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus. He named him mediator of God, surely, before he named him mediator of men; for it is not enough to believe both, unless the order of faith is kept.

84. The answer, then, agrees everywhere; for it was fitting that he who said: I do not know the man, when asked whether he was one of the disciples of the man, should say: I am not. And so he did not deny that he was a disciple of Christ, but denied that he was a disciple of a man. And so both Peter and Paul denied the man whom they confessed to be the Son of God. What Peter perceived, Paul expressed; from this one the other too made progress. The error of Peter is the teaching of the righteous, and the stumbling of Peter is the rock of all. Indeed he stumbles upon the waves, but stretches out his right hand to Christ; he falls on the mountain, but is raised by Christ; the same Peter stumbled on the sea, but he walked. The stumbling of Peter is firmer than our firmness. He falls there where no one climbs; he totters there where no one walks. And yet among the waves, though he stumbles, he does not slip; he totters, he does not fall; he is tossed, he is not cast headlong. And if he fell, yet he fell on the mountain; but he fell more happily than others stood, more happily he fell, whom Christ raised up.

85. Again, John wrote that when asked a second time whether he was one of his disciples, he denied. And rightly did he deny, because he was said to be one of the disciples of him whom they had spoken of above as a man. For the third time too he denied that he had been seen with him; this too is derived from what precedes: I have not been with him whom you call a man, but I have not departed from the Son of God.

86. Luke also wrote that Peter, asked whether he was one of them, answered at his first utterance: I do not know him. And rightly he spoke. For it would have been rash for him to say that he knew him whom the human mind cannot comprehend; no one, indeed, knows the Son except the Father. Again, at the second utterance according to Luke, the same Peter says: I am not; he preferred, that is, to deny himself rather than Christ. Or, because he seemed to deny fellowship with Christ, he surely denied himself. Certainly, when he denies concerning a man, he sinned against the Son of Man, so that it might be forgiven him, not against the Holy Spirit. Asked a third time too, he says: I do not know what you say, that is: I do not know your sacrileges.

87. But we excuse him; he himself did not excuse himself; for what suffices in one who confesses Jesus is not an involved answer, but an open confession. What profit is it to wrap up words, if you want to seem to have denied? And therefore Peter is not represented as having answered thus by design, because afterwards he remembered, and yet he wept. For he preferred himself to accuse his own sin, that he might be justified by confessing rather than burdened by denying — the righteous man, indeed, is in the beginning the accuser of himself — and therefore he wept.

88. Why did he weep? Because fault crept upon him. I am wont to weep if fault is lacking to me, that is, if I do not avenge myself, if I do not obtain what I wickedly desire; Peter grieved and wept because he erred as a man. I do not find what he said; I find that he wept. I read of his tears; I do not read of his satisfaction; but what cannot be defended can be washed away. Let tears wash away the offence which it is a shame to confess by voice. Weeping provides both for pardon and for modesty. Tears speak the fault without horror; tears confess the crime without offence to modesty; tears do not ask for pardon, and they merit it. I have found why Peter was silent: lest so quick a request for pardon should offend the more. First one must weep, then pray.

89. Good are the tears which wash away fault. Indeed those upon whom Jesus looks weep. Peter denied the first time and did not weep, because the Lord had not looked at him. He denied a second time: he did not weep, because the Lord had not yet looked at him. He denied a third time too: Jesus looked at him, and he wept most bitterly. Look on us, Lord Jesus, that we may know how to weep for our sin. Hence even the fall of the saints is useful. It did me no harm that Peter denied; it profited me that he amended. I have learned to beware the conversation of unbelievers. Peter denied among the Jews; Solomon, deceived by companionship with Gentiles, went astray.

90. So Peter wept, and most bitterly; he wept so that he might wash away his offence with tears. You too, if you want to merit pardon, wash away your fault with tears; at the same moment, at the same time, Christ looks upon you. If perhaps you slip in anything, since he stands by as witness of your secrets, he looks upon you that you may remember and confess your error. Imitate Peter, who says elsewhere a third time: Lord, you know that I love you. For since he had denied three times, he confesses three times; but he denied in the night, he confesses in the day.

91. These things were written for this reason, that we may know that no one ought to boast; for if Peter fell because he said: Even if others are scandalized, I will not be scandalized, who else may rightly presume upon himself? Indeed David too, because he had said: I said in my abundance: I shall not be moved for ever, declares that this boasting harmed him, saying: You turned away your face from me, and I was troubled.

92. From where shall I call you forth, Peter, to teach me what you thought as you wept? From where, I say, shall I call you forth? From heaven, where you have already been placed in the choir of the angels, or even from the tomb, since you do not think it an injury to be there too, whence the Lord rose again? Teach us what your tears profited you. But you taught us at once; for you who fell before you wept, after you wept were chosen to rule others, you who before had not ruled yourself.

93. Peter, then, had tears, which he shed with dutiful affection; the traitor had no weeping with which to wash away his fault, but torments of conscience, by which he confessed his sacrilege, so that, while the guilty man is condemned by his own judgment and the crime is expiated by a self-inflicted punishment, there might be proved both the dutiful love of the Lord, who would not avenge himself, and his divinity, who questioned the conscience of the mind by an invisible power.

94. I have sinned he says, in that I have betrayed righteous blood. Although the repentance of the traitor is vain, because he sinned against the Holy Spirit, yet to acknowledge the fault is some shame in crime. And although he is not absolved, yet the shamelessness of the Jews is refuted. Though the profession of the seller convicts them, the wicked men nevertheless claim for themselves the rights of the contract and believe themselves exempt from guilt, when they say: What is that to us? You see to it. Plainly mad, who think themselves released rather than bound by the crime of its author. In money cases the obligation is dissolved when the price is refunded: these men take back the price and pursue the sacrilege, and with obstinate zeal claim for themselves the deadly sale of blood, though the seller was refunding the wages of sacrilege.

95. Plainly, therefore, while the price of blood is kept apart from the treasuries of the Jews, and the potter's field is bought with the money for which Christ is sold, while that place is dedicated to burying the remains of strangers, both the oracle of prophecy is fulfilled and the mystery of the rising Church is revealed. For the field, according to the divine precepts, is this whole world, and the potter is he who formed us from the clay, of whom you have in the Old Testament that God fashioned man from the clay of the earth, having power at his own will to form through nature and to re-form through grace. For even if we fall in ruin through our own vices, yet through his mercy, when the spirit of the mind has been taken up again, we are re-formed according to the oracles of Jeremiah.

96. The price of blood is also the price of the Lord's Passion. So by the price of blood the world is bought by Christ; for he came that the world might be saved through him, the world in which there is alike both the work and the right of the author. He came, then, to keep for the grace of perpetuity those buried together with Christ through baptism and dead together with him. But there is not a place of burial for all indiscriminately; for although the world encloses all, it does not keep all. Although the dwelling is common, yet the lawful burial is that of those, namely, who are now through faith of the household of God, but were strangers in the Law. Who are these, unless those of whom it is said: Remember that once you were Gentiles, who were in the flesh alienated from the way of life of Israel and strangers to the promise of the testaments? But these now are no longer sojourners and strangers, since they have merited by right of faith to be fellow citizens of the saints.

97. There follows an admirable passage, by which a moral patience for bearing injuries with equanimity is poured into human hearts. The Lord is accused and is silent. And well is he silent who has no need of a defense: let those seek to be defended who fear to be defeated. So he does not confirm the accusation by being silent, but despises it by not refuting it. For what should he fear, who did not seek his own safety? The Salvation of all gives up his own, that he may gain that of all. But why should I speak of God? Susanna was silent and conquered; for that cause is the better which is not defended and is proved. And here Pilate acquits, but he acquits by his judgment, he crucifies by a mystery. But this is proper to Christ, and that is human: that before unjust judges he should seem rather to have been unwilling than unable to be defended.

98. Why the Lord was silent he himself said beforehand, saying: If I tell you, you do not believe me; if I ask you, you will not answer me. Yet this was most excellent, that he chose to prove himself a king rather than to say so, so that those who confess the very thing they charge against him could have no ground for condemning him.

99. But when Herod desired to see some wonders from him, he was silent and did nothing, because neither did that man's cruelty deserve to see divine things, and the Lord was avoiding display. And perhaps in Herod all the ungodly are signified as in a type, who, if they have not believed the Law and the prophets, cannot see the wonderful works of Christ in the Gospel either.

100. He is sent to Herod, he is sent back to Pilate. Although neither pronounces him guilty, yet they comply with the zeal of another's cruelty. Pilate indeed washed his hands, but he did not wash away his deeds; for a judge ought to yield neither to ill will nor to fear, so as to hand over innocent blood. His wife warned him, grace shone in the night, the divinity stood out: not even so did he refrain from a sacrilegious sentence.

101. Likewise in this I think a type was set forth beforehand of all judges who would condemn those whom they judged innocent. Yet the person joined to Pilate shows that the Gentiles are more tolerable than the Jews, and that they can more readily be moved to faith by divine works. But what manner of men were those who crucified the Lord of majesty!

102. Not without reason do they ask for the acquittal of a murderer, who were demanding the death of the innocent. Iniquity has such laws that it hates innocence and loves crime. Yet in this the interpretation of the name gives the appearance of a figure; for Barabbas in Latin means son of the father. Those, therefore, to whom it is said: You are of your father the devil, are revealed as about to prefer to the true Son of God the son of their own father, the Antichrist.

103. And having clothed him in a white garment he sent him back. It is not without meaning that he is clothed by Herod in a white garment, which gave signs of a spotless Passion, because the Lamb of God without spot would take up the sins of the world with glory. In the type of Herod and Pilate too, who from enemies were made friends through Jesus Christ, a figure of the people of Israel and of the Gentile people is preserved: that through the Lord's Passion there would be concord between both, yet in such a way that the people of the nations first receives the word of God and by the devotion of its faith passes it on to the people of the Jews, so that they too may clothe with the glory of his majesty the body of Christ, whom they had before despised.

104. He is clothed by the soldiers in a scarlet cloak and a purple tunic, in the one signifying the palms of the martyrs, in the other the insignia of royal power, because his flesh was to take up for us the blood shed over the whole world, and his Passion was to bring forth a kingdom out of us.

105. The crown of thorns too, fastened on his head, what else does it show but the gift of the divine work, that from the sinners of the world, as from the thorns of this age, a triumphal glory would be sought for God? Nor are the scourges without meaning, because he himself was scourged, that we might not be scourged; for a man under the stroke, and knowing how to bear infirmities, grieves for us, turning the scourges away from us, who before were fleeing from God, a Lord so patient that he offered his own hands to the bonds of fugitives, his own body to the scourges of fugitives. So, with a detestable disposition of mind, the Jews nevertheless reveal an honored death; for even while piercing they crown, while mocking they worship. Although they do not believe in their heart, they confess him whom they kill. They may have lacked the affection of a good work, yet God did not lack his honor, who is saluted as king, crowned as victor, worshiped as God and Lord.

106. The reed too, either, according to Matthew, is grasped in his hand, that human frailty may no longer be moved like a reed by the wind, but, strengthened by the works of Christ, may be firmly grounded, and that, with the handwriting that was against us nailed to the cross, the ancient sentence might cease; or, according to Mark, it strikes his head, that our condition, made firm by the touch of the divinity, may be unable to totter.

107. But now let the victor raise up his trophy. The cross is placed upon his shoulders as a trophy, which, whether Simon carried it or he himself, both Christ carried in the man and the man carried in Christ. Nor do the accounts of the evangelists disagree, when the mystery agrees; and it is a good order for our progress, that he himself should first raise the trophy of his cross, then hand it over to the martyrs to be raised. It is not a Jew who carries the cross, but a foreigner and a stranger, and he does not go before but follows, according to what is written: Take up your cross and follow me. For Christ ascended not his own cross, but ours. Nor was that the death of the divinity, but as of a man. Whence he himself says: God, my God, look upon me! Why have you forsaken me?

108. Beautifully, when about to ascend the cross, he laid aside his royal garments, that you may know that he suffered as man, not as God the King; although Christ is both, yet he was fastened to the cross as man, not as God. But the soldiers, not the Jews, know at what time what garments befit Christ. At the judgment he stands as a victor; to the Passion he comes as a humble defendant.

109. Now, since we have already seen the trophy, let the one who triumphs mount his chariot, and let him hang, not on tree trunks or four-horse wagons the spoils won from a mortal enemy, but on the triumphal gibbet the captive spoils taken from the world. Here we do not see nations with their arms bound behind their backs, nor images of razed cities and likenesses of captured towns, nor do we marvel at the bowed necks of captive kings, such as the spectacle of human triumphs is wont to be, nor the bounds of victory marked off by the limit of a region; but rejoicing peoples of the nations, won not for punishment but for reward, kings worshiping with free affection, cities surrendered by willing zeal, and images of towns reformed for the better, which no paint has portrayed but the devotion of faith has colored, arms and the rights of victories running through the whole world, the prince of the world a captive, and the spiritual hosts of wickedness that are in the heavenly places obedient to the command of a human voice, dominions subdued and the forms of various powers, shining not with silks but with virtuous conduct. Chastity gleams, faith shines, and devotion of fortitude, clad in the spoils of death, now rises again. One triumph of God, the cross of the Lord, has made almost all men now triumph.

110. It matters, therefore, to consider in what state he ascends. I see him naked: so let him who is preparing to conquer the world ascend thus, that he may not seek the aids of the world. Adam was conquered, who sought garments; he conquered who laid aside coverings. And he ascended such as nature formed us, God being its author. Such had the first man dwelt in paradise; such did the second man enter into paradise. And that he might conquer not for himself alone but for all, he stretched out his hands, to draw all things to himself, that he might join to heavenly things those which before were earthly, freed from the knot of death and hung upon the yoke of faith.

111. The title too is written. A procession is wont to go before victors; but before the Lord's triumphal chariot went the goodly procession of the dead rising again. A title too is wont to show the number of subjugated nations. Let there be in those triumphs, set out in a certain order, the pitiable and humiliating captivity of conquered and perishing nations; here the grace of redeemed nations blooms. Worthy of so great a triumph is the team that draws it, so that heaven, earth, seas and the underworld are changed from corruption to grace.

112. The title is written and placed above the cross, not below the cross, because the beginning is upon his shoulders. And what is the beginning but his everlasting power and divinity? Whence he himself too, when asked: Who are you? answered: The Beginning, who also am speaking to you. Let us read this title. Jesus it says the Nazarene, King of the Jews.

113. Rightly is the title above the cross, because the kingdom which Christ has is not of a human body but of divine power. Rightly is the title above the cross, because although the Lord Jesus was on the cross, yet above the cross he shone with the majesty of a king. A worm on the cross, a beetle on the cross. And a good worm, which clung to the wood; a good beetle, which cried out from the wood. What did he cry out? Lord, do not lay this sin against them. He cried out to the thief: Today you will be with me in paradise; he cried out like a beetle: God, my God, look upon me! Why have you forsaken me? And a good beetle, which rolled the mud of our body, before shapeless and sluggish, with the footsteps of the virtues; a good beetle, which raises up the poor man from the dung. He raised up Paul, who was counted as dung; he raised up Job too, who sat on the dung.

114. Not slight, therefore, is the title. And the place of the cross itself is either in the middle, that it might be conspicuous to all, or above the burial place of Adam, as the Hebrews argue. For it was fitting that the first fruits of our life should be placed there where the beginnings of death had been.

115. The garments are divided; one thing falls to one man by lot, another to another; for the Spirit of God is not grasped by human opinion, but glides in as by a certain unexpected chance. And perhaps these soldiers bear the type of the four evangelists, who wrote down the title, which we may all read. I read the title of the King of the Jews when I read: My kingdom is not of this world; I read the charge against Christ written above his head when I read: And the Word was God; for the head of Christ is God.

116. These, then, were guarding Jesus, and they guard him today, lest perhaps he should slip away from anyone, lest for anyone he should come down from the Passion, as the people of the Jews was asking. For me, plainly, let Christ die in the Passion, that after the Passion he may rise again. He would not come down for himself, that he might die for me. For us, therefore, Christ is now kept; for our sake his garments are divided. Individuals cannot have everything, and therefore lots are cast over the tunic, because the division of the Holy Spirit is not made according to the will of man; there are divisions indeed of graces, but the Spirit has worked, dividing to each as he wills.

117. Look now at the garments of Christ divided. Where shall I seek them? Seek in Matthew: you will find with him alone the scarlet cloak, with John the purple garment, with Mark the purple only, with Luke the white garment; for with this alone was he content as his portion. How many, then, has Christ clothed with his garment! But I think that he clothed not four only, but all the soldiers, and abounded for all. But let us return to the evangelists.

118. These four parts, then, seem to me to be not parts of garments but qualities of virtues. For one wrote more loftily of the kingdom, another more fully of the formation of man. Luke chose for himself the splendor of the priestly garment; Mark did not want a woven texture of words; finally John wove in, as it were, certain sentences with which to clothe our faith. Does not that seem to you woven in: In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. This was in the beginning with God. All things were made through him? But Mark, as if content with the brilliance of the purple, without any weaving in of words: The beginning he says of the Gospel of Jesus Christ, the Son of God.

119. Therefore the garments of Christ that were divided are either his deeds or his grace, because the tunic could not be divided, that is, faith, since it belongs not to the portion of individuals but by right is common to all; for what is not divided among individuals remains whole.

120. And well woven from above; for so is the faith of Christ woven, that it comes down from divine things to human things, in that he who was born of God before the ages took up in a later time the assumption of flesh. It is signified to us, therefore, by this that faith ought not to be torn but to remain solid.

121. Amen, amen I say to you, today you will be with me in paradise. A most beautiful example of the conversion we should strive after, that pardon is granted so quickly to the thief and grace is more abundant than the prayer; for the Lord always grants more than he is asked. For that man asked that the Lord should remember him when he came into his kingdom, but the Lord says: Amen, amen I say to you, today you will be with me in paradise; for life is to be with Christ, because where Christ is, there is the kingdom.

122. Quickly, therefore, the Lord forgives, because quickly that man is converted. Whence that too seems to be resolved, that the others bring in two thieves reviling, this one brings in one reviling, one asking. Perhaps this one too reviled at first, but was suddenly converted. Nor is it any wonder if he forgave the guilt of one converted, who granted pardon to those who insulted him. He could also have spoken in the plural of one, as in that saying: The kings of the earth stood by, and the rulers gathered together into one; for in the Acts of the Apostles only King Herod and the ruler Pilate are shown by the voice of Peter to have conspired against Christ. So too you have in Hebrews: They went about in goatskins, they were sawn asunder, and they stopped the jaws of lions, whereas only Elijah is taught to have had a sheepskin, Isaiah to have been sawn asunder, Daniel to have remained untouched by the lions.

123. How execrable in deed was the iniquity of the Jews, that they should crucify the Redeemer of all as a thief! Yet in the mystery he is a good thief, who lay in wait for the devil to carry off his vessels. In the mystical sense, however, the two thieves signify that two sinful peoples were to be crucified with Christ through baptism, whose disagreement signifies the diversity of those who alike believe. Indeed, one was on the left, the other on the right. The rebuke too reveals the future scandal of the cross even among believers.

124. And the Jews offered vinegar. Well, to consummate all things, the corruption of purity too is drunk, that all things which had been vitiated might be fixed to the cross. So vinegar is drunk; wine with gall is not drunk, not because it is gall, but because bitterness mixed with wine is refused. For certainly he took up the bitternesses of our life according to the condition of the body. Indeed he himself says: They gave gall for my food, and in my thirst they made me drink vinegar. But bitterness ought not to be mixed with purity, that it might be shown that the immortality of those who rise again will be without bitterness, and this immortality, since it had turned sour in the human vessel, is restored in Christ. Therefore vinegar is drunk, that is, the vice of the immortality corrupted through Adam is abolished on the reed, that it might be drawn out of the human body. Let us too pour over into Christ our vices, which have hardened through carelessness of mind and body; let us pour them over through baptism, that we may be crucified in Christ; let us pour them over through repentance, that from him the incorrupt purity of the heavenly wine and blood may be poured back into us.

125. Indeed, when he had drunk the vinegar, Accomplished he says are all things, because the whole mystery of the mortality he had assumed had been fulfilled, and, all vices having been drained away, the joy of immortality alone remained.

126. And therefore he says: Into your hands, Lord, I commend my spirit. And well is the spirit commended which is kept in reserve; for what is commended is certainly not lost. The spirit, then, is a good pledge, a good deposit. Whence that man too says: O Timothy, guard the good deposit. For the spirit is commended to the Father, and therefore he says: For you will not leave my soul in the underworld. But see a great mystery. Now he commends his spirit into the hands of the Father, now he sits in the bosom of the Father, because no other holds the whole Christ. Indeed I he says am in the Father and the Father in me. The spirit, therefore, is commended to the Father. But while it is in the higher places, it illumines the underworld too, that all things may be redeemed; for indeed Christ is all things and in Christ are all things, although Christ works in each one. The flesh dies, that it may rise again; the spirit is commended to the Father, that heavenly things too might be loosed from the bond of iniquity and there might be peace in heaven, which earthly things would follow.

127. And having said this he gave up the spirit. And well gave up, he who lost it not unwillingly. Indeed Matthew says: he sent forth the spirit; for what is sent forth is voluntary, what is lost is of necessity. Therefore he added: with a loud voice. In this there is either the glorious profession that he had descended even to death for our sins — so neither shall I blush to confess what Christ did not blush to profess with a loud voice — or the evident manifestation of God testifying to the separation of the divinity and the body. For so you have: Jesus cried with a loud voice, saying: God, my God, look upon me! Why have you forsaken me? The man cried out, about to die by the separation of the divinity. For since the divinity is free of death, there could certainly be no death unless life departed, because the divinity is life.

128. What follows now shows that because of the ungodliness of men the end of the world is to come. So it is signified through the Lord's Passion that present things will set, that future things may rise. Darkness, therefore, was poured over the eyes of the unbelieving, that the light of faith might rise again. The sun set for the sacrilegious, or fled back, that it might overshadow the spectacle of a deadly crime. The rocks were split, that through the broken crags of stone it might be shown that it was to come to pass that the force of the Word would penetrate hard hearts, so that those hunters whom Jeremiah foretold might the more easily hunt for the Lord out of the hollows of the rocks. And the opening of the tombs, what else does it signify but the resurrection of the dead, the bars of death being broken? In their appearing there was faith, in their going forth a type, because by going forth into the holy city they declared, under the appearance of present things, the future everlasting lodging of those who rise again in that Jerusalem which is in heaven. The veil too is rent, by which is declared either the division of the two peoples or the profanation of the mysteries of the synagogue. The old veil, therefore, is rent, that the new Church may hang up the veils of her faith. The covering of the synagogue is taken away, that we may discern the inner mysteries of religion with the unveiled gaze of the mind. Indeed now even the centurion confesses as the Son of God him whom he had crucified. O hearts of the Jews, harder than rocks! The rocks are split, but their hearts are hardened. The judge reproves, the servant believes, the traitor condemns his own crime by his death, the elements flee, the earth is shaken, the tombs are opened; yet the hardness of the Jews remains unmoved while the world is shaken.

129. And the women stood seeing these things; his mother too stood, since in the zeal of her dutiful love she put her own dangers second. But the Lord too, hanging on the cross, who despised his own dangers, commended his mother with dutiful affection. Not without purpose did John follow this out at greater length; for the others described the world shaken, the heaven covered with darkness, the sun fled back. Matthew and Mark, who followed out human and moral things more fully, added: God, my God, look upon me! Why have you forsaken me? That we might believe that the assumption of the human condition had reached even to the cross of Christ. But Luke plainly affirmed it as fitting that pardon was granted to the thief by priestly intercession, and that by the same gift indulgence was asked for the Jews who persecuted him.

130. John, therefore, who penetrated the divine mysteries more fully, not without reason labored to declare that she who had borne God remained a virgin. He alone, then, teaches me what the others did not teach: how, placed on the cross, he addressed his mother, thinking it of more account that, as victor over torments and punishments, victor over the devil, he was distributing the duties of dutiful love than that he was granting the heavenly kingdom. For if it is a religious thing that pardon is granted to the thief by the Lord, it is much more religious that a mother is honored by her son.

131. Nor let it be judged out of order that I have written of the thief's absolution before the address to his mother; for if he who had come to save sinners fulfilled first in my writings the task he had undertaken of redeeming the salvation of a sinner, it is not absurd. Indeed he himself says: Who is my mother, and who are my brothers? Because he had not come to call the righteous, but sinners. But there he spoke as the place required; here, even on the cross, not unmindful of his mother, he addresses her, saying: Behold your son, and to John: Behold your mother. Christ was making his testament from the cross, and John was sealing his testament, a witness worthy of so great a testator. A good testament, not of money but of life, which is written not with ink but with the Spirit of the living God. My tongue is the pen of a scribe writing swiftly.

132. But neither was Mary less than befitted the mother of Christ: while the apostles fled, she stood before the cross and with dutiful eyes looked upon the wounds of her son, because she was awaiting not the death of her child but the salvation of the world. Or perhaps, because she had learned that the redemption of the world would come through the death of her son, she, the royal hall, thought that by her own death too she would add something to the public gift. But Jesus needed no helper for the redemption of all, he who said: I have become like a man without help, free among the dead. He accepted indeed the affection of his mother, but he did not seek the aid of man. We have, therefore, a teacher of dutiful love. The reading teaches what a mother's affection should imitate, what the reverence of children should follow: that mothers should offer themselves in the dangers of their children, and that to children their mother's desolation should be a greater grief than the sorrow of their own death.

133. In this place a most abundant testimony to Mary's virginity is brought forward. For a wife is not taken away from her husband, since it is written: What God has joined, let man not separate; but she who for the sake of the mystery had put forward a marriage as a covering, now that the mysteries were completed, no longer needed marriage. Or, if we follow the moral sense, chastity is enjoined in mourning.

134. Yet it is mystical that she is commended to John, the youngest among the others, and this we ought not to receive with idle ears. For union with a young man and the beauty of youth is dangerous for women, lest perhaps some woman, curious about the example, careless of the mystery, desiring to wanton in Christ, should hold up Mary as a pretext in appearance but not imitate her in affection, as the common sort of women now has a bad name for leaving an older husband to follow a younger one. Let her learn, then, that here is the mystery of the Church, which, joined before to the older people in appearance, not in use, after she had brought forth the Word and sown it in the bodies and minds of men through faith in the cross and the burial of the Lord's body, by God's command chose fellowship with the younger people.

135. I ask too why we do not find that he was pierced before his death, but find it after his death, unless perhaps that his death may be shown to have been voluntary rather than of necessity, and that we may know the mystic order, because not the sacraments of the altar before baptism, but baptism first, then the cup. Next, that we may notice that, although the nature of his body was mortal, although its quality was similar, yet its grace was dissimilar. For certainly after death the blood congeals in our bodies, but from that body, incorrupt indeed but dead, the life of all flowed forth; for water and blood came out, the one to wash, the other to redeem. Let us drink, therefore, our price, that by drinking we may be redeemed.

136. What is meant too by the fact that not the apostles, but Joseph and Nicodemus bury Christ? The one righteous and steadfast, the other one in whom there was no guile; for such is the burial of Christ, which has no fraud or iniquity. So room for evasion is blocked, and the Jews are refuted by testimony from their own household; for if the apostles had buried him, they would surely say that he had not been buried, whom they said had been stolen away.

137. The righteous man covers the body of Christ with a linen cloth, the innocent man anoints it with ointment; for we find these things distinguished not without purpose, because righteousness clothes the Church, innocence supplies grace. Do you too, then, clothe the Lord's body with its glory, that you too may be righteous; even if you believe it dead, yet cover it with the fullness of its divinity. Anoint it with myrrh and aloes, that you may be the good odor of Christ. Joseph, that righteous man, sent a good linen cloth, and perhaps it was that which Peter saw let down to him from heaven, in which were kinds of four-footed beasts and wild beasts and birds, figured in the likeness of the Gentiles. With that mystic and genuine ointment, therefore, the Church is buried with him, which has united the diversity of peoples in the communion of her faith.

138. Of this Joseph I find in John alone that he came secretly to Pilate for fear of the Jews. How did a righteous man seek a hiding place through fear of danger? But I think that he asked secretly in order to obtain the body, not to avoid danger. And yet what wonder if the righteous man hid himself, when the apostles too, the teachers of the righteous, hid themselves? Come you too. Whether late or by night, or at whatever hour you come, you will find Jesus ready for you to look up to him, and paying no smaller wage to those who come later; for he who came at the sixth hour was not cheated of his wage, and he who came at the eleventh received the full wage. But Nicodemus too came by night. It was night, because there was not yet the resurrection. Indeed Christ rose again, and the righteous man says: The night is far gone, the day has drawn near.

139. This Joseph Luke called righteous, Matthew rich. And rightly is he called rich in this place, where he received the body of Christ; for in receiving the Rich One he knew no poverty of faith. He who is righteous, then, is rich. So he wraps it in a linen cloth; but the Israelite both mingles diverse fragrances of virtues and puts in aloes, about a hundred pounds, that is, the measure of perfect faith. And they bound the body of Jesus according to the custom of the spiritual Jews, not indeed with the knots of unbelief, but with the bindings of faith, and laid it in a garden, to which the Church is often compared, which has the fruits of diverse merits and the flowers of virtues.

140. Not without purpose did one call it a new tomb, another the tomb of Joseph. Christ, then, did not have a tomb of his own. For a tomb is prepared for those who are under the law of death; the victor over death does not have a tomb of his own. For what fellowship has a tomb with God? Indeed Ecclesiastes says of him who meditates on good things: And there is no burial for him. The death of Christ, then, is special, beyond the common death of all, and therefore he is not buried with others, but is shut alone in a tomb; for the Incarnation of the Lord had all things in the likeness of men, but the likeness is with a difference of nature: from a virgin, with likeness of birth, with unlikeness of conception. He healed the sick, but he commanded. John baptized with water, he with the Spirit. And so the death of Christ is common according to the nature of the body, special according to its power.

141. But who is Joseph, in whose tomb he is laid? Surely that righteous man. Well, therefore, is Christ entrusted to the tomb of a righteous man, that the Son of Man may have where to lay his head and may rest in the dwelling of righteousness. And well in a new one: according to the letter, indeed, lest it be said by the unbelieving that another had been raised; but in the mystical sense what can we understand, unless perhaps because we read: Their throat is an open sepulchre? An open sepulchre, then, is the throat of man, in which deadly unbelief and dead words are buried, which through age and some incursion of beasts crumbles and falls apart. There is, then, on the contrary, a tomb in the secret places of men, which the righteous man has hewn out for himself with the penetrating Word in hearts of Gentile hardness, polished by the works of faith and doctrine, that the power of Christ might reach out among the nations.

142. To it, most beautifully, a stone was rolled, that it might not stand open; for whoever has buried Christ well within himself, let him guard him diligently, lest he lose him, or lest there be an entrance for unbelief. For you see that Peter and John were found worthy to enter first. Indeed John himself did not go in before he believed.

143. And well is it said to have been hewn in rock, that is, in the firmness of faith, from which the true Israelites have sucked the sweetness of honey and the spiritual oil. For the righteous man and he who saw God buried Christ; for none bury Christ except those who believe God.

144. Not all can bury Christ. Indeed, although the religious women stand far off, yet, because they are religious, they carefully note the place, that they may bring ointments and pour them over him. Yet in their anxiety they both withdraw from the tomb later and return to the tomb earlier. Although steadfastness is lacking, diligence is not lacking.

145. Their sex wavers, their devotion is warm. Indeed at the time of the resurrection they are at hand, and when the men were put to flight, they alone are admonished by the angel not to fear. Earlier in zeal but later in confidence, they call Peter out. Indeed he comes without fear, and he who had come later steadfastly enters first, as one who had received the keys of the kingdom, to open to others.

146. The earthquake, moreover, is resurrection to the faithful, fear to the unskilled: to the one because the sluggish body stirs itself from the sleep of death, to the other because, troubled by the trembling of the body and earthly instability, they fall away from faith and belief in the resurrection.

147. But on the morning of the sabbath they came at a very early hour to the tomb. A great doubt arises in this place for many; for although the evangelists do not seem to have said contrary things, yet they said different things, since here: in the morning at a very early hour, Mark very early, Matthew in the evening of the sabbath, John on the first of the sabbath, while it was still dark, they said the women came to the tomb. Then they recorded that here two men were seen, in Mark one young man sitting in white, in Matthew one angel, in John two angels sitting in white. Lastly, what may seem hardly possible to unravel, John wrote that it was said to Mary Magdalene: Do not touch me; for I have not yet ascended to my Father; Matthew wrote that the Lord met Mary Magdalene and the other Mary, and set out in a most evident description that they came up and held his feet and worshiped.

148. How then is it to be resolved, unless you think that the four evangelists spoke of four different times, so that you may infer both different persons of women and different visions? Indeed some come with ointment on the first of the sabbath, others without ointment in the evening of the sabbath. The name of these is expressed; those are described as having followed the Lord from Galilee.

149. In this place, lest at the end the roughness of a thorny interpretation should offend anyone, where perhaps pleasant things were expected, consider that we are now lowering the sails to complete the discourse, since we have come to the harbor; and a ship which has crossed the seas at a swift course, when it begins to draw near the shore, grazes the hidden rocks with its course checked. So, lest my discourse stick in the sands of the shore as if under a sluggish helmsman, I think the way should be slackened rather than rushed, as though I were skirting blind shallows, so that our speech may not crack open, full of leaks.

150. First, then, we must look at this: what is meant by what is written, that the Lord rose again in the evening of the sabbath which dawns into the first of the sabbath. For so you have that in the evening of the sabbath Mary Magdalene came, and the other Mary, to see the sepulchre. And behold, there was a great earthquake. For not on the sabbath day — for they kept silence on the sabbath according to the commandment — but after the sabbath day, certainly in the night, he rose again. Indeed those who came in the morning, though at a very early hour, yet learned that the Lord had already risen.

151. So, then, a balance must be struck, that the resurrection be believed to have taken place neither in the morning of the Lord's day, which is the first after the sabbath, nor on the sabbath. For how else is the three days' period completed? He rose, therefore, not as the day was drawing to evening, but in the evening of the night. Indeed the Greek said late, that is, ὀψέ. And late both marks the hour at the setting of the day and signifies the lateness of any thing, as if you should say: It was suggested to me late, that is, tardily; he came late, that is: he who arrives after the appointed time. Though he came in the morning of the following day, it is late nonetheless, when the time for acting has passed. Late is also the depth of the night, as if you should say: I rose late to work by lamplight, that is: I rose not in the evening time but in the depth of the night.

152. Hence the women too have the opportunity of approaching the tomb, the guards being certainly by then at rest, and the guards themselves are the more terrified, as happens to those roused from sleep. Lastly, the chief priests too, gathered with the elders, confirm that this happened at night, saying to the guards: Say that his disciples came by night and stole him while we were sleeping. For from the time, which they learned from the guards, they seize on a pretext for fraud. For John too indicates that in the morning, while it was still dark, Mary Magdalene came to him and to Peter, and yet presents her as unaware of the resurrection that had taken place; for certainly, if it had taken place as the day was drawing to evening, it could at once have become known.

153. It is morning, and still Peter does not know, John does not know. Would the Lord have allowed his disciples to be tormented longer by uncertainty about his death, the disciples to whom at once the angel, at once the Lord sent the women as messengers of what had been done? And that you may know that it happened at night, some women do not know, others know. Those know who kept watch nights and days; those do not know who had withdrawn. One Mary Magdalene does not know, according to John; the other Mary Magdalene knows, according to Matthew; for the same woman could not both know before and not know afterwards. Therefore, if there are several Marys, perhaps there are several Magdalenes too, since the former is the name of a person, the latter of places.

154. Recognize, then, that she is another. The one is allowed to hold the Lord's feet, this one is forbidden to touch the Lord; the one was found worthy to see an angel, this one, when she first came, saw no one; the one announced to the disciples that the Lord had risen, this one indicates that he has been stolen away; the one rejoices, this one weeps; the one Christ meets already in his glory, this one still seeks him as dead; the one saw the Lord and believed, this one could not recognize him when she saw him; the one worshiped in spirit with faithful affection, this one was saddened with doubting affection.

155. Rightly indeed is she forbidden to touch the Lord; for we touch Christ not by bodily touch but by faith. For I have not yet he says ascended to my Father, that is, I have not yet ascended for you, who seek the living among the dead; and therefore she is sent to the stronger, by whose example she may learn to believe, that they may preach the resurrection.

156. For as in the beginning the woman was the author of guilt to the man, and the man the executor of the error, so now she who had first tasted death first saw the resurrection, first in the remedy as in the order of guilt. And lest she should bear among men the reproach of perpetual guilt, she who had passed on guilt to the man passed on grace too, and makes up for the misery of the ancient fall by the announcement of the resurrection. Through the mouth of a woman death had gone forth before; through the mouth of a woman life is restored.

157. But because her steadfastness is less fit for preaching and her sex weaker for carrying it out, the office of evangelizing is entrusted to men. For just as through Jesus the guilt of women is not only loosed but grace is also multiplied, so that she who had once deceived one man may persuade many, so too the man, who had rashly believed before, had to recover the gift with interest, that he who had himself been unsteady in believing might become fit for preaching to others.

158. But let us mark the very words of the command: Do not touch me; for I have not yet ascended to the Father. But go to my brothers and say to them: I ascend to my Father and to your Father, my God and your God. How had you not ascended, Lord Jesus? How were you absent, who had commended your spirit into the hands of the Father? Or when can you be absent, who are always in the Father, always with the Father? Indeed you yourself said: If I go down into the underworld, you are there: if I take my wings before the light and dwell in the uttermost part of the sea, even there your hand will lead me. Or how do you ascend, who are always everywhere?

159. You came down indeed as the Son of Man, and when you came down you were not absent from the Father; but you came down for us, that we might see you with our eyes and minds, that we might believe in you. So you ascended too for us, that we might follow with our minds you whom we cannot see with our eyes. You ascended for the apostles, to whom you said: He who has seen me has seen the Father too. Indeed John knew where he should seek you. He sought you with the Father and found you, and therefore he says: And the Word was with God. You ascended for Paul too, who, not content to follow you by himself, taught us too how we should follow you and where we could find you, saying: If then you have risen together with Christ, seek the things that are above, where Christ is, sitting at the right hand of God. And lest we should think this a duty of the eyes rather than of the minds, he added: Set your mind on the things that are above, not on those upon the earth.

160. Therefore we ought to seek you not above the earth nor on the earth nor according to the flesh, if we wish to find you; now indeed we no longer know Christ according to the flesh. Indeed Stephen did not seek above the earth, he who saw you standing at the right hand of God; but Mary, who was seeking on earth, could not touch. Stephen touched, because he sought in heaven; Stephen saw you absent, among the Jews; Mary did not see you present, among angels. But why she could not touch, the evangelist himself has taught us, saying that when she saw you, she did not know that it was you. For so you have: She turned back and saw Jesus standing, and did not know that it was Jesus. Rightly could she not touch, who had not been able to see; for he who sees touches.

161. And so the Scripture distinguishes what difference there is between that Mary and this one. The one runs to meet him, that she may see Jesus; this one turns back; the one is greeted, this one is rebuked. Indeed so you have: Jesus says to her: Woman. She who does not believe is a woman and is still designated by the name of bodily sex; for she who believes runs to meet him into the perfect man, into the measure of the age of the fullness of Christ, lacking now the name of the world, the sex of the body, the slipperiness of youth, the talkativeness of old age. So Jesus says: Woman, why are you weeping? As if he said: God requires not bare tears, but faith. Good are tears, if you recognize Christ. Whom do you seek? he says; for the Lord condemns belated efforts. But well did he add whom, not that he doubts whom she is seeking, but because she does not know whom she seeks; for she does not seek Christ, whom she thinks stolen away. Christ is present: how is he sought? He who is sought, then, is not known, when he is not recognized though he is seen.

162. Indeed she saw Christ and thought him the gardener; for so you have: She, supposing that he was the gardener, said to him: Lord, if you have taken him away, tell me where you have laid him, and I will take him away. Though uncertain in faith, yet she is not slippery in speech; though she thought him the gardener, yet she designated the Son of God; though she does not yet believe, yet she strives to believe; for he himself who raised the body took it away. Pardonable, therefore, is the error of the woman, who, although she ought not to have doubted that the body of Christ had been taken up through the glory of the resurrection, yet longs to be taught by Christ. And in her devotion she now promises her faith, that she too may now take him away from the earth and seek him at the right hand of God.

163. Indeed after these words she is called no longer woman, but now Mary; for the one is the common name of the crowd, the other the special name of a person following Christ. And she is sent to the disciples, though not yet a judge of full faith, yet as a messenger. But she is forbidden to touch, because she had not yet learned, as Paul had, that the fullness of the divinity dwells bodily in Christ; she had not yet emptied out the slipperiness of the world, the uncertainty of the flesh; she had not yet lived the life of Christ. Indeed this one neither worships the Lord nor holds his feet, as that other Mary did, in whom surely there is signified not so much bodily homage as the affection of full faith, because she believes Christ to be both man and God; for it is God who is worshiped, man who is held.

164. The Lord, then, whose feet Mary too anointed with ointment, does not disdain to be touched by a woman, nor does he scorn her touch, but teaches progress, because not all can touch Christ rising again who touched him while he dwelt in this life and body. Let him who wishes to touch Christ mortify his members and, made like him who was to rise again, put on the heart of mercy, and not hesitate to renounce earthly things.

165. What, then, is Do not touch me? Do not lay your hand on greater things, but go to my brothers, that is, to the more perfect — for whoever does the will of my Father who is in heaven, he is my brother and sister and mother — because the resurrection cannot easily be grasped except by the more perfect; the prerogative of this faith is kept for those more firmly grounded, but I do not permit women to teach in the church; let them ask their husbands at home. She is sent, therefore, to those who are of the household, and she received the commands prescribed for her.

166. Nor does it escape notice in this place that some have thought that Christ was unwilling to be touched because he had not yet received back the form which he had commended to the Father, as if she ought not yet to touch him.

167. I ascend to your Father, my God and your God. Well did he distinguish, because he was speaking to a woman; for we have no nature in common with Christ except that of the human condition. To him he is Father by his own generation, to us by voluntary adoption; to him by nature, to us by grace; to him he is God by the unity of the mystery, to us by heavenly power.

168. Someone says: How then did Thomas, while he did not yet believe, nevertheless touch Christ? But he seems to have doubted not about the Lord's resurrection but about the quality of the resurrection, and by his touch he had to teach me, as Paul too taught: For this corruptible must put on incorruption, and this mortal put on immortality, that the unbelieving may believe and the hesitant may be unable to doubt; for we more easily believe what we see. Now Thomas had cause for wonder when he saw, with all things shut, a body brought in through enclosures impassable to bodies, the structure unbroken; and therefore it is a wonder how a bodily nature poured itself through an impenetrable body, with an invisible entrance, a visible appearance, easy to touch, hard to understand.

169. Indeed the disciples, troubled, thought that they were seeing a spirit, and therefore the Lord, to show us the form of the resurrection, Handle me he says and see, for a spirit does not have flesh and bones, as you see that I have. Not, then, through an incorporeal nature, but through the quality of a bodily resurrection, he passed through what was shut and impassable to ordinary use. For what is touched is body, what is handled is body; and in the body we rise again; there is sown indeed a natural body, there rises a spiritual body, but that body is finer, this one coarser, as being still compacted with the quality of earthly decay.

170. For how was it not a body, in which there remained the marks of the wounds, the traces of the scars, which the Lord offered to be handled? In this he not only strengthens faith, but also sharpens devotion, in that he chose to carry into heaven the wounds received for us, and would not abolish them, that he might show to God the Father the price of our freedom. Such a one the Father places at his right hand, embracing the trophies of our salvation; such were the martyrs he showed us there by the crown of his scar.

171. And since our discourse has come out at this point, let us consider for what reason the apostles, according to John, believed and rejoiced, while according to Luke they are rebuked as unbelieving; there they received the Holy Spirit, here they are bidden to remain in the city until they are clothed with power from on high. And it seems to me that the one, as an apostle, touched greater and loftier things, this one what follows and lies nearest to human things: this one used the circuit of history, the other conciseness; because neither can there be doubt about him who bears witness of things at which he himself was present, and his testimony is true. From this one too, who was found worthy to be an evangelist, it is right that every suspicion either of negligence or of falsehood be repelled. And therefore we think both true, not distinguished by a variety of statements or a diversity of persons. For although Luke says at first that they did not believe, yet he shows afterwards that they did believe. And if we consider the first things, they are contrary; if the following things, it is certain that they agree. Let us therefore consider the very words of Scripture.

172. John speaks thus: And the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord. So he says to them again: Peace be with you; as the Father has sent me, I also send you. And when he had said this, he breathed on them and said: Receive the Holy Spirit. Whose sins you forgive, they are forgiven them, and whose you retain, they are retained. But Luke says: And how they knew him in the breaking of the bread. And while they were saying these things, he himself stood in the midst of them and said to them: Peace be with you; it is I, do not fear. And troubled and terrified, they thought they were seeing a spirit. And it might seem that more were present here; but because here too it was in the evening of the day of the resurrection — for those two who, as the day was already drawing to evening, had gone in to stay with the Lord, when he suddenly withdrew himself, are shown to have returned to the disciples at the same hour, when he offered himself to be handled — and according to John too he is presented as having appeared to the disciples in the evening on that day, the first of the sabbaths, and as having offered his wounds to be handled, we thought it should be inquired into more carefully, lest it should bring any ambiguity.

173. For he seems to have shown himself separately to these eleven, just as he had already shown himself apart in the evening to Ammaon and Cleopas, and as those two, so too those eleven seem to have been able to come together to strengthen the rest. Indeed they also were troubled, as you have according to Luke, and therefore he opened their understanding, that they might understand the things that are written. And there is no doubt that this one wrote more broadly, that one more succinctly. For how would they say that he had been seen by Cephas alone, if he had been seen by all? But as among the women he was seen by Mary and by the other Mary Magdalene, so among the men he was seen by Peter early in the morning. And Paul speaks thus: For I delivered to you first of all that Christ died according to the Scriptures, and that he was buried, and that he rose on the third day according to the Scriptures, and was seen by Cephas. And therefore Mark specially introduces the young man bidding the women tell Peter and the disciples that the Lord had risen.

174. Peter, therefore, alone saw the Lord; for his devotion, always ready and prompt, believed, and therefore he was eager to gather the more frequent signs of faith. Now with John, now alone, yet everywhere he runs untiring, everywhere either alone or first; not content to have seen what he had seen, he goes back to look again, and, kindled with love of seeking the Lord, he is not sated with seeing. He sees alone, he sees with the eleven, he sees with the seventy, he sees also when Thomas believed, he sees when he was fishing; but not content to have seen, impatient in his longing, heedless of the catch, unmindful of danger, yet not unmindful of reverence, when he saw the Lord on the shore, he covered himself with his garment, thinking it too late if he should arrive with the others in the boat. So, when the Lord was walking on the sea, he ran to meet him over the waves of the sea, forgetful of his own nature; so, when the Lord was being held by the Jews, he alone drew a sword against the crowds; so now too, when the Lord had stood on the shore, by a perilous shortcut he hastened his religious homage.

175. There is no doubt, then, that Peter believed, and believed because he loved, and loved because he believed. Whence he is also saddened, because he is asked even a third time: Do you love me dearly? For he is questioned about whom there is doubt; but the Lord does not doubt, who was asking not that he might learn but that he might teach whom, as he was about to be lifted up into heaven, he was leaving to us as the vicar, as it were, of his love. For so you have: Simon son of John, do you love me? Surely you know, Lord, that I love you dearly. And Jesus says: Feed my lambs. Well aware of himself, Peter testifies to an affection not taken up for the moment but long known to God. For who else is there who could easily profess this of himself? And therefore, because he alone of all professes it, he is preferred to all; for greater than all is charity.

176. That too must be looked at more carefully, why, when the Lord said: Do you love me? he answered: You know, Lord, that I love you dearly. In this, love seems to me to have the charity of the mind, but loving dearly a certain heat conceived from the ardor of body and mind; and I think that Peter signifies the fervor not only of his mind but also of his body in the worship of God. Indeed the third time the Lord asked no longer Do you love me? but Do you love me dearly? And no longer is he bidden to feed lambs, as at first, to be nourished with a kind of milk, nor little sheep, as the second time, but sheep, the more perfect, that he who was more perfect might govern them.

177. And therefore, as to one perfect in all things, whom the flesh could no longer call back from the glory of suffering, a crown is decreed. When you were younger he says you girded yourself and walked where you wished; but when you are old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will gird you and lead you where you do not wish. Good is old age, not weak for use through length of life, but prepared for martyrdom by the maturity of virtue; which represses the wantonness of bodily pleasures and does not indulge desires, turns aside from everything sweet, does not seek after what is fair to see; for the flesh lusts against the spirit, and finds for itself crosswise paths of diverse pleasures by which to walk where it wills. But good is the old age of the mind, which chooses not what it thinks sweet to the body but what is useful to the mind, and is not captured by the willful appetite of the body, but is called back, as though unwilling, by a resisting governance.

178. So Peter too, although he was ready in mind to undergo martyrdom, yet when the danger came, he bent the constancy of his mind; for the enjoyment of the heavenly gift holds us captive by its sweetness. For who would not choose martyrdom, if he could die gladly? So Peter too seems unwilling, but he prepares to conquer. And what wonder if Peter is unwilling, when the Lord says: Father, if it can be, take this cup from me; yet not my will, but yours be done? Indeed after the testing of his presumption Peter no longer dares to promise perseverance of will, but, as if for his own comfort, seeks the companionship of another.

179. Led, then, by so many examples of virtue, we believe that Peter could not have doubted. It is clear too that John believed when he saw the Savior, he who already then believed, after he saw the sepulchre empty of the body. Why then does Luke present them as having been troubled? First of all because the statement about the greater part includes the opinion of the few; next because, although Peter had believed in the resurrection, he could yet be troubled when he saw that the Lord, with his body, poured himself unexpectedly even into places that were enclosed with barred entrances and built walls. Luke, therefore, followed out each thing historically; the one looked to the end, the other to the sequence. For certainly in saying: Then he opened their understanding, that they might understand the things that are written, he himself too professes that the disciples believed.

180. The Holy Spirit he either breathed upon those eleven as the more perfect and promises that he is to be bestowed on the rest later, or he breathed him on the same men there and promised him here. Nor does this seem contrary, since there are divisions of graces; to one indeed is given the word of wisdom, to another the word of knowledge according to the same Spirit, to another faith in the same Spirit, to another the grace of healings, to another kinds of tongues. So there he breathed one operation, here he promises another; for there the grace of forgiving sins was bestowed, which seems to be more august, and therefore it is breathed by Christ, that you may believe that the Spirit is Christ's and believe that the Spirit is from God; for God alone forgives sins. But Luke describes the grace of tongues poured out. Indeed there you have: Receive the Holy Spirit; whose sins you forgive, they will be forgiven. But in the Acts of the Apostles you have thus: And they were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in various tongues, as the Spirit gave them to speak.

181. The diversity of the visions signifies the frequency of the angels ministering, according to what the Lord himself had promised, saying: And you will see angels descending and ascending to the Son of Man. And would that our discourse too might close with the end of the Gospel!

182. Why, according to Matthew and Mark, does he command the disciples: I will go before you into Galilee; there you will see me, but according to Luke and John he offered himself to be seen even within a closed room? And indeed that he often offered himself to be seen, both to more than five hundred brothers and to Peter and James, we have proved also by the testimony of the Apostle, and Luke taught in the Acts of the Apostles that he showed the disciples that he was alive after his Passion, appearing to them in many proofs and discussing the kingdom of God. Therefore, because he appeared quite often and to different persons, since Scripture has by no means marked a prescribed and defined time when he was seen in Galilee, while it has expressed both the day and the hour when he offered himself in Jerusalem, the more timid were visited within closed rooms, the stronger gathered at the mountain.

183. Indeed John presents the disciples gathered within a closed room, the doors shut, for fear of the Jews, which Luke wrote were not eleven but more. But Matthew did not keep silent that these eleven alone met in Galilee. Indeed so you have: But the eleven disciples went into Galilee, to the mountain where Jesus had appointed them, and seeing him they worshiped, but some doubted. To them he gave the power of teaching and baptizing. Mark too writes at the end that he appeared to the eleven disciples as they sat at table, when likewise he commands them the office of preaching throughout the whole world.

184. Hence I think this more fitting: that the Lord did indeed command the disciples to see him in Galilee, but that he offered himself first to them while they stayed within the closed room through fear, and that afterwards, their minds having been strengthened, those eleven made for Galilee. Or certainly — I find that this too has pleased careful writers — nothing prevents us from saying that there were fewer within the closed room, and very many on the mountain.
